The Mist Walker

The Mist Walker

Part I — The place beside the fire

Chapter 1 — A place left open

The carrier would not take his mule past the last cairn.

He said it was the mule. The mule stood in the rain with its ears laid back and its feet planted, and he looked at Riarhin across its wet neck with the expression of a man who had found an animal willing to hold his opinions for him.

“It’s a short walk. You can see the smoke.”

She could. Beyond the cairn the track ran on over the heath and dipped out of sight where the ground fell towards the river. A grey thread of smoke stood above the dip and bent away east.

A week in the back of his cart had taught her that he argued well and lost cheerfully and that the losing took most of an afternoon. She took out the purse and counted the coins into his palm, one at a time, the way he liked to watch them go. At eight she stopped, tipped the rest into her hand without looking, and gave him those too.

“That’s over.”

“For the letters.”

He touched the place inside his coat where they lay wrapped in oilcloth. A man he swore by would take them on from the coast road to Payden’s Port and the mill behind the line.

“If they ask me where I left you?”

“By the cairn. In the rain. With a monk.”

He laughed, and while he was still laughing the mule decided the conversation was over and began to turn. Cadan stepped back from the wheel. The carrier lifted a hand without looking round and went over the rise at the brisk walk of an animal going home.

She checked what had survived the journey before she checked anything else.

There was a flat rock beside the cairn with a lip of heather over it. She knelt there and opened the pack. Spare linen, damp at the folds. The whetstone in its rag. The comb with three teeth gone and a fourth thinking about it. Oilcloth, tinderbox, a coil of rope that had been dry when she bought it. Dried apple, oatcake, a heel of hard cheese. The water bottle. Asta’s packet of clean linen, still tied in Asta’s knot, because Riarhin had not yet needed it badly enough to undo it.

Toothpick was at her belt. The Last Blade beside it, its shortened point dressed and oiled. She touched them both and left them.

Remorse she drew.

She did it left-handed, from the scabbard on her back, as she had drawn it every morning since the fortress. The blade came clear without catching. At the root of the grip, beneath the binding, the little block above her fingers moved against its seat and made a sound. A tick, small and dry, like a fingernail on a table.

On the left side of the grip the binding had been cut through. A blade in the fortress had found it and gone no further. She had tied the cut ends down with thread. The thread was holding.

“It is louder,” Cadan said.

He was standing a little way off with his hands in his sleeves, watching the track.

“Than when?”

“Than at the coast.”

She put her thumb against the block and felt the faint lift of it under the wrapping. She knew how the binding had been laid, every turn, because she had laid it herself in the same pattern for longer than she cared to think about, and before that a man in the Valewood had cut the first length to the width of her hand and given it to her on the day she left.

“I’ll see to it.”

“Very well.”

She sheathed the sword. The block ticked again on the way in. She stood, and her right thigh took the weight with the small cold complaint it made now only after hills. Across the back of her left hand the cut from the fortress had scabbed into a dark line that cracked when she made a fist. She made one anyway, opened it, and picked up the pack.

The Tulkani had camped in a ring above the river with their wheels chocked into wet turf.

There were seven wagons, the wheels painted each a different colour and faded to the colours of things left out: the blue of a washed shirt, the red of brick dust, a yellow gone to cheese rind. Smoke came from two stovepipes and from a cook fire under an awning. Beyond the ring, at the lip of the heath, stood the Painted Stone.

It was taller than a man and narrow, a little bent at the top like someone listening. Bands of colour went round it from foot to crown, ochre and white and a dull green, worn in places to the grey of the stone. Somebody had renewed the lowest band not long ago, and the new paint had run in the rain and made pale fingers down into the grass. Riarhin put her hand flat on it as she passed. It was warm, as a wall is warm on the far side from a hearth.

Between the stone and the slope a post had been driven into the ground. Its foot was shod in brass. A red cord was tied to it at the height of a child’s shoulder, and a lamp hung above the cord, unlit. A boy sat beneath the lamp on a folded sack. When they reached the ring he got up and walked away towards the cook fire as if he had remembered something there.

An old woman came out instead.

She was small and upright and carried a stick she did not lean on. Her grey braid was thick as a wrist and hung to her waist. A cord was knotted round her right wrist, silk and horsehair. Riarhin counted eight knots and stopped counting. There was one more after them, loose, the end hanging.

“Water and wine,” the old woman said in Tulkani. “Take whichever pleases you.”

“Water first.”

The woman looked at her for a moment, then at Cadan, longer.

“You’re the house’s man.”

“I am.”

“We had a letter. Somebody’s cousin wrote it for somebody else.” She turned her stick in the turf. “I’m Gulshat. Sit. There’s a court sitting, and you’re in it now, and nobody will ask you anything until they’ve finished being angry with each other.”

The court sat on a carpet laid over the wet grass in the middle of the ring.

It had a border of red lozenges and a middle worn to the brown weft. A barrel stood at one end, and a woman sat on the barrel. A cord lay on the ground in front of her, from the barrel’s foot to the carpet’s edge and off into the grass.

Riarhin knew the form. She went round the end of the cord, not over it, and sat where Gulshat pointed. Cadan sat beside her and folded his hands.

The woman on the barrel was Nergui: long-faced, about sixty, in a man’s coat with the sleeves turned back. Gulshat lowered herself onto a cushion at the carpet’s other end. The circle stood or sat round the edges under whatever kept the rain off. A man leaned on a wheel with an unlit pipe in his mouth.

In front of the barrel a young man was kneeling with a baby on his back.

The child was tied on with a shawl, asleep, her face turned against his shoulder. He knelt very straight so as not to wake her. Gulshat gave Riarhin the whole of it under her breath, in the flat voice of somebody who had given it many times that week. His name was Sovra. His wife had died on the road a month before, between one camp and the next, of a fever that came up in the night. They had buried her above a ford. Her memory-cord had been left in the wagon because nobody thought to put it in her hand. A wife’s cord went with her. Without it she would keep looking for it, and would not settle.

“And he can’t simply walk down,” Riarhin said.

“Nobody can simply walk down. You can walk down with leave.”

Sovra held the cord in one hand, a short brown and white thing, much knotted. In the other he held a single knot of old red cord gone dark, tied round a wooden toggle.

“Her family’s,” Gulshat said. “From a gathering when they were all at the one fire. It says, when one of ours needs the door, you’ll let them through. I accept the knot,” she said, louder, to the carpet and the barrel and the cord on the ground. “It was tied well. I saw it tied.”

“I saw it cut,” said Nergui.

She had not raised her voice. Everybody heard her. The baby moved her mouth and slept on.

“Her family were barred from this circle the year the river took the lower field. They promised two horses for the crossing, and when it came to it they took the horses north and sold them to a man in a red hat. Our people carried the barrels across on their backs. My brother carried barrels on his back.” She looked down at Sovra. “I don’t hold it against you. You weren’t born. I hold it against the knot.”

“A knot isn’t a horse.”

“A knot is what was said.”

“A knot is what was given. What they did afterwards is afterwards.”

“Then why was it cut?”

Gulshat did not answer. The man at the wheel took the pipe out of his mouth and looked into it.

It was an old quarrel; neither woman needed to finish a sentence. It had been going on longer than Sovra had been alive. He knelt in the middle of it with his wife’s cord in one hand and the old knot in the other and did not look up.

Nergui turned her head.

“You. The one who walks out of the mist. You’ve been on the road with our people.”

“Yes.”

“What would you do?”

Every face under every awning turned. Riarhin felt it as weather. Beside her Cadan did not move at all.

Gulshat lifted one hand from her stick.

“She’ll wait,” Gulshat said. “A guest sits.”

Riarhin sat.

It went on into the afternoon. Nobody shouted. They brought people. An old man was helped down from a wagon to say he had seen the horses go north and had thought at the time it was a pity, because one was a good mare. A woman Sovra’s age stood up to say his wife had nursed her son through the coughing winter and asked nothing for it, and that a cord was not the same as a woman. Nergui said nobody had said it was.

The light turned the colour of tin. Sovra’s knees had sunk into the carpet. Someone brought him water and he drank it without taking his eyes off the barrel.

There was no judgment at the end. Riarhin listened for one and heard instead a shape being found by two people who had found such shapes before. Sovra could go down, on the knot and the circle’s leave together, so that neither woman had given way to the other. He could go as far as the first station and no farther. He could not go alone. The child stayed.

“With who?” Sovra said. It was the first thing he had said.

Nergui looked at Riarhin, then at Cadan.

“They’re going down anyway.”

“We are going for another reason,” Cadan said.

“Then you’ll go for two.”

Gulshat nodded slowly, as if it had been her idea. She picked up the end of the cord on the ground and held it out across the carpet. Sovra took it. The baby woke and began to cry.

Batu showed her the trouble at the ford. He was the boy from under the lamp, about twelve, with a scab on his chin and a habit of answering questions by pointing. He took her to the lip of the heath and pointed down.

The river was brown and quick and not deep. A ford crossed it below a stand of alder, its bed paved with flat stones the Tulkani had laid themselves over generations so that wheels would not sink.

“In the mornings,” Batu said. “When it’s misty. There’s a stair.”

“Going down?”

“Coming up.”

He watched her face to see whether she believed him. She waited.

“Old Hesk came up it. He died before the snow. He came up and sat by the fire and asked who’d taken his pipe. And Nergui’s brother. And a baby nobody knew.” He scratched his chin and made it bleed. “Grandmother says they’re going the wrong way.”

Rain made rings on the brown water. There was no stair.

Nereth’s wards had fastened a copied stair onto something on the far side, and the copy went on pulling after the girl who made it was in custody, teaching other keepers her sequences in a cold room above a mill. Maren Thorne kept the line. The houses behind it were standing. And here, a week’s carting away, the pull came up through a Tulkani ford with Old Hesk on it asking for his pipe.

“Does the lamp help?” she asked.

Batu considered this with care.

“It’s for you. For coming back.”

They made the marker ready in the last of the light.

Cadan did most of it and explained nothing she did not need. The red cord on the post was to stay tied. A second length of the same red he measured against his outstretched arms, cut, coiled and put inside his habit. The lamp would be filled and trimmed and left dark.

“It is lit when someone is to come back. Not before.”

Batu, on his sack, pointed at himself.

“Every night,” Gulshat said. “Until.”

She had come down with a basket and was putting things into Riarhin’s pack without asking whether there was room: salt, dried mutton, a flat round loaf, a pot sealed with wax that she said was for wounds and that Riarhin suspected was for bread. Foot-wrappings of clean wool, rolled tight. Riarhin moved the rope to make space. Gulshat moved it back.

“The check,” Cadan said. “I should like to hear it said.”

“On the seventh day from tomorrow I light the hour candle at dusk. When it burns down I go to the post and speak through to the mouth of your station. Once.” She held up one finger, knotted at the knuckle. “I tell you what the ford did that morning. You don’t answer. Then I come back up and go to bed.”

“Once.”

“I’m old. Once is generous.”

Riarhin thought of the letter going north in the carrier’s coat. She had written it at the hill farm on the back of a bill of sale, small, because the paper was small.

I am at the Tulkani stopping place called the Painted Stone, a week’s carting south and west of you. I am going down with Cadan to find what Nereth’s stair is fastened to, and whether it can be taken off without bringing your line down with it. If I am not back by the first snow, ask at the Stone for Gulshat. If she is dead, ask for the boy who keeps the lamp. Do not send anyone after me. Kelva, eat something yourself.

The note to Coryd had been shorter. Not on the first. I am sorry. I will come when I can. She had added Tell Sivelle the stair still does not creak, and then thought it a stupid thing to send down a road, and sent it.

“You’ll want this.”

Gulshat held out a small knot on a leather thong, old, dark with handling.

“For the sitting. Most people who walk out of the mist can’t sit. Give that to any of us and they owe you a tale. A tale, mind. Not a life.”

Riarhin put it over her head and tucked it inside her shirt. It was warm from Gulshat’s hand.

They ate at the cook fire with Sovra between them. Barley again, mutton in it this time, and something sour she could not place. Sovra fed his daughter from his own spoon, blowing on each mouthful. The child grabbed the spoon and dropped it and was given it again. When she slept he passed her to a woman who had been waiting at his elbow with her arms half out already, and watched her carried into the wagon with the yellow wheel. The door shut.

He did not eat anything else.

Cadan finished his bowl with small neat movements. His lantern stood on the grass by his foot, its little brass door closed, the white candle inside it whole and unlit.

“You will sleep,” he told Sovra.

“I won’t.”

“Then you will lie down and rest your eyes, and in the morning you will tell me you did not sleep.”

Sovra stared at him. Something in his face gave, and he laughed once, wetly, and wiped his nose on his wrist.

Riarhin got up to take the bowls back.

At the edge of the ring a blanket had been put out to air over a wagon rail. It was striped red and dun, the fringe knotted in little bunches. The wind lifted one corner and let it fall. Across the wet air it smelt, faintly, of smoke and clean wool.

She stood with the bowls in her hands until the corner lifted again. Then she set them in the basin by the fire, wiped her hands on her coat, and went to find the wagon where they had said there was room for her to lie down.

Chapter 2 — Conversations in the wall

The mist came up from the river before it was light.

Batu woke her by knocking on the wagon’s side. She had not slept much. The wagon belonged to a couple who snored in turns, as though they had agreed a watch. She pulled on her boots in the dark and went out.

The heath had gone. There was the cook fire, banked, a red eye under ash. There were the nearest wagon wheels and the post with its red cord and its dark lamp. Beyond the post the white began, and the Painted Stone stood half in it, the top bands grey, the lower ones still holding their colours.

Cadan was already there. So was Sovra, with his wife’s cord wound round his left hand and tucked into his fist. His eyes were red. He had shaved, badly, and cut himself under the ear.

“Did you sleep?” Cadan asked him.

“No.”

“Good,” Cadan said, as if a promise had been kept.

Gulshat came out of her wagon wrapped in two shawls and a man’s coat. She did not speak. She went to the post, untied nothing, and put her hand on the red cord for a moment as a woman might rest her hand on a gate she was leaving shut. Then she stood back.

Cadan took the coiled length of red from inside his habit. He tied one end to the post’s cord with a knot Riarhin did not know, a flat turn finished under itself, and paid out the coil as he walked, slowly, towards the stone.

“Walk where I walk,” he said. “Sovra behind me. Riarhin last.”

“And then?”

“When the ground changes, put your right hand on the wall. Do not take it off until I tell you.”

“What wall?”

“You will know it.”

That was all he thought she needed. He was usually right. She disliked it anyway.

They walked past the Painted Stone. Riarhin let her hand brush the lowest band. It was warmer than it had been the evening before. The mist took the cord a few feet ahead of Cadan’s hand, so that he seemed to be paying it out into nothing.

The ground changed under her boots. Turf, then something harder. Then stone, flat and slightly warm, with a grain to it like wood.

She looked back. The post was there, small and clear, with the red cord running from it along the ground to her feet. Behind the post the wagons were shapes. Gulshat had not moved. Batu sat on his sack with his knees drawn up. The Painted Stone stood between her and them with its bands of ochre and white, and the mist on her side of it was a different colour, yellower, like the light inside a horn lamp.

On her right a wall had come up out of the ground without her seeing it rise. It was black, and smooth, and as high as she could reach.

She put her hand on it. It was warm as skin.

They walked with the wall on their right. The cord ran out ahead of Cadan and back behind Riarhin, lying along the foot of the wall like something spilled. When she looked back the post was still there, and the lamp, smaller each time. Then the wall turned, and they turned with it, into a tunnel, and she looked back and there was only black stone and the end of the red cord going round the corner out of sight.

The tunnel was not dark. She could not have said where the light came from. The stone gave it off the way a dying fire gives off heat, from no point in particular. Their footsteps came back to her a little late, a half-beat behind, so that she kept thinking somebody was following.

Then the wall began to speak.

It was not loud. At first she thought it was water running somewhere behind the stone, and then that it was people in another room. It came through her palm before it came through her ears. Voices, many of them, close together, most too faint to be words. A woman counting something under her breath. A man breathing as if he had been running. Somebody saying no, the other one, the other one with a patience that had nearly run out.

Riarhin’s hearing had always been better than she wanted it. She could separate the voices. She could not stop doing it. She walked with her hand on the warm stone and the wall gave her a dozen people dying, each in their own place, each at their own pace, and none of them aware of the others.

“Keep walking,” Cadan said without turning round.

She kept walking.

One voice came back. It was a man’s, hoarse, a little drunk with fever, and he was asking the same thing over and over.

Has someone told Aud the gate’s off its pin? Has someone told her? She’ll let the sow out. Has anyone told Aud?

There was a pause each time, the length of somebody not answering. Then he asked again.

Sovra had slowed. Riarhin saw his shoulders draw up. He had taken his hand off the wall to wipe his face and put it back in a different place, and his head was turned towards the stone as if he were listening at a door.

“Sovra,” she said.

He did not answer.

Ahead of him Cadan had stopped. He did not come back. He stood with the cord running out of his fingers and his face turned towards them, and waited.

Riarhin moved up behind Sovra. She meant to take his elbow. Then another voice came through the stone under her own hand, and she stopped.

Sit down.

It was said quietly, without impatience. The voice of somebody who expected to be obeyed and was grateful each time he was, as though obedience were a courtesy he had not earned. She knew the cadence. She knew the weight he put on the second word.

She had sat. Years ago, on a bench by somebody’s fire, wet to the knees, because he had asked and because she had been too tired to do otherwise. He had put a cup in her hands.

The voice did not say anything else for a time. Then, farther along, or deeper:

Down the little stair.

She stood with her hand against the wall. The voices went on round that one, counting and breathing and asking about the sow. That one said nothing more. She waited for it. She knew she was waiting, and knew what waiting here cost: she could see what it had done to Sovra, standing with his cheek almost on the stone. She waited anyway, and the voice did not come again, and she could not have said whether it had been his at all or only the shape of the way he had spoken, worn into the rock by someone else.

It had not given her a face. It had told her to sit down.

She took Sovra’s wrist.

He pulled. He was stronger than he looked, and he had found something in the wall he wanted, and he pulled against her as a dog pulls on a rope towards a smell.

“It’s her,” he said. “She’s saying my name.”

“Is she?”

“She’s—” He stopped. His face moved. “She’s saying it wrong.”

“Then it isn’t her.”

“She always said it wrong. She said it like her mother. She said Sovro.”

Riarhin did not let go of his wrist. She did not try to pull him either. She held it, and she put her own right hand back on the wall, and she said, in the most ordinary voice she had:

“Your daughter dropped her spoon four times last night.”

He looked at her.

“Five,” he said.

“I counted four.”

“She dropped it once under the bench. You didn’t see.”

“I didn’t.”

“She does it on purpose. To see if I’ll pick it up.”

“Do you?”

“Yes.”

“Walk with me and tell me what else she does.”

He walked. He talked about the spoon, and the word that was not yet a word for the dog, and the woman in the yellow-wheeled wagon who had held out her arms too quickly. Riarhin listened to every word of it and to nothing in the wall, or as near to nothing as she could manage, and Cadan went on ahead of them with the cord running out of his hand.

When the tunnel opened Sovra stopped talking all at once, as a man stops when a door he has been leaning on is opened from the other side.

The waystation was a court open to a sky the colour of an unlit room.

Rooms had been cut into the rock all round it, two storeys of them, with doorways and window-holes and here and there a wooden shutter that someone had fitted and painted. A gallery ran along the upper storey with a rail of black stone. In the middle of the court was a well with a stone curb worn into hollows where people had leaned. A bucket stood on the curb. A woman in a nightgown sat on the ground beside it with her knees drawn up, looking at nothing.

There were other people. A man asleep on a bench under the gallery with his boots beside him, set together, toes out. Two children pushing pebbles about a chalked square. Along the far wall a line of people sat with their backs to the stone, in good clothes and bad, some with bundles, some with nothing, waiting in the manner of people outside an office.

Cadan tied the end of the cord to an iron ring set into the stone beside the tunnel’s mouth. He tested the knot. He stood back.

“Here is the mouth,” he said. “This is where we listen, on the day.”

Riarhin looked at the ring and at the cord running from it back into the tunnel. Her right hand was still warm from the wall. She flexed it.

“Who keeps this place?”

Cadan nodded towards a doorway at the back of the court.

“Ask it about routes,” he said. “I should like to stay with him.”

Sovra had gone to the well. He stood at the curb holding his wife’s cord in both hands, looking along the line of waiting people. His lips were moving.

The keeper sat behind a table in a room with no door.

There was a shelf with three cups on it, a lamp burning without smoke, and an empty chair on Riarhin’s side of the table. The keeper wore a brown coat buttoned to the throat and had its hands folded on the table. Its face was a woman’s, broad, with heavy brows and a mole beside the nose.

When Riarhin sat, the face changed. It did not move; it was another face, a young man’s, unshaven, with one eye half closed as if against smoke. Then a girl’s. Then a woman’s again, a different woman, older, with a scar through the lip. Each stayed for about the length of a breath. None of them looked at her in particular.

“You are not dead,” the keeper said. Its voice belonged to whichever face it was wearing.

“No.”

“Most of the living who come through stop in the wall.”

“One nearly did.”

“The young man at my well.” The face was now an old man’s, with no teeth. “He’ll do. He has something to carry. People with something to carry generally do.”

“There’s a man in the wall,” she said. “Asking whether someone has told Aud about the gate.”

“Yes.”

“Can anyone tell him?”

“People try.” The keeper’s hands did not move. “They come in here and tell me, Aud knows, or the sow is in, or I told her, rest now, and I carry it to the place in the wall where he asks, and I say it there. And then he asks again.”

“Then it doesn’t reach him.”

“I don’t know.” It wore a girl’s face, solemn, with a cold sore. “Perhaps he hears and the asking goes on by itself, as a wheel goes on after the cart has stopped. Perhaps he never hears and somewhere he is still lying in a bed with the fever, asking. Perhaps Aud is here, two rooms from me, and has never come in. I can’t tell which of those I am doing when I answer him. I go on answering. It’s what is asked of me.”

Riarhin said nothing for a moment.

There had been a pause, in the wall, between sit down and down the little stair. She could hear it exactly. She did not mention it.

“I need to know about your routes,” she said.

“Everyone needs to know about my routes.” The face was a woman’s again, the first one, with the mole. “I trade.”

“I’ve been told what you trade.”

“Then you have one.”

She had several. She sorted through them as through a drawer, without hurrying, and took out the smallest.

“A woman on a ship,” she said. “She gave me a packet of linen. She said it wasn’t a gift, she wanted it back if I came that way. I said thank you. I didn’t say whether I’d come that way. I haven’t.”

The keeper unfolded its hands and folded them again in the other order.

“I want these back if you come this way,” it said.

It said it in Asta’s voice. Not near it; exactly, with the flat Linn vowel in back and the little drop at the end that meant the subject was closed. The face it wore was a stranger’s, a heavy man with a broken nose. Riarhin felt the skin tighten across her shoulders.

“Thank you,” the keeper said, in its own borrowed voice again. “That one will keep. Your routes.”

It told her. There were three ways out of the court besides the tunnel. The low door under the gallery went to the plain where the circles were, and the circles were none of her business unless someone made them so. The arch by the well went down, a long way, into country it did not keep. The narrow stair at the back went up, onto the hill.

“The hill road has been coming back on itself since summer,” the keeper said. “People go up and come back to the stone with the hole in it. They try again and come back to the stone with the hole in it. Some of them stop trying and sit down by it. I don’t like it. It brings me more faces than I want.”

“Since summer.”

“Since your summer.” The keeper wore an old woman’s face now, sharp-eyed. “I know when the living have summer. They come down thinner.”

Riarhin stood. The empty chair scraped on the floor.

“Thank you.”

“You’ll come back through,” the keeper said. “Everyone does. When you do, bring me the end of that one.”

“Which one?”

“The linen.”

In the court, Sovra was standing at the well, and a woman was standing in front of him.

She had come from the line along the far wall. She was in a plain brown dress with the hem wet, and her hair was down, which a Tulkani wife would not have allowed outdoors. She stood very close to him without touching him. She was looking at his hands.

Cadan was sitting on the edge of the well curb a few feet away with his lantern on his knee and his eyes on the ground, as if what was happening were a private letter he had been asked to hold.

Riarhin stopped beside the bench where the man was asleep with his boots set together.

Sovra unwound the cord from his fist. He held it out. The woman did not take it. She held out her left wrist instead, and he tied it there, his fingers fumbling, and had to do it again because the first knot slipped. She waited. When it was done she looked at it, turned her wrist to see it from the other side, and put her right hand over it.

Then she said something. Riarhin, who could have heard it from the far side of the court, turned her head away and watched the two children push their pebbles about the chalked square, and did not hear it.

When she looked back the woman was walking towards the low door under the gallery. She did not turn round. Sovra stood with his empty hands at his sides, watching her go. When she had gone through, he sat down on the ground beside the well like a man whose knees had been kicked from behind.

The woman in the nightgown, a few feet away, lifted her head and looked at him. Then she reached across, took the bucket off the curb, and put it into his hands.

Chapter 3 — The woman with the cup

Somebody along the court wall was mending a wheel.

Riarhin heard it before she saw it: the soft knock of a mallet on a spoke, a pause, a scrape, the knock again. It was the only work being done in the court. Everything else there was waiting, or sleeping, or pushing pebbles about a square. The sound drew her the way a fire draws people on a cold night, without anybody deciding to go.

An old man sat on a block of stone under the gallery with a cart wheel across his knees. He was broad in the shoulder and thin everywhere else, with a long grey face and big hands, and he was easing a spoke into the hub with the heel of one palm, checking it against his eye, easing it back out. The rim leaned against his shin. One spoke was missing from it. The hole for it was clean and new.

A woman sat beside him on the stone with a cup in her hands.

She was saying something about the spoke. Riarhin could not hear what. The old man answered without looking up, and the woman laughed, and the laugh was short and low and caught at the end like a door on a sticking hinge, and something silver flashed in her mouth.

Riarhin stopped where she was.

The woman was short and broad through the hips, in a dark skirt and a quilted jacket with the elbows patched. Her hair was mostly grey, pinned up, and had come loose at one temple, and she pushed it back with the hand that held the cup and it came loose again. She was older than Riarhin remembered her. Not as much older as she ought to have been.

She looked up. Her eyes went to Riarhin’s face, to her ears, to the sword hilt over her shoulder, and back to her face. Then she moved a basket that was sitting on the stone beside her, a little way along, and left the space there, a hand’s breadth from her own knee.

“You’ll want to sit,” she said. “He’ll be a while.”

Riarhin sat.

The old man’s name was Radan. He was a carter and had built more wheels than he could remember, he said, and this was the worst one he had ever been given to work with.

“It’s the hub,” he said. “Somebody’s bored it out of true.”

“You bored it,” the woman said.

“Somebody bored it out of true and it was me.” He fitted the spoke again, sighted along it, frowned. “I’ll have it done before Ivo comes.”

“I know you will.”

“He’ll want it.”

“He will.”

He pulled the spoke out and turned it end for end, as if that would help, and the woman drank from her cup and watched him with an expression Riarhin had seen on people sitting by beds. A large bird came down from the gallery rail in two heavy flaps and landed on the basket.

It was a raven. A big one, scruffy at the throat, with one tail feather bent sideways as if it had been slept on. It looked at Riarhin with one eye, then turned its head and looked at her with the other, as though the first had made a report it did not trust. Then it put its beak to the strap of a canvas bag leaning against the basket and began to worry at the buckle. The buckle was loose already. The bird seemed to think it could be looser.

“Leave it,” the woman said.

The raven went on.

“Bird.”

It stopped, tilted its head, and resumed with a slightly different grip.

The woman sighed. She took the bag up onto her lap, out of reach, and the raven stepped down onto the stone between her and Riarhin and stood there with its feathers lifted a little, offended.

Riarhin looked at it. She was aware of being looked at by the woman, who had not yet said anything about green doors or wet shoulders or rabbits, and who did not seem in a hurry to.

She found she was glad. That was what disturbed her. Not the bird, which was only a bird with bad manners. She had sat down beside this woman and every part of her had settled, as an animal settles in a stall it knows, and she had not asked it to, and could not now make it stop.

“You came through holding that young man by the wrist,” the woman said.

“Yes.”

“I saw from the gallery. I thought, there’s somebody who’s been in a wall before.” She drank. “I didn’t know it was you until you walked across the court. You still walk as if you’re expecting the ground to argue.”

“It sometimes does.”

“Not often here. Here it’s mostly people who argue.” She tipped her head towards Radan without taking her eyes off Riarhin. “Hold the rim for him, will you. I’ve been holding it since I don’t know when and my wrists have opinions.”

Riarhin got down on one knee and held the rim upright against the stone while Radan worked the spoke towards its socket. Up close he smelt of axle grease and cold ash. The wheel was good oak, well seasoned, with a shallow crack along one felloe that had been filled and smoothed so carefully that she could only see it by the line in the grain.

“Steady,” Radan said.

She held it steady.

He eased the spoke in. It went to within a finger’s width of seating. He stopped, looked at it, and drew it out again.

“Not yet,” he said.

The woman put her hand flat on his back between the shoulders and left it there.

Cadan came across the court with Sovra a little behind him.

The young man looked as though he had been taken apart and put back together by somebody who had not watched it done the first time. He had washed his face at the well. He was holding the bucket the woman in the nightgown had given him, and seemed to have forgotten he was holding it.

“He wants to go back now,” Cadan said.

“Now?”

“His daughter is up there.” Cadan looked at Sovra and then at Riarhin. “I will take him to the post and come down again.”

“Alone?”

“I will have him with me on the way up. Alone on the way down, yes. The cord is tied.” He paused. “I asked the man who keeps the lamps here about the hill stair. He says the same as the keeper. Up at the back, past the cistern. He says people come back from it to the stone with the hole in it and he has given up counting them. He says it more crossly than the keeper does.”

“Is he right?”

“I do not know yet. I only know that two people who do not like each other have said the same thing.”

Riarhin nodded. Sovra was looking at the bucket.

“Give me that,” she said, and took it from him and set it down by the wall. He let it go without noticing. She put her hand on his shoulder. He was trembling, a fine steady trembling, like a rope under load.

“She’ll drop the spoon,” Riarhin said. “Go and pick it up.”

He looked at her. Then he went with Cadan towards the tunnel, and at the mouth of it he stopped, and put his right hand on the black wall the way Cadan showed him, and went in.

When she turned back the woman was watching her.

“That was kind,” the woman said.

“It was practical.”

“So is kindness, usually. People confuse it with something soft.” She put down her cup. “Who’s the monk?”

“Cadan.”

“He moves like a man who’s never had to wait for a door.”

“He’s very good at waiting.”

“That’s not the same thing at all,” the woman said. “Pass me that basket.”

By the time Radan put down his mallet the light in the court had not changed, because it did not change, but the children had gone and the man asleep on the bench had put his boots on and walked away. Radan set the wheel against the wall with the empty socket uppermost, so he would see it first when he came back to it. He nodded to the woman. He nodded to Riarhin, as a man nods to someone who has held a horse for him.

“Ivo’s a good lad,” he said. “He’ll be along.”

“He will,” said the woman.

He went off slowly under the gallery, rubbing his knee.

The woman watched him go. Then she picked up the basket and the bag and her cup, and looked at the raven, which had moved onto the wheel’s hub and was examining the socket.

“Bird,” she said.

It came, after an interval designed to make clear that it had been going to anyway, and settled on her shoulder with its claws in the quilting. She stood up. She had to brace her hand on her knee to do it.

“Are you hungry?”

“Yes.”

“Then come and eat. I’ve a room. It isn’t much of one.”

Riarhin stood too. She did not move to follow.

“You know me,” she said.

“I know you came to our fire with two rabbits and wouldn’t dance.”

“And the morning.”

The woman stood still. The raven shifted on her shoulder.

“I know the morning,” she said. “I’m not going to stand in a court of the dead and talk about the morning when there’s lentils going cold.” She looked at Riarhin steadily. “I’ve a man who can’t finish a wheel and a promise to keep to somebody else, and I’m not leaving either because a woman I took to bed forty-odd years ago has walked out of a wall. I’ll feed you. That’s what I’ll do.”

“All right,” Riarhin said.

The woman’s mouth moved. It was nearly the laugh.

“Come on, then.”

Her room was up the gallery stair, at the end, with a wooden shutter painted blue on the inside and a curtain across the doorway. There was a pallet against one wall, a chest, a brazier made from an iron pot with holes knocked in it, and a shelf with three books on it, swollen with damp. A string of onions hung from a peg. A canvas apron hung from another, the pocket stuffed with something that clinked.

The raven flew off her shoulder onto the chest and began at once on the clasp of it.

“Leave that,” the woman said, without hope.

She put the lentils on the brazier to warm. There was flatbread too, in a cloth, and a little crock of oil with something green in it. She set out two bowls. She set out two cups and filled a small pot with water from a jug and put it beside the lentils, and took down a packet of tea from the shelf behind the books, and stood holding it.

“The water here makes it bitter,” she said. “Something in the stone. I’ve tried letting it stand. I’ve tried boiling it twice. I’ve tried a pinch of salt, which a woman told me would work and which made it taste of the sea. So you’ll drink it bitter or you’ll drink it plain.”

“Bitter.”

“Good. Plain is for people who’ve given up.”

They ate sitting on the floor with the bowls on their knees. The lentils were good. There was cumin in them, and something sour Riarhin could not place, and when she said so the woman looked at her for a long moment and then went on eating without telling her what it was.

“What’s your name?” Riarhin said.

The woman wiped her mouth with her thumb.

“Dusana.”

“You never told me.”

“You never asked.” Dusana tore a piece of bread in half and gave her the larger. “You didn’t tell me yours, either. I asked the elder after and he said it wasn’t the sort of night for names. He was a sentimental man. Underneath.”

“Riarhin.”

“Riarhin.” Dusana said it carefully, trying the weight. “That’s a great deal of name for a woman who arrived with two rabbits.”

Riarhin laughed. It surprised them both. The raven looked up from the chest.

They ate. The tea was bitter. Dusana drank hers in small sips with her eyes half shut, as if the bitterness were a remark she was choosing not to answer.

“So,” Dusana said. “What have you done with yourself?”

Riarhin looked into her cup.

She could have said a great many things. She had a perfect memory of all of them. She found that none of them would come out in an order that made sense to somebody who had only the fire and the morning.

“I walked a good deal,” she said. “South, and then north. I had a house for a while, in a harbour town. With somebody.”

“And?”

“She died.”

Dusana waited.

“I killed some people,” Riarhin said. “One of them was a queen.”

She heard how it sounded and stopped.

Dusana did not laugh and did not look shocked. She broke another piece from the bread and dipped it in the oil and held it a moment, letting the oil soak in.

“That’s a list,” she said. “I asked what you’d done with yourself.”

“I don’t know how to answer that.”

“No. Most people don’t. They tell you what happened to them, or what they did to other people. It’s like asking someone what their house is like and being told how many rooms.” She ate the bread. “Never mind. You’ll get better at it, or you won’t.”

“Will you ask again?”

“Probably. I’m nosy.” Dusana set down her bowl. “Where are you sleeping?”

“I hadn’t thought.”

“There’s the bench in the court, where the man with the boots was. He snores. There’s an empty room along the gallery, but the shutter’s gone and the draught comes up the stair. Or there’s this.” She nodded at the floor on the other side of the brazier, where there was room for a second pallet, and a rolled one leaning against the chest. “The bird walks about in the night. If you mind that, take the bench.”

Riarhin looked at the rolled pallet. Then at Dusana, who had picked up the cups and was looking into the empty pot as though there might be more tea at the bottom of it if she was patient.

“I don’t mind birds,” she said.

“You will,” said Dusana.

She unrolled the pallet with her foot. The raven left the clasp of the chest, walked across the floor with great dignity, and settled itself in the middle of it.

Chapter 4 — The story she asks for

The bird walked about in the night.

It walked across Riarhin’s legs twice and once stood on her hair, and towards the end, when she had stopped minding, it settled against the small of her back with its feathers fluffed and made a sound like a pot simmering. When she woke Dusana was already up, sitting on the chest in her stockinged feet, tying a parcel with string.

“I said you’d mind.”

“I didn’t.”

“You moved your hair three times.”

Riarhin sat up. The raven, displaced, walked off to the brazier and began to inspect the ash.

The parcel was a loaf and a twist of paper and something hard and round wrapped in cloth. Dusana tied it with a knot she pulled tight with her teeth. Her hair was still down. It reached her shoulder blades, grey, with a few dark strands left in it like threads someone had forgotten to pull.

“I’m going out to the plain,” she said. “There’s a man I promised to walk with. His circle’s judge sits today and he means to ask to come home.”

“Who is he?”

“Toghrul. They call him Cut-Cord. He’d rather they didn’t.” She bit off the end of the string. “You can come and stand at the back, if you like standing at the back. Or you can wait here for your monk. I’d come. The monk will find you. He looks like the kind who finds people.”

“Will they mind me?”

“They’ll mind everything. They’re Tulkani. Minding is how they know they’re still alive.” She looked up. “That was a joke.”

“I know.”

“You didn’t laugh.”

“It wasn’t very good.”

Dusana considered this, and then put her hair up with four pins, quickly, without a glass, and one strand came loose at the temple at once, and she left it.

Toghrul was waiting at the low door under the gallery.

He was a big man gone thin, like a coat left on a peg too long. His face was heavy and grey-stubbled and did not move much. His hair was long and loose except for one lock at the left temple, which had a single knot tied in it, close to the scalp, the hair around it worn smooth with handling. He nodded to Dusana. He looked at Riarhin and did not nod.

“She’s with me,” Dusana said.

“Is she Tulkani?”

“She speaks it.”

“So does a parrot.”

“Then you’ll have someone to talk to,” Dusana said, and went through the door.

Beyond it was a passage, short and plain, and beyond that the plain itself: grey grass under a grey sky, flat as a table to every side, with a line of low trees far off marking where a river had been. The air smelt of dust and of rain that had not come. Their feet made no sound in the grass.

They walked. Toghrul went a little ahead with his hands behind his back, and the raven flew from Dusana’s shoulder to the ground and walked beside him for a while, as if escorting him, and then lost interest and came back.

Riarhin put her hand inside her shirt and drew out the bead on its thong.

Dusana looked at it. She stopped walking.

“Where did you get that?”

“Gulshat. At the Painted Stone.”

“For what?”

“For sitting.”

Dusana made a small sound in her nose. “That’s Gulshat.” She looked at the knot, not touching it. “You know what it’s for.”

“A tale. Not a life.”

“She told you that.”

“Yes.”

“Well.” Dusana started walking again. “Ask, then. It’s a long way to the river and Toghrul doesn’t talk.”

Riarhin walked beside her with the bead in her fist. She had thought, lying awake with the bird against her back, about what she would ask for. She had decided on several things and discarded them, because each one was really a question about Dusana, and the bead did not buy Dusana. It bought a road.

“Tell me about the road you were on,” she said, “the week I came to your fire.”

Dusana looked at her sideways.

“That’s a cheat.”

“Is it?”

“It’s a road I know. I’ll give you that.” She hitched the parcel higher under her arm. “All right. Three days of fog. That’s the first thing. You’ll think fog’s nothing, you walk about in it all the time. Fog with oxen is something else. You can’t see the pair in front, so you go by the bell, and the bell on the second wagon was cracked and sounded like the bell on the fourth, so twice the fourth wagon went the wrong way at a fork, following itself.”

“Following itself.”

“It heard its own bell coming back off a wall and went towards it. The second time it was halfway into a ditch before anyone noticed. That was the old man’s wagon. He was furious. He wanted the bell changed and nobody had another bell.”

The raven landed on the grass ahead of them and walked, glancing back now and then.

“The second day a trace broke on the lead pair. Leather rotten at the buckle, and the man whose leather it was had sworn at the last camp it was new. So we stood in the road for most of a morning while two men sewed it with whatever we had, and the man whose leather it was stood about telling everyone how new it had been. Nobody left him. You don’t. But I remember thinking, if I had a knife and the fog were thicker.”

“I saw that,” Riarhin said. “From the ditch.”

Dusana stopped again.

“You were there? On the second day?”

“From noon.”

“In the ditch.”

“Walking. I’d meant to pass you.”

Dusana looked at her for a long moment and then laughed, the low one that caught at the end.

“Well, you’d have seen me washing the boy’s shirt, then. In a bucket. In the fog. Because his mother was sick in the back of a wagon and someone had to, and it was me. Hung it under the axle and it never dried. He wore it wet the next day and didn’t care.” She shook her head. “He wasn’t mine. People thought he was. I let them.”

“Whose was he?”

“Tanja’s. You remember the woman who sang the name differently in the song to Kuva. Nobody corrected her.”

“Yes.”

“That was her husband’s name she was putting in. He’d been drowned at a ford the spring before, and she wouldn’t sing the proper name, she sang his. So nobody corrected her. We just sang round it. She did that for years. Then one year she sang the proper one and everybody stopped singing for a moment, and she said, what, and went on.” Dusana walked a few steps. “I miss her. She had the worst temper on the road and she could make bread out of anything. Sawdust. She’d have made bread out of you.”

They had come quite close to the line of trees. Riarhin could see the river bed now, a long trench of pale stones.

“And the morning?” she said.

“That’s not the road.”

“It was the road.”

Dusana did not answer at once. Toghrul had stopped ahead of them, at the top of a low rise, looking down at something they could not yet see.

“The morning,” Dusana said, “the fog thinned, and I did my hair in the doorway and watched you go off south without asking where. Then four boys in good coats stopped us on the bridge, and you came back, and you broke a man’s nose with the old man’s staff, and you put a knee in that young one’s back and cut his face open. And I had Tanja’s boy against my skirt with my hand over his ear. And I took him in and shut the door.”

“I know.”

“I know you know. I watched you watch me do it.” She shifted the parcel. “I don’t know what I’d have done instead. I’ve thought about it. I still don’t know. That’s the road.”

Riarhin looked down at the bead in her hand. The knot had slackened. It was not undone, but the loops had eased, as a knot does when it has been carrying weight and the weight is set down.

“Thank you,” she said.

“Don’t thank me. Retie it within the year, or Gulshat will want to know why.”

Toghrul’s circle stood beside the dry river in a ring of wagons, fourteen of them, with painted wheels.

The paint was fresh. It was the first thing Riarhin noticed: every wheel was bright, the reds and blues and yellows wet-looking against the grey grass, as if each one had been done that morning. There were people about the wagons, cooking, mending, carrying water up from the river bed in leather buckets, though there was no water in the river. A woman sat on a wagon step shelling beans into her lap. A dog lay asleep under an axle.

At the centre of the ring, on a carpet laid over the grass, an old man sat on a barrel.

He was very old. His grey braid was thin and long and lay over his shoulder and down into his lap, and the cord he held was longer still. It went from his hands down over his knees to the carpet, and off the edge of the carpet into the grass, and on across the grass a good way, knotted all along its length, so that it looked as though he had been tying it all his life and had never thought to cut it short.

“Altan,” Dusana said quietly. “Circle-Keeper. Don’t step on the cord.”

They stopped at the edge of the ring. Toghrul went forward alone and stood before the carpet with his hands at his sides.

Altan looked at him. He did not speak for some time.

People had stopped what they were doing. The woman with the beans had not; she went on shelling, without looking up, and dropped the empty pods into a basket by her foot. Dusana’s eyes went to her once, briefly, and away. The woman lifted her chin a fraction without raising her head. Riarhin saw the two movements and did not ask.

“You’ve come back,” Altan said.

“I’ve come to ask.”

“You asked before.”

“I’m asking again.”

Altan turned the cord a little in his hands.

“The year of the dry summer,” he said, to nobody in particular, “the Red Ford circle sent to us for water. Our wells were holding. Theirs had gone. We gave our word to send them six barrels a week, by cart, until the rain. Toghrul took the cart.” He looked at Toghrul. “Tell them where.”

Toghrul’s face did not move.

“South,” he said. “To the market at Kesh. I sold it.”

“All of it.”

“All of it. Six barrels a week for three weeks. I had a buyer.”

“And the Red Ford circle?”

“I don’t know. I didn’t go back.”

Somebody at the edge of the ring spat into the grass. Altan let the cord lie still in his hands.

“You cut your own cord,” he said.

“Before you could.”

“Yes. Before I could.” Altan looked at the knot in Toghrul’s hair. “And now you have that.”

“It’s a promise to come back.”

“It’s a promise to yourself. I didn’t tie it.”

Dusana stepped forward.

She did not cross onto the carpet. She stood at its edge with the parcel under her arm and spoke to Altan directly, and Riarhin, who had watched people plead before thrones and before ditches, saw her decide to be reasonable and then lose the decision about four sentences in.

“He knows what he did. He’s been sitting outside your fire in the dark for longer than half these children have been dead. He doesn’t ask to be forgiven. He asks to be let near. To be counted. To be where he can be seen being sorry instead of out there being sorry where nobody can see it, which does nobody any good at all, Altan, least of all the Red Ford people, if any of them are even here to—”

“Some are,” said a voice.

A woman had stood up from beside one of the wagons. She was younger than Dusana and thin, with her sleeves rolled up, and her forearms were burned dark by a sun that did not shine here. She was holding a leather bucket.

“I’m Red Ford,” she said. “My two are here somewhere. They were small. It was the third week. I’d like him to stay where he is.”

Dusana turned to her.

“Odval—”

“No.”

“If you’d hear him—”

“I’ve heard him. I heard him say I had a buyer.” The woman’s voice did not rise. “I’m not interested in where he’s sorry. Let him be sorry out there.”

Dusana opened her mouth and shut it again. Her hands had gone tight on the parcel.

“He can’t put it right.”

“No,” said Odval. “He can’t.”

She picked up her bucket and walked away across the grass towards the dry river, and went down into it among the stones, and did not look back.

Nobody spoke. Toghrul stood where he was.

Altan sat on his barrel and let the silence go on until it had done whatever he wanted it to do. Then he lifted a length of the cord and ran his thumb along it, knot by knot, as if reading.

“Not today,” he said. “She’s one of theirs. There are more of theirs, and they’re not all here, and some of them may want to say something different, and I’ll not settle it with half a circle listening. When there’s a gathering, they’ll be sent for. Then he can stand there again.” He looked at Toghrul. “That’s not a yes.”

“I know what it is.”

“Good.”

Dusana did not say anything for a long time on the way back.

She walked fast, faster than Riarhin would have guessed she could on those legs, with her mouth shut and the parcel under her arm. Toghrul had stayed behind. He had not gone into the ring. He had walked to a place at the edge of the grass, a good stone’s throw from the nearest wagon, and sat down there facing the fires.

Halfway back across the plain Dusana stopped.

“I’ve left him the bread,” she said. “I meant to leave him the bread.”

She turned round. She had turned too quickly. Her foot came down on a hummock and twisted and she caught herself, and the parcel went out from under her arm and hit the grass and split. The loaf rolled. The hard round thing in its cloth bounced once and lay still. The twist of paper came open and something fine and brown spilled out across the grey.

“Oh, damn it.”

Riarhin was already down. She picked up the loaf and brushed it. The round thing was a small clay pot, sealed, unbroken. The paper had held tea, and most of it was in the grass now. She scraped up what she could with the edge of her hand and folded the paper round it.

Dusana held out her hand for it.

It was her right hand, open, palm up. A white scar ran across it from the heel of the thumb almost to the far edge, old and healed and a little raised. Riarhin put the folded paper into it. Dusana’s fingers closed.

“That was good tea,” Dusana said.

“There’s some left.”

“There’s some left and a good deal of grass.” She looked at the twist of paper in her fist, and then back the way they had come, towards the line of trees, where a small figure sat by itself at the edge of a ring of bright wheels. “I’ll take it him anyway. He won’t know the difference. He drinks it like water.”

“I’ll come.”

“You’ll wait,” Dusana said. “You’re good at it. Gulshat says so.”

She took the loaf out of Riarhin’s hands and went back across the grass. Riarhin watched her go, a short broad figure in a patched jacket, the raven walking behind her in the grass and then, impatient, flying the rest of the way.

At the edge of the ring Dusana squatted down beside Toghrul. She tore the loaf and gave him half. He took it. They sat together facing the fires and ate.

Chapter 5 — Other

Cadan was asleep on the bench in the court.

He lay on his back with his hands folded on his chest and his lantern on the flagstones beneath him, and his habit was dark to the knee with wet from somewhere. His boots were beside the lantern, set together, toes out, as the other man’s had been. Riarhin stood over him for a moment. He did not wake. She had seen him asleep perhaps three times in all the years she had known him and every time he looked like this, like a man laid out by people who were fond of him.

She went to the well and drew water and washed her face and hands. The cut on the back of her left hand had softened in the night and she picked a little of the scab away and made it bleed, and held it under the cold water until it stopped.

When she turned round he was sitting up.

“He is with his daughter,” Cadan said. “She had dropped her spoon.”

“Did he pick it up?”

“He did. Then he cried a good deal. Gulshat gave him something in a cup that I think was not only tea.” He reached down for his boots. “The boy was asleep under the lamp. The lamp was not lit. The cord had not moved.”

“And the ford?”

“I did not go down to look. It was night. Gulshat said nothing about it and I did not ask, because she would have told me to wait for the check, and I would have had to agree.” He pulled on one boot. “You found someone.”

Riarhin looked at him.

“On the gallery,” he said. “I saw the curtain. There is a bird on the rail outside it which is looking at me as if I owe it money.”

“It looks at everyone like that.”

“Then I will not take it personally.”

He put on the other boot. She waited. He laced it, and stood, and picked up the lantern, and did not ask anything else.

“Her name is Dusana,” Riarhin said.

“Yes,” said Cadan. “I asked the man with the lamps.”

Dusana was frying bread.

She had a flat iron pan on the brazier and a slick of oil in it, and she was laying in strips of yesterday’s flatbread, which spat and curled. The room smelt of hot oil and onions. The raven was on the windowsill with its back to the room, ostentatiously not interested.

“Your monk’s back,” she said, without turning round.

“He’s coming. He’s washing.”

“Good. I’ve only four pieces of bread and I’m not cutting them smaller.” She turned a strip with her fingers and licked them. “Sit. There’s the tea from yesterday.”

There was. She had saved what she had scraped out of the grass, and made it, and strained it through a cloth, and still a few dark blades of grass lay at the bottom of Riarhin’s cup like weed at the bottom of a pond.

“It’s better with the grass,” Dusana said. “Don’t tell anyone.”

Riarhin drank it. It was bitter, and faintly green, and not better.

Cadan came in and sat down by the door with his hands in his lap, taking up less room than a man of his height should have been able to. Dusana gave him a strip of bread on a cloth. He thanked her. She looked at him for a long moment, the way she had looked at Riarhin across the court, from his shaved head to his folded hands to his boots, and then turned back to the pan.

“He eats like a cat,” she said to Riarhin.

“He does.”

“I am right here,” Cadan said mildly.

“I know you are. That’s why I said it out loud.”

They ate. The fried bread was salty and hot and burnt at one corner. Dusana gave the burnt piece to herself without comment. The raven turned round on the windowsill, walked along it, and stood looking at the pan.

“No,” Dusana said.

It stayed looking.

Riarhin sat with the cup in her hands and the grass at the bottom of it, and thought about the fire.

She had been thinking about it since the court, the way a tongue keeps going to a gap in the teeth. The wet turf and the smell of onions and the elder’s hands held open. The woman beside her with flour in her thumbnail. The blanket. And the word, which she had not known, and had told herself since that she ought to have known, because she knew enough Tulkani to quarrel in it and buy a horse in it and discover afterwards exactly how she had been cheated, and she had sat by a Tulkani fire and been given a Tulkani word and had nothing to put against it.

“Your grandmother had a word,” she said.

Dusana’s hand stopped over the pan.

“For people who arrived like me. You said you thought it meant someone she wanted to keep away from the hens.”

“I did think that.”

“You said you wished you’d asked her.”

“I did wish it.” Dusana took the last strip out of the pan and laid it on the cloth. “I’ve asked her since.”

Riarhin waited.

“She’s at Altan’s,” Dusana said. “You saw her. Shelling beans on a step, pretending not to know me because I’d come with Toghrul and she has opinions about Toghrul. She’s been there a long time. Longer than me. I found her after I’d been here a while and sat down on the step and asked her before I’d even said hello, because I’d been carrying it about for so long. She thought that was very rude.”

“What did she say?”

“She said it wasn’t Tulkani. That’s why I’d never heard it from anyone else. It was her grandmother’s word, and her grandmother had it from hers. Khashuran, she said. From before.” Dusana wiped her fingers on the cloth. “It means other.”

“Other.”

“Other. Like you’d say, not that cup, the other one. She said it like that. Very plain.” Dusana picked up her own cup and looked into it. “I asked her what it meant to call a person that, and she said it meant they were the other one, and gave me some beans to shell, and that was all I got.”

Riarhin sat with it.

She remembered the hens. She remembered the woman by the fire saying she had been nine and had thought the word meant someone to keep from the hen coop, and she remembered how sure she had been, by that fire, that the failure was hers. That she had missed a word in a language she knew. She had turned it over in the wagon in the night along with the ravens and put it away with the things she had not attended to closely enough.

It was not a Tulkani word. She had not missed it. There had been nothing there for her to know.

She did not know what to do with that, so she drank the tea, and got a blade of grass in her mouth, and picked it off her tongue.

Dusana watched her do it and did not say anything else about the word. Neither did Riarhin.

“What did your mother call the end of the loaf?” Dusana said.

Riarhin looked up.

“The end. The crust end. Everyone’s family calls it something. Ours called it the grandmother’s end, because the grandmother always got it, because she had the teeth for it. Mine didn’t, at the end, but she still had it, out of spite.” Dusana bit into her bread. “What did yours call it?”

Riarhin thought.

“The heel.”

“Everyone says the heel.”

“Then the knuckle,” Riarhin said. “Sometimes. When she was in a good mood. She’d say, give me the knuckle, and hold out her hand, and if you gave her the soft part she’d hit you with it.”

“The knuckle.” Dusana nodded slowly, as if filing it. “That’s good. What else?”

“I don’t know what else.”

“Everybody’s got a word for something that nobody else uses. A word for the smell after rain. A word for the bit of a sock that goes over the toes.”

“The toe.”

“You’re not trying.” Dusana pointed at Cadan with her bread. “What did your mother call the end of the loaf?”

Cadan considered.

“I do not remember my mother.”

There was a short silence. Dusana did not apologise, and did not look as though she thought she had anything to apologise for.

“What did the house call it, then?”

“The house did not waste bread,” Cadan said. “So it did not have ends.”

Dusana stared at him. Then she put back her head and laughed, the whole of the laugh, and the silver tooth flashed, and the raven turned on the sill to see what had happened.

“I’ll not ask about your house,” she said. “I can see I’d have to sit down for it.”

“You are sitting down.”

“I’d have to sit down more.”

She refilled Riarhin’s cup from the pot. More grass came out. She tipped some into Cadan’s cup too, and he drank it without changing his expression, which made her laugh again.

“Somebody once asked me to come and see his loom,” she said. “This was in a market town, a weaver’s son, very handsome, with his hair in his eyes. Come up and see my loom. I thought I knew what he meant. I’d been travelling with Tulkani all my life, I knew what a young man means. So I went up. And it was a loom.” She shook her head. “He showed me the loom for an hour. The heddles. The beater. How his grandfather had made the warp beam. I’ve never been so bored in my life, and I’ve sat through Altan reading his cord.”

Riarhin smiled into the tea.

“A man once asked me to come and look at his roof.”

“And?”

“It leaked.”

“That’s not a story. That’s a roof.”

“He wanted me to hold the ladder.”

“Did you?”

“I held it for most of an afternoon. He was a terrible roofer. He fell off twice.” Riarhin turned her cup. “He bought me supper. It was a good supper.”

“Then it wasn’t wasted,” said Dusana. “That’s all you can ask of a roof.”

She leaned back against the chest. The raven, seeing the pan unattended, hopped down from the sill, crossed the floor, and stood with its head on one side looking at the oil.

“There’s a verse,” Dusana said. “About a priest whose trousers were stolen by an otter. Have you heard it?”

“No.”

“It’s very good. I made it up.” She cleared her throat. “There was a priest of the river crossing, who went in to wash his feet. And the otter came up out of the reeds and—” She stopped. Her face went still. “And. Something about his hat.”

“His hat.”

“No. Not his hat. It rhymes with feet.” She frowned at the floor. “It used to go on for ages. I’ve lost the third verse.”

“You made it up.”

“I made it up a long time ago.”

Riarhin tried to think of something to put against it. She thought of the lute player at the fire, singing in appalling Viterran, pointing at her with his whole hand.

“There was a song,” she said. “At your fire. The Golden Locks of Claire. He sang a word wrong, the lute player, so that instead of a copper she’d hidden a cooper under her skirts.”

Dusana looked at her.

“No,” she said. “She’d hidden a copper and he sang it cooper. So she had a cooper.”

“That’s what I said.”

“You said the other way round.”

Riarhin went back over it. She had said it the other way round. She had a perfect memory of what she had heard and she had told it backwards, and Dusana was looking at her with an expression of enormous satisfaction.

“It was funnier at the time,” Riarhin said.

“It was very funny at the time. You laughed so hard he lost a chord.”

“He found it again.”

“He did. He’d have found it if the wagon had been on fire.” Dusana wiped her eyes with the heel of her hand. “There. That’s one joke neither of us can tell. We should keep it.”

Cadan said, without inflection, “The otter took his trousers and left him on the bank, and said to him, I’ll bring them back when you’re clean.”

They both turned to look at him.

“That is not the line,” Dusana said.

“No. I do not know the line. I thought someone ought to finish.”

Someone knocked on the doorpost outside the curtain.

Dusana’s face changed. Not much. It went from the face she had been wearing to the one she wore before she had decided how to answer a thing. She got up, knees cracking, and pulled the curtain aside.

A man stood on the gallery. He was elderly and very neat, in a long grey coat buttoned up the front, and he had a lamp-trimmer’s scissors hanging from a cord at his belt and a smudge of soot along one cheekbone that he had obviously not been told about. He looked past Dusana at Riarhin and Cadan, and at the raven, which was now standing in the frying pan.

“Ambrus,” said Dusana.

“I’m sorry to come up.”

“You’re not sorry. You’d not have come up if you were sorry.”

“No.” He did not smile. “It’s the hill stair. There are four at the stone now. One of them’s been round eleven times, she says, and I can’t make her stop counting. She’s asking for you.”

“Me? Why me?”

“You told her about the stair. In the first place. When she came through.”

Dusana’s mouth went thin.

“I told her the stair went up the hill. It did go up the hill.”

“I know.”

“It isn’t my fault it comes down again.”

“I know that too. She’s asking for you anyway.” He touched the smudge on his cheek, as if he had felt them looking at it, and did not find it. “I’ll go back and say you’re coming. Or not.”

Dusana stood in the doorway with one hand on the curtain.

Riarhin watched her. She could see that there were things Dusana would rather do. Sit on the floor. Finish the tea. Go out to the plain again and sit with Toghrul where he could see the fires. She could see her weighing those against a woman she had told about a stair, and the way the weighing went.

“Tell her I’m coming,” Dusana said.

Ambrus nodded and went.

Dusana let the curtain fall. She turned round and looked at the room: the pan, the bird in it, the cups with grass at the bottom, the two of them sitting on her floor.

“That’s your hill,” Cadan said quietly.

“I know whose hill it is.” Dusana took her jacket from the peg and put it on, and buttoned it wrong, and unbuttoned it, and did it again. “I’m not going for you. I’m going because I told a woman a stair went up and she believed me.”

“Of course.”

“You can come if you like. You’ll come anyway.”

She reached down and lifted the raven out of the frying pan with both hands, firmly, around its body, as you lift a hen. It allowed this with an air of having been about to leave. She put it on her shoulder. There was oil on its feet. It left two dark prints on the quilting.

“Look at that,” Dusana said. “Look what you’ve done.”

The bird looked at her, and then at the prints, and then away.

Chapter 6 — A darkness without night

The hill stair went up behind the cistern, where the court’s water was kept under a lid of black stone with a crack across it. The crack had been mended with lead. Somebody had scratched a fish in the lead with the point of a knife.

Ambrus had gone ahead to tell the woman Dusana was coming. He had given them directions as he went, over his shoulder, in the voice of a man who had given them a great many times and had begun to resent the stair personally: up to the top, out onto the hill, follow the wall to the gap, through the gap and along the ridge to the stone.

“The gap’s quicker,” Dusana said, climbing. “There’s an arch, an old one, at the corner of the wall. You go through it and you’re on the ridge. If you follow the wall round you’ll be walking till you’re sick of it.”

“Has the arch changed?” Cadan asked.

“Not that I know. The bird goes through it every time. Flies ahead and sits on the top and shouts at me to hurry.”

The stair was steep and narrow, cut into the rock in a long slant, and long before the top Riarhin’s thigh had made its objections known. She went up with her hand on the wall, out of habit. The wall was only stone here. It said nothing.

At the top there was a door with no door in it, and then the hill.

It was open ground, rough grass and outcrops of grey rock, rising to a long ridge on their left. A drystone wall ran along the slope below the ridge, waist-high, built of flat stones without mortar, and here and there a stone had fallen out of it and lay in the grass. The sky was the same colourless grey as the court’s. She had seen no sun since coming down. Riarhin had noticed that in the court and put it aside, and here, with the whole sky over her and no ceiling of rock, it came back.

They followed the wall.

The raven flew ahead in short stretches, landing on the coping stones, waiting, flying on. Dusana walked steadily with her hands in her jacket pockets. Cadan came last with the lantern in his left hand, its little door shut.

After a while Riarhin noticed that Dusana was counting.

She did not count aloud. Her lips did not move. But at intervals she stopped, for no reason Riarhin could see, and looked about her: at the wall, at the slope, back the way they had come. Then she went on. Riarhin watched her feet. Eight steps, and a pause, and a look round. Eight more. On the open grass she did not do it. Where the wall had a gap, or a fallen stone, or a place where two outcrops made a sort of doorway, she did.

Riarhin did not ask about it.

The light began to go.

It did not go as light goes in the world. There was no west to it, no reddening, no long shadows thrown across the slope. The grey thinned, as though somebody were drawing water out of a basin a cup at a time, and the stones and the grass and the wall grew less and less distinct, and there was nothing to tell them where the day had gone. No sunset behind them. No stars coming out ahead.

Riarhin’s eyes adjusted. They always had, faster than other people’s. She could still see the wall, the ridge, the shapes of the others. She could not see anything that would tell her why.

“Is it always like this?” she said.

“On the hill,” Dusana said. “It comes and goes. In the court it doesn’t. You’ll be sitting there and it’s grey, and you go up a stair and it’s dark, and you come down again and it’s grey, and nobody’s had their supper.” She took a little lamp out of her pocket, a clay thing with a wick, and stopped to light it from a tinder she struck on the wall. The flame came up yellow and small. “I don’t mind it. You stop expecting a morning. It’s restful.”

“I don’t find it restful.”

“You’ve not been here long.”

Cadan did not light his lantern. Riarhin had not expected him to.

The raven called.

It was the first sound Riarhin had heard it make. It came from ahead, from the wall’s coping in the dark, a single harsh croak with a knock at the end of it, like a stick dropped on a step. Then, after a pause, again.

Dusana stopped.

The flame of her lamp stood straight up. Nothing moved on the hill.

Riarhin had stopped too. She stood in the grass with her hand resting on the cold top of the wall, and she was not on the hill. She was in a low wagon with a copper pan hanging over the bed, with an arm across her ribs and the wagon rocking as somebody climbed past the wheel, and she was listening past all of it for exactly this sound, and it had not come.

The raven called a third time.

“They don’t usually call at night,” Riarhin said.

Dusana turned her head. In the little light from the lamp her face was all planes and creases. The silver tooth showed for a moment and was gone.

“There isn’t any night here.”

“I know.”

“Then it’s not calling at night.”

“At your fire,” Riarhin said. “You said they’d followed you since the ford.”

“They had. Four or five of them. They do, with wagons. There’s always something dropped.”

“Was it this one?”

Dusana was quiet. The raven called again, farther off, from where the wall turned the corner.

“I don’t know,” Dusana said. “I didn’t know any ravens to speak to, then. I had this one later.” She walked on a few steps towards the sound, and then a few more, and Riarhin heard her say, low, not to Riarhin at all but out into the dark where the bird was, in the voice people use to a dog that has done something foolish and loved, “What do ravens know?”

The bird did not answer that.

The arch stood at the corner where the wall turned.

It was old. Its stones were bigger than the wall’s and better cut, and someone had set them a long time before the wall was built, so that the wall came up to it on either side and stopped as if it had been told to. It was not much taller than a man. Beyond it Riarhin could make out the dark shoulder of the ridge going up, and a path, pale in the grass.

The raven was not on the top of it.

It was on the wall, three stones short of the arch, hunched, with its feathers drawn in tight. When Dusana came up with the lamp it did not fly to the arch. It sidled along the coping away from it, towards her, and stopped.

“Go on,” Dusana said.

It did not go on.

“Bird. Go on. You go through it every time.”

The raven looked at the arch, first with one eye and then with the other. Then it hopped down off the wall onto her shoulder and pushed its head in against the side of her neck, under her ear, and stayed there.

Dusana put up her hand and held it. She did not say anything.

Cadan had come up behind them. He looked at the bird and then at the arch and said nothing either.

“Is it only the dark?” Riarhin said.

“It doesn’t mind the dark. It’s flown through this arch in the dark. It’s flown through it in rain, which it hates.” Dusana’s fingers moved in the feathers at the bird’s throat, slowly. “It’s never done this.”

Riarhin looked at the arch for a long time.

It looked like an arch. Two uprights, a lintel, the path going on beyond. She could see nothing different about it, and she knew she would not, because she had not seen it before and had nothing to compare it with. The bird had. The bird was the only one of them that had something to compare it with, and all it could tell them was that the thing was wrong. It could not tell them how.

She bent and felt in the grass at the foot of the wall until she found a pebble. Round, flat, about the size of the end of her thumb. She weighed it.

“Stand back,” she said.

They stood back. She stepped up to within a stride of the arch and tossed the pebble through it, underhand, low, so that it would land on the path a few yards beyond.

She heard it land. A small click on stone, then a skip, then a rolling sound, going away from her up the path.

Then it stopped going away.

The rolling went on, but it was coming nearer, and it was coming from behind her, from the slope they had climbed. She turned. In the faint lamplight something small came out of the grass and rolled the last foot down the gentle slope of the path and stopped against her heel.

She picked it up. It was the same pebble. She knew the shape of it exactly, the little chip on one edge.

Dusana had drawn in her breath.

“Again,” Cadan said.

“Something else.”

“Yes,” he agreed. He reached inside his habit and drew out a short cord, knotted along its length, with a small iron weight tied to one end. He held the free end and let the weight hang. Then he stepped up beside her, a little to one side of the arch so that his own body would not pass under it, and swung the weight out through the opening, gently, as a man casts a line.

The cord went out through the arch and lay along the path beyond.

He drew it in. It came. He drew it in further, hand over hand, and when he had nearly all of it back, it went taut.

He stopped. He did not pull.
