Riarhin looked down. The cord ran from Cadan’s hand forward through the arch, as it should. And it ran back, too, from the arch’s far side, out of the dark on the path beyond, round in a curve that had no business being there, and across the grass behind them, and the iron weight hung from the end of it a yard behind Cadan’s left heel, turning slowly, just clear of the ground.

Cadan looked at it. Then he let go of the cord, and stepped back from the arch, and bent and picked up the weight from behind him, and drew the rest of the cord after it, round the wrong way, until it came back to his hand.

He coiled it. His face showed nothing in particular.

“I do not think we should go through that,” he said.

“No,” said Riarhin.

“I don’t understand it,” said Dusana.

“Neither do I.”

“I came through it.” Dusana’s voice was tight. “Not long since. I came through it with a basket of eggs from the woman on the ridge and the bird sat on the top and shouted at me. It was an arch. It went through to the other side.” She stood with the lamp in one hand and the other on the raven. “What’s changed it?”

“I don’t know.”

“You’ve come down here to find out.”

“Yes.”

“Then find out.”

“I’ve found out it’s changed,” Riarhin said. “I’ve found out that a pebble and a cord think so too. That’s all I’ve found out.”

Dusana looked at her. Then she looked at the bird against her neck, and something in her shoulders let go a little.

“It knew before the pebble.”

“It did.”

“It still doesn’t know why.”

“No.”

“Well.” Dusana let out a breath. “That’s ravens.”

They took the long way.

It went down the hill again, along the outside of the wall, and round the bottom of the ridge where a stream ran in a gully, invisible in the dark, loud. They crossed it on stepping stones. Riarhin went first and found the stones with her feet and called them back. Dusana came next with the lamp held high and the raven on her shoulder, which had not moved since the arch. Cadan came last. He slipped once and went into the water to the shin and said nothing at all.

Then up the far side of the gully, on a sheep track, through heather that soaked them to the knee. The light did not come back. Somewhere far off Riarhin heard a bell ring once and stop. Nobody remarked on it.

Dusana went steadily, but she had stopped counting her eight steps and was walking without pausing anywhere, fast and a little too heavily, like a woman who wanted to be somewhere with a door.

At the top of the track she stopped to get her breath and sat down on a rock.

Riarhin sat beside her. Cadan stood a little way off, looking back down into the dark, where the gully was.

“I’ve used that arch for years,” Dusana said.

“I know.”

“It’s nothing. It’s an arch. There’s a longer way. People walk the longer way all the time.” She set the lamp on the rock between her feet. “It’s only that I knew it.”

Riarhin said nothing. She reached into her pack and found the flat loaf Gulshat had put there, and broke off the end of it, and held it out.

Dusana looked at it. She took it.

“The knuckle,” she said.

“The knuckle.”

Dusana broke a piece from it and held it up to the raven on her shoulder. The bird looked at the bread. It did not take it. It went on pressing its head against her neck.

She held the bread there for some time. Then she ate it herself, slowly, and stood, and picked up the lamp.

Chapter 7 — The mark still there

The light came back while they were on the ridge, as it had gone, from nowhere in particular.

Below them on the far side the ground fell away into a broad valley, and the valley was full of weather that was not weather. Riarhin stopped to look at it. A long grey shape drifted over the nearer slope like a squall of rain, and when it came closer it was a kitchen: a table with a cloth on it, a dresser, a woman with her back turned stirring a pot, all of it thin as breath on glass, moving slowly across the hillside and fraying at the edges. Behind it, farther off, a field of barley rippled where there was no field, gold under a sun that was not in the sky. A boat with one man rowing went past high up, upside down, without hurry.

“People asleep,” Dusana said. “Up there. Your side. On the hill you see what they’re dreaming, when it’s thin.” She did not look at it long. “Don’t watch them too much. It’s rude.”

They went on along the ridge. The barley faded. The kitchen went on across the valley and into a fold of ground and was gone.

The stone was at the ridge’s end, where the path came down a little into a hollow.

It was grey, waist-high, rough, rounded at the top, and there was a hole worn through it from one side to the other, about the size of a fist, smooth inside as the bowl of a spoon. Grass grew close round its foot. Somebody had left a cup on the top of it.

Four people sat in the hollow. Ambrus stood beside them with his hands behind his back, looking as though he had been standing there for some time and would go on doing it.

One of the four got up when she saw Dusana. She was a heavy woman in a carter’s smock, with her hair tied up in a scarf and her hands red and chapped, and she came across the hollow with her arms already out.

“Twelve,” she said. “That was twelve. I went up again while he was fetching you.”

“Sabeth.” Dusana let herself be held. “Sit down.”

“I went up the stair and I came back to the stone.”

“I know. Sit down.”

“Twelve times.”

Dusana sat her down on the grass, and sat beside her, and took her red hands in both of her own, and the woman went on saying the number under her breath, and Dusana let her.

Riarhin looked at the stair.

It went up the slope beyond the hollow, cut into the hillside: a flight of grey steps, narrow, turning left at a pitch a little too steep for comfort, so that a climber would put a hand to the rock on the inside of the turn. And there, at the height of a shoulder, the rock had been worn into a shallow hollow by hands.

She had seen this stair before. Not one like it. This one, at the coast, where it had no business being. She had looked at the pitch of the turn and the worn place in the rock and kept them, because she had been attending to them as if her life depended on it, which it had. She could have drawn it from memory. She could have drawn the chip out of the edge of the third step.

The chip was there.

“It’s hers,” she said.

Cadan had come to stand beside her.

“Nereth’s?”

“The one from the coast. The angle. The handhold. She copied it from something, and the copy’s here.” Riarhin went closer. The rock of the hillside around the stair was the same grey as the hill, weathered and lichened. The stair was not. Its edges were sharper. It sat in the slope the way a new tooth sits in an old jaw. “It’s been fastened on.”

“To what?”

“I don’t know. Whatever’s under the hill.”

She put her hand on the worn place in the rock. It was cold. It said nothing.

“And people climb it,” Cadan said.

“And come back to the stone.”

He looked up the stair, to where it turned out of sight.

“I should like to try something,” he said. “Briefly. It will need someone to keep the way out.”

He explained it to her in the plain words he used for things she needed to do, and no more. He would go up the stair. At the top, where it turned back on itself, he would hold. He did not say what holding was. She had seen him do it before, in other places: stand in a doorway in such a way that the doorway forgot to close. He would hold for the length of his cord, and let what was pressing on the stair pass through, once, and then let go.

“And if it doesn’t let go of you?”

“Then you will pull me down by the stone.”

“Pull you how?”

He looked at the holed stone in the hollow.

“Keep it,” he said. “Keep it where it is. It is where everything comes back to. If it stays where it is, it will be there for me to come back to also.”

Dusana had been listening. She left Sabeth with Ambrus and came over, and looked at the stone, and at the stair, and at Cadan.

“You want to let it through,” she said.

“Once.”

“Then close it.” She said it as if repeating something. “You let something pass. Just once. Then close it. And if the edge gives, you don’t pull it tight at once.”

Cadan turned his head and looked at her.

“Yes,” he said. “Where did you learn that?”

Dusana did not answer. She knelt down on the far side of the holed stone and put both her hands flat on it.

Riarhin knelt opposite. She put her own hands on the stone beside Dusana’s. The rock was rough and cold and slightly damp. Through the hole between them she could see Dusana’s face, the broken vein below her left eye, her mouth set.

“What do we do?” Riarhin said.

“Keep it still.”

“It’s a stone.”

“Then it shouldn’t be hard,” said Dusana.

Cadan went up the stair. He took his lantern with him. He went quickly and lightly and did not touch the worn place in the rock at the turn, and then he was out of sight.

Riarhin waited. The others in the hollow had gone quiet. Sabeth had stopped counting.

For a while nothing happened. Then the stone moved under her hands.

It did not move far. It did not tip or shift on its foot. It moved the way a horse’s flank moves under a hand when the horse breathes in: a swelling, and a pressure against her palms, slow, from inside. She pressed back. Across from her Dusana pressed too. The stone pushed against both of them and went on pushing and Riarhin put her weight forward onto her hands, and felt the scab on the back of the left one crack and open.

Through the hole between their hands the light changed.

She was looking through the stone at Dusana’s face, and then she was not. She was looking at mist. White, morning mist, low over brown water. And in the water a stair: grey stone steps coming up out of the ford below the Painted Stone, turning left at a pitch too steep for comfort, water running off the edges. Above it, on the bank, a brass-shod post with a red cord, and a dark lamp, and a boy asleep on a folded sack with his knees drawn up.

The pressure against her palms rose.

On the stair in the ford a figure was coming up. An old man, wet to the waist, feeling his way with one hand.

Then the pressure broke.

It went out from under her hands as a held breath goes, all at once. Through the hole she saw the stair in the ford fold down into the water, one step after another, from the top, without a splash, like a ladder laid flat. The old man on it stopped, and looked about him, and turned, and went down with it. The brown water closed and ran on over the paving stones. Mist lay on it. On the bank the boy slept.

Then there was only Dusana’s face again through the hole, with sweat on her lip.

Riarhin did not take her hands off the stone.

“Is that it?” Dusana said.

“I don’t know.”

Footsteps on the stair. Cadan came down round the turn, not fast. His face was grey. He came down the steps one at a time, with his free hand on the rock, and stepped off the bottom step onto the grass of the hollow, and stood there.

He had come down the stair. He had not come back to the stone.

“It went,” Riarhin said.

“Something went.” He sat down on the bottom step. He set the lantern beside his foot with care. “I let it pass. Then I closed. It closed.”

“At the ford. I saw it.”

He looked up at her.

“Saw what?”

“The stair in the water at the Painted Stone. It went down.”

Cadan was quiet for a moment.

“That is something,” he said. “That is one place.”

Cadan kept the days.

He did it with his knotted cord: one knot at each waking, slid along with his thumb. The house kept its hours, he said, and he had never found a place where they did not still apply to him, though he had found places where they did not apply to anybody else. They went back to the court by the long way, by the gully and the wall, and slept, and ate Dusana’s lentils and Gulshat’s bread, and Riarhin sat by the well and rebound the cut on her hand with a strip of Asta’s linen, which meant untying Asta’s knot. It took her some time to bring herself to do it.

On the day Cadan’s cord said was the agreed one, they sat by the iron ring at the tunnel’s mouth.

The red cord ran from the ring into the dark of the tunnel, and lay along the foot of the wall, and went round the corner. Dusana sat with them. She had brought her cup. Ambrus came by once and looked at the three of them sitting against the wall and went away again without asking.

They waited a long time.

Riarhin did not count. She listened. The wall behind her made its sounds, the faint voices, the man asking about Aud and the sow. She kept her hand off it.

The red cord moved.

It did not jerk. It lifted a little from the floor along its length, as a rope does when somebody at the other end takes it up, and lay down again. Then, from the dark of the tunnel, quite near, as if the speaker stood just round the corner with her mouth to the stone, came Gulshat’s voice. It was dry and old and in no hurry.

“The stair didn’t come this morning. Mist on the ford and no stair in it. Batu watched from first light. That’s all.”

Riarhin was on her feet. She did not remember standing.

There was a great deal she wanted to ask. Whether the old man’s pipe had been found. Whether Sovra was eating. Whether the child had dropped her spoon, and whether the mist had come up the heath, and whether anything had come up the ford that was not a stair. She stood at the mouth of the tunnel with her hand on the iron ring, and Cadan, sitting where he was, said nothing at all, because they had agreed. You don’t answer. Then I come back up and go to bed.

The cord lay still on the floor.

After a while Riarhin sat down again.

“One place,” Cadan said.

“One place.”

Dusana drank from her cup. She did not say anything for a while. Then she reached across and put her hand on Riarhin’s knee, briefly, and took it away.

They went back to the hill to look, because there was nothing else to do with the news but test it.

The stair was there in its slope beyond the hollow, as it had been, with the chip out of the third step. Riarhin went up it herself. She went to the turn, and put her hand in the worn place in the rock, and went round, and up a long flight beyond that she had not seen, and came out at the top onto the ridge, and walked along the ridge in the grey light, and came down the path into the hollow, and the holed stone was in front of her. The cup was still on top of it.

She stood looking at it.

“It’s still doing it,” Dusana said.

“Not here.” Cadan was looking along the slope, past the stair. “There.”

A hundred yards along the hillside, where there had been rough grass and a fall of rocks, there was a second stair.

It was not a copy of the first. Its steps were wider and shallower, worn in the middle, the stone a warmer grey. It turned right, not left. There was a rusted iron rail on one side of it, bent outwards at the top as if someone heavy had once fallen against it.

None of them had seen it come.

Sabeth was sitting by the holed stone with her hands in her lap. She looked at the new stair for a long time.

“I could try that one,” she said.

“It’ll bring you back,” said Riarhin.

“You don’t know that.”

Riarhin went and climbed it. It took her up through rocks she did not recognise and along a ledge and down a short slope of scree, and the holed stone was in front of her, and Sabeth was sitting beside it.

“Thirteen,” Sabeth said, though she had not been the one climbing.

Riarhin sat down beside her on the grass.

She took off her pack.

“You’re going on,” Dusana said.

“I’m going to follow it. Wherever it takes things before it brings them back.”

“And Sabeth?”

Riarhin looked at the woman beside her. She took out the flat loaf, what was left of it, and broke it in half, and gave Sabeth the larger half. She took out the twist of salt and the dried mutton and one roll of the clean wool for the feet, and put them in Sabeth’s lap. Sabeth looked at them.

“Don’t climb either stair again,” Riarhin said. “Go down the ridge the way we came in, along the wall, not through the arch at the corner. Go round by the gully. There are stepping stones; the fourth one rocks. Up the sheep track on the other side. You’ll come to the cistern with a fish scratched on the lid. Ask for Ambrus. If he isn’t there, ask for anyone who’s mending a wheel. Tell them Dusana sent you.”

“I didn’t,” Dusana said.

“You will have done.”

Sabeth was holding the bread in both hands. Her lips were moving. Riarhin thought she was counting again. Then she saw it was the directions: the wall, not the arch, the gully, the fourth stone.

“Say it back to me,” Riarhin said.

Sabeth said it back. She had it right, all but the fish.

“The lid has a fish on it,” Riarhin said.

“A fish.”

“Scratched in the lead.”

Sabeth nodded. She put the bread inside her smock and stood up, and stood for a moment, and then went off along the ridge towards the wall without looking back at either stair.

Riarhin watched her until the path dipped. Then she stood, and shouldered the pack, and went to the foot of the new stair with the bent rail, and put her hand on the rail. It was cold, and flaking, and real.

“Well,” Dusana said behind her. “Go on, then. I’m not carrying you.”

Chapter 8 — Which way the rain goes

The stair with the bent rail brought them back to the stone three times.

Each time it went farther before it turned. The first time it took them up through rocks and along a ledge and down a slope of scree, and that was all. The second time the ledge went on past the scree and round the shoulder of the hill, and there was a hut there with no roof and a goat in it, which watched them pass, and then the path went down and the stone was in front of them. The third time there was no goat and no hut. There was a lane between walls, and rain.

Riarhin tied a strand of red wool to the bent rail before each climb. She had pulled it from the fringe of her own scarf. The first time she found it on the rail when they came back. The second time it was gone from the rail and lying in the grass beside the holed stone, wet, as if it had been carried. The third time she did not find it at all.

“It’s taking us somewhere,” Dusana said, “before it brings us back.”

“It’s taking everything somewhere.”

“That’s what I said.”

Cadan said nothing. He was looking at the rain.

The lane opened into a street, and the street was full of doors.

They were on both sides, close together, in walls of dark wet brick: doors painted and unpainted, doors with knockers and doors with only a hole for a string, doors set two steps up and doors set a step down into the street, so that water stood on their sills. Some were open a crack. Most were shut. Above them the houses went up three and four storeys and leaned in towards each other, and between their roofs was a strip of grey sky, and out of it came the rain.

It did not fall down.

It fell sideways, across the street, in long slanting lines, the way rain falls in a gale. There was no gale. There was no wind at all; Riarhin’s hair hung straight. The rain went across, from one side of the street to the other, and struck the doors on the far side, and ran down them. And a little farther along it went the other way, from the far side to the near, towards a different set of doors. And at the corner it went straight along the street, and struck a single green door at the end, and ran in a sheet down its panels and pooled at its foot.

Riarhin stood in it and got wet from the left.

“I’ve heard of this,” Dusana said. She had her shawl over her head and the raven inside the shawl, where it had gone without being asked. “I’ve never been. They call it the Rain Doors. People say if you go where the rain’s going you’ll get where it’s going too.”

“Where’s it going?”

“Doors.”

“Which doors?”

“That,” said Dusana, “is the whole difficulty, I should think.”

Riarhin walked slowly up the street. She watched the rain. Not where it came from, because it came from the sky like any rain, but where it went: which door it struck, which way the water ran in the gutter afterwards, which way the puddles leaned. A puddle in the middle of the street had a skin of ripples all moving towards the right-hand wall, as a puddle’s ripples move when someone has thrown a stone into one side of it, except that nobody had. A dead leaf in the gutter went upstream.

She took the comb out of her pack, the one with three teeth gone, and set it on the cobbles in the middle of the street, and stood back.

The rain struck it. It skated, slowly, a hand’s breadth, towards a door on the left with a brass knocker in the shape of a fist. It stopped.

She set it down again a little farther on. It went right, towards a door with no paint on it, then back, and fetched up against her boot.

“It’s sulking,” Dusana said.

“It’s been put down where two ways meet.”

“Yes, it’s sulking.”

Riarhin put the comb back in the pack.

The streets branched.

Every few houses another lane went off between the walls, narrow, wet, its own rain falling its own way. They went down one because the rain went down it and came out into a street she had not seen. They went down another and came out into the street they had just left, from the other end, by the green door where the rain pooled.

She stopped there.

“We’ve come round.”

“I can see that,” said Dusana.

Riarhin looked back down the lane. It had gone off from the main street at a slant, and curved, gently, all along its length, and the curve had carried it round and back to the trunk it had come from. She had walked it attending to the rain and the doors, and she had not attended to the curve.

The memory came up whole, the way they did, with its weather.

A wood. Late autumn. A root as thick as her arm running out from the foot of a beech across a slope of dead leaves towards the sound of water. She had been walking it, balanced, her feet bare and cold, because he had told her to, and she had been nearly at the end, nearly at the stream, when he called her back. Ghere, sitting on a stone above her with his arms on his knees. She had come back. He had not said anything. He had turned his hand over, palm down, and pointed with it along the root she had been walking: it bent, near the end, and ran back in under the leaves towards its own tree.

It returns, the hand had said. You were following it home.

She had been fourteen, or fifteen, and so angry she could feel it in her teeth. She had wanted the water. She had been so near the water. And he had made her stand on the slope and look at the direction of a root.

She was still angry. It came up with the memory, fresh, and she let it come.

“There,” she said.

Dusana looked at her.

“The lanes that curve back,” Riarhin said. “Towards where they started. They’re roots. Going home.” She pointed with her hand, palm down, before she knew she was going to. “The ones that don’t curve back go somewhere else.”

“How do you tell?”

“You look at the direction of the root,” Riarhin said, “instead of the water.”

She heard her own voice say it. It was his voice. She had not known she had it.

After that they went more slowly and got lost less. She stood at the mouth of each lane and looked at the first few yards of it, since the end was hidden: whether the walls began to bend, which side the gutter ran, whether the doors on one side were set a little forward of the doors on the other. Twice she was wrong. Once Dusana saw it before she did and said nothing until Riarhin turned to her, and then said, “That one’s going home,” and was right.

The raven rode in the shawl with its head hunched into its neck. It had no opinions about the lanes. Every so often it shook itself and spattered all three of them.

In a crooked street lit by a single lamp they found an old woman stuck in a door.

The door was in a wall where the rain went one way, hard, towards it, and the door was open no more than a hand’s width. Rain was going in through the gap. The old woman was on the street side of it with her shoulder in the gap and her bundle pressed against her chest, pushing, and the gap was closing. Riarhin could see it close. Not fast. A finger’s width while she watched. The edge of the door came on, and the old woman pushed against it and it did not care.

“I want to go in,” the old woman said, to nobody, in a reasonable voice. “My sister’s in.”

Riarhin put her shoulder to the door. It was like leaning on a hillside. She set her feet and pushed and her thigh woke up and complained and the door came on anyway, steady, unhurried, a finger’s width, another.

“I’ll take her,” Cadan said.

He was beside the old woman. He had his lantern in one hand and the coil of Riarhin’s rope over his shoulder. He put his free hand on the old woman’s back, between the shoulders, and stepped into the gap with her.

There was no room in the gap for a man his size. Riarhin saw him go into it anyway. Then he was in it, with the edge of the door passing through his upper arm as though the arm were smoke. His face did not change. The old woman went through in front of him, bundle first, as easily as through an open gate. Cadan went after her. The edge of the door passed through his shoulder and his hip and his far leg. The lantern fell out of his hand. It struck the wet sill and rolled. The coil of rope slid down through his shoulder, through it, and dropped at his feet in the street.

The door shut.

It shut with an ordinary sound, the sound of a heavy door meeting its frame, and the rain went on striking it and running down.

Riarhin stood with her hands flat on the wet wood. The lantern lay on the sill against her boot. The rope lay in a wet heap beside it.

“Cadan.”

Nothing. Dusana had come up beside her. The raven had gone back into the shawl.

Then the door opened again, a little, a hand’s width, and Cadan came out through it sideways, solid, his shoulder catching on the frame and bumping off it like anyone’s would. He stood in the street. He looked down at the lantern and the rope at his feet.

He bent and picked up the lantern. His hand closed on it and missed, slightly, and closed again. He picked up the rope. He held them both and looked at them.

“She is with her sister,” he said. “There is a kitchen. They were glad.”

“You went through the door.”

“Yes.”

“Through it.”

He looked at his hand, the one holding the lantern. He opened and closed the fingers.

“Here the house’s discipline goes further than it did above,” he said. “I did not know that it would.”

His voice was level. His lips were grey. Riarhin took the rope off him and put it over her own shoulder. He let her. His hand when she touched it was cold as stone in winter.

“Are you hurt?”

He did not answer at once. She saw him hear the question a moment after she had asked it, as if it had come to him from farther off.

“No,” he said. “Cold.”

“Can you walk?”

“I can walk.”

Dusana unwound her shawl, shook the raven out of it onto her shoulder, and put the shawl round Cadan’s neck, and tucked the ends into the front of his habit, roughly, as she might have done it for a child who was going to complain about it. He stood still and let her.

“There,” she said. “Now you look ridiculous, which is better than looking dead.”

“Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me, walk.”

It was Dusana who saw the chalk.

A blue mark on a doorpost at the corner of a street, low down, at the height of a hand: a little circle with a cross through it and a tick beside it. Then another, two doors on. Then another at the mouth of a lane. The lane did not curve back.

“Pell,” Dusana said.

“Who’s Pell?”

“Sells maps. Down at the Turning Market. He comes through here on his rounds and marks where he’s walked. He says where he walks things stay walked. That’s his tale. He’s a liar about most things, but he’s a careful liar.” She looked along the line of blue marks. “That’ll go to Hemmet’s. Everything he walks goes past Hemmet’s. He likes the beer.”

“What’s Hemmet’s?”

“Somewhere with a roof.” Dusana pulled her jacket tighter. She had nothing over her head now and her hair was plastered to her skull. “And a fire. And somebody who’ll feed us and tell us off. I’ll take you that far.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“That far?”

“That far. It’s a refuge. People know it. You’ll be all right there, and the monk can thaw out, and you can find someone who knows where these stairs go when they’re not coming back.” She did not look at Riarhin. “I’ve Toghrul’s hearing to go back for. When Altan sends. I said I’d stand with him, and I will, and I’m not going so far I can’t hear when he sends.”

“All right,” Riarhin said.

Dusana gave her a sideways look, quick, and then the laugh came, short, catching.

“Listen to you.”

Hemmet’s was a narrow house at the end of the last street, where the rain stopped. It did not thin; it stopped, at a line in the cobbles, and on the far side of the line the stones were dry. There was a lamp over the door and a boot-scraper by the step with three pairs of boots already standing beside it.

Hemmet came to the door before they knocked. He was a big soft man in a leather apron, bald, with a red face and forearms like hams, and he looked at their feet first.

“Boots,” he said.

They took off their boots.

“Not there. There. By the others. And not in a heap. I’m not sorting them out in the morning.” He watched Cadan set his beside the others, together, toes out, and seemed mollified. “Come in. Sit down. There’s a place.”

There was a place for each of them. There was a place, Riarhin saw when she came in, for a good many more people than were there: a long table down the middle of a low room with a fire at one end, and every place at it set, with a bowl and a spoon and a heel of bread, from end to end, though there were only five other people sitting at it. An old man asleep with his head on his arms. Two women playing cards. A young man with a fiddle case he did not open. A child.

“Sit anywhere,” Hemmet said. “It’s all set.”

“For who?”

“For whoever comes.” He went to the fire and lifted the lid on a pot. “I set a place for everybody. It’s simpler than counting.”

Cadan sat at the end nearest the fire, with the shawl still round his neck. He put his lantern on the bench beside him, and the coil of rope, and his knotted cord, and his wet outer coat, folded with the wet inside, and then his hands in his lap.

Dusana came and stood over him.

“That’s mine,” she said.

He looked up.

“The place by the fire. I always sit there. I’ve sat there every time I’ve come. Move your things.”

“I did not know.”

“Well, now you do.”

He moved them one at a time—the lantern, the rope, the cord, the coat—along the bench to the next place, and then moved himself after them. Dusana sat down where he had been and stretched her stockinged feet towards the fire with a groan. The raven got down off her shoulder and walked along the table to the next bowl and looked into it.

“Off,” said Hemmet, without turning round.

The raven got off.

Hemmet ladled from the pot. It was a stew of something dark, with a sweetish smell, and dumplings in it that had a little green in them. Riarhin could not name the meat. She ate it anyway. It was very good. There were pickled walnuts that turned her mouth black, and beer, which Dusana drank most of. Cadan ate slowly, and stopped twice to put his hands round the bowl, and the colour came back into his lips by degrees.

When she had eaten, Riarhin took Remorse off her back and laid it across her knees and took out the whetstone and the rag.

She drew it. The block at the root of the grip moved against its seat and ticked, small and dry, and Dusana, half asleep by the fire, opened one eye.

“That’s loose.”

“I know.”

“You should see to it.”

“I mean to.”

“You’ll not mean to for long,” Dusana said, “if it comes off in somebody.” She shut her eye again.

Riarhin wiped the blade. Rain had got under the binding on the cut side and darkened the cord. She worked the thread there a little tighter with her thumbnail, and it held, and the block, when she pressed it, lifted under the wrapping exactly as much as it had before.

Hemmet came down the table with the pot and filled her bowl again without asking. He looked at the sword across her knees.

“Not at the table,” he said.

She sheathed it. It ticked going in. Hemmet nodded, satisfied, and went on to fill Cadan’s bowl, and then the bowl at the empty place beside Cadan, and the one beyond that, all the way down the table, though nobody was sitting at them.

Part II — What was left in the wood

Chapter 9 — A map not yet true

Hemmet found Cadan’s boots on the hearthstone in the morning and carried them to the step at arm’s length.

“Boots go by the scraper,” he said. “Wet boots on a hearth crack. Then they let the wet in. Then you’re back here wanting them dried.”

“I am sorry,” Cadan said.

“Don’t be sorry. Be by the scraper.”

Cadan went out in his stockings and stood his boots beside the others, heel to heel, and came back and sat down. His colour had come back in the night. When Riarhin spoke to him across the table he answered at once, without the small pause there had been the evening before, and she found she had been listening for the pause and was glad not to hear it.

Breakfast was porridge with a skin on it and something stirred in that tasted of hazelnuts. The places down the table were set again, every one, with a bowl and a spoon. Nobody came to them. Hemmet filled them anyway and later took the bowls away full and did not seem to mind. Dusana ate two helpings and gave the raven the skin off the second. The bird carried it to the far end of the table, beyond the reach of anyone who might want it back, and ate it with its back to them.

Riarhin drew Remorse a hand’s length to look at the binding in daylight. The block ticked against its seat. Rain had dried in the cord on the cut side and left it stiff and pale.

“You’ll want that seen to,” Hemmet said, passing with the pot.

“So I’m told.”

“Not at the table.”

She put it away.

“I said Hemmet’s,” Dusana said, on the step.

“You did.”

“This is Hemmet’s.”

“Yes.”

Dusana pulled her boots on, one and then the other, stamping each into place. The raven walked up her arm to her shoulder and settled with its feathers out, as it did when it meant to be carried somewhere it had not chosen.

“Pell will sell you the sky if you stand still long enough,” she said. “Somebody ought to be there to make faces. And there’s a carter at the market goes out past Altan’s plain. I can leave word where I am, so they know where to send.” She straightened. “That’s not the same as coming with you.”

“No.”

“Good. As long as we know.”

They followed the blue chalk down out of the Rain Doors. Beyond Hemmet’s the streets were dry and the doors fewer, and then there were no doors, only walls with ivy on them and gaps where gates had been. The path went down a slope of short grass between hedges. Below it Riarhin could see awnings, a great many of them, striped and patched and plain, spread over a hillside like washing laid out to dry.

The Turning Market did not stay where it was.

She noticed it first by a stall that sold kettles. It stood at the top of the slope when they came down into the awnings, with a brass bell on a bracket that the kettle-seller rang whenever someone picked up a kettle and put it down again. Riarhin marked it. She marked the cheese stall beside it, a woman selling candle-ends by weight, a boy with a tray of hot pies. They went along a row and down a step and along another row, and when she looked up for the kettles they were below her and to the left, and the cheese had gone somewhere else, and the pie boy was beside her selling pies.

Nobody else seemed troubled. A woman with a basket on her hip called, “Where’s Agna this morning?” and somebody shouted, “By the ropes!” and she went off towards the ropes. A man asked the pie boy where the knife-grinder was and the boy pointed with a pie.

Riarhin stopped trying to keep the kettles.

She had done this before. In Silkstrand, thirty-one days of it, in a house where linen was done on any morning the mistress liked and deliveries came when they came. She had built plan after plan on the days things happened, and every plan had needed the house to be what it had been the day before, and the house never was. Lena had pushed her bowl away across a table and told her to stop. Build one that needs them to be who they are for a minute.

She had hated it. It had worked.

So she listened instead. The kettle-seller’s bell. The woman who called the weight of her candle-ends in a voice that cracked on the high numbers. A cooper somewhere knocking a hoop down, steady, two blows and a rest. They moved, but each held still for a while where it was, and while it held she could walk by it.

Dusana watched her doing it.

“You’ve got the trick of it,” she said.

“Somebody taught me.”

“Who?”

“A woman who wanted me to stop talking.”

Dusana laughed and turned aside to a stall that sold tea in twists of paper, and smelt three kinds, and bought none of them, and told the seller why.

Across the row a household was loading a cart. It was a low cart, broad in the bed, with a painted rail; two porters in leather aprons were lifting a chest onto it while a woman in a brown hood directed them and a younger man argued with her about which way up it went. The springs were flat under the load. One of the porters sat down on the tail when the chest was on and wiped his face with his sleeve.

Riarhin looked at him a moment. Somebody was moving house. People did, here as anywhere, and paid men to carry for them, and the men sat down when they could. She went after Dusana.

Pell’s stall was halfway down the slope, under a yellow awning that had faded nearly white along its folds. He had strung lines between the poles and pegged his maps on them like shirts. There were dozens. Some were small as a hand. One was the size of a bedsheet and hung down to the ground, its lower edge brown with damp.

The maps moved.

Not in any wind. The lines on them moved. A road on the nearest sheet ran down to a river while Riarhin watched, thought better of it, and bent away along the bank. A wood on another put out a straggle of trees towards the margin. Lettering crawled. On the bedsheet a whole coast was redrawing itself in slow dark ink, and as it reached the edge of the paper a fresh edge seemed to be waiting for it, though when she looked for the join there was none.

“They go on,” said a voice behind the bedsheet. “There’s always one more page. You’d think it’d be good for business. People want the page after.”

He came round the sheet. He was a small round dead man with a cheerful face and a fringe of grey hair, and his hands were black with ink to the wrist, as though he had dipped them. There was ink on his cuffs and on one ear.

“Dusana,” he said. “You’ve brought customers. You never bring customers.”

“I’ve brought people who’ll want their money back.”

“Everyone wants their money back. It’s what people are like.” He looked at Riarhin, and at Cadan behind her, and at the sword. “You came down through the Rain Doors. You’ve got Hemmet’s porridge on your sleeve.”

Riarhin looked. She had.

“We followed your chalk.”

“Then it works. I tell people it works.” He beamed. “What are you after?”

She told him. She told it plainly, the stair with the bent rail, the stone with the hole worn through it, the three returns, each farther than the last before it turned. He listened with his head on one side. When she said the holed stone his face changed a little, and he stopped smiling while she finished.

“That stone,” he said. “That’s new. That’s this year, that stone. I walked past there in the spring and there was a stile and a sheepfold with nobody’s sheep in it.” He wiped his hands on his apron, which made no difference to either. “Everything that goes up past it comes back to it. I’ve had three people in this month who’d walked to it and walked to it. One of them gave up and lives at Hemmet’s now. Washes pots.”

“We want to go past it.”

“You want to go round it,” said Pell. “Not through. Through’s what it’s for. It’s a thing that brings you back.” He began to go along his lines, lifting a corner here and there and letting it drop. “Round’s a different matter. Round’s a way people find when they’ve lost theirs.”

He unpegged a sheet the size of a tray and laid it on the board at the front of the stall, weighting the corners with a stone, a cup, an inkpot and his own elbow.

It showed a hillside cut through by a ravine. The ravine went down in steps, drawn as little ladders of strokes, with dots along it that might have been houses or might have been people. At its foot the paper went pale. While she looked, a second line of steps began to draw itself beside the first, faint, stopped halfway, and faded.

“There,” said Pell. “Down there’s the lower ways. Under your stone, if you like. Past where it pulls.”

“What’s at the bottom?”

“Whatever’s at the bottom.” He tapped the pale place. “I’ve not been. Nobody I know has been and come back up to tell me, which might mean it’s lovely.”

Riarhin leaned closer. There was a smell coming off the paper, faint, under the ink. Salt. She touched her tongue to her lip and tasted it.

“It opens for people who’ve lost their road,” Pell said. “That’s what I hear and it’s what I’ve seen. Households that have been moved on. Places that went somewhere in the night. When a road goes out from under people, that comes open, and down they go.” He looked at her. “You’ve lost yours, from the sound of it. The stair’s had it off you three times.”

“When does it open?”

“When it opens.”

“That’s not a map. That’s a hope with ink on it.”

“Every map’s a hope with ink on it,” said Pell. “Mine just tell you.” He reached up and unpegged another sheet from the back line, older, gone brown, its corners soft as cloth. “Here. Look at this. I keep it to be honest.”

It was the same kind of map: a slope, a way down drawn in steps. The steps were firm and black. At the foot of them someone had written in a small careful hand, Here, on the turn of the season, and underlined it.

“Sold the twin of that,” Pell said, “to a man who wanted to get to his brother. He sat at the top eleven days. It never came. It’s never come since. The ink’s stopped moving on it, you see; that’s how I know. When they stop moving, they’ve stopped meaning anything.” He pegged it back. “He comes by sometimes and tells me what he thinks of me. I let him. He’s right.”

“Then why should I buy this one?”

“Because this one’s still moving.” Pell put his black finger on the faint second line, which had come back while they were talking and drawn itself a step further. “And because I’ll come and watch it with you. That’s what you’re buying. Me at the top of the ravine with my eyes open, telling you what it’s doing. And a try. If it opens, you go down. If it doesn’t—”

“Then what?”

“Then you’ve had a nice walk.”

Dusana made a face. Riarhin saw it from the corner of her eye. It was the face the raven made when someone took a crust away.

“No,” Riarhin said.

Pell spread his hands.

“I want it written,” she said. “On the map. How long we wait. What we do if it doesn’t come. Where we go back to and by what road.”

“You don’t trust it.”

“I don’t trust anything to be what it is for three days. I’ll wait for it a while. I want to know before I start where the while ends.”

Pell looked at her for a moment longer than he had so far. Then he took a pen from behind his ear, where she had not noticed one, and dipped it, and wrote in the margin of the tray-sized sheet, small and quick. She watched each word go down. Wait two lamplightings at the top. If closed at the second, come back up by the blue chalk to Hemmet’s. Pell owes the next sheet free.

He blew on it.

“There. Now I’ve to give you another one if this one’s no good. That’ll ruin me.”

“It’ll keep you honest.”

“That never did anyone’s business any good,” said Pell, and laughed, and held out his inky hand. “Price.”

They argued about the price. Dusana did most of it. She had a way of saying a number as if it had been insulted and she was only reporting the fact. Pell wanted mortal copper, and Riarhin had some, the coins from the bottom of her purse that had come down through the tunnel with her and still had the Mistlands’ salt in their creases. He weighed them on a little brass scale with a chain that hung from the awning pole.

“Living copper,” he said with satisfaction, watching the pan settle. “Honest weight. Ours drifts. You put a coin down here in the morning and by night it weighs what you think it ought to.” He tipped them into a pouch. “Twelve. And I’ll be at the top of the ravine by the second bell.”

“Ten,” said Dusana.

“Eleven, and I bring a lamp.”

“You’ll bring a lamp anyway. You’re frightened of the dark.”

“Everyone’s frightened of the dark,” Pell said. “Eleven.”

Riarhin gave him eleven. He gave her back the twelfth without being asked, and she put it in her purse and did not remark on it, and he grinned at her as though they had agreed on something.

Dusana found her carter by the water trough at the foot of the slope, watering a pair of mules. He was a lean dead man with a pipe he did not light, and he listened to her message twice through and said it back to her once.

“Pell’s ravine. Then down. Tell Altan’s people she’s gone down with the red-headed one and the monk and she’ll come up when she’s sent for.”

“And tell Toghrul I haven’t forgotten him.”

“And tell Toghrul she hasn’t forgotten him.”

“He’ll say I have.”

“He’ll say you have,” the carter agreed, and knocked the cold pipe against the trough.

Cadan had waited all this while beside a stall that sold rope, looking at a coil of tarred line with the expression of a man reading a letter. When Riarhin came back he said, “This is better than ours.”

“Do we need it?”

“We do not need it. It is better.”

She bought a length of it. He carried it over his shoulder with the old rope, and she saw him settle the two coils so that they lay one on the other without crossing, and then put his hand on them once.

They went down to the ravine in the afternoon.

It was not far below the market. The grass ran out and there was bare rock, grey and wet, and then the land stopped and went down. Riarhin stood at the edge. The ravine was narrow at the top, a long crack in the hill, and its sides went down in ledges and shelves and broken steps, with water running somewhere unseen at the bottom. Mist lay along it below the first few turns. There were marks on the near ledges, scrapes on the rock and a dropped peg and a scatter of straw, as though people had gone down this way carrying things and had not been tidy about it.

The air coming up out of it tasted of salt.

Pell arrived with his lamp, unlit, and a folding stool, and sat down on the stool at the edge with the map across his knees. He looked at the ravine. He looked at the map.

“Nothing,” he said. “Yet.”

Riarhin unrolled her own copy beside him. The faint second line of steps had drawn itself almost to the bottom of the sheet. It wavered there, uncertain, like a hand that has lost its place in a page.

She sat down on the rock. Dusana sat beside her and took bread out of her bag and broke it into three. The raven came down off her shoulder and stood on the rock between them, looking at the bread, and then at the ravine, and then, for longer, at the ravine.

Behind them on the path there was a creak and a grinding of wheels. Riarhin turned.

It was the low cart, with its painted rail, coming down from the market with the woman in the brown hood walking at the mules’ heads and the young man walking beside the chest, one hand on it, still arguing. The two porters came behind. They went past Pell’s stool without a glance and stopped at the head of the steps, and the woman looked down into the ravine with her mouth set, the way a person looks at weather they must go out in.

The cart sat on its flat springs at the edge, riding low.

Chapter 10 — The crowded descent

The woman in the brown hood was called Teodora, and Dusana knew her.

“I sat with your Fenn,” Dusana said, “at the station. The winter he came through. He wanted to know if anyone had seen a grey mare with one white foot.”

Teodora looked at her for a while from under the hood.

“He found her,” she said. “Out on the downs. He rides her now. He won’t come down here with us, he says the mare doesn’t like stairs.” She looked at the ravine. “Nor do I.”

Her sons were called Andras and Pim. Andras was the elder, broad and slow, and did not argue; Pim did all the arguing for both of them. They were grown men with their mother’s chin, and they were dead, as she was, and they had been put out of their house two nights ago when the lane it stood in went somewhere else.

“Went where?” Riarhin asked.

“Away,” said Pim. “We came back from the well and there was a hedge. A hedge, where the door was. Mother walked up and down it all night.”

“I wanted to see if it’d come back.”

“It wasn’t going to come back.”

“You don’t know that.”

“I know a hedge.”

The porters had been paid to the ravine’s edge and no farther. They took their money and went back up towards the market, and the cart stood at the head of the steps with its load roped on: a bed-frame in pieces, sacks, a crate of crockery packed in straw, a wicker cage with a hen in it, and the chest. The chest was the size of a coffin and painted all over in red and green, flowers and birds and a pair of figures dancing on the lid, the paint rubbed down to the wood where hands had lifted it for years.

“It was my grandmother’s,” Teodora said, to nobody in particular.

“It weighs what your grandmother weighed,” said Pim, “and she’s not in it.”

More people came while they waited. They came down from the market and along the rim of the ravine from both sides, by paths Riarhin had not seen: a man and a woman pushing a handcart with a sleeping child wedged among the bundles, three sisters carrying a table between them with its legs in the air, an old couple with nothing at all but a birdcage and a clock. They did not talk much. They found places along the edge and sat down and looked into the mist, the way people at a ferry look at the water.

An old man came and sat down beside Riarhin without asking. He had a stick and a small neat bundle tied in a blue cloth, and a sprig of something green stuck in his hatband.

“You’re for down?” he said.

“Yes.”

“So am I. My sister’s at the bottom. Not the very bottom. There’s a place partway, a churchyard with apple trees. She’s in it.” He took the sprig out of his hat and looked at it. “It’s her day. I go every year on her day. I take her rosemary. She never liked it, she says it smells of funerals, but she’d be cross if I came without.”

“Is that the way down? This?”

“It’s the way when it’s open.” He put the sprig back. “I’m Imre. I’m not moving house. I don’t know what all these are doing.”

“Their roads went.”

“Ah,” said Imre. He looked along the ravine’s edge at the households and their carts. “Well. I hope they find them.” And then, with more interest, “Is that a raven?”

Pell sat on his stool with the map across his knees. When the market lamps came on up the slope behind them, one after another, he stood and lit his own from his tinderbox and set it on the rock beside him.

“First lamplighting,” he said. “Nothing yet. The line’s still going down the paper. Look.”

Riarhin looked. The faint second line of steps on her own map had reached the bottom edge and was trying to go past it. Where it met the margin the ink thickened and pooled.

Dusana had the raven in her lap and was working a burr out of the feathers at its throat. It let her, with its eyes shut.

“It doesn’t like waiting either,” she said.

The mist moved at full dark.

It drew back down the ravine as a sheet draws back off a bed, and under it there were steps. They were cut into the rock, wet, black, uneven, going down in short flights between ledges with turns at the end of each, and they had not been there before. Riarhin had looked at that rock all afternoon and remembered every seam in it. The seams were still there. The steps had come up between them.

“Open,” said Pell, standing. “That’s open. Go on.”

The whole edge of the ravine moved at once.

Nobody ran. But everyone stood, and lifted what they had to lift, and went towards the steps, and the steps were narrow, and there were a great many people. The handcart with the sleeping child went first because it was nearest. The three sisters turned their table on its side to get it down the first flight. The old couple went down with the clock between them.

Riarhin held her company back.

She had seen it before she had thought it: the first flight was wide enough for two, the second for one, and the turn at the bottom of the second would take a cart only if the cart was emptied. If she and Cadan and Dusana went down now they would be in the way of the people with the furniture, and a household with a table would wait behind them on a wet step for want of the room they were taking. She put her arm out and Dusana stopped against it.

“Let them go,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t going to push.”

“I know.”

Cadan had already stepped back to the rock beside Pell’s stool, where nobody needed to stand.

Teodora’s cart went to the head of the steps. Andras and Pim began to unrope the load. The bed-frame came off, and the sacks, and the crockery crate; Andras carried them down the first flight two at a time and came back. The hen went down in its cage, complaining. Then there was the chest.

“Leave it,” Pim said.

“I will not leave it.”

“Mother, it won’t go round the turn. Look at it. Look at the turn.”

“It came up the lane. It came out of the house when the house was going. It’ll go down a step.”

“It’s not a step, it’s a—”

“Lift your end.”

They lifted it. They got it down the first flight between them with Teodora walking ahead backwards, one hand out as though she could steer it. At the head of the second flight they had to tip it on its end. At the turn it stuck.

It stuck across the step with its painted lid against one wall and its feet against the other, and Andras on the step below it and Pim on the step above, and behind Pim on the flight there were the three sisters with their table and the man and woman whose handcart had gone on ahead with their child in it.

“My boy’s gone on,” the woman said. “He’s gone on down. Let us by.”

“We’re trying.”

“Lift it.”

“It’s stuck.”

Riarhin went down.

She went down the first flight on the wall side, past the sisters, who flattened themselves and their table to let her, and stood on the step beside Pim and looked at the chest. It had jammed by one corner of the lid, where a carved bird stood out a thumb’s width from the wood. She put both hands under the lower edge.

“When I say,” she told Andras. “Not up. Towards you, and round.”

“Round which way?”

“Your left.”

She took the weight. It was heavy, solid oak and old iron, and there was something inside that slid and knocked. Her thigh pulled. She set her feet and said now and they went round, Andras walking it backwards down three steps, and the carved bird scraped along the rock and lost a wing and came free.

Teodora made a small sound.

“It’s a bird,” said Pim. “It’s a wooden bird.”

The handcart woman went past them all at a run with her husband after her. The three sisters followed with the table.

Above, Pell shouted something.

Riarhin looked up. The mist was coming back. It came up the ravine from below, slowly, as the sheet had drawn back but in the other direction, and as it reached each flight of steps the steps went soft under it. She could see the edges going. The sisters with the table were below it and moving fast. Teodora was below it. Andras with the chest was at its edge.

“Down,” Riarhin said. “Go.”

Andras went, with the chest on his back now and his mother taking the foot of it, and the mist touched the step where he had been and it was rock, plain grey rock with a seam in it. Riarhin was on the step above. She put her foot down. It was rock. The steps below her were not there.

Pim was beside her, breathing hard.

“Mother,” he said, to the rock.

From somewhere below, under the mist, came Teodora’s voice, quite close and quite cross.

“Pim! Where’s the hen?”

The hen was on the ledge at Pim’s feet in its cage, where Andras had set it down to take the chest.

“Well,” said Dusana, when they were back on the rim. “That was a quarrel that cost something.”

Pim sat with the hen cage on his knees and did not answer. Imre gave him a piece of cake out of his blue bundle. The rest of the people who had not got down sat where they had been sitting before, and some of them lay down, and one or two went back up towards the market.

Riarhin unrolled her map in the light of Pell’s lamp.

The faint second line had stopped at the bottom of the sheet. It sat there, thickened. But there was a third line now, fainter still, that came off the side of the ravine near the top and went down a different way: not steps, but a long wavering stroke, like a crack.

“That,” said Pell, leaning over, “is the water.”

“What water?”

“There’s water down the side there. You can hear it. There’s a runnel comes off the hill and goes down the face.” He looked at the map, and at her. “It’s moving. That’s opening, or about to. It’s not steps. You won’t like it.”

“How long till the second lamplighting?”

Pell looked up the slope at the market lamps.

“Long enough. Not much longer.”

The runnel came off the lip of the ravine thirty paces along, where a fold in the rock had channelled the hill’s water into a single cold rope that went over the edge and down. Below it the rock was black and green with weed, and there were holds in it, ledges and cracks and knobs, and they were wet, and the water ran over all of them.

Riarhin went first.

She went down with her face to the rock and the water running into her collar and down her back and out of the bottoms of her sleeves. It was cold enough to make her teeth ache. The holds were good where she could find them and slick where she could not. Her left hand, with the healing cut across its back, found a crack and the cut opened a little in the wet and stung. She went on.

Ten feet down she found a ledge, broad as a bench, and stood on it and called up.

The packs came down on Cadan’s new rope. They came down wet. Hers struck the rock twice on the way and arrived streaming, and when she opened it she knew without looking further: the dried food was in the top, in its cloth, and the cloth was soaked through. The bread had gone to paste. The dried meat would keep. The salt was in its own pouch and the pouch had held.

Dusana came down with her skirt knotted up at her knees and her mouth set, swearing in Tulkani with great steadiness at each hold. The raven came down on its own, in three short furious flights from ledge to ledge, and landed on Riarhin’s shoulder and shook the water off itself into her ear.

Then Imre. Cadan lowered him in a sling of the old rope, with the blue bundle tied to his chest, and he came down slowly, turning a little, with his stick held out in front of him like a man feeling his way along a dark passage.

“My sister,” he said, when his feet were on the ledge, “is going to laugh at me.”

Then Pim, with the hen cage hung round his neck by a cord. The hen had stopped complaining. It looked out between the withies with an expression of contempt.

Cadan came last. He came down neatly and without hurry and stood on the ledge with the water running off his shaven head, and Riarhin watched his hands on the rope and saw them close on it each time.

“Here,” she said, and held out her hand for the coils.

“I can carry them.”

“I know you can. Give me one.”

He gave her the old rope. She put it over her shoulder with the wet pack.

They went on down. The runnel went down the face in long drops with ledges between, and at each ledge they stopped and redid what they carried. Dusana took the soaked bread, what was left of it, and wrapped it in her own shawl, which was only damp, and said it would do for frying if there was anything to fry it in. Riarhin took Imre’s bundle so he could use both hands. Pim would not give up the hen. Cadan took the salt pouch from Riarhin and put it inside his habit against his chest, because it was the one dry place any of them had.

Halfway down, Remorse caught.

The scabbard’s end struck a knob of rock as she turned on a ledge, and the sword jarred in it, and the block at the root of the grip ticked against its seat, loud in the space between the rock and the falling water.

Pim heard it.

“That’s loose,” he said.

“I know.”

“That’s Lethai work.” He was looking at the grip where it stood up over her shoulder, at the binding with its cut and its stiff pale cord. “My father took a buckle down once that was Lethai work. You want the woman under the overhang. At the bottom, where the shelter is. Saelith. She mends that kind of thing.”

“Is she Lethai?”

“She’s whatever she is. She fixed his buckle. Charged him a story.” Pim hitched the hen cage. “She’ll want to see it.”

The bottom of the ravine was a stony floor with a stream down the middle of it and mist lying along the stream. The mist had come down with them, or they had come down to it. Riarhin could see, back up the way they had come, nothing at all of the steps, only the face with the runnel on it, and above that dark.

There were people on the floor. The households who had got down by the steps were spread along it, sorting their goods on the stones, calling to each other. Teodora found them before they found her. She came along the stream at a pace Riarhin would not have thought she had in her and took Pim by both ears and kissed him on the forehead and then took the hen cage from round his neck.

“You left her,” she said to Andras, over her shoulder.

“I had the chest.”

“You had the chest.”

The chest was sitting on the stones with one wing gone off its carved bird. Teodora put the hen cage on top of it.

Imre took his bundle back from Riarhin and thanked her and asked whether she was going his way, which she was not. He pointed down the floor of the ravine, where it bent left under a lip of rock.

“The churchyard’s that way. The shelter’s this.” He pointed the other way, right, where the ravine opened out under a great overhang of stone. There were lights under it. “Saelith’s is at the back. You’ll smell it before you see it.”

He settled his hat with the rosemary in it, and set off down the stream with his stick, alone, at the slow sure pace of a man who had made the same visit every year and meant to make it every year after.

Riarhin watched him go round the bend.

Then they went the other way.

Under the overhang it was dry. The rock went up and out above them in a great curve like the roof of a mouth, black with old smoke, and under it there were lean-tos and fires and people sleeping and a well with a wooden cover. At the back, where the overhang came down nearly to the ground, there was a long low building of stone and timber built into the rock, with its doors open and light coming out of them, and a smell came out with the light: hot metal, and oil, and the sharp clean smell of a hide being scraped.

There was hammering inside. It stopped as they came to the door. A woman at a bench at the far end looked up.

She had a pair of tongs in one long hand and something small and glowing in their jaws. She looked at them, and at the sword standing up over Riarhin’s shoulder, and her eyes went to the grip and stayed there.

“Put it on the bench,” she said. “Before it comes off in somebody.”

Chapter 11 — The repair beneath her thumb

“That’s what I said,” Dusana remarked, to the workshop in general.

Nobody took any notice of her. The woman at the bench had gone back to what was in her tongs. She laid it on a small anvil, a rivet no bigger than a barleycorn, and set it with three light taps into a bracket of blackened iron, and turned the bracket over and looked at the back of it, and handed it to a dead woman waiting at her elbow.

“It’ll hold. Don’t hang anything on it heavier than the lamp.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You were going to hang the kettle on it. You said so last time.”

The dead woman took the bracket and went out, past Riarhin, with an air of having been unjustly found out.

Riarhin unbuckled the scabbard from her back. Her fingers were stiff from the climb and the wet, and the buckle fought her. She laid the sword on the end of the bench, where there was a clear space among the scraps of leather and the coils of wire, and stood back from it.

The workshop was long and low, a single room running back under the rock, with benches down both sides and a forge at the far end whose chimney went up into a crack in the overhang. Lamps hung from the beams. On racks along the walls there was work waiting, or work abandoned, it was hard to tell which: a quiver with its seam half stitched and the needle still in it, a spear-socket with no spear, a saddle tree, a long knife in three pieces laid out on a cloth in their proper order, a cloak pin, a helmet with a dent in it that somebody had begun to raise and stopped. At the next bench a young dead man with spectacles was gluing the horn panes back into a lantern and swearing at them under his breath. A boy was sweeping filings into a dustpan. Nobody looked at the sword.

The woman wiped her hands on her apron.

She was tall, taller than Riarhin, and narrow, and her hair was the pale colour that was almost no colour at all, cut short and pushed back off her forehead. Her face was long. Her hands were longer. They were the first thing Riarhin had seen of her, closing the tongs, and she looked at them now: long fingers, square at the ends, with old white nicks across the knuckles, and the right thumb shiny along its pad and up its inner side, the skin there smooth and tight and faintly pink, as skin is after a burn has healed.

The ears were Lethai. Lethai-al, by the height and the colouring; Riarhin had lived among them long enough to know the look of the forest people from the look of the scholars.

“Saelith?”

“Yes.” She did not ask who had sent them. She picked the sword up by the scabbard, at the middle, as a person picks up a thing they are used to the weight of, and drew it an inch, and let it go back. It ticked. She drew it again and held it there and turned it so the lamp fell along the grip.

“Somebody’s cut this.”

“In a fight.”

“I’d hope so. I’d not like to think you did it carving a joint.” She put the scabbard down and drew the blade clear and laid it flat on the bench on a fold of felt. She did not look along the edge. She did not look at the metal at all. She looked at the grip, and put the burn-shiny thumb on the cut place in the binding, and pressed, and felt the block move under the wrap.

“This wrap’s good,” she said. “Who did it?”

“I did.”

“Who taught you?”

“Someone who taught me a great many things.”

Saelith glanced up at that and down again.

“Well. They knew their work. It’s cut to your hand. The end’s finished on a slant.” She ran her thumb down to the end of the binding, where Riarhin had always finished it, as Ghere had finished the first one, the last turn cut at an angle and tucked. “That’s a habit. Somebody taught you that or you’d have cut it straight like everybody else.”

She took a small knife from the bench and slid it under the last turn.

“May I?”

“Yes.”

She unwound it.

Riarhin had rewrapped that grip more times than she had kept count of. She had done it on ships and in barns and once in a cell with the cord held in her teeth. She knew how it looked bare. The block at the root, with its ornament too fine to read. Beneath it, the grip itself, and on the grip, close to the wood or whatever the grip was made of, an old dark cord-work, laid flat and tight and gone hard and smooth as horn with age. She had always wrapped over it. It had never occurred to her to take it off. It was part of the sword, as the block was.

Saelith unwound Ghere’s pattern turn by turn and laid the cord aside in a loose coil. The cut place came away in two pieces. Under it the old cord-work showed, dark, a little frayed on the left side where the blade that had cut the binding had touched it too.

Saelith stopped.

She did not say anything. She bent closer, and turned the grip a little in the lamplight, and put the shiny thumb on the old cord where it ran up under the block and ended, and moved the thumb along it, feeling it. There was a knot there that Riarhin had felt every time under her own wrap and never seen: a flat knot, three strands laid over and under each other and drawn down so close they had become one surface, finishing in the shadow of the block where nobody would see it unless they took the block off.

“That’s mine,” Saelith said.

The young man with the lantern looked up and went back to his panes.

“That’s my knot. I finish them so. Three strands, flat, and under the block where it won’t catch.” She turned the grip over. “And this.” She put her fingertip on the block, on the edge where it met the grip, where there was a fine line, a seam so close Riarhin had always taken it for a scratch. “I cut this seat. The old one was cracked through when he brought it to me, and I cut it out and cut a new one and set the block in it, and it’s held all this time. It’s only loose now because your fight’s cut down to it. Look. There. The cord’s let go and the block’s lifted.”

“When he brought it to you.”

“The Ghe’hai who carried this.” Saelith set the sword down gently on its felt. She looked up. Her eyes were grey-green, the colour of lichen on a wet stone, and they were puzzled. “I did his gear for years. All of it. He’d bring it in at the end of a season and leave it and grumble when I took too long.” She looked from the sword to Riarhin and leaned forward, her tongs resting on the bench. “Where is he? He went out one autumn and said he’d bring this back for the binding to be done properly. He’d done something to it in the wet and the old binding was going. He never came. People said he’d gone south to one of his women and stopped there.” She almost smiled. “Did he send you down with it? Is he outside?”

Riarhin heard herself breathe.

“I found it.”

“Found it.”

“In the Valewood. On a dead man.”

Saelith’s face did not change at once. She looked at Riarhin a while longer. Then she looked down at the sword, and put her long hand flat on the felt beside it, not touching it.

“Ah,” she said.

At the next bench the young man held a pane up to the lamp, decided it was the wrong one, and put it down.

“Was there anything else?” Saelith asked, after a time. “With him.”

Riarhin could have said no. She found that she was going to answer.

“A bow.”

“Describe it.”

“Short. Recurved past the hand, a long way. Silvery wood. The layers are joined with something I’ve never seen anywhere else. It pulls harder than it should for the size. There’s carving along the back of the limbs, fine, following the grain. You can’t see it from more than a pace away. You feel it.”

“Carving.”

“Yes.”

“There was no carving when it went out of here.” Saelith frowned. “That bow was plain. Back and belly both. I never had my hands on the limbs; the bow-work wasn’t mine and I’d not have touched it. I did the string and the nocks and the grip.” She shook her head. “Somebody’s worked it since I last saw it. I’d like to know who. I’d like to tell them what I think.”

Riarhin said nothing. She had carried Silence for more than half her life. She had assumed the carving was as old as the bow. She had never thought to ask anyone, because there had been nobody to ask.

“What else?”

“A cloak. Green when I found it. It doesn’t stay one colour.”

“No, it doesn’t. That’s not mine either. He came by it somewhere and never said where.” Saelith’s mouth went thin. “What else?”

“Mail. Fine mesh, Lethai. A hauberk. People take it for cloth.”

“They would.”

“The collar fastens with a clasp. Silver. A hook and an eye, made like two leaves.”

Saelith put her face in her hands.

It was so sudden that Riarhin thought for a moment she was weeping. Then she heard the sound she was making, and it was a laugh, short and helpless, that went on longer than she meant it to.

“I made that,” Saelith said through her fingers. “I hated that clasp. I made him four others. Plain ones, good ones, that would open with a cold hand. He wanted the leaves. He insisted. I told him the stem was too thin to take the eye and it would bend the first time he pulled it hard.” She took her hands away. Her eyes were wet. “Does it bend?”

“It bent the first winter. I’ve straightened it with the flat of a knife more times than—” Riarhin stopped. “Often.”

“Of course it did.” Saelith wiped her face with the back of her wrist. “Of course it did. Stupid thing. I told him.”

She asked for nothing more. She took up a lamp and held it close and went back to the grip, and began, with the point of a little awl, to tease the frayed strands of the old cord-work apart at the cut place, slowly, to see how far the damage went without undoing the knot. Riarhin stood and watched her hands.

She knew what they were doing. She knew the knot now. And under her own thumb, under every wrapping she had ever put on that grip, there had been this woman’s work, this woman’s finish, waiting where nobody would see it.

She had found it in rain.

He had been lying among roots, under a bank, on his side. A beech or an oak, something old, its roots come out of the bank in an arch where the stream had cut under it, and he was in the hollow of the arch as if he had crawled in there out of the wet, or been put there. She had been hungry, she had been walking for two days on what she had carried out of Ghere’s house and she had eaten the last of it the night before. She had seen the sword first: the hilt standing out of the leaves, the block, the wrapped grip, dark with wet. She had thought it was a root.

She had got down on her knees and then lower, on her side, because the arch was low, and reached in. The leaves were wet against her cheek. She could feel them still, cold and slick and smelling of rot and iron. His hand had been there in front of her face, lying open on the leaves, the near one, the left, long-fingered, grey, the nails dark, and for one moment she had thought it would move. It had not moved. Beyond the hand there had been the blue.

She had not looked at his face for long. She had looked at what he had.

There had been a strap at his shoulder, the strap of the sword’s carrying belt, and it was stiff and swollen with wet and the buckle had rusted in its tongue, and she had worked at it for a long time, lying in the leaves with her arm in under the root, until it came loose. She remembered the buckle exactly: an iron buckle, square, with a bar across it worn bright where the tongue rubbed. She had attended to the buckle because she had needed it to open.

She remembered the hand. Long. The fingers a little curled, and straight, each of them; she had looked at that hand for a long time. The other one she had not seen. It had been under him.

She looked at Saelith’s hands on the grip, and looked away.

“I can send for him,” Saelith said, without looking up.

Riarhin did not answer at once.

“He’s down here?”

“Everyone’s down here, one way and another.” The awl went on, delicately, in and out of the frayed strands. “There’s a way of asking. If he’s anywhere he can see it, he’ll come. He always came when I had his things.” She paused. “You’ve had them a long time.”

“Yes.”

“Then he’ll want to see you.”

“No,” said Riarhin.

Saelith nodded as though that were an answer she had expected, and went on with the awl.

There was a stool by the door. Riarhin sat on it.

Dusana had found a bench along the wall where a man was waiting with a broken harness, and had sat down beside him and begun to talk to him about horses. Cadan stood just inside the door, where he would be out of everyone’s way, with the two coils of rope still over his shoulder and the salt pouch inside his habit. He looked at Riarhin once. She saw him decide not to ask, and then saw him decide that he would.

“What would you like me to do?”

“Nothing yet.”

“Very well.”

He took the ropes off his shoulder and laid them by the door, one on the other, without crossing, and then sat down on the floor beside them with his back against the wall, and closed his eyes, and was still.

The workshop went on. The young man finished his lantern and held it up and was pleased with it, and lit it, and it smoked, and he blew it out and was less pleased. A woman came in with a cooking pot whose handle had come off and was told it would be tomorrow. The boy with the dustpan emptied it into a bin by the forge and came back down the room sweeping. Saelith worked at the grip and did not look up. Once she said something to herself under her breath, in Lethai, that Riarhin heard and understood: there you are. It was said to the knot.

The raven had left Dusana’s shoulder. Riarhin saw it on the end of Saelith’s bench, among the scraps, walking about with its head on one side. It stopped beside a little dish of brass rivets. It looked at Saelith. It looked at the dish. It took one.

“Put that back,” said Saelith, without turning round.

The raven put it back. It did not seem surprised to be told. It walked off along the bench with its feathers up and stood on the coil of Ghere’s old binding and began to worry the cut end of it with its beak.

“Bird,” said Dusana from the wall. “Leave it.”

It left it.

Riarhin watched it go back to Dusana. She watched Saelith’s thumb, which had stopped moving, rest on the knot under the block. She sat with her hands between her knees. Her breathing had steadied. She could feel the wet in her clothes going cold, and her thigh, and the sting of the cut on the back of her hand where the climb had opened it.

He had gone out one autumn, Saelith had said. He had done something to the binding in the wet. He had meant to bring it back.

He had lain under the bank with it instead.

She got up.

Saelith looked round.

“Send,” Riarhin said.

Saelith looked at her a moment longer. Then she nodded, and put down the awl, and took up the little knife again, and with great care cut a short length of the old dark cord from where it was frayed through past mending: a finger’s length, no more, with the three strands still twisted in it.

“It won’t be quick,” she said. “Asking doesn’t go quickly. It might be tonight. It might be days. You’ll not mind waiting?”

“No.”

Saelith carried the piece of cord to the door. Beside the doorpost, at the height of a hand, there was an iron hook driven into the timber, and on the hook there were other things hanging: a twist of red wool, a horseshoe nail, a scrap of blue cloth with a button on it. She hung the cord among them and went back to her bench.

The cord turned a little on the hook in the draught from the door, one way and then the other, and settled.

Chapter 12 — The place she remembers

Saelith let them have the room behind the workshop. It had been a store once, and there were still shelves on the walls with jars on them and a smell of linseed, and three pallets on the floor stuffed with something that crackled. A brazier stood in the corner. Saelith lit it with a coal carried from the forge in her tongs, and went back to her bench, and they heard her working.

Dusana fried the paste that had been bread in Saelith’s small iron pan, with the last of the dried meat chopped into it and a handful of something green she had begged from a woman at the well.
