It came out brown and crisp at the edges and soft in the middle. They ate it from the pan with their fingers. The raven had the burnt bits. Cadan ate his share and thanked Dusana for it with great formality, and she told him not to start.

Riarhin ate. She could taste it. She could not have said whether she liked it.

Through the wall she could hear Saelith’s awl, small sounds, a pause, small sounds again. Then the hammer, twice, lightly, as on something that needed only to be persuaded. Then nothing for a long while.

The others slept. Dusana slept on her back with her mouth open and one hand over the raven, which had gone to sleep in the crook of her arm with its head under its wing. Cadan slept as he sat, against the wall, without seeming to have lain down at any point. Riarhin lay on the crackling pallet and looked at the jars on the shelves and listened to the workshop.

When the light under the door had not changed for an hour she got up and went through.

Saelith was sitting at her bench with the sword in front of her on its felt. She had not touched the block. She had cleaned the old cord-work where the cut had frayed it, and laid the loose strands down with something that shone, and she was sitting with her hands in her lap looking at it.

She heard Riarhin and did not turn.

“I can re-seat that in the morning,” she said. “The seat’s sound. It only wants the block taking off and the old glue cleaning out and setting again. I’ll not do it at night. My eyes aren’t what they were.”

“You’re dead.”

“My eyes aren’t what they were when I was alive, either.” Saelith turned her head. “You can’t sleep.”

“No.”

Saelith looked at her a little longer. Then she got up, stiffly, a hand on the bench, and went to the far end of the workshop, past the forge, to a shelf set into the rock above it where it was warm. There were things on the shelf that did not look like work: a cup with no handle, a child’s shoe, a bundle of dried flowers gone the colour of paper. She took down a stone and brought it back.

It was a river stone, the size of a fist, grey and smooth, with a hole worn clean through one end of it as water wears a hole, round and polished inside. Saelith held it in her open palm.

“I’ve only got the going,” she said. “Not where he went. You’ll want to know where he went. I can’t give you that. I’ve only got him going out.”

“What is it?”

“It’s the stone off my doorstep. In the old place, up in the wood. There was a step of stones down to the path and this was the bottom one, with the hole, and I used to put my thumb through it when I sat there.” She turned it. “When I came down here it was in my apron. I don’t know how. I put my hand on it and he goes out of my door again. Every time. The last time. I don’t know why it’s that and not something else.” She held it out. “It turns, after. It points where he went. Only that one way. I’ve had it turn in my hand a hundred times and it’s never pointed anywhere else.”

Riarhin looked at it.

“Things keep, here,” Saelith said. “That’s all I know about it. Some things keep.”

Riarhin went back to the store-room door and opened it.

“Dusana.”

Dusana woke at once, as people do who have slept in wagons. She looked at Riarhin’s face in the brazier light, and did not ask anything, and got up with the raven clutched to her chest, still asleep, and came. Cadan was already standing.

Riarhin knelt on the floor of the workshop by the bench, because she did not want to be standing if her knees went. Saelith gave her a folded sack to kneel on. It was the same kindness the keeper had done her with the sphere, years ago, a pad under the knees, and she disliked how much it frightened her.

“I’ll name the room,” she said.

She named it aloud. The workshop under the overhang. The bench. The door with the hook beside it. Saelith. Dusana, with the bird. Cadan.

“And me,” said Dusana, when she had finished. “And you. Riarhin. And the bird’s asleep, so it doesn’t count.”

“If I don’t answer, take it out of my hand.”

“Take it out of your hand.”

“Don’t touch it yourself. Push my hand open.”

“Push your hand open,” Cadan said, from behind her. He had come to where she could not see him and would know where he was.

Saelith put the stone in her palm.

It was warm from the shelf. Its weight was a stone’s weight. Riarhin closed her fingers round it and her thumb went, without being told, into the hole worn through the end.

Rain.

Not rain on her. Rain on leaves above, a lot of leaves, the whole wood dripping, and she was sitting on a step of stones outside an open door with her thumb in the hole of the bottom stone and her apron wet at the knees. Behind her a forge was banked low and ticking as it cooled. In front of her a path went down through ferns. She was waiting for him to go so she could go in and shut the door.

He came out past her.

She saw his back. He was tall, and he moved quickly, his foot finding the path while his weight was still on the step. The sword was on his back in its scabbard, the block standing up over his shoulder, and she knew without looking that the binding was going at the root and he had not let her see to it. The bow was across his back with it. The cloak over both, green, wet already at the hem. Under his left arm, against his side, he carried something wrapped in oilcloth, round, the size of a child’s head. Where the oilcloth folded, at the top, there was a gap the width of a finger, and in the gap there was blue.

He did not turn round. He lifted his hand, the right hand, over his shoulder, without looking back, and went on down the path.

She watched him to the bend.

At the bend he did not go left, down to the stream, as everyone went. He went straight on, into the ferns, where there was no path, and she knew he would cross the stream higher, at the stones, and go up the far side by the old way.

And then the blue.

It was not in the stone, or it was not only in the stone. It was in the gap of the oilcloth as he went, and it came with him, and it was the same blue. A line crossed it. She knew where the line turned. Her body knew. The stones in the stream, the far bank, a climb, and then openings, one and then another, the third displaced above the second, and a passage narrowing where it should have widened, and somebody ought to be waiting at the far end, and their face would not come.

She knew it. She had known it for most of her life without knowing where it began. And it began here. It began at a door in the wood, on a step of stones, with the forge cooling behind.

The stone turned in her hand.

It turned as a needle turns, of itself, the hole swinging round under her thumb, and stopped, and pointed. Up the room. Past the forge. Into the rock.

“Riarhin.”

She opened her hand. Nobody had to push it. The stone lay on her palm with its hole pointing at the back wall of the workshop, and she looked at it and breathed.

“I’m here,” she said.

She drew it on the bench in chalk. She did it fast, before she could stop, the route she had drawn once on slate in a keeper’s house with her sisters watching: the line and its turns, the openings, the third above the second, the narrowing. Her hand did not shake. It never did when she was drawing a thing she knew.

Saelith watched over her shoulder. Then she took the chalk out of Riarhin’s fingers and drew beside it.

She drew a door. A little square with a step under it. A path going down, and a bend, and the path going on left to a stream, and a fainter line going straight on from the bend through nothing, across the stream at a place she marked with three dots, and up. Then she stopped, and put the chalk down, and moved Riarhin’s drawing with the flat of her hand, smudging it, to bring its first turn to the end of her own line.

They met.

The first turn of the route Riarhin had carried for more than half her life was the crossing of the stream at the three stones, below a workshop door in the Valewood.

“That’s the old way,” Saelith said. “Up to the old place. He went up there every season. I never asked what for. He wouldn’t have told me.” She looked at the drawing. “He’d have been coming back down it after. The same way. Back to my door. With that.” She meant the sword.

Riarhin looked at the chalk.

She had walked out of Ghere’s house with a coat and a bundle and a binding, and had gone where her feet went, angry, for two days. She had never known where she was. She had come to a bank with a stream cut under it and roots in an arch, and a hand in the leaves, and blue beyond the hand, and the blue had given her a road that began nowhere. For years it had begun nowhere. She had known every turn of it and could not find the first, because the first was a door she had never stood at, on the other side of a stream she had crossed without looking.

She put her finger on the narrowing. A passage narrowing where it should widen. The bank. The stream cut under the bank. The roots.

He had not been far from home.

“He had a finger,” Saelith said, “that he’d broke in a door. The second finger, on the right hand. He set it himself, badly, and it went crooked, and he’d not let me break it again to set it straight. He said it was his finger to have crooked.” She was looking at the chalk, not at Riarhin. “It made him hold the grip wrong. That’s why the binding went at the root. I told him.”

Riarhin heard it.

She heard every word of it, exactly, the way she heard everything, and she kept it, and she could not do anything with it.

The right hand. The hand she had seen in the leaves had been the left, straight, long, the fingers a little curled. She had looked at it for a long time. She could see it now with her eyes open. The right hand had been under him. She had not seen it. She had worked the strap loose at his shoulder with her arm in under the root, and she had felt his coat and his shoulder and the cold of him through the coat, and she had not once put her hand on his right hand, and she had not looked.

Bea, sitting on the edge of a bed in Silkstrand with her hair down. Like this. Her own finger, straight, long, laid along Riarhin’s nose, from the bridge to the tip. Only crooked. Here. A little push at the middle joint. Like a stick in water. And Riarhin crossing her eyes to look at it, and Bea laughing.

She had not looked. The child under the blanket had not looked at the face either.

She put the stone down on the bench.

It rolled, a little, on its round side, and stopped, and turned, and lay with its hole towards the back wall.

“I can’t,” Riarhin said.

Nobody asked her what.

Dusana took her out.

She did not take her arm. She walked beside her, close, the sleeping raven held against her chest in one hand, and with the other she picked up a lamp from the end of the bench, and she went out of the workshop door and Riarhin went with her because Dusana was going and it was a direction. Under the overhang the fires had burned down. People slept by them in heaps of blankets. The well with the wooden cover stood a little way off, and there was a bench of stone beside it, and Dusana went to the bench and put the lamp on the ground and sat down.

Riarhin sat down.

Dusana did not say anything. After a while she put the raven down on the bench between them, where it shifted and grumbled and settled with its head still under its wing. She took the cover off the well and let the bucket down and drew it up, and filled a tin cup that hung on a chain from the windlass, and gave it to Riarhin.

Riarhin held it.

She sat with it in her two hands. The water was cold through the tin. Under the overhang it was quiet in the way of a place where a great many people are asleep, with small sounds all through it, somebody coughing, somebody turning over, a child saying a word in its sleep and stopping.

She had left the boots. They had been too large. She had pulled at one of them and it had not come, swollen on the foot with wet, and she had looked at the size of it and left it. She had taken the sword, and the bow from his back, and the cloak, wet through and heavy as a sodden blanket, and the mail from under the cloak, which had taken longest, which she had had to roll him for. She had rolled him. She had rolled him towards her, out of the arch, onto his back, and pulled, and she had not looked at his face, and she had rolled him back.

She had not buried him. She had not thought of it. She had been hungry.

She had sat on the bank afterwards with all of it in her lap and eaten a handful of beech-nuts, and they had been bitter, and she had been glad of them.

Dusana sat beside her and looked at the well.

After a long time, Riarhin lifted the cup to drink. She turned it in her hands first, a little, round, looking at the rim.

“You did that at the fire,” Dusana said. “With my cup.”

Riarhin stopped. She looked at the rim of the tin cup. There was no chip in it.

She drank.

Chapter 13 — The man who asks

In the morning Saelith took the block off.

She did it with a thin blade warmed at the forge and a great deal of patience, working the old glue soft a little at a time, and the block came away from its seat at last with a small reluctant sound, like a tooth. She laid it on the felt. It was smaller off the sword than it looked on it. The ornament on it was still too fine to read, close to the eye; it looked like grain, or like writing that had been rubbed until only the shape of writing was left.

“Don’t touch that,” Saelith said to the raven, which had come to see.

The raven considered the block for some time and then walked away along the bench with an air of never having wanted it.

Riarhin held things. She held the grip steady while Saelith scraped the seat clean, and held the lamp, and passed the little pot of glue from the brazier when it was warm enough, and was told she had passed it too soon and passed it again later. Saelith worked without talking. Her burned thumb pressed the block home into the clean seat and held it there, and held it, and the workshop went on round them, and the young man with the spectacles came back to say his lantern still smoked.

“It smokes because you’ve trimmed the wick crooked,” Saelith said, without moving her thumb.

“I trimmed it straight.”

“Then it’s smoking out of spite.”

He went away to trim it again.

When she took her thumb off, the block stayed. She did not let Riarhin pick the sword up.

“Tomorrow,” she said. “It wants a night. Then you can bind it.”

“You’ll not bind it?”

“You’ll want to,” Saelith said, “I should think,” and went to wash the glue off her hands.

Dusana spent the day with Teodora’s household, who had found a corner under the overhang and were making a home of it with the bed-frame and the chest and a great deal of argument. She came back at noon with a bowl of soup for each of them and a report that the hen had laid.

“Pim says it’s a sign.”

“Of what?”

“He didn’t say. I don’t think he knows. I think he just wanted something to be a sign.” She gave Riarhin her bowl. “Eat that.”

Riarhin ate it.

Cadan mended the old rope where the runnel had frayed it on the rock, splicing the ends with neat, unhurried fingers, and coiled it again beside the new one. Then he sat by the door. Riarhin saw him look at the hook with its hanging things, once, and then not again.

The cord hung on the hook. It turned a little whenever the door moved.

The afternoon went. People came in with work and went out without it. A woman brought a pair of shears to be ground and stayed to talk about her daughter, who was alive and would not write. Saelith listened and ground the shears and said, “They don’t, at that age,” and the woman said, “She’s fifty-two,” and Saelith said, “Well, then.”

Lamps were lit. Riarhin sat on the stool by the door with her hands between her knees, and looked at the bare grip on the bench with the block standing up at its root, and did not look at the hook.

He came in the evening, out of the dark under the overhang, with the people round the fires not turning to look at him.

She heard him first. A quick step, a long stride shortened at the last moment, the step of somebody who moved before he had finished deciding to. He stopped outside the door. She saw his hand come up to the hook and touch the cord on it, a long hand, and turn it over with one finger.

Then he came in.

He was tall. He had to stoop for the lintel and did it without looking, as Linias never could. His hair was pale and long and tied back, and it was wet, as if he had come through rain, though there was no rain under the overhang. He wore a plain coat, brown, belted, with nothing at the belt. As he came through the door his right hand went up to his left shoulder, to the place where a strap would cross, and found nothing there, and came down again. It was so quick and so old a movement that she did not think he knew he had made it.

His face was long and Lethai, and not old. She looked at it. She made herself look. It was a face she did not know. There was nothing in it she could take hold of. The eyes were dark and quick and went everywhere in the room at once, to the forge, to the benches, to Cadan by the door, who had stood, to Dusana at the wall, to the raven, to Riarhin on her stool, and on, without stopping, to Saelith.

“You hung my cord,” he said. He spoke Lethai. His voice was lighter than she had thought it would be. She had not known she had thought anything.

“Someone’s brought your sword,” Saelith said.

He saw it then. It lay on its felt on the bench in the lamplight, bare, the blade clean and the grip naked, with the block new-set at its root. He went to it. He did not touch it at first. He stood over it with his hands at his sides, and then he bent and looked at the block, close, as Saelith had looked, and put out one finger and touched the seat where the glue had dried.

“You’ve re-set it.”

“It was coming loose. Somebody cut the binding.”

“Somebody cut it.” He straightened. He looked at Saelith, and then, for the first time properly, round the room, at the people in it, as a man looks round who has come in expecting one face and found several. “Who brought it?”

“I did,” said Riarhin.

He turned.

She was standing. She did not remember getting up. He looked at her, at her face, her ears, her hair, at the way she stood, and she saw him look, and saw nothing come into his face but curiosity and a kind of courtesy.

“I carried that,” he said, and nodded at the sword, as a man might give his name. “For a long time. Where did you get it?”

She answered in Lethai, so he would not have to change.

“I took it off you.”

The workshop had gone quiet. At the far bench the young man with the lantern had stopped trimming the wick.

He did not say anything for a while. His eyes stayed on her face. Then he said, still courteously, “Where?”

“In the Valewood. Under a bank, where the stream’s cut under it. There’s a tree with its roots come out in an arch. You were in the arch.”

“Above the three stones.”

“I didn’t know about the stones. I came from the other side.”

“Above the three stones,” he said again, quietly, not to her. He looked down at his own hands. “I remember rain.”

Saelith brought a stool and put it behind him, and he sat down on it as though his knees had decided for him.

“You’re Lethai,” he said after a time. “In part. The ears. Thora? Al?”

“Neither. Both.”

He looked at her. It was a different look.

“Who was your mother?”

She had answered that question in many rooms, to many people who wanted something from the answer. She had answered it with lies and with half the truth and with the whole truth when it was convenient. She stood in Saelith’s workshop with her hands at her sides and found that her mouth was dry.

“Her name was Bea.”

He did not move.

“Bea,” he said. “In Silkstrand.”

“Yes.”

“A Rocke. Red hair. A room up a stair with a shutter that wouldn’t close. She sang—” He stopped. His face had changed. It had come open, all at once, as a door comes open in wind. “She sang badly. When she was pleased. She knew she did. She did it anyway.”

“Yes.”

“There was a child,” he said.

Riarhin said nothing.

He looked at her face again, and she watched him look for something in it, and she did not know whether he found it.

“How is she?” he said. “Bea. Is she well? Does she still have the room? I meant to go back. I meant—” He stopped himself. “Is she well?”

“She died,” Riarhin said. “When I was ten.”

He sat on the stool.

He did not cover his face. He sat with his hands on his knees and looked at her, and she watched it go into him and stay there. His right hand moved on his knee, once, as if it meant to get up and go somewhere, up a stair, to a room with a shutter that would not close. Then it lay still.

“Ten,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Who had you? After?”

“Nobody. For a while.” She heard her voice and kept it level. “Then a man came and took me to the Valewood.”

He looked up at that, quickly. She saw a question come into his face, and saw him put it away for later, the way she put things away. She did not help him with it.

Saelith had gone to the brazier. She came back with a cup of the tea she kept stewing there all day, dark and bitter, and put it into his hands without asking.

He took it. He turned it.

He turned it a little, round, in his two hands, with his eyes on the rim, looking for a chip before he would put his mouth to it. His right hand was nearest her in the lamplight. The second finger of it lay along the cup crooked at the middle joint, bent a little sideways, like a stick in water.

Riarhin looked at the finger.

She looked at it for as long as she needed to. She did not look away from it, the way the child under the blanket had looked away. She attended to it, every part of it, the angle of the joint and the thickened knuckle and the way the nail sat slightly askew to the line of the finger, and she kept it.

He drank. He put the cup down on the bench beside the sword.

She could not say any of the things there were to say. She found she was asking about the clasp.

“The mail,” she said. “The collar. The clasp’s made like two leaves.”

He looked up.

“Why leaves?”

It was the wrong question. She knew it as she asked it. She saw him hear it as the wrong question, and saw him decide to answer it anyway, as she would have.

“Because I liked them,” he said. “Because she made me four plain ones and I didn’t want a plain one. I wanted to look down at my own collar in the morning and see something that grew.” He glanced at Saelith. “She told me the stem was too thin.”

“It was too thin,” said Saelith.

“It was too thin.” He almost smiled. He looked back at Riarhin, and his face was eager, all at once, in a way that hurt to look at. “Does it still open badly? In the cold? You have to get your thumbnail under the near leaf and lift, and if your hands are cold—”

“It bent the first winter,” Riarhin said. “I’ve straightened it with a knife. I don’t know how often.”

“With the flat of the knife. Against your knee.”

“Yes.”

“That’s how I did it.” He leaned forward. “Did it serve you? The mail. Did it keep you? Has it kept you well?”

She could not.

She turned away from him to the bench. Ghere’s old binding lay there in its loose coil where Saelith had put it, cut through in two places, and beside it a hank of new cord that Saelith had laid out in the afternoon: dark, waxed, three-strand. Riarhin picked up the new cord. She found its end. She measured a length of it against the grip of the sword, not touching the block, the cord’s end lying along the naked grip from the root to the end and back, and she held it there with her thumb and began, without thinking, to lay the first turn.

Her hand stopped.

She stood with the cord half round the grip and her thumb on it, and behind her he did not say anything, and the lamp on the bench ticked as it burned.

Chapter 14 — The message that arrived

Saelith went to bed at last, in a narrow room of her own at the back by the forge. She did not say goodnight. She banked the forge and put the guard over it and took the lamp from her own bench and left the one on Riarhin’s, and they heard her door shut.

Dusana had gone before that. She had stood up from the wall at some point while Riarhin was standing at the bench with the cord in her hand, and had come past and put a hand on Riarhin’s back between the shoulders, flat, for as long as it takes to draw a breath, and gone through into the store-room with the bird. Cadan had not gone. He sat by the door on the floor where he had sat all day, with his eyes closed. Riarhin knew he was not asleep.

She had put the cord down. It lay on the bench beside the grip with one turn on it, unfinished.

Her father sat on the stool and looked at the chalk.

The two drawings were still on the bench where she had made them, his workshop door and its path and the crossing at the three stones, and her route coming off the end of it with its openings and its narrowing, smudged where Saelith had pushed it into place. He put out his hand and followed her line with one finger, not touching the chalk, a finger’s breadth above it.

“You’ve got all of it,” he said.

“I have what it gave me.”

“That’s all of it. That’s the whole road.” He followed it to the end, to the place past the narrowing where she had stopped drawing because there was nothing more. “That’s where it comes out. The old place.”

“What old place?”

He looked at her. For a moment she saw him weighing what to say and how much, as she had weighed things all her life, and she disliked seeing it in another face.

“There’s a place up in the wood,” he said, “past where anyone goes, where the Lethai kept the account of how we came to be three peoples and not one. What’s left of the account. My mother knew of it. I found the way to it when I was young. I used to go up there and sit.” He took his hand back. “I thought someone ought to be able to find it after me.”

“So you put it in a ball.”

“I put the road into the blue. Yes.”

“How?”

“I walked it holding the thing in my two hands, and attending, all the way. Every turn. And at the end I held it a long time.” He turned his right hand over and looked at the palm. “It keeps what you give it, if you give it everything. And it gives it back to anyone who holds it and attends. To anyone who keeps what they attend to.” He looked up. “My mother’s people do that. Some of my children do it. I thought, if one of them found it, they’d have the road.”

“Found it where?”

“I meant to leave it at the stones. Under the bank by the crossing, where it’s dry. Everybody who comes to Saelith crosses there.” He was quiet for a moment. “I was bringing it down.”

So she told him.

She had not meant to. She found she was telling it because he did not know it, and somebody had to, and there was nobody else alive or dead who could.

She told him the two days of walking out of the Valewood with a coat and a bundle and a binding. She told him she had been angry with the man who taught her, angry enough to taste it, and had not cared where she went provided it was somewhere he could not tell her how to stand. She told him about the rain, and being hungry, and the hilt in the leaves that she had taken for a root. Lying down on her side to reach in under the arch. The leaves wet against her cheek. His hand there in front of her face, the left one, open, and the moment she had thought it would move.

“And beyond it,” she said, “the blue. In the leaves. Where the cloth had come off it.”

He was looking at her and not at the chalk.

“I put my hand on it,” she said. “I didn’t mean to. I was reaching past it for the strap. I touched it and it gave me the road.”

“All of it.”

“All of it. Every turn. And nothing else.” She heard her voice go hard and let it. “Not where it started. Not what was at the end. Not who you were, or that you’d made it, or why. Not that anyone had made it at all. I thought it was something in the wood. I thought the wood had done it to me. I carried it for years and I didn’t know where the first turn was, because the first turn was at a door I’d never seen, across a stream I hadn’t looked at.”

“I was going to tell them,” he said. “Whoever found it. I was going to be there to tell them.”

“You weren’t there.”

“No.”

“You put a road in a ball and left it for whoever was hungry enough to lie down in the leaves.”

“For my kin.”

“For whoever.” She leaned on the bench. “It doesn’t know who’s holding it. You said so. Anyone who attends. It didn’t know it was me. It didn’t know anything.”

“No,” he said. “It didn’t.” And then, after a while, quietly: “It found you, though.”

“I found it.”

He did not argue. He sat with his hands loose on his knees and his eyes on her face, and she saw that he was not going to argue, and that made her angrier than if he had.

“What was it like?” he said.

She looked at him.

“When it gave you the road. I never felt it from that side. I made it. I never had it given me.” He leaned forward a little. “What was it like?”

She did not want to tell him. She found that she was going to.

“Like knowing a stair in the dark,” she said. “Your foot knows where the step is before you put it down. There’s no moment of learning. You just know. And there’s something to be carried with both hands, at the end, and you can feel how far apart your hands ought to be, and you can’t see what’s between them.”

“Yes,” he said, low.

“What’s at the end?”

He began to tell her.

He talked about the old place: a hall cut back into a hill, he said, older than anything the al had built since, with the account cut into its walls in a hand none of the living could read straight through. He said his mother had read some of it, years ago, before. He began on what was at the end of the road.

He talked with his hands. He held them up to show the width, and turned them, and the crooked finger on the right one stood out against the lamplight, bent at the joint, and Riarhin watched it. She watched it turn and go still and turn again. She watched the way the hand closed round the bad finger and favoured it without his knowing.

He stopped.

“You didn’t hear me.”

“I heard you.”

“You weren’t listening.”

“No,” she said. “I was looking at your hand.”

He looked down at his own hand as though it belonged to somebody else. Then he held it out to her, flat, across the bench, palm down, the crooked finger lying along the wood.

“Bea used to do that,” he said. “Look at it. She’d hold it and turn it about. She said it was the only thing about me that wasn’t handsome and she liked to have something to complain of.” He took the hand back. “I’ll say it again.”

He said it again. She listened.

Later, because there was nothing else to do with her hands, she told him where his things were.

“The bow’s in Dhahara. On the Bronze Coast. In a room above a cloth-cutter’s workroom, unstrung, on two pegs over the far end of the cutting table. The mail’s in a chest under the window there, folded in oiled linen.”

“Whose room?”

“A friend’s. Her daughter has it now.”

He did not ask anything more about that, and she was grateful, and angry at being grateful.

“The bow’s been restrung more times than I can tell you,” she said. “The grip’s been rewrapped. I’ve had the nocks seen to twice. The carving was there when I took it.”

“The carving.” He almost laughed. “Saelith told you.”

“She said it wasn’t there when it left her.”

“It wasn’t. I did it.” He rubbed the back of his neck. “The winter before. I’d nothing to do and the wood was asking for it. I never told her. She’d have had the skin off me for putting a knife to a bow she’d strung.” He looked towards the door at the back where Saelith slept. “She knows now.”

“She does.”

“The mail?”

“There’s a place at the right hip where the rings are newer. A wagon went over it. Over me. The mail held and the hip didn’t.” She did not say whose hip it would have been, without it. “And the clasp. You know about the clasp.”

“I know about the clasp.” He was quiet a moment. “And the cloak?”

“I gave it away.”

He looked up.

“In Valora. To a woman who was cold.” Deliane, shaking, in the dark. Riarhin had pulled it off her own shoulders and put it round her and fastened it at the throat. “I didn’t ask for it back.”

“Good,” he said.

She had expected him to mind. He did not seem to. He nodded slowly, as at something sensible.

“I won it,” he said. “I’ll not tell you how. It doesn’t do me any credit.” He looked at the sword on the bench, at the bare grip with the one turn of new cord on it. “And that.”

“That’s yours. The block’s yours, and Saelith’s seat, and her knot under it. The binding’s mine. It’s always been mine. I put it on within a week of finding it. A binding I was given the day I left.” She did not say who had given it. “I’ve renewed it the same way since. It was cut in a fight not long ago, on the left side. That’s why it ticked.”

“Ticked.”

“When I drew it. The block moved.”

“It’ll not tick now,” he said. “Not with her seat.”

She asked him once.

She asked it with her eyes on the chalk, because she could not ask it looking at him.

“What happened? Under the bank.”

He did not answer quickly. When she looked up he was looking at the dark beyond the door.

“I was alone,” he said. “It rained. I was cold. I remember being cold.” He turned his head and met her eyes. “You know the rest better than I do.”

She waited. He did not say any more. She saw that he was not going to, and that it was not a thing he was keeping back so much as a thing he did not have, and she let it go.

The lamp had burned down and she trimmed it. He watched her do it.

“I came to see you once,” he said.

Her hands stopped on the lamp.

“You were small. Bea said you were asleep, and not to wake you, so I didn’t. I sat at the table. She made me tea in the cup with the blue rim and complained I’d let it stand on the wood.” He smiled a little. “I’d brought you fish. Two little fish, carved, out of pear wood, with the scales cut in. A pair, so they’d have each other. I put them on the table for you to find in the morning.”

She remembered the fish.

She remembered them exactly, two of them, no longer than her finger, pale yellow-brown, with the scales cut in tiny overlapping crescents and a hole drilled for the eye, and the smell of them, which was faintly sweet. She remembered finding them on the table by the cup mark in the morning, and Bea saying don’t put them in your mouth. She remembered carrying them about in her apron pocket for most of a winter.

She remembered the day they were gone, and Bea coming back up the stair from the market with a sack of coal on her shoulder and her mouth set, and not saying anything, and lighting the fire.

“Did you like them?” he said.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

He looked pleased. It went across his face and stayed there.

“I thought you might,” he said. “Bea said you’d lose them inside a week.”

“I didn’t lose them.”

“No?”

“No.”

He nodded, satisfied, and looked at the door, and then back at the bench, at the cord lying half round the bare grip of the sword.

“You were going to bind that,” he said.

“In the morning. Saelith says it wants a night.”

“She always says that.”

“Is she right?”

“Always.” He stood up. He had to put a hand on the bench to do it, as Saelith had. “I’ll be by the well. If you want me again before you go.”

He went to the door. Cadan opened his eyes. Her father stooped under the lintel without looking, the way he had come in, and his right hand went up to his left shoulder for the strap that was not there, and came down. Then he was outside, a tall shape going away across the dark under the overhang between the banked fires.

Riarhin stood at the bench. After a while she put her hand flat on the chalk drawing and wiped, slowly, until the door and the path and the three stones were gone, and there was only her own road left, with nothing at the start of it.

Then she drew the door back in.

Chapter 15 — The people he imagined

He was at the well in the morning, as he had said.

He sat on the stone bench with his long legs out in front of him, watching Teodora’s household make breakfast. They had set up the bed-frame against the rock in their corner and hung a blanket from it for a wall, and Teodora was frying something in a pan over a borrowed fire while Pim told her it was burning and Andras sat on the painted chest and ate whatever he was handed. The hen walked about at their feet. Every time Pim said burning Teodora moved the pan an inch farther from him.

“They’ve been at it since first light,” her father said, as Riarhin sat down. “I like them.”

“They lost their house.”

“I know. I heard. A hedge.” He watched Pim reach for the pan and get his hand slapped. “They brought the chest.”

Riarhin had slept, in the end, a few hours towards morning, and woken with her face against the crackling pallet and the raven standing on her hip looking down at her. Dusana had laughed from across the room. Cadan had been at the door already, sitting, with his eyes open.

She had a cup of Saelith’s tea. She turned it before she drank and did not stop herself.

He saw her do it. He did not say anything.

“Did you meet any of the others?” he said. “My children.”

“Some.”

“Which?”

“Zemia. Harissa. Linias. Coryd.”

He said each name after her, softly, as she said it, as if he were checking it against something he had kept.

“Grown,” he said.

“Grown.”

“I saw them small. Most of them. I’d come by in a year, two years, and they’d be a head taller and not know me again, and I’d have to start over.” He looked at his hands. “Linias used to hit his head on the table. Getting up from under it. Every time.”

“He hits it on lintels now.”

Her father laughed, suddenly and much too loudly, so that Teodora looked round from her pan.

“Does he?”

“Every lintel I’ve seen him go under.”

“And Harissa? She danced. She’d dance on anything. A cart, a bench.”

“She teaches it. She hurt her leg. She teaches it anyway.”

“And Zemia remembers where everyone lives.”

“Yes.”

“And Coryd?”

“Coryd mends things. Bridges. Roofs. She writes short letters.” Riarhin drank. “She married a woman with a house in Valora. The stair doesn’t creak.”

“Good,” he said. “Good. That’s good.”

He sat a while with it. She watched him take each one and turn it and put it somewhere, the way she did.

“Both gifts,” he said then. “All of you. Do you know what that is?”

She did not answer.

“The al keep their bodies,” he said. “The thora keep what they attend to. You have both. All of you, in your different measures. In one person. Do you know when that was last seen among the Lethai?”

“Tell me.”

“Before we were three,” he said. “Before we were divided.”

He had not meant it, he said.

He said it plainly and looked at her as he said it, so that she would see he was not offering it as an excuse. He had not gone to Bea, or to any of them, to make anything. He had gone because he loved them. He had loved women, and songs, and the forest, and his mother had despised him for every one of the three. He had gone to Bea because she sang badly and knew it, and because she laughed at his finger, and because he liked to sit in her window with the shutter that would not close and watch the street.

“My mother used to say I wanted women and songs and to be left alone,” he said. “She wasn’t wrong. That was most of what I wanted.”

“And the rest?”

“The rest came later.”

It had come with his children. He had seen the first of them run, a girl who could keep her feet on a wet roof-ridge and recite back to him every word he had said the week before. He had seen another. He had begun to wonder.

“And I remembered what my mother used to talk of,” he said. “When I was a boy. Before.” He did not say before what. “She used to talk at night about the Lethai made whole. The al and the thora and the ar. The greatness before the division, she called it. How we’d been one people once, with everything, and had it cut in three to keep us from reaching too high. She’d talk about it as if it were a house we’d been put out of and might go back to. I grew up inside that. I didn’t think about it. I thought it was the sort of thing mothers say.” He turned his crooked finger with the other hand. “Then I had children, and they had both gifts, and I thought: it’s coming back together. In them. On its own. Without anyone meaning it.”

“And then you meant it.”

He did not look away.

“I hoped,” he said. “I hoped one of you might be formed into someone who could go among all three. The al and the thora and the ar. Who could stand in a forest hall and a scholar’s room and the valley and be at home in each, and be listened to. And bring them back to one table. Slowly. Over a long life. You have long lives.” He nodded at the workshop behind them, where Remorse lay on its felt. “That sword was made for a ruler. The people who made it meant it for one. It’s patient. It rewards patience, and it rewards deciding when the time comes. I’ve always thought it shapes the hand that holds it, a little, over years. I hoped it would find the right hand.”

“And the ball. The blue.”

“So that whoever came after me could find the old place. Where the account is. Any of you. All of you.” He spread his hands. “I didn’t make it for one child. I didn’t know which one. I didn’t know if any. I only wanted the road to be there.”

“Did you meet her?” Riarhin said. “My grandmother. Did she know about it? What you hoped?”

“She knew the talk. It was her talk.” He looked at her. “Did you ever meet her?”

“Yes.”

“Then you know,” he said, and did not ask anything further, and she did not offer it.

“Not all of them wanted it,” he said. “The ar.”

He told her about a woman from the Crimson Valley. He did not name her; he called her the one from the valley, and said it in a way that made Riarhin think he had once said her name often. He had met her in the forest at the edge of the al country, where the valley’s people sometimes came to trade, and they had sat at a fire for three nights while he told her everything he hoped.

“She was peeling a root,” he said. “With a little hooked knife. She didn’t look up the whole time I talked. When I’d done, she said: you’d give us back a name we put down. She said the ar shed the old name on purpose. That they’d chosen to go out from under it and they did not want it handed back to them like a coat somebody had found on a road.” He smiled, crookedly. “She said, if you come to the valley with your one table, I’ll see you out of it myself. And she would have.”

“Did she?”

“I never went.” He was quiet a moment. “She went home. I never saw her again. She was the one who left.”

“And you still think it was worth it.”

“Yes,” he said. “I still think it’s worth trying. I think she was wrong. I think she was frightened of something she’d chosen and didn’t want to look at again. I think if you could see it whole—”

“What if I’d wanted something else?”

He stopped.

She had not raised her voice. She sat with the tea going cold in her hands and looked at him.

“What if I had wanted something else? Any of us. Did you ask? Linias wanted to play. Harissa wanted to dance. Coryd wants to mend a bridge in a town in the rain and go home and have her supper. Zemia wanted somebody to want her to be useful and she got someone, and it wasn’t you.” She heard that one go in and did not take it back. “Did you ever ask any of us?”

“You were children.”

“We weren’t always.”

He looked at her for a long time.

“No,” he said. “I never asked.”

He began, after a while, to tell her how he had pictured it. She could see he could not help it. A long table, he said. A hall, he did not know where. All of them at it, his children, grown, and their children, and people from the three peoples down both sides, and someone at the head of it who could speak to each of them in the way they would hear.

She stopped hearing him.

She was in the workroom in Dhahara, at night, with the cutting tables pushed back and her brother and sisters arranging themselves underneath them to sleep, leaving the aisle clear for the morning’s work. Harissa had put a comb on the chest beside her and told everyone it was not communal. Coryd had offered to sleep by the yard door. We didn’t come to put you on a doorstep. I’ve slept in worse places. So have I. It isn’t a competition. Linias had tuned in the lamplight, with the repaired split near the base of his instrument where somebody had sat on it, and had played Bea’s phrase with its ending going up instead of down. And Harissa, with her chin on her knees, had said that their father must have been a poor judge of music if he preferred the other ending, and Coryd had said there was nothing wrong with the other ending, and they had argued, and the room had become easier.

And later, a long way from there, in a hall: Harissa standing with her weight off her bad leg, watching Riarhin show her a turn. No. It’s the turn you showed me. It’s the turn before you stab somebody. And everyone laughing. And Coryd, another time, on a road in Valora, laughing so loudly that the guard ahead of them turned round.

She came back to the well. He was still talking about the table.

“Harissa says you were a poor judge of music,” Riarhin said.

He stopped mid-word. He looked at her.

“Because of the ending,” she said. “Bea’s song. Linias plays it going up. He says that’s how you sang it. Bea’s went down.”

He opened his mouth, and shut it, and then, quite quietly, under his breath, he sang it.

It was the phrase. She knew it in the first two notes. His voice was light and true and a little rough with disuse, and he sang it through, and at the end it went up, as Linias played it, rising, unfinished, like a question somebody asks on a doorstep.

“That’s how it goes,” he said.

“Bea’s went down.”

“Bea was wrong.”

“Harissa says you were.”

He laughed. He laughed until he had to put his hand over his eyes, and Teodora looked round again from her pan, and Pim said, “What’s funny?”, and nobody told him.

When he had stopped laughing he sat with his hand still over his eyes for a while. Then he took it away and looked at the hen.

Riarhin looked at the hen too. She had been going to ask something. She had felt it coming, all through the talk of the table and the three peoples, and it had come up now, in the quiet, and she knew what it was. There was a man who had come to Silkstrand when she was fourteen and taken her to the Valewood and spent years trying to make a Ghe’hai of her, which was the family trade, he had said. She had not asked about him. She had not asked once.

She did not ask.

Teodora came across from the fire with a bowl. In the bowl was an egg, boiled, still in its shell.

“For the tall one,” she said. “Pim says it’s a sign, so somebody had better eat it.”

“I’m dead,” said Riarhin’s father.

“So am I. Eat it.”

He took the bowl. He looked at the egg as though he had never seen one. Then he took it out and cracked it on the edge of the stone bench and began to peel it with his long fingers, the crooked one held a little away from the shell, carefully, in one piece, the way you peel an egg for a child.

Chapter 16 — The things beside the bed

Saelith gave her the sword in the afternoon.

“It’s had its night,” she said. “And a day. Bind it when you like. Not at my bench; I want the bench.”

Riarhin carried it into the store-room on its felt, like something that had been ill, and laid it down on the floor beside her pallet with the hank of new cord. Then she sat on the pallet and looked at it.

After a while she began to take everything else off.

She unbuckled her belt and laid it down with Toothpick in its sheath and the Last Blade beside it, the short dressed point of the one and the worn grip of the other. She took off her coat and laid it over the end of the pallet. She emptied her pack onto the floor: the spare linen, the whetstone, the comb with the missing teeth, the oilcloth, the tinderbox, Cadan’s old rope which she had not given back, the water bottle, the pouch of dried meat with the wet still in its corners. She laid them out in a row, as she had laid them out on every floor she had ever slept on, so she could see what she had.

Then she looked at the sword again.

It lay on its felt with its bare grip and its new-set block, the length of it running from the pallet nearly to the wall. Narrow, straight, two edges. Neither bright nor dull. She had carried it on her back for longer than some of the people she had loved had been alive. She had cleaned it at a thousand fires. She had put it into men and taken it out and wiped it. She had put it into her grandmother under the left arm.

She had taken it off him in the rain, and she had never once asked whose it was.

She got up and picked it up by the blade, carefully, and put it on the high shelf among the jars of linseed, where she would not see it from the pallet.

Then she sat down again, and the room was wrong.

She could not have said how. The jars were where they had been; the brazier ticked; the door was shut. But there was a place on the floor beside her where a weight had been, and her body knew it was not there, and kept telling her so. When someone dropped something in the workshop beyond the wall, her right hand went up over her shoulder for the grip before she had heard what it was, and closed on air.

She sat with her hand closed on nothing, by her own ear, and was angry.

It was useful. That was the trouble. It had always been useful. It sat in her hand as though it had been cut to the hand, which it had not; it had been cut to his. It turned an edge before she knew there was an edge to turn. It had kept her alive in places where she had not deserved to be kept alive and she had been glad of it every time, and it had been his, and he had hoped it would shape her.

She got up and took it down off the shelf and put it back on the floor beside the pallet.

The room came right. She disliked that more than anything else that had happened that day.

Dusana came in towards evening with the bird on her shoulder and a cloth bundle in one hand.

“Teodora sends fried bread,” she said. “Pim sends his opinion of the fried bread. I’ve left that outside.”

Riarhin was sitting on the pallet with her back against the wall and her knees up. She did not look round.

“Not now,” she said.

Dusana stood in the doorway a moment. Then she put the bundle down on the end of the pallet, on top of Riarhin’s coat, and went out.

“All right,” she said, from the other side of the door.

Riarhin heard her go away through the workshop. She heard Saelith say something and Dusana answer, and the outer door, and then nothing but the workshop’s evening noises.

She sat. The fried bread smelt of lard and onions through its cloth. The brazier needed feeding and she did not feed it. She looked at the things on the floor in their row, and at the sword, and at the hank of cord, and none of it would be anything but what it was.

After a while she got up and opened the door.

Dusana was sitting on Saelith’s stool by the outer door, beside Cadan, with her shawl round her and her feet on the rung. The raven was on the floor between them eating something it had found. Cadan’s eyes were shut.

Riarhin stood in the store-room doorway.

“Dusana.”

Dusana looked up.

“Come back,” Riarhin said.

Dusana got up, and picked up the raven, which objected, and came.

They ate the fried bread sitting side by side on the pallet with their backs to the wall. Dusana fed the brazier without being asked. The raven had a corner of the bread and took it to the top of the shelf among the linseed jars and dropped crumbs on them.

Dusana did not ask anything. She talked a little, about Teodora’s corner and the bed-frame and the hen, about Pim, who had decided he was going to build his mother a house against the rock out of whatever came down the ravine, and had begun by collecting a door.

“Only a door?”

“He says you start with the door and the house comes to it.”

“That’s backwards.”

“That’s what Teodora said. He said she’d brought a chest down a ravine and could keep her opinions about backwards.”

Riarhin laughed. It surprised her. It came out of her short and rough and she did not stop it, and Dusana looked sideways at her and smiled, and the silver tooth showed in the brazier light.

They sat. The bread was gone. The brazier settled.

“I took his boots off,” Riarhin said. “Tried to. They were too big. I left them on him.”

Dusana did not say anything.

“I took everything else.”

“You were hungry,” Dusana said.

“I was hungry.”

“Then you took what would keep you.” Dusana pulled her feet up onto the pallet and tucked them under her skirt. “I’ve taken things off the dead. Everyone on the road has. A coat. A pair of mittens off a woman in the snow who’d not be wanting them. You say thank you and you take them.” She was quiet a moment. “You didn’t know whose they were. They knew. That’s all the difference.”

Riarhin looked at the sword on the floor.

“He wanted me to be something.”

“Most fathers do.”

“Not this.”

“No,” said Dusana. “Not this. That’s a big one.” She leaned her head back against the wall. “My father wanted me to marry a man with eleven goats. I thought that was a great deal to want of me at the time.”

“Did you?”

“Marry him? No. He married my cousin. They had a great many more goats after.” She turned her head. “You don’t have to be what he wanted. You know that. You’ve not been anything anyone wanted for a long time, by the look of you.”

“That’s not a comfort.”

“It wasn’t meant as one,” said Dusana. “It was meant as true.”

The brazier ticked. On the shelf the raven had stopped dropping crumbs and was looking down at them with one eye.

“What happened to the green wagon?” Riarhin said. “With the red flowers on the door.”

Dusana did not answer at once. She was looking at the brazier.

“It burned,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“I’ll not tell that one.” Dusana’s voice was quite even. “Not that one. Ask me another.”

“All right,” Riarhin said.

Dusana turned her head and looked at her, and then the laugh came after all, short, under her breath.

“That’s mine,” she said. “You’ve taken that off me.”

“You can have it back.”

“Keep it. It suits you better than I thought.”

Her shoulder was against Riarhin’s. Riarhin could feel the warmth of it through the shawl and her own shirt. She could feel Dusana breathing. She knew the shape of her, still, from the wagon: the weight of an arm across her ribs in the night, the smell of smoke and clean wool. It had been long ago and she had kept it all, as she kept everything she had attended to, and she had attended to all of it.

She did not move closer. She did not move away.

After a while Dusana unlaced her boots and pulled them off and put her stockinged feet, which were cold, against the side of Riarhin’s calf.

“Your feet are cold.”

“They’re always cold. It’s a great trial to me.” Dusana did not take them away. “Lie down. You’ve not slept. I’ll stop here till you do, if you want. Or I’ll go and sit with the monk. He’s poor company but he doesn’t snore.”

“Stop here.”

They lay down on the pallet in their clothes, under Riarhin’s coat and Dusana’s shawl, with the sword on the floor beside them on its felt. Dusana lay on her back. Riarhin lay on her side facing the wall, and after a little while Dusana turned over and put an arm across her, over her ribs, the way it had been in the wagon, and left it there.

That was all. Riarhin lay with the arm across her and listened to the brazier and to the raven on the shelf shifting its feet among the jars, and to Dusana’s breathing go slow, and after a long time, she slept.

She woke before Dusana. The arm had gone in the night. Dusana was on her back again with her mouth open, and the raven was asleep on her stomach.

Riarhin got up without waking either of them and went out into the workshop, and out under the overhang to the well.

He was not there.

Teodora was there, at her fire, with the pan. She looked up.

“The tall one went off up the stream at first light,” she said. “He said to tell you he’d be about. He said you’d know how to ask.”

“Thank you.”

“He ate the egg,” Teodora said, with satisfaction. “All of it.”

Riarhin went back into the workshop. Saelith was at her bench already with something small in her tongs.

“He’s gone up the stream,” Riarhin said.

“He’ll do that. He’ll be back if the cord’s hung.”

“Will you keep it hung?”

Saelith looked at her.

“Not now,” Riarhin said. “I don’t want him back now. We’re going on today. But I’ll come back this way. After. When we’ve done what we came to do, if we do it.” She heard how it sounded and went on anyway. “If he wants to talk then, he can. Tell him that. Tell him after. Not before.”

“Is that all?”

“That’s all.”

Saelith nodded. She put the tongs down and went to the door and looked at the hook, where the short length of old dark cord still hung among the red wool and the horseshoe nail and the scrap of blue cloth. She did not take it down.

“After,” she said. “I’ll tell him.” She came back to her bench. “He’ll not like waiting.”

“No.”

“Good for him,” said Saelith, and picked up her tongs.

Riarhin bound the grip at the end of Saelith’s bench, the clear end, which Saelith let her have without a word.

She measured the cord against the grip as she had begun to do two nights before, from the root to the end and back, and cut it long. She laid the end along the grip under her thumb, below the block, where Saelith’s old knot lay finished in the shadow, and laid the first turn over it, and drew it down. Then the second, half over the first, and back, the over-and-back that Ghere had taught her in a cold room in the Valewood with his own sword across his knees, the pattern she had put on this grip within a week of finding it and had renewed in the same pattern ever since. She drew each turn down hard. The cord was new and stiff and took the wax and squeaked a little under her fingers.

Saelith did not watch her. Once, passing with a bucket of quench water, she glanced at the grip and went on.

At the end Riarhin cut the cord. She cut it on a slant, the last turn angled, as Ghere had cut the binding he had left under the cloth by her door, and tucked it.

She held the sword up. She closed her left hand on the grip and it was hers again: cut to her hand, the way she had always wrapped it, over his cord and his block and Saelith’s knot.

She sheathed it, and drew it a hand’s length, and sheathed it again.

There was no tick. The block sat in its seat and did not move.

“It’ll hold,” Saelith said from her bench, without looking up.

“What do I owe you?”

“A drawing.” Saelith turned something over in the tongs. “Of the carving on that bow. When you see it next. I want to see what he did to it.”

“I’ll bring it.”

“After,” said Saelith.

They left in the middle of the morning.

Dusana had been out early to the carter who came down the ravine on Pell’s rounds, and had sent word up again to Altan’s people that she had gone farther down. She came back and said so, and then said, before Riarhin could ask: “I’m coming as far as the river. There’s a ferry. I know the ferryman, after a fashion. He’ll want someone to make faces at him.”

“And Toghrul?”

“Toghrul’s hearing isn’t till Altan sends. When Altan sends I’ll go. That hasn’t changed.” She hitched the strap of her bag. “Down’s where I am now. Altan can send down.”

Cadan had the two ropes over his shoulder, coiled one on the other without crossing. He had given the salt pouch back to Riarhin that morning, dry, and she had put it in the top of her pack.

Teodora’s household came to see them go. Pim had his door. It was leaning against the rock beside the bed-frame, a plain board door with a latch and no house, and he stood by it with his hand on the latch as though it might go somewhere if he let go. Andras sat on the painted chest. The hen walked about.

“Go carefully,” said Teodora. “Mind the stones by the water. They roll.”

“We will.”

“You won’t,” said Teodora, “but I’ve said it.”

Saelith did not come out. Through the workshop door Riarhin could hear her hammer, light and steady, on something that only needed persuading. The cord hung on the hook beside the doorpost. It turned a little as they went past, in the draught they made, and settled.

They went down the ravine floor beside the stream, the way Imre had gone and then past it, where the overhang ended and the rock walls opened out and the mist lay along the water. The stones by the water rolled. Riarhin’s thigh complained of the cold. The raven rode on Dusana’s shoulder with its feathers out and its eyes half shut, being carried somewhere it had not chosen.

Remorse lay along her back in its scabbard, where it had lain for most of her life. It made no sound at all.

Part III — Other people’s dead

Chapter 17 — The ferryman’s passengers

The stream went into the river without any fuss, the way a child goes into a crowd.

The ravine walls stood back on either hand, the stream ran out between two flat stones and was gone, and beyond the stones lay water so black and still that she could not tell where the bank ended. Mist lay along it in a long low bolster. Somewhere out on it a lamp moved, very slowly, from left to right.

“That’s him,” Dusana said.

There was a landing. It was a jetty of grey planks with a bell on a post at its end. Halfway along, a rounded stone the size of a loaf had been set into the timber, polished on top. Somebody had left a copper coin on it.

Dusana walked past the stone without looking at it. The raven, on her shoulder, looked at it the whole way.

Riarhin stopped. She put out a hand and laid two fingers on the stone beside the coin. It was warm. Not sun-warm; there was no sun. Warm the way a step is warm where somebody has been sitting.

“Leave it,” Dusana said, from the end of the jetty.

“Whose is it?”

“Whoever put it there.” Dusana had her hand on the bell rope. “Some people leave one. Some don’t. Ring or don’t ring, Cadan?”

Cadan looked at the water for a while.

“Ring,” he said.

Dusana rang it twice. The sound went out over the water and did not come back. The lamp out on the river stopped moving left to right and began to grow.

Riarhin looked down at the stone again. The coin was gone.

She looked at Cadan. He had seen it too. He said nothing, and after a moment neither did she.

The ferry was a broad flat boat, low in the water, with a single oar over the stern. It came in out of the mist stern first, which Riarhin did not understand until it had touched the piles and she saw that the man at the oar had been facing them all the way.

He was big through the chest and the neck, in an oilskin coat that came to his shins and shone where the lamp caught it, with a cap pulled down over a broad, patient face. He looked at each of them in turn the way a carter looks at a load.

“Dusana,” he said.

“You’ve not changed.”

“No.”

“I said you’d want someone to make faces at you,” Dusana told Riarhin, and made one. The ferryman watched her do it without any change of expression at all, and then held out his hand for her bag.

The boat was full of luggage: bundles in cloth, baskets, a chair with its legs in the air, a long box like a coffin that turned out to be full of rakes, a sack that rustled as though it were full of paper. In the bow, on top of everything, sat a saddle.

Among the luggage, on a plank seat, sat a woman.

She was perhaps forty, with a brown shawl crossed over her chest and tied behind, the way women tie a shawl when they mean to work in it. On her lap she had a pair of men’s boots tied together by their laces. Somebody had resoled them not long ago; the new leather was paler than the uppers.

“Sit where you like,” the ferryman said. “Don’t sit on the chair.”

They sat. Riarhin found a place on a bundle that gave a little and smelt of apples. Cadan stood until the ferryman pointed at the plank beside the woman, and then sat, with the ropes on his knees. Dusana went to the bow and sat beside the saddle and put her feet up on the rakes, and the raven got down off her shoulder and walked along the saddle’s pommel and looked at the river.

The ferryman pushed off.

He sculled, and the boat went out into the mist with no sound but the drip from the blade. The landing went grey and then went.

“This is the way to the Reed Landing?”

“It’s the way to all of them.”

“He was at the Reed Landing. They said.”

“They said a man in a brown coat,” the ferryman said. “There’s a great many brown coats.”

The woman turned the boots over in her lap. Riarhin saw that the left boot had a knot of red wool tied through the top eyelet.

“Will you take me to him?”

“No.”

“You take people.”

“I take people across,” the ferryman said. “I take luggage where it’s going. I take words where they’re going. I don’t take a person to a person.” He sculled. “That’s not a crossing. That’s a delivery.”

“I’m not asking you to deliver me. I’m asking you to take me where he is.”

“Same thing.”

“It isn’t.”

“It is to him,” said the ferryman.

The woman did not seem surprised. She seemed like someone who had asked again because there was nothing else on the boat to do.

“Hedda,” said Dusana from the bow, not unkindly. “You asked him last time.”

“You know her?” Riarhin said.

“I know her from the boat. She’s on it most times I am.” Dusana settled her feet among the rakes. “She’s looking for her brother.”

“Arvo,” said Hedda.

“And he’s not looking for her.”

Hedda did not deny it. She smoothed the red wool on the boot.

“He went out on a wet morning in his old boots,” she said, to nobody in particular. “The good ones were upstairs, and he’d fallen out with our father, and he didn’t want to go past the old man’s room. That was all it was.” She looked at the ferryman’s back. “I only want to give him the boots.”

“I’ll take a word,” the ferryman said.

“A word.”

“A message. If you’ve one. He can have it or not have it. That’s his.”

Hedda looked down at the boots for a long time. The boat went on through the mist. Cadan, beside her, had folded his hands on the ropes and was looking at the water with his head a little on one side, so that he was very nearly not there at all.

“Tell him I’ve got his boots,” Hedda said. “Tell him they’re mended. Tell him Father’s not here.”

“That’s three things.”

“Then tell him the boots.”

The ferryman nodded once. He did not write it down. He did not seem to put it anywhere. Riarhin, watching his face, could not see it go in.

She took the folded description from inside her coat. The crease had begun to split beside the words about Davin’s shoulder.

“There’s someone I’m looking for too. A carrier. He went through a stair in the Mistlands.”

“Alive?”

“When he went.”

She read the description to him: the coat, the scar on the chin, the shoulder worn lower by the carrying frame. She left the smaller fold shut.

The ferryman waited until she had finished.

“I haven’t carried him.”

“Would you remember?”

“A living man with a frame? Yes.” He lifted the oar clear of something she could not see beneath the water. “I don’t go everywhere.”

“If you do meet him, his brother’s waiting. Will you tell him that?”

“Yes.”

She folded the paper along a fresh crease, away from the tear, and put it back. Hedda had drawn her feet beneath the seat to keep the spray off the boots.

The mist thinned enough to show a second landing on their left: a stone step coming down into the water between alders, with a path going up from it into trees. The ferryman lifted the oar and let the boat drift.

“Stream Landing,” he said. “Anybody?”

Nobody moved.

He looked at Riarhin. He looked at her for a while, and then at the sword, and she felt the thing she had felt at the lower shelter when Saelith first turned the grip in her long hands. She sat still on the bundle that smelt of apples.

“There’s a man walks down to this step,” the ferryman said. “Tall. Forest-made. He’s asked me to carry a word if I saw a woman with that sword. He came yesterday, or what passes. I’ve not taken it yet.” He paused. “I can put you off here. He walks the stream most days. Or I can give you the word. Or I can keep it.”

Riarhin looked at the step. There was moss on it, and a fallen alder leaf, and the path went up between the trees into a light that was greener than the river light. She knew, without wanting to know, that if she went up that path she would find a stream, and three stones, and a bank with roots in it.

Dusana did not say anything. Cadan did not say anything. The raven turned its head to look at her with one eye and then the other.

“Keep it,” Riarhin said.

“Keep it till when?”

“Till after.”

The ferryman considered this. Then he nodded and put the oar back in the water.

“He’ll not like it,” he said.

“So I’m told.”

The step went into the mist behind them. Riarhin found that her right hand had gone up to her left shoulder, as though for a strap, and she brought it down and put it in her lap.

“Grass Landing next,” the ferryman said. “Then Reed. Then the long reach and the grey rooms, and then I don’t go.”

“What’s past the grey rooms?” Dusana asked.

“Not river.”

“Reed,” said Dusana. “There’s a fire at the Reed Landing. Sarnai keeps it. I’ve sat at it. She does a thing with millet that I’d walk a day for.” She glanced at Riarhin. “And it’s your way, if your stone was pointing the way you said.”

It was. Saelith’s stone had turned in Riarhin’s palm and pointed into the rock behind the forge, downstream and down, and the river was carrying them along that line.

“Reed,” Riarhin said.

“Reed,” said the ferryman, as though it had been settled long before.

Cadan had been looking at the river behind them.

“The river goes down,” he said.

“It’s a river.”

“When we wish to go back up, towards the first station,” he said to the ferryman, with great politeness. “Do you take people upstream?”

“No.”

“Never?”

“It’s a river,” the ferryman said again, with no more emphasis than before. “Things go down it. If you want up, there’s the towpath.” He sculled. “There’s a stair comes up to the towpath, under the grey rooms on the near side. People use it. It goes up along the bank as far as the doors with the lamps, where the old man is.”

“Ambrus,” said Dusana.

“That’s him.”

“The towpath stair,” said Cadan. “Under the grey rooms, on the near side. It goes up along the bank to the doors with the lamps.” He said it back exactly, at the same pace, and then he looked at Riarhin. “I would like to mark it, when we pass it.”

“When we pass it.”

“Yes.”

Riarhin looked at the red cord coiled at his belt.

“Mark it,” she said.

She saw the saddle properly when the raven tried to eat it.

It took hold of something at the saddle’s side and pulled. Dusana said “Bird,” and it let go and looked innocent.

It was a braided horsehair cord, black and grey, knotted to the near side and finished in a tassel. Under it was a grey felt saddle-cloth with a border stitched in red. The saddle was a wooden frame with a high front, bound in rawhide, and the stirrup leathers had been knotted, not buckled, at the length somebody wanted them, with a knot she had seen tied in a ship’s forepeak by a man sitting on the floor with his leg out in front of him.

She had watched him tie it a hundred times. Under the thumb, round, back through. So that it does not walk.

“Where’s that going?” she said.

“Grass Landing,” said the ferryman. “The riders.”

“Whose riders?”

He looked at her as though she had asked whose river it was.

“The ones on the grass.”

“Is there a man among them,” Riarhin said, “called Na’ar?” She heard her voice and steadied it. “Big. Bigger than you. Ykrul. A scar down the outside of his forearm. A coat that won’t close.” She stopped, because there was too much, and then said the thing that mattered. “He cooks.”

The ferryman sculled. He sculled for long enough that she thought he was not going to answer.

“I don’t know names,” he said. “I know luggage.”

“Will you ask?”

“I’ll ask.” He looked at her under the cap. “Asking’s not fetching. I’ll put the saddle off at the Grass Landing and I’ll say, there’s a woman with a sword at Sarnai’s fire asking for a big man who cooks. That’s all I’ll say. If he’s there, he’ll hear it. If he wants to come, he’ll come. If he doesn’t, he won’t, and you’ll not hear from me about it either way.”

“That’s enough.”

“It’s what there is,” said the ferryman.

Dusana was watching her from the bow, over the saddle, with an expression Riarhin could not read. The raven had gone back to the tassel. Dusana put one finger on its beak, lightly, and it let go again.

The Grass Landing was a low bank with no jetty at all, only a post driven into the mud and a rope tied to it, and beyond the bank, under the edge of the mist, the beginning of a great deal of grass. Riarhin smelt it before she saw it. It was the smell of a summer evening on the steppe after a day of heat: dust and crushed stems and horse. Something in her chest turned over at it, and she held still and let it.

The ferryman tied up. He lifted the saddle out of the bow with the cloth and the cord and the tassel the raven had been worrying, and carried it up the bank and laid it by the post, carefully, the way a man lays down a saddle that belongs to somebody else.

He stood a moment by the post, looking out at the grass. He said something. Riarhin could not hear it, though she heard everything. He might only have been speaking to the grass.

Then he came back down and untied and pushed off, and the bank went.

The Reed Landing smelt of smoke.

It was a shelving beach of grey sand between reeds taller than a man. At the top of the beach, where the sand gave way to grass, there was a fire in a ring of stones, and a wagon drawn up beside it with its shafts down, and a woman sitting on the wagon’s step with a pot between her feet.

The wagon had painted wheels. Each one was a different colour. The near front was yellow.

The ferryman ran the boat up onto the sand.

Dusana was out before it had stopped, with the raven on her shoulder flapping for balance, and was walking up the beach with her arms out. The woman on the step put the pot down and stood up. She was older than Dusana and thin, with grey plaits pinned over her head, and she said something in Tulkani about people who arrived at mealtimes, and then they had their arms round each other and were rocking a little.

Riarhin got out of the boat. Her thigh had stiffened on the bundle.

Cadan lifted out Dusana’s bag, which Dusana had forgotten, and Riarhin’s pack.

Hedda got out too.

“Not here,” the ferryman said.

“Here,” said Hedda. She had the boots in her arms. She was looking along the beach to their left, where it curved away under the reeds towards a line of low dark shapes in the mist that might have been buildings. “There was a man. On the sand. Going that way.”

Riarhin looked. There was nothing on the sand but sand. She looked at the reeds, and the line of dark shapes, and saw nothing that moved.

“In a brown coat,” said Hedda.

“Hedda,” said the ferryman.

“You said you’d take the word.”

“I will.”

“Then take it,” said Hedda, and went.

She went quickly along the water’s edge with the boots against her chest, and the mist came down along the beach and took her a little at a time.

Nobody went after her. Riarhin found that she had taken two steps along the sand and that Dusana, halfway up the beach, had turned and was looking at her.

“She does that,” Dusana said. “She’ll come back to the landing when she’s done looking. She always has.”

“Always?”

“So far.”

Riarhin looked at the place where the mist had closed.

“Where does that go? That way.”

The ferryman answered. He had his hand on the oar.

“Grey rooms,” he said. “The long reach. Where I don’t go.” He looked at Cadan. “Your towpath stair’s that way too. Under them. On the near side.”

He pushed off. The boat slid back off the sand, and turned, stern first, so that he could see the landing all the way out, and the lamp went into the mist and was a moving point, and then was nothing.

Sarnai’s millet had raisins in it, and something sharp, and butter that had been browned in the pan first. Riarhin ate two bowls. She sat on an upturned bucket by the fire with her pack at her back and Remorse across her knees, and listened to Dusana and Sarnai talking in Tulkani about people she did not know, a wedding that had gone wrong, a wheel that had come off on a bridge and taken a goat with it.

“Was the goat all right?” she said, in Tulkani.

Sarnai looked at her for the first time with interest.

“The goat was perfectly well,” she said. “The wheel was ruined. Nobody has forgiven the goat.”

Cadan sat apart on the wagon’s step, watching the beach where Hedda had gone.

There was no evening here, only the mist and the fire. At some point Sarnai began to talk about beds, and the raven, asleep on the wagon’s roof, put its head up out of its feathers and looked inland.

Riarhin heard it a moment after the bird did. Hooves. Two horses, unshod, coming at a walk through grass.

She stood up. She did not decide to.

They came out of the mist at the top of the grass where it met the sand: two horses, small and rough-coated, a dun and a bay, and on the dun a boy of perhaps fourteen with a rider’s coat belted over a shirt and his hair in two braids. He was leading the bay by a rope. The bay had a felt saddle-cloth under its saddle, grey, with a border stitched in red.

The boy stopped at the edge of the firelight and looked at the four of them, and at the bird, and at the sword in Riarhin’s hand. Then he looked at Riarhin.

“You’re the one with the sword who asked,” he said, in Ykrul.

“Yes.”

He considered her. He had a rider’s way of sitting, easy and indifferent, and he did not get down.

“He says,” the boy said, and frowned, getting it right, “come and eat. He says the story is not finished and he is not stopping it for you. He says bring your own bowl.”

Riarhin stood on the sand. She heard Dusana say something quietly to Sarnai, and Sarnai laugh. She heard the reeds, and the horses breathing, and the fire.

She bent and took her bowl from beside the bucket, the wooden one Sarnai had given her the millet in, and wiped it on her sleeve.

“May I borrow this?” she asked Sarnai.

“Bring it back,” said Sarnai.

Chapter 18 — The bowl in his hands

Dusana did not come.

“He didn’t ask for me,” she said. “And Sarnai’s only got the one pot, and if I go, she’ll give my share to the monk.” She was sitting on the wagon step with her feet in Sarnai’s lap, which Sarnai was allowing. “Go on. If he’s your big man who cooks, you’ll want him to yourself for the first bowl. Everybody does.”

Cadan stood by the fire with his lantern.

“I will stay at the landing,” he said, “in case the woman with the boots comes back along the sand. Is that what you would like?”

“Yes.”

“Then I will stay.”

The boy watched all this from the dun with the patience of someone who had been sent with a message and was not responsible for what people did about it. When Riarhin came to the bay he did not offer to hold its head. She put her foot in the knotted stirrup and swung up, and the bay shifted under her and then stood, and her thigh, which had been complaining since the boat, settled into the shape of a horse as though it had been waiting.

She had not been on a horse like this, small and rough and indifferent, with a saddle that held her like a cupped hand, for longer than she wanted to reckon.

“Can you ride?” said the boy.

“Yes.”

He looked at her seat, and at the sword on her back, and seemed to accept it.

“It’s not far,” he said. “It’s never far. He just likes them to walk.”

The mist ended at the top of the beach as though someone had drawn a line.

Beyond it the grass went out under a sky. Riarhin had forgotten there could be sky here.
