It was a high pale evening sky with the sun low in it, off to her left, and long clouds lit underneath in copper, and under the sky there was nothing but grass, rolling, silver-green where the wind turned it and dun where it lay still, all the way to the edge of the world. The wind came across it warm and dry and smelling of dust and crushed stems. A lark went up somewhere and kept going up.

She pulled the bay up without meaning to.

The boy looked back.

“Yes,” he said, as though she had said something. “It’s like that.”

They rode. The horses walked through grass to their bellies and the seed-heads whispered against Riarhin’s boots. After a while there was smoke on the wind, and then the sound of horses, a great many, and then the camp came up out of a fold in the ground: round felt tents, grey and white, with their doors to the south, and a herd beyond them grazing loose in the evening light, and fires, and people at the fires, and dogs, and children running between the tent ropes with a bladder for a ball.

Nobody stopped what they were doing to look at her. A woman mending a bridle glanced up, saw the boy, and went back to the bridle. She was somebody’s guest.

The boy took her to the largest fire.

There were perhaps twenty people round it, sitting on felts and saddles and on the ground. There was a pot on the fire, a great black pot with a wide flat base, set right down in the embers, and steam coming off it that smelt of mutton and onions and something green. And beside the pot, on a saddle, with his back to her, sat a big man in a coat that did not meet across his chest, talking.

She knew the back. She knew the set of the shoulders and the way the head was carried a little forward, and the hand that came out sideways to make a point and went back to the knee. She knew the voice before she could hear the words: slow and deep, every word put down where it would stay.

She got down off the bay. Her legs were not entirely reliable.

The boy took the horse. He said, quite loudly, “She’s here,” and the big man held up one hand, palm out, without turning round, and kept talking.

“—so the saddle is on the ground. In the morning. And there is a piece missing. The whole back of it, the cantle, gone. And there are tooth marks. And the horse is standing there.” He made the hand into a horse standing there. “Looking at the hills. As if he has never in his life seen a saddle.”

Someone laughed. The big man waited for it to finish.

“So my uncle,” he said, “goes to the horse.”

Riarhin sat down at the edge of the firelight, on the grass, with Sarnai’s bowl in her lap.

She had heard it before, in a ship’s forepeak, with a sailor laughing too hard to swear. It was longer now. He had added a second uncle and a dispute about whose fault it was, and a dog, and a moment where the uncle tried to make the horse give the saddle back by leaving it out of the evening’s oats. The riders had heard it before too. She could tell by the way they laughed at the places before the funny parts, ready for them. They laughed anyway.

A woman leaned across and took the bowl out of Riarhin’s lap. She went to the pot and filled it and brought it back, and Riarhin took it in both hands.

The woman sat down again across the fire and picked up her own bowl. She held it with both hands round it, cupped, the thumbs hooked over the rim, and lifted it and bent her head to it with her eyes looking over the rim at the man who was talking.

Riarhin looked round the fire.

They all held them like that: the man on her left with the scar through his eyebrow, the boy squatting by the pot, a girl of eight or nine with her braids tied together at the back, an old man with no teeth. Both hands. Thumbs over the rim. Eyes over the bowl at whoever was talking, as if the talk were the more important thing.

She looked at her own hands. She had taken it in both hands, without thinking, and her thumbs were over the rim.

It was how you held a bowl.

“—and the horse,” said the big man, “eats the oats.”

The riders roared. Riarhin laughed. She laughed too loudly, too suddenly, with a mouthful of mutton, and had to put her hand over her mouth, and the man with the scarred eyebrow looked at her sideways and grinned. She could not stop. She bent over the bowl with her shoulders shaking and the broth slopping, and did not know whether it was the oats or the bowls or the sky.

When she could see again the big man had turned round on his saddle and was looking at her.

He had the same face. Broad and heavy and brown, with the small eyes that looked as if they were not paying attention and missed nothing. A little grey in the stubble she did not remember. The hatchet scar white along the outside of the forearm, where the sleeve had ridden up.

“It is not finished,” Na’ar said.

“No.”

“There is the part with the second uncle’s wife.”

“I remember.”

“You do not remember. I have put it in since.” He looked at her for a moment longer. Then he put his hand flat on the felt beside his saddle and shoved the man sitting there along it, a full arm’s length, and the man went along with his bowl held level and did not spill a drop and did not appear to mind.

Riarhin got up and came round the fire and sat in the space.

He was warm. She had forgotten, or not forgotten, that he gave off heat like a stove. His shoulder was a hand’s breadth from hers. He smelt of smoke and horse and onions.

“Eat,” he said. “It is getting cold.”

She ate. He turned back to the fire and told them about the second uncle’s wife.

When it was done, and the riders had stopped laughing and begun to talk among themselves, and the girl with the tied braids had been sent to bed and had not gone, Na’ar reached across and took Riarhin’s bowl out of her hands and looked into it.

“You have left the onion.”

“I was listening.”

“You were laughing.” He gave it back to her. “Too loud.”

“Yes.”

“Good.” He took up a ladle with a long handle and stirred the pot, slowly, and lifted some and looked at it and let it fall back. “The pot,” he said. “You see the pot.”

“I see it.”

“Mairi made one like this. In Ubral. You remember Mairi.”

“I remember.”

“This is better.” He tapped the side of it with the ladle. It rang dully. “Wider. Look at the base. You can put it straight in the fire, it doesn’t tip, the heat comes up through all of it. Mairi’s was good. This is better.” He considered. “I would not say that to Mairi.”

“No.”

“She would hit me with it.”

He said it with perfect seriousness and stirred the pot. Riarhin looked at the side of his face in the firelight and felt something come up through her that she had to sit very still to hold.

“Who made this one?” she said.

“Nobody. It was here.” He tapped it again. “It was here when I came. On my fire. I am not asking questions about that.”

The sun did not set. It went down to the edge of the grass and stayed there, a long red-gold light lying across everything, making every tent rope a line of fire and every horse in the herd a dark shape with a bright edge. The wind dropped. Somewhere a man was singing, not well, and someone else told him so.

“Who is the horse?” Riarhin said.

Na’ar looked at her.

“There’s a horse,” she said. “Isn’t there. There’s always a horse.”

He looked into the pot for a while.

“There is a woman called Tash,” he said. “Over there. With the grey.” He did not point. Riarhin looked where he did not point, and saw, at the edge of the firelight two fires over, a woman with a grey horse on a halter, combing its mane with her fingers. It was a good horse. Even from here she could see it: deep through the girth, short in the back, a big kind eye.

“She says,” said Na’ar, “it will bear anyone.”

“Anyone.”

“Anyone. She says it at every fire. Anyone who sits on it, it will carry, all day, at any pace, and never complain.” He laid the ladle across the pot. “So I sat on it.”

“And?”

“It lay down.”

Riarhin put her hand over her mouth.

“It did not fall,” Na’ar said. “It did not stumble. It looked round at me, and it thought, and it lay down. Carefully. In the grass. With me on it.” He looked at the fire. “Tash says it was the saddle. She says it was a bad saddle, the tree was wrong, it pinched. I said it was a good saddle. She said, then it is you.” He was silent a moment. “So I have sent for another saddle.”

“The ferryman brought it.”

“You saw it?”

“I came with it. On the boat.” She did not say that a raven had tried to eat it. “It’s a good saddle. The tree’s broad. Somebody knotted the stirrup leathers properly.”

“Everybody knots them properly,” said Na’ar. “It is how you knot them.”

She looked at him. He looked back, mildly, as if he had said nothing at all.

“Tomorrow,” he said, “I sit on it again.”

“With the new saddle.”

“With the new saddle. And Tash will see.” He picked up his own bowl from beside his foot, and held it in both hands, thumbs over the rim, and drank, looking at her over it. “It will bear anyone. She says. I am anyone.”

She told him about Temür because she could not think how else to begin.

She had not meant to. She had meant to say something else, she did not know what, and the riders round the fire had gone quiet in the long red light, and Na’ar was holding his bowl the way he held it, and it came out.

“I met a boy in the north,” she said. “Ykrul. From the east. A rider.”

“How far north?”

“The Linn coast. Past it. On the ice.”

Na’ar’s eyebrows went up a little.

“His horse died on the ice,” Riarhin said. “He came the rest on foot. They brought him into a hall full of men who didn’t trust him, and somebody asked him something he didn’t want to answer, and he touched the tooth at his throat. Twice.” She touched her own throat, twice, with two fingers. “And I told them what it meant.”

Na’ar looked at her throat. He did not say anything.

“I told them it meant he’d been asked something that wasn’t his to tell. That it was a courtesy.” She looked at the fire. “He said I’d been taught wrong. He said it meant, I have heard you and I am thinking whether you are worth an answer. I am still thinking.”

The deep laugh started somewhere low in Na’ar’s chest and did not come out. She watched it not come out. His shoulders moved.

“He said that.”

“In front of the whole hall. In Ykrul. Then he said please.”

“Please.”

“Don’t answer for me. Please.”

The laugh came out. It came out of him the way it always had, from the belly, enormous, so that the riders across the fire looked up and the girl with the tied braids, who was still not in bed, laughed too without knowing why. Na’ar put his bowl down on the grass so as not to spill it and laughed with his hands on his knees.

“He was right,” he said, when he could.

“Was he?”

“He was right about the tooth.” He wiped his eyes with the back of his wrist. “I taught you wrong. I did not teach you. You watched me. You decided.” He picked up the bowl again. “What did I tell you? Did I tell you that?”

She went back, and looked, and found she could not find it. She had watched him touch the tooth at a hundred fires. She had attended to the touch and the weather afterwards. She had never once asked him.

“No,” she said. “You never told me.”

“No. You decided.” He drank. “What else?”

“He held the bowl like that. Like you.” She nodded at it. “I watched him. I couldn’t stop watching. He asked me why I was looking at his hands, and I told him. I said, you hold it like somebody I knew. And he said, my grandmother’s brothers all held a bowl like this. And my mother. And everyone at every fire. It is how you hold a bowl.” She stopped. “He said, I am sorry for your friend. I am not him.”

Na’ar lowered the bowl a little and looked at her over it.

“No,” he said. “He is not.”

“Then he looked at the fire and said, I don’t cook either.”

Na’ar was silent. Then he laughed again, differently, shorter, almost with approval.

“He saw your face.”

“Yes.”

“He took the cook away from you before you could give it to him.” He nodded slowly. “That is a good boy.”

“He’s not a boy.”

“Everybody is a boy,” said Na’ar, “who cannot cook.” He held out his hand for her bowl. She gave it to him. He filled it from the pot, and looked into it, and fished in it with the ladle until he had found a piece of onion that satisfied him, and put it in, and gave it back. “Where is he now?”

“Home. With his great-aunt. She sings bones.”

“Ah.” He seemed to find this entirely reasonable. “Then he will be fed.”

She told him the rest because he asked. She told him about the bread on the harbour wall and the cheese Temür had scraped clean of lint with his thumb before he handed it to her, and the dog from the bread stall that had followed her and sat at a respectful distance and breathed through its mouth.

“He told it no.”

“Good.”

“It put its head on his knee.”

Na’ar waited.

“He looked down at it,” Riarhin said, “as if he couldn’t believe the audacity.”

Na’ar thought about that for a long time. The riders had begun to drift away from the fire, to their tents, to the horses. The red light lay along the grass.

“What did he give it?”

“The end of the fish skin.”

“Then it was right,” said Na’ar. “It knew he meant later.”

“He said he meant no.”

“He gave it the skin.” Na’ar shrugged one enormous shoulder. “He meant later.”

She laughed again, not so loud this time. She had not told him about the ice. About the sled going down under her backwards into the black water and the line coming out of nowhere across her back, and Temür’s voice behind her shouting in this same speech, take it, take it. She had meant to. She found that she wanted to keep it for a little while longer, as she wanted to keep everything about this fire, and the light, and the warmth coming off his arm.

She did not tell him about the gate.

He did not ask. He took his bowl and hers and wiped them out with a handful of grass and set them upside down on the felt beside the pot, side by side, and then he sat back on his saddle with his hands on his knees and looked out at the grass, where the woman called Tash was leading her grey horse away to the herd.

“Tomorrow,” he said.

“With the new saddle.”

“Yes.” He was watching the grey. “You will help me put it on.”

“Will I?”

“You are here,” said Na’ar. “Somebody has to hold its head.” He reached over without looking and took the ladle, and stirred the pot once, and lifted a little and blew on it and held it out to her. “Taste. Tell me the onions.”

She tasted.

“They’re still onions,” she said.

He nodded, satisfied, and laid the ladle across the pot.

Chapter 19 — What he stayed for

She slept in the boy’s tent, because the boy’s mother said so, on a felt that smelt of sheep, with the girl with the tied braids asleep across her feet. When she woke the light was the same long red-gold it had been when she lay down, and Na’ar was outside the tent door with the new saddle on his shoulder.

“Up,” he said. “Tash is awake.”

She went out barefoot into the grass. The dew was warm. The whole camp lay in that low light as if the sun had been stopped on its way down and asked to wait, and had agreed.

The grey stood by itself at the edge of the herd on a long halter, cropping. Tash was not with it. Tash was sitting by her own fire two tents away, with a bowl, watching, and when she saw Na’ar coming with the saddle she raised the bowl to him, slightly, and drank.

“She’s not going to help,” Riarhin said.

“She is going to watch.” Na’ar set the saddle down on the grass. “Hold his head.”

Riarhin took the halter rope. The grey lifted its head from the grass and looked at her with its big kind eye, and then at Na’ar, and then at the saddle. It blew out through its nose. She put her hand flat on its neck and felt it shift its weight from one hind foot to the other.

“He knows you,” she said.

“He knows me.” Na’ar shook out the felt. “We have an understanding. He does not understand it yet.”

He did it slowly, the way he had always done things with horses, talking. Not to her. To the grey, in Ykrul, in a low steady voice that said nothing in particular: here is the felt, here is the felt going on, this is a good felt, a woman downriver made it, she has small hands. The grey’s ear turned back to him and stayed there.

He laid the felt on and smoothed it with both palms from the withers back. He lifted the saddle and set it on the felt, and settled it, and stepped back and looked at it, and moved it forward a finger’s width and looked again.

Riarhin watched his feet.

She had not meant to. She had been watching the grey’s ear and the saddle and his hands, and her eyes had gone down of their own accord to the grass, and to his boots in it, and to the way he stood. His weight was on the right. When he stepped round to the far side of the horse he went the long way round its tail, and he put the left foot down and then brought the right to it, and only then went on. He did not limp. It was smaller than a limp. It was the way a man steps who has had a leg give under him and has taken it into account.

He came back round to the near side and bent for the girth.

“It is still there,” he said, without looking up. “The leg.”

She did not say anything.

“I asked. When I came. I said, there is a pot on my fire and a horse that will bear anyone and the grass goes to the edge of the world, so why is the leg still bad?” He drew the girth under the grey’s belly and caught the buckle. “They said, it is yours.”

“Who said?”

“The ones who say things.” He shrugged. “I thought about it. I decided they were right. It is mine. I carried Meret’s pannier across a bad floor and the floor took my leg. That is mine. I would not give it back.” He took the girth up one hole, and the grey grunted and swung its head round, and Na’ar put his hand on its nose and pushed it back to Riarhin without looking. “Hold his head.”

“I’m holding it.”

“Hold it better.”

She held it better.

He did the stirrups next. He let the leathers down and measured them against his arm, from his armpit to the tips of his fingers, and found them short, and began to undo the knot.

Under the thumb, round, back through. She watched him undo it the way she had watched him tie it in a ship’s forepeak, years ago, with his leg out in front of him wrapped in a Taharkan envoy’s wrap and his face grey under the brown.

“It went sideways,” she said.

He did not stop working the knot.

“At the gate. Your leg. You went towards the hedge so the man on the granary roof couldn’t see past you. And it went sideways.”

“Yes.”

“I saw it.”

“I know you saw it. You were turning the cart. You saw everything.” He got the knot loose and let the leather down two hands and began to tie it again. “I felt it go. I knew it would go some day. The stone told me in Ashaan, it will go, some day, sideways, when you are not thinking of me. And I was not thinking of it. I was thinking of the man on the roof.” He drew the knot tight and tested it. “So it went.”

The grey shifted. Riarhin moved with it and kept the rope short.

“And the sword,” Na’ar said. “Under the arm. Here.” He lifted his right arm and showed her, on his own side, the place, as though she might have forgotten it. She had not forgotten it. “That hurt. I will tell you, it hurt. More than the leg in Ashaan. Different. The leg in Ashaan was loud. This was quiet, and then it was everything.”

“Na’ar—”

“I am telling you. You want it. I can see you want it.” He went round the tail, the long way, the left foot and then the right, and began on the far stirrup. “I was afraid. One breath. Less. I thought, ah, this is the one. And then the man was still there, and I hit him with the pole, and I was busy.” He worked at the far knot. “You came back.”

“I came back.”

“You cut his wrist. I saw that. Good.” He tightened the knot. “I took your coat. I said move it. I said the cart. You said, I will. You said, I know.” He came back round the horse and stood beside her at its shoulder and looked at her. “I had decided before that. At the gate. When I said, I’ll hold it. That was when. Not on the bank.”

“I left you on the bank.”

“Yes.”

“I put my hand on your neck and there was nothing and I put you against the bank and I left you there.”

“Yes,” said Na’ar. “I told you to.”

She had the halter rope in both hands. She found she was twisting it.

“If I’d taken the farm road,” she said. “If I’d come to the gate first instead of the granary. If I hadn’t brought you to Valora at all. You were going back to Glenruan. You’d written to Mairi. You had a pot—”

“Stop.”

She stopped.

He did not say it loudly. He did not say it unkindly. He took the halter rope out of her hands, because she was twisting it and the grey had begun to toss its head, and held it himself, loosely, and the grey quieted.

“The leg was bad,” he said. “You knew it was bad. You asked me to come. I came. I could have said no. I said no other times.” He looked at the grey’s ear. “You decided the yard. You decided the cart, and the granary, and who went where. Those were yours. They mattered. I am not going to tell you they did not matter, so that you can go home lighter.” He scratched the grey under its jaw, and it leaned into his hand. “The gate was mine. The leg was mine. The man on the roof was his. I am not going to cut it up into pieces for you. I am saddling a horse.”

Riarhin stood in the warm grass with her empty hands.

“All right,” she said.

“Good.” He gave her back the halter rope. “Hold his head.”

He did not get on at once. He stood at the grey’s shoulder with one hand on the saddle and looked across at Tash’s fire, where Tash was still sitting with her bowl, and seemed to be making up his mind about something that was not the horse.

“Mara,” he said.

Riarhin had been waiting for it.

“She’s in Theona,” she said. “Or she was. In Eilis’s household. She stayed because she chose to. She made me say it aloud.” She heard her own voice go careful and let it. “That was years ago, Na’ar. A great many years. She wrote once after. She said she’d want the money and want it said what it was for, and not to send her a present, and not to write to say I was sorry. And she sent word by Eilis that she wasn’t staying for me.”

Na’ar was smiling.

“That is old,” he said.

“It’s all I have. I don’t know where she is now. I don’t know if she’s alive.”

“She is bossing someone,” said Na’ar, with complete confidence. “Wherever she is. She is telling someone the stew has caught on the bottom before they have tasted it.” He was quiet for a moment. “Good.”

He moved his hand on the saddle.

“And Berent,” he said. “Who has my sword?”

Riarhin had known that was coming too. She had known it since the ferryman lifted the saddle out of the bow.

“Berent has it.”

“Good. He can keep it. He was always looking at it.” Na’ar nodded. “How is the leg? The bolt. Mara said he would ride on it too soon.”

“It mended. A healer in the east mended it enough to ride. He took escort work for a merchant.” She stopped. “That was a long time ago too.”

“And?”

She did not answer at once. He turned his head and looked at her, and she saw him see that there was more, and wait for it.

So she told him. She told him about Midwinter Eve in Valora, and the old range, and Lyra, and Nessa on the stair. She told him about the door song and the man with the lantern who had known Berent’s face and said his name, and then looked at him and asked, where’s the knight? She told him about the loft afterwards, and the man by the stair with Na’ar’s sword over his shoulder, who said It’s me, and how she had looked at him with everything she had, and the name had slid off him like water off oilcloth.

“Nobody can know him,” she said. “Not since the song. Nobody. I can’t. I look at him and I know the name and I know the man and they don’t join. It’s like being told a stranger in the street is your brother.”

Na’ar said nothing.

“So I wrote it down.” She reached into the lining of her coat, on the left, where she had carried it through more than one country, and brought out the paper. It was soft as cloth now, folded small, gone brown along the folds. She unfolded it and held it out to him. “I wrote it so that I’d have it every time. Every time I met him. So I’d know to believe him.”

Na’ar did not take it. He looked at it in her hand. She did not know whether he could read the letters; she had never known. She read it to him.

“The man with Na’ar’s sword is Berent. He brought Lyra out. Nessa sang the door song on the stair to save his life, and nobody can know him now. Nessa is dead. Believe him.“

The grey cropped grass. Somewhere behind them a child was being told to come and eat.

“You wrote him down,” said Na’ar.

“I had to—”

“You wrote him down.” His voice had not risen. It had gone flat and slow, the way it went before he hit something. “Berent. Who rode at the back of Ewen’s train and complained about the broth. Who sat in my cart with his leg on a sack and bled on Mara’s hands and did not make a sound until we lifted him. Who looked at my sword every time I cleaned it like a boy looking at a girl.” He was looking at the paper. “You put him on a paper. Like a thing you buy at market. Remember. Onions. Berent.”

“I couldn’t know him, Na’ar. I tried. You don’t understand what the song—”

“I know him.”

She stopped.

“I know him now,” Na’ar said. “Here. I can see him in my head. I can see him as well as I see you. Better. The way he holds his head when he is angry about something small. The way he says aye.” He looked at her at last. “Your song does not reach here. Or it reaches and I do not care. I would know Berent in the dark. With my eyes shut. I would know him by his complaining.”

She did not know what to say. She stood with the paper in her hand.

“And you wrote him down,” said Na’ar, “and put him in your coat, and went away.”

“What else could I do?”

“Look at him.” His voice came up for the first time, not much, enough. “Ride with him. A season. Two. Sit at his fire every night and look at him until it joins. Or until it doesn’t, and you are still sitting there. That is what you do with a friend you cannot see. You sit where he is.” He breathed out through his nose, exactly like the grey. “You did not sit. You wrote a paper and you went to the next thing. You always go to the next thing.”

Riarhin folded the paper. Her fingers did it without her; they knew the folds. She put it back into the lining of her coat.

“Yes,” she said.

He looked at her for a long moment. Then he put his hand out, the big hand with the white scar along the outside of the forearm, and laid it on top of her head, the whole of it, heavy and warm, the way he had once in a station camp when she had sat up three nights over a fever that was not hers.

He left it there a moment. Then he took it away.

“I am still angry,” he said.

“I know.”

“Good,” said Na’ar. “Now I am going to sit on this horse.”

Tash had got up from her fire. So had the others. They were coming across the grass in ones and twos with their bowls, not hurrying, the way people come to watch something they have been told about at every meal for longer than anyone remembers. The boy was there. The girl with the tied braids was on the boy’s shoulders. The man with the scarred eyebrow had brought a saddle to sit on.

Na’ar put his hand on the pommel and his left foot in the stirrup.

He stopped there. He looked down at Riarhin.

“You will come again,” he said. “When you like. Not because you owe me a bowl. Because you want to eat.” He looked out at the grass, the long light. “You do not have to stay. Nobody is asking you to stay. It is a good place. It is not your place.”

“I know.”

“Bring your own bowl.”

“I will.”

“And tell me about the ice. You did not tell me about the ice. I saw your face, you kept something.”

She laughed. It came out unsteady.

“He threw me a line,” she said. “The boy. Temür. The sled went through and took me with it, backwards, and there was a line across my back from nowhere and he was shouting in Ykrul, take it, take it. So I took it.”

Na’ar thought about it.

“He cannot cook,” he said. “But he can throw.”

He swung up. He did it carefully, putting his weight down into the saddle slowly, the right leg over and settling, the left foot finding its stirrup. The grey took the weight. Its back dipped a little and came up. Its ears went back.

Tash folded her arms.

The grey turned its head and looked round at Na’ar. It looked at him for a long time, with its big kind eye. Riarhin, at its head, felt it think. She felt its weight go back onto its hocks, the beginning of the movement she had been warned about, the long careful folding of the legs.

Na’ar leaned forward and said something into its ear in Ykrul. Riarhin, a hand’s breadth away, heard it, and kept it.

The grey stood. It considered. Then it put its weight forward again and took a step.

Somebody whistled. The man with the scarred eyebrow said something rude to Tash, and Tash said something ruder back, and the girl on the boy’s shoulders began to drum her heels on his chest. Na’ar, high on the grey, did not look round. He sat it with his hands low and quiet and his bad leg hanging long, and he let it walk out into the grass, away from the fires, at its own pace, towards the edge of the world where the sun lay.

Riarhin let the halter go.

The riders went after him in a loose crowd, with their bowls, arguing. Tash went past Riarhin without looking at her and said, to nobody, “It’s the saddle,” and somebody laughed.

The boy came back for her with the bay. She found her boots in his tent, and Sarnai’s bowl, which the girl had washed. She put the bowl in her pack.

When she looked back from the top of the first fold of ground, the camp was small behind her and the grey was a pale shape far out in the red-gold grass with a big man on it, and riders walking after it, and none of them was looking her way.

Chapter 20 — A court without a country

Sarnai took the bowl back and looked into it.

“Washed,” she said. “Who washed it?”

“A girl with her braids tied together.”

“Then it’s clean.” Sarnai put it on the wagon step with the others. “He was there, your big man?”

“He was there.”

Sarnai looked at her face for a moment and asked nothing else. She put a cup of something hot and green into Riarhin’s hands and went back to her pot.

Dusana was asleep on the wagon’s roof with the raven on her hip. Cadan was where Riarhin had left him, on the step, with his lantern beside him and his eyes on the beach.

“The woman with the boots did not come back,” he said.

“No.”

“I walked a little way along the sand. As far as the reeds go. Then I came back, because you had not said I should go farther.” He looked at her. “There are buildings beyond the reeds. Low ones, grey. I could not see the towpath stair. I did not go near enough.”

“Good.”

He nodded. He seemed to have expected that.

“And inland,” he said, “there is a road.”

The road began at the top of the beach where the reed path came out, and it was a good road.

That was the first thing. Riarhin had walked on a great many roads and she knew a good one under her feet before she knew anything else about it. It was cambered, so that the water ran off. It had ditches on both sides, cleared. It was surfaced with packed stone that somebody had rolled. And at the side of it, at the edge of the verge, there was a milestone.

It was waist high and squared, with a number cut in it, and beneath the number a mark.

She stopped in front of it.

“What?” said Dusana, coming up behind her with the raven.

Riarhin did not answer. She was looking at the mark. It was a small device cut deep and clean, a crowned shape with something beneath it, and she had seen it last on a stone like this at the verge of a road south of Fairport, every half mile, all the way to the market town.

“Whose is it?” said Dusana.

“A queen’s.”

“Which queen?”

“One I killed,” Riarhin said.

Dusana looked at the stone, and then at her. Then she went on past it up the road without saying anything, and the raven on her shoulder turned its head round to keep looking at the milestone until they were well beyond it.

At the second milestone Riarhin found she could read it.

Not the number. She had always been able to read the number. Below the number and the mark, low down on the face where the stone met the verge, there were scratches: a line, a hook, two short strokes crossing, a circle with a dot in it. Carters’ marks. She had seen them on gateposts and stiles and the doorposts of inns all her life, from Silkstrand to the Linn coast, and she had known a few of them, the ones for dog and water and no, the way she knew a few words of every language spoken at a horse market.

She looked at these and knew them all.

Good water half a mile, left. Kitchen door soft, ask for the girl with the red hands. Keep to the left of the arch. And under those, fresher, scratched in an angry hurry:

Long sword on the back. Knocked my corner off. Went through without asking.

The top corner of the milestone, on the road side, had been broken away. The break was clean and pale and new. It was at the height of her shoulder blade.

She put her hand up over her shoulder and touched Remorse’s scabbard where it came past her right ear. Then she looked at the corner again.

“Dusana.”

Dusana came back.

“Can you read these?” Riarhin pointed at the scratches.

Dusana bent and looked. “Carters’ marks. Some of them. Water. That one’s a dog.” She straightened. “Why? What do they say?”

“That one says I knocked its corner off.”

“Did you?”

“I’ve never been here.”

Dusana looked at the broken corner and at Remorse’s scabbard and at Riarhin, and her mouth turned down at one side.

“Then you’ll be careful at the next one,” she said. “The stones talk, on some roads. Near a big house, mostly. They’ll tell you which way the house goes, if you can hear them, and complain about everybody who ever went by.” She took the raven off her shoulder, because it had begun to lean towards the stone with its beak open, and put it on the other side. “It doesn’t last. A road lends it to you while you’re on that road. When you’re off it, it’s gone.”

“How do you know?”

“I’ve had it. Once. On a road to a house that wasn’t mine.” Dusana looked up the road ahead, where it ran on between hedges into a brightening haze. “It lasted me to the back door and out again. Then it was gone, and I’ve never had it since.” She glanced at Riarhin. “Make use of it.”

Cadan had come up and was looking at the stone. He did not touch it. He put his hand near the broken corner, flat, a finger’s breadth from the stone, and held it there.

“It is warm,” he said.

“They’re all warm,” said Dusana. “Come on.”

The palace came up out of the haze all at once, the way a ship comes out of fog.

It was Valora. It was not Valora. The river was there, and the bridge, and the long front of pale stone with its windows, and the roofs going up behind in their pitches and turrets and chimneys. But there was no city round it. The road ran straight up to the gates between hedges, and on either side, behind the hedges, there were fields, and orchards, and beyond the orchards the land rose into blue hills that had never stood behind Valora in its life. It was a palace that had been set down in the country, as a child sets a toy house on a table, and the country had grown up round it to fit.

Every window was lit.

It was not evening. It was not anything. The light was the even white haze of the road. But every window in the long front had candles in it, and the light from the candles was yellow and warm and steady, and lay out across the forecourt in long bars. Riarhin looked at the windows and thought of a room with no shadow anywhere a person could stand in.

She did not stop walking.

The milestones had told her to keep to the left of the arch, and she kept to the left of it, along the side of the forecourt and through a gate in a wall, and found the laundry.

Steam came out of a long low building with its doors propped open, and women went in and out of the steam with baskets on their hips. In the yard in front of it there were lines strung from post to post with sheets on them, a great many sheets, white and heavy and dripping, and between the lines three women with wooden paddles were beating a wet tablecloth on a stone and talking about somebody’s sister. Nobody looked up. A dog asleep by the door lifted its head and put it down again.

It smelt of lye and hot linen and wet stone. Riarhin had smelt it before. She had come through a laundry yard like this one, in this palace’s original, with a basket on her hip and her head down and her hair pinned up under a cap, more than once, and nobody had looked at her then either.

“Here,” said Dusana, low. “Is this the way?”

“This is the way in.”

“For who?”

“For anybody who carries something.”

Dusana looked at her sideways and did not ask how she knew that.

Cadan had stopped by the propped door. Two maids came out past him with a basket of wet sheets between them and did not see him, and one of them stepped round him as though he were a post, and neither of them looked back.

Inside, the steam was thick enough to wet her face. There were coppers along one wall with fires under them, and a mangle, and long tables where women were folding, and at the far end a door open onto a stair that went up. A woman at the nearest copper was stirring with a pole. She was big-armed and red-faced and had her sleeves rolled above the elbow, and she looked at Riarhin through the steam, and at the sword, and at Dusana and the raven.

“If you’re for the Blue Room,” she said, “they’re still arguing.”

“Who is?”

“The Lady Ormand and the Lord Chancellor’s widow. They both say it was promised them. It’s got the window over the river.” She went on stirring. “It’s been promised them both since I came, and they’re both still arguing, and nobody’s slept in it.”

“How long since you came?”

The woman thought about it. She seemed to have to look for the answer somewhere.

“I don’t know,” she said. “Since the fever.” She lifted a sheet on the end of the pole and looked at it and let it fall back. “I’d been here eleven years when the fever took me. Valora. The real one, I mean. The laundry under the east wing, where the stair comes down.” She looked at Riarhin. “You’re not one of the household. Are you a guest?”

“I’m passing through.”

“Nobody passes through,” said the laundress. “Not here. They come and they stay.” She jerked her head at the stair. “Up there’s the service passage. It goes all along the back of the house. That’s where the household goes, so it doesn’t have to be seen. If you’re passing through, that’s how you’ll do it.”

“Which stair is that?”

“The linen stair.”

Riarhin stood very still in the steam.

“There’s a tread on it,” said the laundress, “the sixth from the top, that’s higher than the others. A finger higher. I told them when they put the new boards in. Nobody listened. Every day of my life I took a basket up that stair and every day of my life I caught my heel on the sixth tread.” She shook her head. “They’ve got it here too. Same tread. I still catch it.”

“I know the tread,” said Riarhin.

The laundress looked at her through the steam. Something in her face changed a little, very little.

“Do you,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Then you’ve been up it with a basket.” She went back to her stirring. “Colette,” she said. “If anybody asks who sent you.”

The stair at the far end of the laundry was not the linen stair. It was a plain stone service stair, wide and worn, going up into the house. Riarhin climbed it with Dusana behind her and Cadan behind Dusana, and at the top there was a passage, long and plain and whitewashed, with doors along one side and narrow windows along the other, and servants in it.

Some of them were going somewhere. Most of them were waiting. They sat on a long bench under the windows, or stood against the wall, with their hands folded, in livery: footmen, pages, a woman with a tray, a man with a pair of boots. They were waiting to be called. Riarhin could see it in the way they held themselves, the way their heads were turned a little towards the doors. Some of them had the look of having waited a long time. A page of perhaps twelve had fallen asleep upright against the wall with a candle-snuffer in his hand.

The house was being kept. Somebody was keeping it. The floor of the passage had been swept that morning, whatever morning was here. The window glass was clean. At the end of the passage a door stood open into a room where two women in court dress were standing on either side of a bed with its curtains drawn back, and a steward between them with his hands raised, and the voice of one of the women came down the passage quite clearly:

“—promised it, he promised it to me in so many words—”

“He promised it to everybody, he was a fool—”

“Ladies,” said the steward.

Riarhin walked past the door. Through it she saw a window over a river, and a blue silk coverlet, smooth, that nobody had slept under.

The passage turned, and went along the back of a great hall, and there were openings in its inner wall, screened, so that a servant could look into the hall and see whether he was wanted. Riarhin stopped at one and looked.

The hall was full of light. Candles stood along the tables and on stands and in sconces up the pillars, so many that there was nowhere in the whole long room a shadow could fall. People moved in it in court dress, slowly, talking, and the sound of their talk came through the screen as a warm continuous murmur. At the far end, under a canopy, there was a chair, and the chair was empty.

At the near end, by the doors, on a bench against the wall, sat an old man.

He was not in court dress. He had on a farmer’s coat, brown, worn at the cuffs and mended at one shoulder with a different brown, and he had his hat in his hands, a broad felt hat with the brim gone soft, and he was turning it round, slowly, by the brim, the way a man turns a hat when he has been told to wait and does not know how long. His boots were muddy. Nobody in the hall was looking at him. People went past him to the doors and out, and in, and he sat on his bench and turned his hat.

“Who’s that?” said Dusana, at her shoulder.

A footman on the bench in the passage behind them answered, without being asked.

“The headman,” he said. “From the upper Tarne.” He did not look up. “He comes every day to be heard.”

“And is he?”

“Not yet,” said the footman.

Riarhin looked at the old man’s hat going round and round in his hands.

She did not hear the steps. She heard them only when they stopped, which was a thing that had not happened to her in a long time. She turned from the screen.

There was a woman in the passage.

She had come along it from the far end, from the direction of the room with the blue coverlet, with a steward a pace behind her carrying a sheaf of papers. She wore red over black, and her hair had been arranged with more skill than patience, and a pin stood at a slightly wrong angle above one ear. Every servant on the bench had come to their feet. The page with the snuffer had woken and was standing with his eyes very wide.

She had stopped three paces from Riarhin. She was looking at her face. Then at the sword over her shoulder. Then at her face again.

There was a narrow black ribbon at her throat. Nothing hung from it.

“There you are,” said Veralyn.

Riarhin did not move. She was aware of Dusana behind her, perfectly still. She was aware of Cadan somewhere, not where she expected him to be. She was aware of her own left hand, which had not gone up over her shoulder, and which she kept where it was.

“You have been,” said the Queen, “very difficult to invite.”

“So I’m told.”

Veralyn looked at her a moment longer. Her face did not change much. A little at the corner of the mouth. Then she held out her hand, without looking, and the steward put a paper into it, and she glanced at the paper and gave it back.

“Supper is at the bell,” she said. “You’ll eat. You look as though you’ve been eating out of other people’s pots.” She turned to go on along the passage, past the screen, past the openings into the hall. She did not look through the screen at the old man on the bench. “And then you’ll tell me what you want. I can see that you want something.”

She went on. The steward followed. The servants on the bench sat down again, slowly, one by one.

Riarhin looked back through the screen.

The headman had stopped turning his hat. He was holding it still, by the brim, on his knees, and looking at the doors through which the Queen had not come.

Chapter 21 — An excellent queen

The bell for supper was the Midwinter bell. Riarhin knew it by the third stroke, and stood in the service passage with her hand flat against the whitewash until it had finished.

Cadan was beside her. She had not seen him arrive.

“I will stay in the passage,” he said. “With the people waiting. Nobody here has asked for me, and I would rather the Queen did not find a reason to.” He paused. “Is that what you would like?”

“Yes.”

He sat down on the end of the servants’ bench. The footman beside him moved up to make room without looking round, and Cadan folded his hands in his sleeves and was, at once, one more person waiting to be called.

Supper was not in the hall. A page took Riarhin and Dusana along the passage and through a door into a small panelled room with a fire and a round table, laid for three, and a window that looked down into a courtyard. There were candles on the table and on the mantel and in a branch on the floor in the corner. Riarhin counted them without wanting to, and stopped at eight, and did not count the rest.

Veralyn came in by another door and sat down without ceremony.

“Sit,” she said. “Both of you. I don’t know your name,” she said to Dusana.

“No,” Dusana agreed, and sat.

Veralyn looked at her with mild interest and let it go. A man came in with a dish of eels in green sauce and another of something in pastry, and bread, and a jug, and went out. The raven, which Dusana had brought in under her shawl against Riarhin’s advice, put its head out and looked at the eels.

“Is that bird going to eat at my table?”

“Not if you’re watching,” said Dusana.

“I’m always watching.” Veralyn served herself a little eel, and then, after a moment’s thought, put a piece on the edge of a saucer and pushed it across the cloth towards Dusana’s shoulder. The raven took it, and went back under the shawl, and could be heard dealing with it. “There. Now it’s a guest. Now it can’t steal.”

Dusana looked at the Queen for a long moment and then, very slightly, smiled.

Riarhin ate. The eel was good. The pastry had mushrooms in it and something sharp. She ate because she had been told to and because she was hungry and because she did not know what else to do with her hands, and Veralyn watched her eat, and ate a little herself, and said nothing at all until Riarhin had finished the first plate.

“Well,” she said. “And what did you do then?”

It was the question. Riarhin had heard it in a lighted room in Valora, over a green case on a black ribbon, a long time ago, and she had answered it then for an hour with things she had not meant to say. She had forgotten nothing about the way it was asked: the head a little on one side, the hands still, the whole attention laid on the table between them like a cloth.

“After what?”

“After me.” Veralyn broke a piece of bread. “You came into my house on Midwinter Eve with a sword, and then you went out of it. I’d like to know where you went. I’ve had nobody to ask.”

“I went east. Then north.”

“That’s a direction. I asked what you did.”

So Riarhin told her some of it. Not the containment, not the mountains. She told her the shape of the years the way she would have told a stranger at an inn: a house in Dhahara, a forest she had gone back to, and the north, and the ice. Veralyn listened. She asked short questions. Who paid for that? How many of them? Did it hold? She laughed once, at the Linn skipper and the pans, warm and tired and pleased, and Riarhin heard the laugh and had to look at the fire.

“And now you’re here,” said the Queen. “In my house. With a sword and a Tulkani and a bird. Going where?”

“Down.”

“Why?”

Riarhin was quiet for a moment.

“There’s a hurt in the ways,” she said. “Something a girl did on the Mistlands coast to keep the dead off her neighbours’ doors. It worked. It’s still working. It’s pulling at something here that it shouldn’t, and it’s getting worse, and I’m going down to see if it can be mended.”

“By you.”

“By whoever can.”

“And this house is in your way.”

“Your house is on the road.”

Veralyn considered that. She did not seem offended. She seemed, if anything, pleased, the way a person is pleased when a thing she has built turns out to be load-bearing.

“It would be,” she said. “I put it on the road.” She poured wine for herself, and for Dusana, who had held out her cup. “And you want to know how to get through it without being kept.”

“Yes.”

“That’s the first honest thing you’ve asked me in two lives.” Veralyn drank. “Ask the rest. What else? You’ve been sitting on something since the eel.”

Riarhin had not meant to tell her about her father. She found she was telling her.

She told it badly. She told it in pieces: a workshop under an overhang, a sword’s grip unwrapped, a man who carried what she carried. A hope. A people divided into three who had once been one, and a child who might be shaped, patiently, into someone who could go between them and lead them back to a single table.

Veralyn listened to all of it with her chin on her hand.

“One table,” she said, when Riarhin stopped.

“That was his word. Or near it.”

“For three peoples who left it.”

“Yes.”

“Did they ask to come back?”

“No. One of them told him to his face that they’d put the name down on purpose, and if he came to their valley with his one table she’d see him out of it herself.”

Veralyn laughed. It was not unkind.

“Then she’s the only one of them who’s thought about it,” she said. She set down her cup. “I’ll tell you what your father wanted, and you can tell me if I’m wrong. He wanted a line. A line drawn round a people, with everyone inside it, and someone at the centre of it to say where the line goes. That’s what one table is. Somebody sets it. Somebody decides how many chairs. Somebody sends to the ones who didn’t come, and asks again, and then sends someone else to ask.” She turned the stem of the cup with her fingers. “And the ones who still won’t come have to be got in somehow, because a table with gaps at it isn’t one table. It’s two.”

“He didn’t say that.”

“No. People don’t. They say together. They say whole.” Veralyn looked at her. “I drew a line once. You know the one. Every village inside it that hadn’t sent its headman to swear. I had wagons for the old people. It was very orderly.” She said it without any change in her voice at all. “It worked. I want you to understand that. It was the right line and it worked. If you’re going to sit at the head of your father’s table, you’ll draw one too. Everybody does. The only question is whether you’ll admit it at supper.”

Dusana had stopped eating.

“I’m not going to sit at the head of anybody’s table,” Riarhin said.

“Good,” said Veralyn. “Then you’ll not need me to tell you how.” She picked up her bread again. “But you came here asking, so I’ve told you.”

“It worked,” Riarhin said. “You keep saying that.”

“Because it did.” Veralyn did not lift her voice. “You walked my roads. Don’t tell me you didn’t. You walked from Fairport to the market town on a road that had been mended by people who were paid to mend it, and paid on the day. There was a culvert on that road with the year cut in its lintel. There was a man on a stool at the causeway with a slate, counting carts. Not a toll. A count. So we’d know what to mend first.” She tore the bread. “Did you see any of that anywhere else? In all your east and north? Did you see a single road where somebody had sat down and counted the carts?”

Riarhin had not. She did not say so. Veralyn saw it anyway.

“My soldiers paid for their billets,” the Queen said. “My grain arrived the week it was needed. A woman at an inn on that road had a sister’s boy in my foot and he sent his wages home and she was proud of it. She said it to you, didn’t she? I know she did. She said it to everybody.”

“She said it to me.”

“Well, then.”

“There’s a man on a bench outside your hall,” Riarhin said. “In a farmer’s coat. With his hat.”

Veralyn reached for the dish of eels.

“Have you had enough of these? They don’t keep.”

“He’s from the upper Tarne.”

“I know where he’s from.” Veralyn served herself. “He’ll be heard.”

“When?”

“When there’s time.” The Queen ate a piece of eel. “There’s a great deal to be got through, in a house this size. You’ve seen it. Two women have been quarrelling over the Blue Room since before either of them came here. Somebody has to decide it.” She glanced at Dusana’s cup, which was empty, and filled it. “Your friend drinks well.”

“Seventeen thousand people,” said Riarhin. “In the first frosts.”

Veralyn put the jug down.

For a moment there was nothing in the small room but the fire and the candles and the sound of the raven under Dusana’s shawl, still working at its piece of eel.

“Have you finished?” Veralyn said. “Because I have.” She stood. “You’ll want the service passage. Go along it past the nursery wing. At the end there’s a door to the drying ground, and the laundry keeps the key, and the laundry doesn’t give it to guests. You’ll have to find someone who’ll let you through.” She looked down at Riarhin. “You managed before. I imagine you’ll manage again.”

She did not go at once. She went to the window, and stood looking down into the courtyard, with her back to them.

Riarhin got up. She came to the window too, not near, at the other side of it, and looked down.

The courtyard was small and paved and lit by the candles in the windows round it. There was no one in it but a man.

He was tall, a head taller than Riarhin, with a long arm, in his shirt, and he had a long sword in his hand, and he was drilling. Alone. He went through the guards slowly, one after another, the point coming up and round and down and back, his feet moving carefully on the stones. He did it the way a man does a thing he has done every day of his life. Riarhin watched him go out from the wall and come back, go out and come back. He never went farther from the wall than a single step.

She stood at the window and watched him and did not move.

“He does that,” said Veralyn, without turning her head. “Every morning. Whatever passes for morning.” She watched him too. “He doesn’t come in. I’ve sent. He says he’ll come in when he’s finished, and he doesn’t finish.”

The man in the courtyard turned on his back foot and brought the point round. The candlelight ran down the blade.

“You needn’t go down,” said Veralyn. “He wouldn’t speak to you. He’s busy.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“No.” The Queen was quiet a moment. “I didn’t think you were.”

She turned from the window then and looked at Riarhin, close, for the first time since the passage. Her face in the candlelight was the face Riarhin had last seen by a hearth, looking past her at a door.

“Was she alone?” said Veralyn.

Riarhin had known it would come. She had not known it would come like this, so quietly, by a window.

“No,” she said. “I sent for Margery before I went in. So that she’d be the one who came to her.”

Veralyn said nothing for a long time.

“Margery,” she said at last. “Good.”

She went back to the table. She did not sit down. She stood with her hand on the back of her chair.

Riarhin found herself somewhere else. Not here. In a long room in Valora, years after the hearth, with a girl in green standing in front of her with her father’s shape round the eyes, taller than Riarhin had let herself remember, in borrowed slippers that were too large. Will you answer if I write? the girl had said. Yes. That doesn’t mean I want you here. I know.

She did not say any of it. She stood at the window and kept it.

“She’s well?” said Veralyn. She did not look round.

“She was, when I saw her last. She’s queen. She has a council and she argues with it.” Riarhin paused. “She doesn’t want me in Valora. She told me so.”

The Queen’s shoulders moved. It might have been a laugh.

“That’s mine,” she said. “She got that from me.” She took her hand off the chair. “Go and find your laundress.”

Colette was folding sheets.

She looked up when Riarhin came through the steam and did not stop folding. Corner to corner, and corner to corner, and smooth it with the flat of the hand, and onto the pile.

“The door to the drying ground,” Riarhin said.

“Is locked.”

“You’ve the key.”

“I’ve a key.” Colette took another sheet from the line above her head. “So has Agathe, and so has the old woman at the end, and none of us gives it to guests. The Queen’s rule. Guests come in, they don’t go out the back. It upsets the arrangements.”

Dusana had come in behind Riarhin. She was looking at the pile of folded sheets, and at the empty basket at the end of the table, with an expression Riarhin recognised. It was the expression of somebody who has seen a tool lying where it could be picked up.

Riarhin picked up the basket.

She did not say anything. She held it out to Colette, and Colette looked at it, and at her, and after a moment put the folded sheet into it. Then another. Then she went on folding, and putting them in, and Riarhin stood and held the basket and let her fill it, and when it was full Colette took a second basket from under the table and filled that and handed it to Dusana.

“They go out to the drying ground,” Colette said. “To air. Every day. Through the door at the end of the service passage.” She wiped her hands on her apron and took a key from her pocket, a big iron key on a loop of tape, and held it out. “Whoever’s carrying them takes the key. And brings it back.” She looked at Riarhin. “That’s the household’s way. Nobody asks who’s carrying.”

Riarhin took the key.

“Thank you.”

“Don’t thank me. I’ve done nothing. Sheets want airing.” Colette turned back to the copper and picked up her pole. “Mind the tread,” she said, to the steam. “On your way down. You know the one.”

They went along the service passage with the baskets on their hips. Riarhin walked as she had walked a hundred passages like it: head down, shoulders a little bowed, the basket held a little out from the body as though it were heavier than it was, her step the step of somebody who has carried this up and down this passage every day and expects to carry it every day after. Dusana, beside her, had fallen into the same walk without being told, and the raven rode under the top sheet of her basket with only its beak showing.

The servants on the bench did not look up. A steward coming the other way stepped round them without seeing. They passed the screened openings into the hall, and Riarhin did not look through, and passed a door from behind which a child’s voice was singing something tuneless, and did not stop.

Cadan was waiting at the end of the passage, by a narrow door with an iron lock. She did not know how he had known which door. He had his lantern and his ropes and was standing with his hands in his sleeves, and he looked at the baskets and then at the key in Riarhin’s hand and said nothing at all.

She put the key in the lock. It turned.

Beyond the door there was a slope of grass with lines strung across it for drying, empty, and a gravel path going down between them, and at the foot of the slope, alone in the grass, an arch. It was old stone, with no wall on either side of it and no door in it, and through it the light was a different colour.

Riarhin set the basket down on the step, inside the door, where whoever came next for the sheets would find it. She put the key on top of the sheets.

Dusana set hers beside it. The raven climbed out of it, shook itself, and dropped a feather on the clean linen.

“Leave it,” said Dusana. “They’ll think it’s a gull.”

Chapter 22 — The shape of a welcome

The arch stood alone in the grass at the foot of the drying ground, and the gravel path went straight to it and through it, and there was no other way down.

Riarhin stopped a pace short of it. Dusana stopped beside her. She had been counting under her breath since the door; Riarhin had heard her reach eight at the top of the slope and stand still, and look round, and then go on, and reach eight again at the bottom.

The arch was old stone, grey, with lichen on the north side. There were carters’ marks cut low on the right-hand pier, and Riarhin bent and read them.

Through, not round. Don’t answer.

“What does it say?” said Dusana.

“Don’t answer.”

“Answer what?”

Riarhin straightened and looked through the arch.

There was a woman on the other side of it.

She was standing on the gravel exactly where Riarhin would be standing if she took one step forward. She was Riarhin’s height. She had Riarhin’s face. She wore a long coat of a grey-green Riarhin had never owned, cut close at the waist and falling to the boot, and her hair was bound up off her neck with a pin of pale wood, and she had Remorse on her back. Through her left eyebrow, from the brow bone up into the hairline, there ran an old white scar.

Riarhin had never had a scar there.

The woman looked at her through the arch. Her eyes went to Riarhin’s left eyebrow, the smooth one, and rested there with an expression of courteous interest, the way one looks at a stranger’s unusual ring.

“How did you avoid it?” she said.

Riarhin opened her mouth.

Dusana’s hand closed on her wrist, hard, the fingers digging in. Riarhin felt the scar on Dusana’s palm against the inside of her wrist, a ridge, warm.

She closed her mouth.

The woman on the other side waited. She did not seem impatient. She tilted her head a little, and Riarhin saw that the pin in her hair was carved at the end with a shape like a leaf, and that the coat had a clasp at the collar, silver, two leaves, bent.

“Through,” said Dusana, very low. “Not round. Go on.”

Riarhin went through.

There was nobody on the gravel. There was only gravel, and the grass on either side, and the path going on down, and the air on the other side of the arch was cooler and smelt of beeswax. She looked back. The arch stood in the grass with the drying ground beyond it and the open door at the top of the slope, small, with the baskets on its step.

Dusana let go of her wrist. There were four white marks on it, going red.

“Don’t do that again,” Dusana said.

“I didn’t.”

“You nearly did.” Dusana rubbed her own palm against her skirt, once, as though it hurt. “Come on.”

Cadan came through the arch behind them. He did not look back at all.

The path went down into a hollow, and in the hollow there was a hall.

It had not been there from the top of the slope. Riarhin was sure of that the way she was sure of everything she had looked at, and she had looked down the slope from the door and seen grass and the arch and the path and a hollow with trees in it. Now there was a hall where the trees had been, long and low and built of pale stone, with its doors standing open and light coming out of them. Not candlelight. A green light, the light that comes down through leaves.

She went down to it because the path went down to it, and stood in the doorway.

It was a long room with a table down the middle. The table was laid. There were cloths on it, grey-green, and bowls, and cups, and at every place a folded napkin, and there were a great many places. The roof was held up by pillars that had been cut to look like trunks, with the bark carved on them, so that the room felt like the inside of a wood. Along the walls there were hangings with patterns on them she did not know.

Nobody was in it.

She went in. She did not decide to. Her feet took her along the side of the table, past the places, and she looked at them as she went the way she looked at everything, and they were not all the same.

Some were set for people she did not know. Those had a plainness to them, a cup and a bowl and a napkin, and the chairs were all alike. There were a great many of those, running away down the table towards the far end. But nearer the head of the table the places changed. They had been set for particular people. She could see it in them, the way one sees a person in their coat hung on a peg.

The first she came to had a hurdle-maker’s knife laid beside the bowl, short and hooked, and a scrap of willow, and the chair was pulled in close and square to the table. It faced down the length of the hall. It had its back to the door.

Riarhin stopped.

Coryd would not sit there. Coryd never sat with her back to a door. In every inn and kitchen and cold house where Riarhin had eaten with her, Coryd had taken the seat by the stair or the seat by the door, without comment, the way she put her coat on a peg, and if someone else was in it she stood until they moved. She would come into this hall and look at that chair and stand behind it with her arms folded, and say nothing, and wait.

Somebody had set Coryd’s place who had never watched her sit down.

Riarhin looked up the table.

The next place had a pair of soft shoes beside the bowl, a dancer’s shoes, the toes scuffed, and the chair was set close against the table with its arms in, so close that there was no room at all under the board for anyone to put a leg out straight. Harissa would sit sideways in it, if she sat at all. Her bad leg would not go under that board. She would have to put it out into the room where people could trip on it, and she would make a joke about tripping people, a sharp one, and nobody would laugh.

The place after that was under a beam. The roof came down lower there than anywhere else in the hall, a crossbeam carved like a bough, and the chair stood right beneath it with a small stringed instrument laid on the seat. Linias would stand up from that chair, in the middle of something, to make a point, and strike his head on the bough, the way he struck his head on every lintel in every house he had ever been in, and swear, and sit down again holding it.

There was a fourth place with a hand-drum by the bowl and an inkhorn, and a list laid under the cup in a round neat hand that Riarhin did not let herself read.

And there was the head of the table.

The chair there was larger than the others, but not by much. It had been made to look as though it were not much larger. It stood at the end of the hall under a hanging of the grey-green cloth, and beside its bowl there was nothing at all, no knife, no shoes, no instrument, nothing laid out for the person who would sit there, because whoever sat there would need nothing laid out. The others would bring it.

Riarhin stood halfway up the table, with her hand on the back of Harissa’s chair, and understood what she was looking at.

There was no sound in the hall. The green light came down from nowhere. The places waited. Nobody’s voice came, not Coryd’s, not Linias’s, not any of them. The chairs stood empty with their particular things laid by them, and it was all carefully done, and nobody who had done it had ever seen any of them eat.

She thought of Harissa in the dancing room in the Valewood, saying No, and then, when Riarhin protested, It’s the turn before you stab somebody, and Linias laughing until Zemia told them the street could hear. She thought of Coryd’s letters, short, square, Come when you can. She thought of Linias refusing the first thing she had ever asked him to do. None of them had been asked about this hall. None of them had been asked about the chairs.

Her hand was still on Harissa’s chair. She took it off.

“Riarhin.”

Dusana was not in the hall. Her voice came from the doorway, flat and practical, the voice of somebody calling across a yard about a gate.

“Riarhin. It’s not this way.”

Riarhin turned. Dusana stood in the doorway with the raven on her shoulder and her bag over her arm, and she was not looking at the table at all. She was looking at the floor just inside the door, at the stones there.

“How do you know?”

“Because this was laid for you,” said Dusana, “and the road wasn’t. Come out. Come and look at the floor.”

Riarhin came back along the table. She did not look at the places as she passed them. She looked at the floor, the way Dusana was looking at it, and saw what Dusana had seen.

The hall’s floor was pale stone. But just inside the doorway, under the first flags, there was earth showing at a crack, dark and turned, and in the earth a row of something green. Bean shoots. A short row of them, coming up through the gap between two flags, leaning towards the door.

“There’s something under this,” said Dusana. “Something that was here first. Look at the doorstep.”

The doorstep was not pale stone. It was an old worn slab of red sandstone, dipped in the middle, the kind that lies in front of a cottage door for a hundred years and is never replaced. The hall’s pale walls had been built up to it on either side, neatly, as though it had not been there. But it had.

Cadan came round the corner of the hall.

“There is a garden,” he said. “At the back. And a woman in it.”

The garden had a wall round it, low, of rough stones with moss on them, and a gate of grey wood hung on leather hinges. Inside it there were bean rows on poles, and cabbages, and a currant bush, and a water butt, and a small low house with a thatched roof and a red sandstone doorstep exactly like the one at the hall. The hall had grown out of the back of the house. Its pale wall stood against the cottage’s end wall like a stranger leaning on a neighbour.

The woman in the garden was old and stout and wore a man’s hat. She was tying beans to their poles with strips of rag, and she did not stop when they came to the gate.

“If you’re from the big house,” she said, “I’ve told them.”

“We’re not from the big house,” said Riarhin.

The old woman looked round. She looked at Riarhin’s face for a while, and at the sword, and then at Dusana, and seemed to make up her mind that Dusana was the one to talk to.

“It came up one morning,” she said, nodding at the hall. “Like a mushroom. I came out to see to the beans and it was standing on my bottom row. Nobody asked. Then people started coming through my garden to get round it. Then somebody from inside came and said I could have a place at the table if I liked, a good one, near the top.” She tied a knot in a rag. “I said I’ve got a table. I said it’s in my kitchen and it’s been there forty years and I’ll eat at it. He went away.” She pulled the rag tight. “They want the garden for a courtyard. I’ve told them no. I’m not going anywhere. I was here first.”

“You were,” said Dusana.

“I was.” The old woman looked at the hall’s pale wall leaning on her house. “It’s not a bad building. I’ll say that. But I didn’t ask for it.”

Riarhin stood at the gate.

“We need to get past,” she said. “Down. Below the hall. Is there a way?”

“There’s my way,” said the old woman. “Through the garden and out the bottom gate and down the lane. That’s what people use. I don’t mind people. I mind them trampling.” She looked hard at Riarhin’s boots. “Single file. Along the path. Not across the cabbages.”

“Single file,” said Riarhin.

“And shut the gate.”

They went through in single file. Dusana went first, along the narrow path of trodden earth between the bean rows, and then Riarhin, and then Cadan, who walked so carefully that not a leaf moved. The beans smelt green and sharp. The old woman went on tying. At the bottom of the garden there was a second gate, and beyond it a lane going down between banks of nettles.

Riarhin stopped at the bottom gate and looked back.

The hall stood against the cottage. Its open doorway glowed green. She could see down its length, through the doors, to the head of the table and the larger chair under its hanging. From here it looked smaller: a room somebody had made for somebody else.

She shut the gate and dropped the loop of rope over the post.

The lane went down. It went down between nettle banks and then between hawthorns and then between nothing, between grey slopes of shale, and the light went from green to grey, and the air got colder. There were milestones at the side of it still, the same squared stones with the number and the mark, though the numbers had stopped making sense; and at each one Riarhin bent and read the marks low down on the face, and they told her where the water was and where the lane forked and which fork to keep to.

At the fifth stone the marks were faint.

She bent to them. Right fork. Grey rooms. Stair on the near side, under, for the river. And below that, very faint, as though the stone were tired:

Last one. After this you’re on your own.

She read it twice. Then she stood up and looked down the lane.

It went down into a valley of grey. Not mist. Stone. A long low sprawl of grey stone buildings, or one building, she could not tell, with ribbed roofs like the bones of something, going away down the valley floor, and over all of it a thin mist lying close to the ground, not rising, the way mist lies in a room. To the right of it, far off, there was a flatness that might have been the river.

The next milestone, when they reached it, had marks on it. She bent and looked at them. They were scratches. A line, a hook, a circle with a dot. She did not know what any of them meant.

“Gone?” said Dusana.

“Gone.”

“It lasted you as far as it said it would.” Dusana looked down at the grey valley. “That’s more than most things do.”

The towpath stair was where the stone had said, under the grey rooms on the near side.

It was a flight of rough steps cut into the bank where the lane came down to the edge of the grey buildings, going up and to the right, away from the ribbed roofs, towards the flatness that was the river. It was narrow and old and the treads were worn hollow in the middle. A rusted iron ring had been set into the rock at its foot, for a lantern or a rope.

Cadan stood at the foot of it and looked up.

“I would like to go up a little way,” he said. “To see that it goes where the ferryman said. Is that what you would like?”

“Go.”

He went up. He climbed steadily, not fast, the ropes on his shoulder and the lantern in his hand, and the stair turned and he was gone round the turn. Riarhin stood at the foot of it and listened to his steps go up, and up, and stop, and stay stopped for a while, and then come down.

He came round the turn.

“It goes to the river,” he said. “There is a path along the top of the bank, going up. I could see it a long way. I could see lamps at the end of it, very small.” He came down the last steps. “I think they are the doors. Where Ambrus is. I could not be sure.”

“It’s the towpath,” said Dusana. “That’s where it goes.”

Cadan took the coil of red cord from his belt. He measured out an arm’s length of it, and cut it with the little knife he kept in his sleeve, and tied one end through the iron ring at the foot of the stair with a knot Riarhin had seen him tie at the waystation, a long time ago, at another ring: three turns and a tuck and a hitch over. He drew it tight and let the end hang.

“There,” he said. “If we come back this way, we will know it.”

Riarhin looked at the red cord hanging from the ring against the grey rock. It was the only colour anywhere.

“Thank you,” she said.

“It is what you asked,” said Cadan.

The raven had got down off Dusana’s shoulder and was walking along the bottom step. It looked at the red cord, and then up the stair, and then, for a long time, down the valley towards the grey ribbed roofs. Then it walked back to Dusana and climbed up her skirt and onto her shoulder, and put its head down into its feathers, and would not look at anything.

Chapter 23 — Her grandmother’s way

The first room had a floor of pale paving and a roof of ribs.

They came into it from the foot of the lane, through a doorway with no door, and Riarhin stopped on the threshold so suddenly that Dusana walked into her back.

It was long and narrow and high. The ribs came down out of the walls on either side and met overhead in a ridge, grey, curved, close-set, like the inside of something that had once been alive. Between them the walls were smooth. The light came from nowhere in particular and was grey. On the floor, to the depth of her ankles, lay mist. It did not move. It lay as water lies in a basin, flat, filling every hollow in the paving, and where her boot went into it at the threshold it parted round the leather and closed again without a sound.

She had stood in a room like this. She had knelt on its floor.

“What is it?” said Dusana.

“Nothing.” Riarhin did not move. “I’ve been somewhere like it.”

“Is it the same?”

Riarhin looked at the ribs. They were not the same. They were set closer, and they had no cracked flakes under them, and there was no throat at the far end going up to a dark where chocks held something back. But the grey was the grey, and the mist lay the way that mist had lain, and the air had the same taste, cold and dry and faintly of stone dust.

“No,” she said.

She went in.

There was another doorway at the far end, and beyond it another room, the same, and beyond that a third. The rooms did not go in a straight line. Each doorway was a little to one side of the last, so that they went down the valley in a long shallow curve, like a spine. Dusana stopped in each doorway. Riarhin heard her count, under her breath, eight, and stand, and look round, and go on.

Cadan walked behind them with his lantern unlit. Twice Riarhin looked back and he had his hand out flat towards a wall, a finger’s breadth from it, the way he had held it towards the milestone. He did not say what he felt.

In the fourth room there was a boot.

It lay on its side in the mist near the wall, half sunk, so that only the top of it showed and the laces trailing. It was a man’s boot, good leather, recently resoled. Through the top eyelet there was a knot of red wool.

Riarhin picked it up. It was dry inside. The mist had not got into it.

“She came this way,” said Dusana.

“She dropped it.”

“She’d not drop it.” Dusana looked at the boot in Riarhin’s hand. “She’s been carrying those since before I first rode that boat. She’d not drop one unless she needed the hand.”

Riarhin listened.

She heard the mist, which made no sound. She heard Cadan breathing, and Dusana, and the raven shifting its feet on Dusana’s shoulder. She heard, a long way off, through several walls, a voice. It was a woman’s voice, and it was saying a name, the same name over and over, not loud, the way a person calls a name when they have been calling it for a long time and no longer expect an answer.

“Arvo,” said the voice. “Arvo.”

Riarhin tied the boot by its laces to her pack strap.

In the fifth room the doorway they had come through was not there.

She had not heard it go. She turned, because the raven had made a small hard noise in its throat, and where the doorway had been there was wall: smooth, grey, between two ribs, as if it had always been wall. The mist lay against the foot of it undisturbed.

Cadan was standing beside it. He had his hand out towards it. This time he did not hold it off; he laid it flat against the wall, and Riarhin saw the wall take it, solid, his palm flat on grey stone.

“It was here,” he said. “I was looking at it. It was here, and then it was not.” He took his hand away. “I did not see it close.”

“Nobody sees them close,” said a voice. “That’s the use of them.”

She was standing in the far doorway.
