Riarhin had not heard her come. She heard everything, and she had not heard this. She looked at the doorway and the woman was in it, as the wall had been in the other one: as though she had always been there.

She was lean, and straight, and grey. A grey robe, belted, falling to the boot. Grey in the skin, grey in the hair drawn back from the face, and the eyes grey, pale, steady. She held a spear the way a person holds a spear who has held one a long time, close and low, the blade moving very slightly. Her weight was on her right leg. The left stood a little out from her, straight, and did not take its share.

“Granddaughter,” said Farieth.

Riarhin did not answer. She heard Dusana, behind her, go still. She felt Cadan, somewhere to her left, not move.

Her left hand had gone up over her shoulder to Remorse’s grip. She had not sent it. It was there, closed on the new binding, Ghere’s over-and-back, and the sword was a finger’s breadth out of the scabbard.

She did not draw it. She did not put it back. She stood with her hand there.

Farieth looked at the hand. Then she looked at Riarhin’s face, and something in her own face moved, a very little, at one corner of the mouth.

“You can put that away,” she said. “You’ve used it on me once. It did what it did. It won’t do it twice.” She shifted the spear, a hand’s width, no more. “And I’ve not come to fight you. I’ve come to stop you.”

“Stop me doing what?”

“Going down.”

Riarhin waited.

“You’re going down to where the sleeper is,” said Farieth. “I can smell it on you. You’ve had it on you since you came off the river. You and the monk, both. And that.” She looked at the raven on Dusana’s shoulder, which had put its feathers up. “That most of all.”

“I’m going down to mend what’s broken.”

“You’re going down to open what’s shut.” Farieth’s voice was flat and unhurried, the voice that told Riarhin what she already knew. “It doesn’t matter what you call it. Down there, under all this, something is kept. Kept asleep, kept quiet, kept from finishing what it would finish. I knew that before your mother was born. I spent a long life on the edges of it.” She looked at the ribs overhead. “I didn’t finish what I meant to. You saw to that. So I’ll do this instead. You’ll not go down through my rooms.”

“Your rooms.”

“Mine now.” Farieth’s mouth moved again. “You’d be surprised what’s left lying about here for anyone with a purpose to pick up. These were empty. I’ve furnished them.”

“I met my father,” Riarhin said.

She had not meant to say it. It came out of her across the mist and the grey floor, flat, and she heard it land.

Farieth’s face did not change.

“Did you.”

“In a workshop. He came to a hook with his own cord on it. He told me what he’d wanted.”

“It doesn’t matter what he wanted.”

It came across the room the way it had come across a cutting in the Valewood, quick and flat, like something brushed off a table. Riarhin had known it would. She had remembered it every day since. It struck her exactly as hard as it had the first time.

“He wanted the three of you made one,” she said. “The al and the thora and the ar. He said he grew up hearing it. He said you talked of it. The greatness before the division.”

Farieth looked at her for a long moment.

“I did,” she said.

Riarhin had not expected that.

“I did. I talked of it at his cradle, and at his table, and over his head when he was supposed to be asleep.” Farieth’s voice did not soften. It did not do anything. “I was young and I’d read three lines on a wall and thought I’d read the whole of it. I talked of a great many things when I was young that turned out to be about something else. He listened. He always listened, when it suited him. Then he went off into the forest with his women and his songs and made it into something he could sing.” She shifted the spear. “I stopped talking of it a long time before he died. I found out what was underneath. He never did. He went on singing.”

“He thought it was worth it.”

“He thought a great many things.” Farieth looked at her, and for a moment the grey eyes rested on Riarhin’s face with something in them that was not contempt, was not anything Riarhin had a name for. Then it was gone. “He hoped. That was all he ever did. He hoped at things, and left them lying, and somebody else had to pick them up. You’re one of the things he left lying.” She lifted her chin, a fraction. “I’ll not pretend to you it was more than that.”

Riarhin said nothing.

She did not argue. She had thought, once, in the Valewood, that there was something she wanted to ask this woman, and had knelt by her on bloody stone and not known what it was. She did not know now. She was not going to find it by standing here. She was not going to talk Farieth into anything, any more than she could have talked the ribs out of the walls.

“There’s a woman,” she said instead. “Beyond you. In the rooms. She came through looking for her brother.”

“Yes,” said Farieth. “I let her by. She’s nothing to me.”

“Then let us by to fetch her.”

“No.”

“She’s done nothing. She’s looking for a man who doesn’t want her to find him. She’s carrying his boots.”

“Then she’ll carry them on the far side.” Farieth did not even glance behind her. “Go back up your lane, granddaughter. Go up your little stair with the red string on it. I know where it is; I watched the monk tie it. Go back to your well and your doors and your lamp-keeper. I’ll leave the stair open for you. I’ll leave it open as long as you’re on it.” Her eyes went past Riarhin to Cadan, and to Dusana, and the raven. “All of you. Go up. And I’ll shut these behind you and sit in them, and nobody will come down this way again, and whatever’s kept will stay kept.”

“And the woman?”

“Will be on the far side,” said Farieth, “with the rest of what’s down there.”

The room breathed.

Riarhin felt it before she heard it. The mist on the floor stirred, all of it at once, lifting a finger’s breadth and turning and lying down again. Overhead the ribs creaked, three or four of them, from different sides of the room. Then a long low groan, from all round, the beginning of a breath.

She knew that sound. She had heard it come down a throat in the dark with no holding note to stop it.

Behind Farieth, through the doorway, she could see into the next room. It was grey, ribbed, mist on the floor, like all the others. On the far side of it there was another doorway, and through that doorway, a long way off, sitting on the floor in the mist with her back against a rib and one boot held in her lap, was Hedda.

She was not calling any more. She had her head down over the boot.

The groan went on. On either side of Farieth, the ribs that framed the doorway moved. They did not fall. They leaned in towards each other, slowly, the way two people lean together to say something private, and the doorway between them grew narrower by the width of a hand.

Farieth did not look round. She stood in the narrowing doorway with her spear close and low and her bad leg out straight, and the grey ribs came in on either side of her shoulders and stopped, and held.

“There,” she said. “That’s how they close. Now you’ve seen it.”

Through the gap beside her shoulder, a long way off, Hedda lifted her head from the boot and looked towards them. Her face was a pale shape in the grey. Riarhin could not tell whether she could see them.

“Arvo?” said Hedda.

The ribs groaned again. The gap closed by another hand’s width.

Riarhin took her hand off Remorse’s grip. She let the sword settle back into its scabbard, the whole length, and heard it seat. Then she bent, without taking her eyes off Farieth, and undid the knot that held the boot to her pack strap, and took the boot in her hand by its laces.

“Dusana,” she said, not loud. “Cadan.”

“Here,” said Dusana.

“I am here,” said Cadan.

“Find me another way into that room,” said Riarhin. “Not through her.”

Farieth watched her. The corner of her mouth moved.

“There isn’t one,” she said.

Dusana had already turned away. Riarhin heard her go along the wall behind her, quick and quiet, her skirts dragging in the mist, and stop, and go on, counting. She heard Cadan follow. She did not turn to watch them. She stood in the middle of the grey room with the boot hanging from her hand, and her grandmother stood in the closing doorway with the spear, and between them the mist lay on the floor and lifted and lay down, lifted and lay down, like something asleep.

Chapter 24 — The passage they lose

Dusana found it at the foot of the south wall.

Riarhin heard her stop. She heard her kneel, the mist parting round her knees with a sound like a sheet drawn off a bed, and then Dusana’s voice, low and flat:

“Here. There’s a drain.”

“How big?” Riarhin did not turn. She kept her eyes on Farieth.

“Big enough for you, if you take that thing off your back. Not for me with my hips. It goes along under the wall towards the next room. The mist’s running into it.” A pause. “There’s a grating on it. Iron. It lifts.”

“Will it stay lifted?”

“If someone holds it.”

Farieth’s eyes had gone past Riarhin’s shoulder to the south wall. She did not move from the doorway. The ribs were leaning on her shoulders like a yoke.

“That’s the outflow,” said Farieth. “Where the mist goes when the rooms breathe. You’ll drown in it.”

“Mist,” said Riarhin.

“You’ll see,” said Farieth.

Riarhin backed away from her, step by step, until her heel touched Dusana’s skirt. Then she turned.

The drain was a square mouth in the foot of the wall between two ribs, a little wider than her shoulders and not as high as her knee. An iron grating covered it, hinged at the top, rust-brown, heavy. Dusana had both hands under its lower edge and had lifted it the width of a palm, and the mist was going in under it, slowly, in a long grey tongue, the way water goes into a culvert after rain.

Behind the grating there was dark.

Cadan stood beside Dusana. He had put down his lantern and his ropes on the paving, neatly, together, and was looking at the grating with his head on one side.

“It wants to close,” he said.

“It’s heavy,” said Dusana.

“No. It wants to close.” He put his hand out flat towards it, a finger’s breadth away. “Like the doorway. The room is pulling it.”

The groan came again, from all round them. The mist on the floor lifted and turned and lay down and ran faster into the drain. The grating bore down on Dusana’s hands, and she grunted and set her feet.

“Riarhin,” she said.

“I see it.”

“Then be quick.”

Riarhin unbuckled Remorse’s belt and slid the scabbard off her back. She held it a moment. Then she held it out to Cadan.

He looked at it. He did not take it.

“If I am to hold the grating,” he said, “I cannot hold that. It will fall. I may not be able to pick it up again until afterwards.” He said it quite evenly. “Here it goes further than it did above.”

“Then hold the grating,” said Riarhin, and gave the sword to Dusana.

Dusana took it in one hand and kept the other under the iron, and her face did not change, though the grating dipped.

Cadan stepped into the mouth of the drain.

He did it the way he did everything, without hurry. He knelt in the mist and put his shoulder under the grating beside Dusana’s hands, and then he was not quite putting his shoulder under it. Riarhin watched it happen and could not have said when. The grating’s edge rested on the grey of his habit and the habit did not dent. His shoulder was under the iron and the iron was through his shoulder, a little, the way a stick is through the surface of a pond. His face went pale and smooth and still. The knife he kept in his sleeve slid out of it and dropped into the mist without a sound.

The grating stopped bearing down.

It hung where it was, the width of two palms open, and held. Dusana took her hand away, slowly, and it held.

“Go,” said Cadan. His voice came late, a little after his lips had moved, as though from farther off than he was.

Riarhin lay down in the mist and went in under the grating.

It was a square stone channel no wider than her shoulders, dry and smooth, going along under the rooms in the same long curve. The mist ran through it with her, cold in her mouth, tasting of stone dust. She went on her elbows and knees in the dark with the boot tied to her belt, banging against her thigh.

She counted the ribs as she passed under them, a ridge in the channel roof every few feet, the way she would have counted the treads of a stair in the dark, and when she had passed under eleven she knew she was beneath the room where Farieth stood, and when she had passed under nineteen she was beyond it.

The room breathed. The mist slowed round her, stopped, and came on faster, pulling at her clothes. The stone above her creaked. Something shifted in the roof of the channel a little way ahead, a grinding, and a rib came down an inch.

She stopped.

Ghere had made her go under fallen trees on her belly with his supper on her back. Not fast. Not slow. When the tree is still. She waited, flat, with her face in the mist and her breath held, until the groan had finished and the room was still, and the rib had stopped moving.

Then she went under it. Her pack scraped. The boot caught and pulled free.

At the twenty-third rib there was light above her, grey, and another grating, and she put her back against it and pushed, and it lifted, and she came up out of the floor of the far room into the mist like a swimmer coming up through weed.

Hedda was sitting against the wall with the boot in her lap.

She looked at Riarhin coming up out of the floor, and did not seem surprised. She seemed past being surprised.

“He was here,” she said. “I saw him. In the doorway, there. In his brown coat. He looked at me. Then he went through.”

Riarhin looked where she was pointing. There was a doorway on the far side of the room, going on down, into more grey. Nobody was in it.

“I dropped the other one when I ran after him,” Hedda said. “I couldn’t get my hand to the wall.”

Riarhin untied the boot from her belt and held it out.

Hedda took it, and tied the laces of the two together again, carefully, a bow, the way they had been on the boat.

“He saw me,” she said. “He saw me and he went on.”

“Yes.”

“Then he doesn’t want them.”

“The ferryman has the word. That you’ve got them. That they’re mended.” Riarhin crouched in the mist beside her. “He can have that or not. But he can’t have it from here. Not if you’re shut in with it.”

Hedda looked past her, at the doorway where her brother had gone.

Then she looked the other way, towards the doorway they had come through, and Riarhin looked too.

It was barely a doorway now. The ribs had leaned in until there was a gap the width of a hand between them, from floor to ridge, and in the gap, filling it, stood Farieth. She had her back to them. Riarhin could see the grey robe, the spear’s butt resting on the paving beside the bad foot, the straight back. Farieth had not turned round. She did not need to. She must have heard the grating lift.

“You’ll not get her back through that hole,” Farieth said, without turning. “Not before it shuts. Look at her.”

“Hedda,” Riarhin said. “Can you crawl?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then you’ll find out. Give me the boots.”

“No.”

“I’ll carry them. You’ll need your hands.” Riarhin held out her own. “I’ll give them back. On the far side. I’ll put them in your hands myself.”

Hedda held them against her chest for a breath longer. Then she gave them over.

It was slower going back. Riarhin went first, on her elbows, with the boots tied round her neck by their joined laces so that they hung against her chest, and Hedda came behind, and Riarhin could hear her breathing in the dark, short and frightened, and the small sounds she made when her knees struck the stone.

“Count,” Riarhin said. “Count the ridges in the roof. Put your hand up and feel them.”

“Why?”

“So you’ll know where you are.”

Hedda counted. She counted aloud, in a thin voice, and when she reached eight she stopped, and Riarhin heard her stop, and waited.

“Go on,” Riarhin said.

“I don’t—” Hedda’s voice caught. “My mother said—”

“Then start again. One. It’s a new one.”

A breath in the dark.

“One,” said Hedda, and came on.

The room breathed over them. The mist pulled. At the place where the rib had come down an inch it had come down another, and Riarhin had to turn on her side and put her shoulder through first and then drag her hips after, with the stone pressing on her ribs, and reach back for Hedda’s wrist and pull. Hedda came through with a sound like a sob and did not stop counting.

At the twenty-third ridge there was a grey light ahead, a square of it, low, and in it, very faint, a shape kneeling.

Cadan had not moved. He knelt in the mouth of the drain with his shoulder through the iron and his face smooth and pale and his eyes open, and when Riarhin came out under the grating past him, close enough to touch, she put her hand on his arm and her hand went into the grey wool a little, the way a hand goes into cold water, and she took it away.

“Hedda’s behind me,” she said.

He did not answer at once. Then, late: “Yes.”

She turned and reached back in for Hedda’s wrist and pulled her out into the room, and Hedda came out of the drain on her hands and knees in the mist and stayed there, breathing.

“Cadan,” said Riarhin. “Come out.”

He came out. He drew back from the grating the way a man draws back from a window, and the iron came down behind him with a dull clang, solid, and the mist stopped running into it.

He stood up, swaying a little, and bent and felt in the mist for his fallen knife, and when his fingers closed on it he looked at them as though they had done something surprising.

“I am cold,” he said. “I will be cold for a while. I will not hear you well. It is nothing.”

Dusana gave Riarhin back her sword.

The room breathed again, and this time it did not stop.

The groan went on and on, from all round, rising. The ribs creaked. The mist lifted off the floor to Riarhin’s knees and began to flow, all of it, towards the doorway at the far end where Farieth stood, and the walls on either side of the doorway they had come in by, the one that had closed behind them two rooms back, began to move.

“Out,” said Dusana. “Out, now. Back up the lane. Back the way we—”

She was looking at the near wall. Riarhin looked too.

There was a seam in it. A straight line, between two ribs, where there had been no line before, and as Dusana spoke it opened a finger’s width, and light came through it: not grey. Yellow. Lamplight. And beyond the lamplight, just visible, the bottom of a flight of rough steps, worn hollow in the middle, with an iron ring at their foot and a red cord tied to the ring.

“There,” said Dusana. “The lane’s just there, past the fifth stone, and the stair beside the—”

The seam widened. The steps beyond it were going the wrong way. Riarhin saw it with the part of her that saw everything: the treads were worn on the wrong edge. They went down. The red cord hung up from the ring, not down, as though the ring were on the ceiling. And the lamplight was coming from under the steps, from below them, where there should have been rock.

Dusana stopped.

Her hands came up from her sides. They came up towards her face, both of them, the scarred palm and the other, and Riarhin saw them come and saw Dusana’s eyes over them, wide, fixed on the opening seam. Her fingers were a hand’s breadth from her mouth.

They stopped there.

Dusana did not finish the sentence. She stood with her hands raised and her mouth shut, and the seam in the wall stayed open a finger’s width with the wrong lamplight in it, and did not open further. Then, slowly, it began to close.

Dusana lowered her hands. She took hold of Riarhin’s arm, hard, above the elbow, and her other hand took Hedda by the shawl, and she turned them both, bodily, away from the wall, towards the south, towards the drain and past it.

“Not that way,” she said. Her voice was perfectly ordinary. “This way. Come on. Monk.”

“I am here,” said Cadan, late.

Dusana walked them along the south wall, fast, past the grating to the corner of the room, where the paving had cracked away from the wall and there was a gap, and through it grey light and a smell of wet reeds.

“Out,” said Dusana.

They went out.

They came out on a riverbank.

It was a bank of grey shingle below the last of the grey rooms, where they came down to the water. Behind them, when Riarhin turned, the ribbed roofs went up the valley in their long curve, and the last room had a crack at its corner the width of a body, and as she watched, the crack closed. The paving settled against the wall. There was a small dry sound, like a stick rolling.

She looked up the valley, past the roofs, to where the lane came down to the near side.

The grey had grown. Where the lane had met the rooms, and the stair had gone up the bank beside them, there was only grey wall now, ribbed, long, running out to the water’s edge and into it. There was no stair. There was no lane. Half-way up the wall, sticking out from between two ribs like a thread caught in a closed door, there was an inch of red cord, and as she looked at it, it was drawn in, and was gone.

Cadan was looking at the same place. He did not say anything. His lips were pale.

In the last room’s wall, near the water, there was still one gap: a narrow doorway with the ribs leaning in on either side, and in it, with her back to the dark, stood Farieth.

She was looking at them across the shingle. She had the spear grounded beside her bad foot. She did not come out. She did not lift the spear.

Riarhin looked back at her.

Farieth’s mouth moved, a little, at one corner.

“You’ll want to come back up,” she said. Her voice came across the shingle quite clearly. “There’s no other way up I know of. You’ll have to find one I don’t.” She looked at the raven on Dusana’s shoulder, and then at Riarhin. “Go on down, then. See what you open.”

She stepped back. The ribs leaned in. There was no doorway.

There was a post at the water’s edge with a bell on it.

Hedda rang it. She rang it with both hands on the rope, hard, three times, and then stood by the post and held out her arms, and Riarhin untied the boots from round her neck and put them into them.

“There,” Riarhin said.

Hedda held them. She looked out at the black water, where a lamp had begun to grow in the mist.

“He saw me,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I’ll wait at the Reed Landing. He might go back there.” She looked down at the boots. “He might not.”

The ferry came in stern first, low in the water, with its bundles and its chair and its rakes, and the ferryman at the oar looked at Hedda, and at the boots, and nodded, and held out his hand for her. She stepped in and sat on the plank with the boots in her lap.

Then the ferryman looked at the rest of them, standing wet on the shingle, and at the grey wall behind them going down into the river.

“Upstream?” said Riarhin.

“No.”

“The towpath stair’s gone.”

“I see it is.” He looked at the wall a while longer. “I don’t go up. It’s a river.”

“Is there another way up?”

“Not that I take.” He pushed off. The boat slid back off the shingle and turned, so that he faced them all the way out. “There’s ways down,” he called, over the water, not loud. “There’s always ways down. Down’s easy.”

The lamp went into the mist.

Riarhin sat down on the shingle. She had not meant to. Her legs had decided it.

She was wet to the skin, her elbows were raw through her sleeves, and her thigh had locked from knee to hip. A bruise was coming up along her left side where the rib had pressed.

Cadan sat down beside her, carefully, with his arms wrapped round himself. He was shivering. She could hear his teeth.

Dusana did not sit. She stood at the edge of the water with the raven on her shoulder and looked up the river, the way they had come, for a long time. Her hands were at her sides. She rubbed the right palm against her skirt, once, slowly.

“I didn’t finish it,” she said.

Riarhin looked up at her.

“What I was saying. About the stair. I didn’t finish it.” Dusana did not turn round. “Don’t ask me why.”

“All right,” said Riarhin.

Dusana breathed out. It might have been a laugh.

“We need somewhere dry,” she said. “All of us. Him most. Somewhere with a roof and a fire and nobody standing in the door.” She looked down the river, the other way, where the bank went on along the black water into the mist. “There’s places down the long reach. I’ve heard of one. A dry arch, people say. A doorframe with no door.”

Riarhin had heard of it too. She had read it in a letter in a careful small hand, in the Mistlands.

“How far?”

“Far,” said Dusana. “Get up.”

Riarhin got up. It took two tries. Cadan got up after her and picked up his lantern and his ropes, and touched the coil of red cord on his belt, shorter now by an arm’s length.

They went down the bank in the mist, along the edge of the black water, with the grey wall going on beside them and then ending, and the shingle wet under their boots, and nothing behind them.

Part IV — The time they spend

Chapter 25 — No price at the door

The long reach went on beside the black water until Riarhin stopped expecting it to end.

The shingle gave way to mud, the mud to tussocks, the tussocks to a bank of short wet grass that rose away from the river at a slant gentle enough for Cadan to manage without help. He had stopped shivering some time before. That worried her more than the shivering had. When she spoke to him he answered after a pause, the way a man answers from the next room, and twice he walked on after she had stopped.

Dusana went ahead. She had not said how far. The raven rode on her pack with its feathers out and its eyes shut, a dark untidy lump that swayed with her stride.

The mist thinned as they climbed. It did not lift; it lay down. It went into the hollows of the slope and stayed there, so that the grass above it stood clear and green, wet, very green, as if it had been rained on an hour since and the sun had come out without anybody seeing it do so. There was no sun. There was a light that was enough.

Halfway up the slope stood a doorframe.

It was built of grey dressed stone, two uprights and a lintel, a little taller than a man. There was no wall on either side of it and no door in it. The grass ran up to its threshold and round its feet and on up the hill. Under the lintel, on the threshold stone, the ground was dry. Riarhin could see the difference from twenty paces: the pale dry stone, the dark wet turf on either side.

Dusana stopped in front of it. She took eight steps towards it, slowly, and stopped again. She looked at the frame, then at the grass beside it, then at the frame.

“That’ll be it,” she said.

Riarhin walked round it. She had to. The slope behind the frame was the same slope, the same grass, a few sheep-paths and a long view down to the mist lying in the valley like milk in a dish. Nothing else. When she came back round to the front, Dusana was watching her with an expression of patient interest.

“Finished?”

“Yes.”

“Then come through properly.”

Dusana went through. Riarhin followed her, and Cadan came after with the lantern held in front of him in both hands, and on the far side of the threshold there was a house.

It stood a little higher up the slope with its back to the hill, long and low, its roof slates furred with moss. There was a yard before it with a well in the middle, a stone trough, a mounting block with a cat on it. To one side an orchard ran down the slope, old trees, crooked, with grass grown long between them and lines strung from trunk to trunk. Beyond the orchard was a shed with a wide door and a thread of steam coming out under its eaves. Smoke went up from the house chimney, straight, and the yard smelt of it, and of bread.

A woman came out of the house door wiping her hands.

She was broad, in the shoulders and the hips and the face, and she had flour to the elbow and a smear of it on one cheekbone. Her sleeves were pushed up. She had a wooden spoon in her apron pocket with the handle sticking out. She looked them over the way a woman looks at washing that has been brought in from rain: what was wet, what was torn, what could go straight to the fire.

“Boots off at the step,” she said. “You’ll want to sleep. Beds are up the outside stair, the long room over the barn. Take any that hasn’t a shawl on it. If it has a shawl on it, it’s somebody’s.” She looked at Cadan. “He wants the kitchen first.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“He can have the settle by the oven. I’ll find him a blanket.” She looked at Riarhin’s sleeves, which were torn through at both elbows and dark under the tear. “There’s salve on the kitchen shelf. Blue pot. Use what you want and put the lid back, the flies get in it. Well water’s good. The copper in the wash-house is lit for sheets. You can have it after the sheets.” She looked at the orchard. “Apples off any tree except the one by the wall. That’s Hob’s and he counts them.”

Riarhin stood on the wet grass with her hand on the strap of her pack.

“What do we give you?” she said.

“Nothing.”

“For the beds.”

“Nothing for the beds.” The woman took the spoon out of her pocket, looked at it, and put it back. “People ask. I tell them. They ask again in the morning and I tell them again. I’m Wenna. Boots off.”

She went in. The door stayed open behind her.

Dusana sat down on the mounting block, displacing the cat, which went without hurry, and began to unlace her boots. The raven climbed down off her pack onto the block and looked at the well.

“Jelka’s here,” Dusana said.

Riarhin looked at her.

“A friend. She used to sell needles on the road to the winter yards. She’s got a room past the orchard, I’m told.” Dusana pulled one boot off and turned it upside down. A little water ran out. “I’m going to see her. I’ll sleep there.”

“Now?”

“After my feet are dry.” She pulled off the other boot. “Don’t come for me unless something’s on fire.”

“And the bird?”

The raven had gone to the edge of the well and was leaning over it.

“It’s found the well,” said Dusana. “It won’t come now.”

Cadan sat on the settle by the oven with a grey blanket round his shoulders and his habit steaming. Wenna had put a cup of something hot into his hands and told him to hold it, not drink it, until his fingers worked. He held it. He was looking at the oven door.

Riarhin knelt on the flags in front of him and took the lantern out of the crook of his arm. Its little door was fogged. She opened it. The white candle inside was dry and untrimmed, as it had always been.

“I will do that,” Cadan said, a breath late.

“Hold the cup.”

“I would like to do it.”

She put the lantern on the settle beside him. Then she went out to the yard, where he had left his ropes, and brought them in, the old coil and the tarred one, and the knotted interval cord with its iron weight, and the red cord, shorter now. She laid them on the flags by the oven where the heat would reach them. He watched her do it with a slight frown, as though she had put them down in the wrong order.

When his fingers worked he put the cup down and began. He unrolled each coil the length of the kitchen floor, from the oven to the door, and felt along it with his fingers for wet and wear, and coiled it again so that it would lie as it had lain before. He did the tarred rope second. He did the red cord last and slowest. At the end where it had been cut by the grey wall he stopped, and held the end between his finger and thumb, and then he took a knife from Wenna’s table without asking and trimmed it square and whipped it with thread from his sleeve.

He did not say anything about it. Wenna, coming past with a tray of loaves, looked at her knife on the floor beside him and stepped round it.

Riarhin went up the outside stair.

The long room over the barn had a floor of wide boards and a row of beds along each wall, low frames with straw mattresses and folded blankets. Three had shawls on them. One had a pair of shoes under it, side by side, toes to the wall. She chose a bed at the end, under the gable window, with her back to the wall and the door in sight, and she sat on it with her pack between her knees and did not lie down.

The window was open a hand’s breadth. Outside it the orchard went down the slope in the clear grey light, and the long lines strung between the trees were hung with cloth, lengths of it, motionless. There was no wind.

She sat. Nothing came up the stair.

After a while she took off her coat and looked at her arms.

Both elbows were raw through the skin, grazed and grained with grit from the drain. The left was worse. She had a bruise along her left side from the armpit to the hip where the rib in the channel had pressed her, purple at the middle, going green at the edges already, because she healed quickly and her body did not wait to be asked. Her right thigh had set hard from the knee to the hip. When she straightened it, it told her about the shingle and the long reach and every step of the slope.

She went down to the kitchen for the blue pot.

She slept. She had not meant to sleep so long, or so deeply, and when she woke she did not know at first where she was, which had not happened to her in a long time. The light in the window was the same light. There was a weight on the blanket over her feet, and it was the cat.

Somebody downstairs was beating a mattress.

She lay and listened. A door. A voice calling for the long-handled brush. Water drawn at the well, the chain going down, the bucket coming up, the bucket set on the stone. A man’s voice, complaining. A woman’s voice telling him he could complain where she couldn’t hear him. The beating stopped and started again.

None of it was for her.

She got up. The thigh had eased in the night. The elbows had dried under the salve and stuck to the shirt sleeves, and she peeled them free, carefully, and put the coat on over them, and went down the outside stair into the yard.

The raven was in the trough.

It stood in the water up to its belly with its wings half open, and as she watched it dipped its head under and threw the water up over its back with a flick, and shook, and dipped again. The water went everywhere. It went over the edge of the trough and across the flagstones of the yard, and in at the open kitchen door, where somebody had swept, and over the step. The raven ducked and flung. A drop struck Riarhin on the cheek. It shook itself, all its feathers at once, so that for a moment it was twice its size and looked like a black hen that had been put through a hedge backwards, and then it climbed out onto the trough’s rim and stood there, dripping, its feathers sticking out in points, looking at her with one eye.

“Bird,” she said.

It turned its head and looked at her with the other.

Then it hopped down and walked into the kitchen, leaving wet prints.

Wenna was at the table, kneading, with her back to the door. She heard the prints. Riarhin saw her shoulders hear them. She did not turn round.

There was a cloth on the rail by the door. Riarhin took it. She wiped the step, and the threshold, and the flags of the kitchen floor from the door to the place under the table where the raven had stopped to shake itself again, and the leg of the table, and the hem of Wenna’s skirt, which Wenna lifted for her without breaking the rhythm of her kneading. Then she went out and wiped the yard as far as the trough, which was pointless, because the yard was stone and would dry. She did it anyway.

When she brought the cloth back, Wenna took it from her hand, wrung it out over the slop bucket and hung it on the rail.

“It does that,” Wenna said.

“Has it been here before?”

“Not that one.” Wenna went back to her dough. “Ravens do it. They get into anything wet and then they come and sit by the fire and steam.”

The raven had gone to the hearth. It sat on the fender with its feathers fluffed, steaming.

Cadan was not in the kitchen. Wenna said he had gone out to sit on the mounting block where he could see the arch.

“He was sitting there when I came down,” she said. “He’ll be all right.”

“He gets cold.”

“So I saw.” She turned the dough and folded it. “He asked me where the bell was. I said we haven’t one. He said that was restful.”

Riarhin went and stood in the yard.

There was nothing she had to do. She found that out by trying several things. She went to the wash-house, where two girls were feeding sheets through a mangle, and asked whether she could turn it, and the elder said no thank you, she had a way of doing it. She went to the woodpile beside the barn, where a stooped old man in a leather apron was splitting kindling on a block, and asked if he wanted the heavier rounds split, and he looked at her and at the sword on her back and said he didn’t, she’d split them wrong, they were for his own fire and he liked them a particular size. That would be Hob. He had the eye of a man who counts apples.

She sat on the edge of the well.

From there she could see all of it. The girls at the mangle, laughing about something to do with a boy at the far end of the orchard who had tried to carry two buckets on a yoke and gone down the slope faster than he meant to. Hob at his block. A woman shelling beans on the house step into a bowl between her feet, with a basket of empty pods beside her that she would empty and fill and empty. Wenna’s shape moving past the kitchen window. Steam going up from under the eaves of the shed beyond the orchard, slowly, and a smell coming from it on no wind at all, a sour, sharp, fermenting smell, like old bran and something worse. It smelt like the shed below the house at Glenruan where the vat had stood. She let it go by.

Cadan sat on the mounting block with his hands in his sleeves, looking at the arch. The cat sat beside him and looked at it too.

She took out the whetstone. She drew Remorse halfway out of its scabbard over her shoulder and looked at the edge in the grey light and put it back, because there was nothing on the edge to take off, and she had known that before she drew it. She looked at her rebinding on the grip, the over-and-back she had cut on the slant at the end. It was holding. She put the whetstone away.

She took her comb out and combed her hair, which needed it, and put the comb away. Three teeth were missing near the end; she had known that too.

She sat.

The woman on the step finished the beans and began on another basket. Hob stacked his kindling in a shape that pleased him and stood back to see whether it still pleased him. It did. He went inside. The girls took the sheets out of the mangle in a long wet rope and carried it between them to the lines in the orchard, and pegged them out next to the lengths of cloth already hanging there, and stood back and argued about whether a sheet would dry in no wind. One said it would, here. The other said it was a sheet and didn’t know where it was.

Riarhin was bored.

She sat with it. It was a long time since she had been bored. There was a kind of waiting that had always been work, at the side of a road or on a roof or behind a door, and she was good at that, and this was not that. Nobody was coming. There was nothing to watch for. She looked at the yard and the yard went on being a yard, and the beans went into the bowl, and the steam went up from the shed, and her thigh told her, kindly now, that it had been sat on too long.

Then the kitchen door opened and the smell of new bread came out into the yard, all of it at once, warm and brown, and her stomach turned over so hard that she put her hand on it.

She could not remember when she had last eaten. She could, of course. It had been a strip of dried meat on the shingle below the grey rooms, half of Dusana’s, chewed while she walked. Before that, Sarnai’s millet. She had not been hungry since. She was hungry now. It came up through her like water through sand.

Wenna stood in the doorway.

“There’s bread,” she said.

The table in the kitchen was long enough for twenty and had been scrubbed white along its middle by years of it. Wenna put a loaf on it, whole, and a knife beside it, and a dish of soft white cheese with a cloth over it, and a bowl of broth with barley in it and onions, and a dish of apples cut in quarters with the cores out. Then she went back to her dough, because the next batch was rising, and left Riarhin to it.

Riarhin cut the heel off the loaf. The crust broke under the knife with a small crackle. The inside was soft and still warm enough to steam when she pulled it open. She put cheese on it and ate it, and cut another piece, and ate that, and drank the broth from the bowl because the spoon was too slow. The barley was soft and the onions had gone sweet. She ate an apple quarter. It was sharp and very cold, as if it had been in the well.

The raven came down off the fender and stood on the bench beside her and looked at the bread.

She gave it the end of the crust. It took it to the fender and held it under one foot and pulled it to pieces.

Cadan came in from the yard. He sat down opposite her, without being asked, and Wenna put a bowl in front of him without being asked either. He looked at it, and then up at her, with the small courteous inclination of the head he gave to people who had done something for him.

“Thank you,” he said.

“Eat it.”

He ate it with small neat movements, the way he did everything. His hands had stopped being white at the knuckles. He finished the broth and set the spoon in the bowl with its handle towards Wenna, and Wenna took the bowl and filled it again and put it back, and he looked at it with something close to dismay, and ate that too.

Riarhin cut another piece of bread.

Through the window over Wenna’s table she could see down into the orchard. The girls had gone. The sheets hung white and straight on the lines between the trees, and beyond them the other lengths hung, the cloth that had been there when she came. Blue. Several blues, from one so pale it was nearly grey to one as dark as the river. The nearest length hung end-on to the window, and along its edge, where it had been turned and stitched, there was a narrow border in a darker thread. Narrow and dark and very even, the width of a fingernail.

She stopped chewing.

Wenna looked up from the dough, followed her eyes to the window, and looked back.

“That’s the dyers’ line,” she said. “Mind you don’t walk into it in the dark.”

Chapter 26 — Ailsa’s afternoon

“Whose is the cloth?” Riarhin said.

Wenna pressed the heel of her hand into the dough and pushed it away from her.

“The blue? That’ll be Ailsa’s. Some of it’s the boy’s, but you can tell his. His edges wander.” She turned the dough a quarter and pushed again. “She’s had him at the vat since she came. He came first, he was apprenticed to a dyer in the river towns before, and his mistress never got a true blue out of anything in her life, he says. Ailsa says that’s unkind and probably right.” She looked up. “You’ll know her, then.”

Riarhin put the bread down.

“Yes.”

“She’s in the shed. She’ll be cross if you open the door on the vat while it’s working. Knock and wait.” Wenna went back to her kneading. “Take the boy an apple. He never eats unless somebody puts it in his hand.”

The path through the orchard went between the trees on the slant, worn to bare earth in the middle, wet at the edges. Riarhin walked it with an apple quarter in each hand. She did not hurry. She had found that she could not, that her body had decided the pace for her and it was the pace at which you approach something you have been told about and do not yet believe. The sheets hung still on either side. The blue lengths beyond them hung still. She passed the nearest one close enough to touch it and did not.

The shed was built of planks gone silver, with a wide door on the downhill side and a smaller one at the end, half open. The steam came out from under the eaves and from round the edge of the small door. So did the smell, close now, sour and thick and alive, bran and old urine and something green, and she knew it, all of it, and stopped three paces from the door.

Inside, a voice said, “Don’t splash it. You’re putting air in it. The air’s for after.”

The vowels were open. They went wide where another voice would have narrowed them.

“I wasn’t splashing.”

“You were stirring like a man rowing a boat. Gently. Pretend it’s asleep.”

Riarhin stood on the wet path with the apple in her hands.

She had heard something like that voice in the Mistlands, among the whispers that came in off the fog. She had not believed it then. She had been right not to. That had been a voice with nothing round it, a voice in a draught. This one had a boy beside it, being told off.

She knocked on the door frame and waited.

“Who’s that?”

“Riarhin.”

There was nothing for a moment. Then the sound of something put down on wood, and the boy saying “What?” and nobody answering him.

“Come in,” said Ailsa. “Shut the door, the vat doesn’t like a draught.”

It was dim inside and close and warm, warmer than the yard, with steam lying along the roof. A vat was sunk into the floor at the far end, a great barrel let into the earth up to its rim, with a wooden lid half off and the surface of whatever was in it dark and coppery with a scum of blue bubbles at the edges. Planks were laid round it to kneel on. Poles leaned on the wall. Cloth hung over a rail. There was a stool and a crate and a lamp on a shelf, and a boy of about sixteen standing at the vat with a long paddle in his hands and blue to the elbows, his mouth open.

Ailsa was kneeling on the planks at the vat’s edge with her sleeves pushed up.

Her hair was escaping its fastening. There was grey in it, at the front, the strands Riarhin had lifted off her mouth on a pillow in Glenruan. Her hands were blue to the wrist. She was broad in the face again, not drawn, not hollow under the cheekbones, and the colour in her cheeks was the colour of somebody who has been working in a warm shed. She looked up at Riarhin over the rim of the vat.

“Oh,” she said.

Riarhin could not say anything. She had several things. She had made beginnings, once, on the road down to the Bronze Coast. She had nothing now. She stood inside the door with the apple quarters going brown in her hands and her mouth open like the boy’s.

“Ailsa,” she said.

“Yes.”

“You’re here.”

“I live here.” Ailsa sat back on her heels. She looked at Riarhin from her boots to her hair, the torn sleeves, the coat, the sword over her shoulder. Something moved in her face and was set aside, carefully, as you set aside a full cup to deal with later. “Sit on the crate. It’s clean. I can’t stop. This has to come out when it’s ready, not when I am, and it’s nearly ready.”

Riarhin sat on the crate.

“Ned,” said Ailsa. “Shut your mouth. This is Riarhin. Riarhin, this is Ned, who stirs like a man rowing.”

“Hello,” said Ned.

Riarhin held out an apple quarter. He looked at his blue hands and at her, and she leaned over and put it in his mouth. He held it there between his teeth, astonished.

“Wenna sent it,” Riarhin said.

Ned chewed.

She watched them lift the cloth.

They did it with two poles, one each side of the vat, working them down into the dark liquid and under the cloth that lay folded in it, and then up, slowly, Ailsa saying “Slowly, slowly, let it drain, don’t drip on the floor, that’s it,” and the cloth came up out of the vat in a long sagging fold, heavy, streaming.

It was a dirty greenish yellow, the colour at the edge of the bruise along Riarhin’s ribs.

“Now,” said Ailsa. “Hold it. Don’t touch it. Let it have the air.”

They held it between them over the vat, and the drips ran off it back into the dark, and Riarhin watched the yellow go green. It went from the edges inward, where the air reached first, green and then deeper green, and then, at the very edges, as she watched, blue. It came up through the green like colour coming up in a cheek. It spread. The whole length darkened in front of her eyes, unevenly, in patches and drifts, and went on darkening, and the boy made a sound in his throat.

“It’s going,” he said.

“It’s going.”

“It’s going true.”

“Don’t say that yet. Say it when it’s dry.” But Ailsa was smiling. “Over the rail. Gently. Don’t fold it on itself or it’ll go stripy where it touches.”

They hung it over the rail. It went on turning blue, slowly, by itself, while Riarhin watched. Ailsa stood back and wiped her hands on her apron and looked at it with her head on one side, the way she had used to look at a length on the cutting table in the evening light when she was deciding whether to charge more for it.

“Second dip tomorrow,” she said. “Third the day after, if it’ll take it. Ned, put the lid on. Gently.”

She came across the shed.

She did not hurry either. She came across the earth floor with her blue hands held a little away from her apron, and stopped in front of the crate, and Riarhin stood up, because she could not sit. Ailsa had to tip her head back. She was shorter. Riarhin had forgotten nothing about her and had still forgotten that, the angle of it, the way her own neck bent.

Ailsa put her hands up to Riarhin’s face.

They were warm from the vat and damp, and they smelt of it. They went over her cheeks and her jaw and round to the back of her neck, under the hair, and stayed there.

“Oh,” Ailsa said again. “Look at you.”

Riarhin bent her head. She put her forehead down against Ailsa’s, and then that was not enough and she put her arms round her, the whole of her, and held on, and Ailsa held on back, with her blue hands flat against Riarhin’s back between the shoulder and the scabbard. Riarhin could feel the shape of her through the apron. She could feel her breathing. She was crying, and she had not known she was going to, and she did not stop.

Ailsa let her.

She let her for a long time. Ned, behind them, put the lid on the vat very gently, and then stood holding the paddle, and then put the paddle against the wall, and then went and looked at the cloth on the rail with great attention.

At last Ailsa said into her coat, “You smell of drains.”

“One drain.”

“That’s one more than I’d like.” She leaned back to look up at her, without letting go. “And you’re not crying on the cloth. Do it this way, towards the door.”

Riarhin laughed. It came out wet. She turned her face away from the rail, towards the door, as instructed, and Ailsa put her head against her breastbone, and they stood like that.

“You’ve got blue on your face,” Ned said.

They were sitting outside the shed on the bench under the eaves, the three of them, with the steam going up over their heads and the orchard below. Ailsa had sent Ned for more apples and he had come back with six and a heel of bread, which he had not been sent for and had eaten half of on the way.

Riarhin put her hand to her cheek.

“Leave it,” said Ailsa. “It won’t come off for a day or two in any case. I’ve had blue ears since I came.”

“Have you?”

“Look.” She turned her head. There was blue on the rim of her ear, faint, where she had pushed her hair back with a wet hand. “Wenna says I look like a drowned woman. I said that’s a fine thing to say to someone in my position.” She bit an apple. “She laughed for the rest of the morning.”

Ned was looking at Riarhin’s sword, over her shoulder, with the frank interest of a boy who has been dead long enough to stop being shy.

“Is that a real one?”

“Yes.”

“Have you killed people with it?”

“Ned,” said Ailsa.

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

He thought about that. “My mistress’s husband had a sword,” he said. “He kept it over the door. It was rusted into the scabbard. He said it was his grandfather’s and his grandfather had been at a battle. She said his grandfather had sold eels.”

“Which was it?”

“She was usually right.” He finished the bread. “Not about blue, though.”

“No,” said Ailsa. “Not about blue.” She turned to Riarhin. “Her vat was too cold and she fed it too much. She thought a vat was like a husband. You put more in when it sulks.”

“And isn’t it?”

“A vat,” said Ailsa, “is like a vat. You keep it warm, you don’t stir it like a boat, you feed it bran and a little madder and you leave it alone. And you don’t let the air in until you mean to. Ned knows all that. He just doesn’t believe it.”

“I believe it,” said Ned. “I just forget it while I’m doing it.”

Riarhin ate an apple. She sat with her shoulder against Ailsa’s and listened to them argue about the vat, the temperature, the amount of bran, whether you could tell by the smell, and found she knew some of it already, from Glenruan, from the workroom on the Bronze Coast, from years of a woman talking at the edge of her hearing about something that mattered to her. She had attended to more of it than she had thought. She could have joined in. She did not.

Ailsa was not thin. Her wrist on her knee had flesh on it. The knuckles were not big the way they had gone at the end. She had good nails on both hands now, blue to the quick, even on the hand she used to open parcels. Riarhin looked at the nails for a long time.

“Stop it,” said Ailsa, without looking at her.

“Stop what?”

“Counting me.”

Riarhin looked away, at the orchard. After a moment Ailsa’s hand found hers on the bench and held it, blue against her brown, and stayed.

“I was meant to set the second vat this afternoon,” Ailsa said, later. “That’s gone. And tomorrow I’m to walk to the fair at Cold Ford with Ned and Wenna’s sister. There’s a woman there sells madder who swears her red will take on anything, and I’ve said I’ll be the one to tell her it won’t.”

“Go,” said Riarhin.

“I’ll go the day after.” She looked at Ned, who had gone down the path to look at the lines. “He can wait. He’s dead, he has time.”

“So are you.”

“Yes. I have time too.” Ailsa stood up and stretched, with her hands in the small of her back, and winced in a way that had nothing to do with illness and everything to do with kneeling on planks. “Walk down with me. I want to see whether the pale one’s dry.”

They went down through the orchard. The path dipped at the bottom to a stream that crossed under the lowest trees, shallow, over brown stones. There was a plank upstream where it narrowed, three or four paces up, dry. Ailsa did not take it. She went straight through the stream with an annoyed sound, holding her skirt, and stood on the far side with water to her ankles and looked down at her feet.

“I always think it’ll be lower,” she said.

“Yes,” said Riarhin, wading after her. “I was there.”

Ailsa looked up at her. Then she laughed, the whole of her broad mouth, and put her wet hand on Riarhin’s arm, and kept it there while they walked up the far bank to the line.

The pale length was dry. It was the colour of a sky with weather coming, nearly grey, and the border along its edge was in a thread so dark it was almost black. Ailsa took the edge between her finger and thumb and looked at it against the light.

“Ned’s,” she said. “Look at that edge.”

Riarhin looked. The border wandered.

“He’ll do,” Ailsa said. “Another season.” She let the cloth go. “Where have you come from? Not the sword part. I can see the sword part. Where were you?”

Riarhin told her. She told her about the Mistlands, and the line, and Haldren, who had wanted second helpings because his wife always gave him more, and the support that had given while he brought a carrier through. She told her about the Tulkani stone on the heath and the boy who tended the post. She did not tell it well. She left out the order things had happened in and put in a great many boats. Ailsa listened with her arms folded, standing in the orchard grass with her wet feet, and asked whether Haldren’s wife had got him home.

“His body went home with Arngrim.”

“Good,” Ailsa said. “That’s good. She’ll want to have him to shout at.”

Then she asked after her children.

Riarhin gave her what she had. A letter at midwinter in the daughter’s hand, short, a list: the roof was holding, the yard gate had been rehung the right way round, there were three girls in the workroom now and one of them could cut. The boy was on the coast still with the harbour crews and had sent money home and a shell. That was all. It was old.

Ailsa stood with her arms folded and took it, item by item, the way she would take a delivery, turning each piece over for the flaw. When Riarhin got to the girl who could cut, she nodded twice.

“And the bird?” she said.

“The bird?”

“The yellow bird. On the shelf.”

“It was on the shelf. When I left.” Riarhin thought. “It was dusty.”

“It would be.” Ailsa smiled. “My mother used to say I was too fond of pretty things. I had better learn to love something that didn’t deserve it. I thought of her when I saw that bird on the stall.” She turned and began to walk back along the line. “It worked, you see.”

Riarhin stood in the grass.

She remembered the stall. The feet, the unequal blue eyes, Ailsa smiling down at it between their bowls. She had carried it back wrapped in paper. She had asked why Ailsa wanted it. Look at it, Ailsa had said, and Riarhin had looked, and laughed.

“Are you coming?” said Ailsa.

Riarhin caught up.

The light did not change much in that place. It went on being enough. But somewhere a bell was struck in the house, once, a pan lid hit with a spoon, and Ned came running up through the trees and said Wenna wanted to know how many for supper, and Ailsa looked at Riarhin.

“I’ve a room past the shed,” she said. “It’s small. There’s a bed and a table and a stove that smokes when the door’s shut, so I leave the door open. And the window opens.”

Riarhin waited.

Ailsa waited too, with her blue hands on her hips. Then she made an annoyed sound, the same one she had made in the stream.

“Will you stay the evening?”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

“There.” Ailsa turned to Ned. “Tell Wenna two less for supper. Tell her I’ve taken one of hers and I’ll give her back in the morning.” She looked at Riarhin. “That was easier.”

Chapter 27 — A room for the evening

The room was past the shed, at the end of a path through long grass, a single room built against the bank with a turf roof and a door that stood open. Inside there was a bed with a blue cover, a table, two stools, a stove with a crooked pipe, a shelf, a basket of cloth, and more cloth on the bed and on the stools and over the end of the table, folded, waiting. A cup stood on the shelf by itself, a plain brown cup with a chip at the rim.

The window was open. It looked down the slope into the mist.

“Sit,” said Ailsa. “Not there, that’s Ned’s madder. On the bed. Move the grey.”

Riarhin moved the grey and sat on the bed.

Ailsa knelt at the stove and opened it and put in two sticks from the basket beside it, and the stove smoked, and she swore at it in a low steady Ubral that Riarhin had not heard in years, and opened the door wider with her foot. The smoke found its way out. She got up and dusted her knees.

“Coat off,” she said. “Shirt too. Let me see.”

“They’re only grazes.”

“Then it won’t take long.”

Riarhin took off the coat and the shirt. It hurt, where the shirt had dried onto her elbows again. Ailsa stood with her hands on her hips and looked at the elbows, and at the bruise along her side, green and purple, from the armpit to the hip. She did not touch it. She looked at it the way she had looked at the cloth coming out of the vat.

“You did that in your drain.”

“Yes.”

“On purpose?”

“There was a woman on the other side.”

Ailsa nodded, as if that were the answer she had expected and did not care for. She went to the shelf and took down a little pot, not Wenna’s, a squat brown one, and sat on the bed beside her and took her left arm by the wrist and turned it to the light.

The salve smelt of the oil she had used since Ubral. Riarhin breathed it in and shut her eyes.

“Hold still.”

“I am still.”

“You’re still the way a dog is still when it wants to go out.” Ailsa worked the salve into the edge of the graze with her thumb, carefully, round and round, not touching the middle. “There. The other.”

When she had done both elbows she got up and found a needle in the front of her own sleeve, and thread on a card from the basket, and held them out.

“Your sleeves.”

“Now?”

“You’ll want them tomorrow. I’ll do supper.”

Riarhin sewed her sleeves on the edge of the bed in her shift, with the shirt across her knees, while Ailsa cooked.

Ailsa cooked the way she always had, which was talking, and putting things down in places and then looking for them. She had onions from Wenna, and potatoes, and a twist of salt, and three eggs in a bowl, and a piece of bacon fat in a paper that she unwrapped and smelt and approved of. She cut the onions on the end of the table without moving the cloth off the other end. She put the potatoes in a pan of water on the stove and told them to hurry, which they did not. She talked about the fair at Cold Ford, about Wenna’s sister, who had been a carter and drove everybody as if they were horses, about a dead weaver in the next valley who wanted Ned to come to her and learn a twill, and whom Ailsa had told he would come when he could get a blue to stay true, and not before, because a boy who could not finish one thing would not finish two.

Riarhin sewed. Her stitches went in too big and pulled.

Ailsa came and looked over her shoulder.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said.

“I’ve seen,” said Ailsa, and went back to the stove.

The table wobbled when Ailsa leaned on it to slice the bacon fat. She put her hip against it to steady it.

“Ned made that,” she said. “He says it fitted when he made it.”

Riarhin looked up.

Ailsa looked back at her over the onions, and her mouth began to twitch at the corners.

“Tavish,” said Riarhin.

“In the trough.”

“With the board.”

“It fitted when I made it,” said Ailsa, in a deep loud voice that was very like Tavish’s, and Riarhin laughed so suddenly that she drove the needle into her thumb. She could see him. She had attended to that afternoon with every part of her: the man with his sleeves rolled up standing in the empty water trough, holding one end of a board, the shorter broad woman at the other end examining it, the two dogs at a prudent distance. We will agree when you stop standing on it. She had stood at the gate with Na’ar beside her and had not known yet that the loud one was her cousin.

“He never stopped standing on it,” said Ailsa. “Elspeth said he stood on it for a week. She said he was standing on it in his sleep.”

Riarhin sucked her thumb. She was still laughing. It went on longer than the joke deserved, and Ailsa let it, and turned the bacon fat in the pan.

They ate at the table, on the two stools, with the cloth pushed back to make room for two plates. The potatoes were soft at last and broke under the fork, and Ailsa had fried the onions in the bacon fat until they were brown and sweet, and the eggs on top, and there was bread from Wenna’s, and salt in a twist of paper, and water in a jug. She served it. She stood to do it, leaning across the table with the pan in one hand and the spoon in the other, putting the onions on Riarhin’s plate, and the eggs, one and a half, the broken one going on her own, and her hair came loose at the side and fell forward, and she blew it out of her way without stopping.

Riarhin put down her fork.

She stood up. She had got as far as the door, with a hand on the frame, before she knew what she was doing, and then she knew, and she stopped in the doorway with her back to the room and began to laugh again, silently, with her forehead against the wood.

Behind her the spoon stopped scraping the pan.

“Where are you going?” said Ailsa.

“For water.”

“There’s water in the jug.”

“I know.”

There was a pause. Then Ailsa said, in a different voice, “Oh,” and Riarhin heard her put the pan down on the stove.

“Glenruan,” said Ailsa.

Riarhin turned round.

Ailsa was standing at the table with the spoon in her hand. Her face had gone red at the throat, the colour coming up the way the blue had come up through the cloth, and she was looking at Riarhin with an expression of great and delighted outrage.

“The neighbour,” she said. “With the apology. About the hens getting into the beans. You went out for the salt.”

“Yes.”

“We had salt on the table. I asked you after where the salt was and you said—”

“I didn’t say anything.”

“You didn’t say anything for a whole evening. And then you told me. In bed. Like confessing to a theft.” Ailsa put the spoon down. “And I asked you whether next time I might be allowed to finish serving supper.”

“You did.”

“Well,” said Ailsa. She pulled her stool out and sat on it. She folded her hands on the table. “I have served it. You may sit down and eat it. I mean to eat mine. It took a great while for those potatoes.”

Riarhin sat down.

They ate. Ailsa did not look at her for the first several mouthfuls, and then she did, and the red came back up her throat, and she said “Eat your egg,” and Riarhin ate her egg. The onions were very good. The bread had a crust that cracked. She ate all of it and wiped the plate with the last piece, and Ailsa watched her do it with her chin on her hand.

“Tell me about the sword,” said Ailsa.

She had made tea, of a kind, from something dried in a jar on the shelf that she said was Wenna’s and tasted of hay. They had it in the brown cup and a bowl, passing them across the table, because there was only the one cup.

Riarhin told her.

She told it plainly, the way she would have told the route of a road. The workshop under the overhang. Saelith, who had made the block and knotted the cord under it and knew her own knot when she unwrapped it. The stone on the shelf. The bank in the Valewood above the stream, the three stones, the roots, the hand among the wet leaves. That he had been lying there a generation when she found him. That she had taken the sword and the bow and the mail and the cloak off him and left the boots because they were too big, and had not buried him. That he had come to the workshop when he was asked, and had a broken finger badly set, and turned his cup before he drank to look for a chip. That he had known Bea. That he had not known Bea was dead. That he had hoped one of his children would lead the Lethai back into one people again, all three, and that he had carried the sword about with that hope tied to it, and gone and died in the rain.

Ailsa listened. She held the cup in both hands and did not drink from it. When Riarhin stopped, she was quiet for a while.

“Did he ask you?” she said.

“Ask me what?”

“Whether you wanted to lead anybody.”

“He’s dead.”

“Before.”

“No.”

“Hm,” said Ailsa.

She drank some of the tea and made a face at it and passed the cup across.

“Do you want me to say something about it?” she said.

“No.”

“Good. Because I haven’t anything.” She considered. “I’ve the one thing. I never looked at that sword properly in seven years. You never let anyone. You used to put it on the bed as if it were a cat that bit.”

Riarhin looked at her. Then she reached over her shoulder and drew Remorse, slowly, so as not to catch the cloth, and laid it on the table between the plates.

Ailsa looked at it. She did not touch the blade. She bent close to the grip and looked at the binding, the over-and-back, the end cut on the slant, and she put out one blue finger and pressed the binding where it went round the block.

“That’s not your best work,” she said.

“It’s Ghere’s pattern.”

“It’s still not your best work. It’s lumpy there.”

“It’s holding.”

“Lumpy things hold,” said Ailsa. “I’m only saying.” She sat back. “It’s very plain, for a king’s sword.”

“Saelith said it was meant to be plain.”

“Then she did it well.” Ailsa looked at it a while longer. “I’d have put a border on the scabbard.”

Riarhin laughed and put it away.

The quarrel came out of the bow.

Ailsa asked where the rest of it was, the bow and the mail. She asked it lightly, gathering the plates. Riarhin told her: the bow on two pegs over the far end of the cutting table, unstrung; the mail in oiled linen in the chest under the window; the daughter keeping them. She had left them there when she went north under the safe-conduct to Valora.

“And you haven’t been back for them.”

“No.”

“Since the grave.”

“No.”

Ailsa put the plates down in the basin.

“She’s had your bow over her table for how long? Years? She can’t cut at that end without ducking.”

“She said she would keep them.”

“She would. She’d keep a dead horse in the workroom if you asked her nicely and never said a word. That’s why you oughtn’t to have asked her.” Ailsa turned round. “Have you written to her?”

“At midwinter.”

“Before that.”

Riarhin did not answer.

“Write,” said Ailsa.

“I’m not going back there.”

“I didn’t say go. I said write.”

“There was a woman,” said Riarhin. “At the grave. The morning after. She’d waited seven years for you to die so she could start on me.” She heard her voice get harder and could not stop it. “I wasn’t going to bring her to the door. I went away so she’d follow me and not come to the house.”

“And you decided that.”

“Yes.”

“For my daughter. For the boy. For their father. You decided it on the hill with your sword out and then you went.” Ailsa’s face had gone still. “And they woke up and you were gone, and that was your kindness.”

“It was safer.”

“It was yours,” said Ailsa. “It wasn’t theirs.”

Riarhin stood up. The stool scraped.

“You did the same,” she said. “You sent me out for fruit. You and the physician. You told him not to make me decide for you. I came back and you were talking about the window.”

Ailsa looked at her.

“Yes,” she said.

“I’d have—”

“You’d have decided,” said Ailsa. “I watched you decide for people for seven years. Kindly. The best way. Always the best way, and nobody could say no to it, because it was so kind and so useful.” She put her hands flat on the table. “I wanted one thing to be mine. Only the one. And it was going to be mine whatever you did. I wanted to do it without you carrying it.”

“It was mine too.”

“Yes,” said Ailsa. “I know it was. That’s why I sent you for plums.”

Riarhin went to the window. She stood at it with her hands on the sill. Outside, the slope went down into the mist. There was no wind. She put her hand on the casement.

“Don’t shut it,” said Ailsa behind her.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You’d begun thinking about it.”

Riarhin let go of the casement. She stood with her hands on the sill and her back to the room, and after a while she heard Ailsa come across the floor, and felt her stop behind her, not touching.

“You brought the dark ones,” Ailsa said. “The plums.”

“They were the ones you liked.”

“In the illness. Not before. Before, I liked the yellow.” A breath, that might have been a laugh. “You never noticed. All that noticing and you never once noticed I’d changed plums.”

Riarhin turned round.

Ailsa was standing very close, looking up. Her eyes were wet. She did not wipe them. Riarhin put her hands on Ailsa’s face, both of them, the way Ailsa had done in the shed, and Ailsa leaned into them.

“I didn’t want you to come back to the house and find me gone either,” Ailsa said. “That’s the same thing, isn’t it. I do know that.”

Later, in the bed, with the blue cover pushed down because the stove had warmed the room, Ailsa lay with her head on Riarhin’s shoulder and her hair across both of them.

“You could stay,” she said.

Riarhin lay still.

“I’m not asking you to,” said Ailsa. “I’m telling you there’s room. Wenna would find you work. She found Ned work. There’s the vat. There’s the fair. There’s Hob, who wants somebody to tell him his kindling is the right size. You could do that for a hundred years.” She moved her head. “That’s all. You’d asked me once what I wanted. I’m telling you what there is.”

Riarhin looked at the roof. There were turfs, and a beam with a crack along it, and a spider’s thread from the beam to the window. She could stay. She had a body, and the body could live here; she did not know how she knew that, but she knew it. She could sleep in this bed and wake to the window open and walk down to the vat in the morning and lift cloth out of the dark and watch it go blue. She could do it again the next morning. She could learn a twill. The thought sat in her chest, warm, heavy, real, and she did not push it away, and it did not go.

“I know,” she said.

Ailsa waited.

“I’m going after breakfast,” Riarhin said. “Tomorrow. That’s when.”

It was very quiet in the room. Outside, somewhere in the orchard, something small moved in the grass.

“Thank you,” said Ailsa. “For telling me.”

She combed Ailsa’s hair by the light from the stove door, sitting on the edge of the bed with Ailsa on the floor between her knees, leaning back against her shins.

It was her own comb, from the pack. It had three teeth missing near the end, and the gap caught every tangle and held it, and Ailsa swore at it in Ubral each time, softly, steadily, and Riarhin worked the tangle loose with her fingers and went on. The hair was brown and grey and thick and smelt of warm cloth and the oil. It took a long time. She did not hurry. She began at the ends, the way you must, and worked up, and each time the comb went through clean from the root she felt Ailsa’s shoulders go down a little more.

“You’ve lost teeth,” said Ailsa.

“Three.”

“You should get another.”

“This one’s mine.”

Ailsa tipped her head back against Riarhin’s knee and looked up at her upside down.

“The second dip’s at first light,” she said. “Before breakfast. Ned won’t be up. You can hold the other pole.”

“I can do that.”

“Don’t stir it like a boat.”

“I won’t.”

“And then breakfast at Wenna’s. She makes a thing with oats and cream if there are people she likes.” Ailsa shut her eyes. “There’ll be people she likes. Go on. You’ve missed a bit at the back.”

Riarhin found the bit at the back and combed it.

They slept with the window open. Some time in the night Riarhin woke with Ailsa’s foot pressed against her calf, cold. She moved her leg. Ailsa followed it without waking and found the warm place again, and stayed.

Chapter 28 — Leaving after breakfast

The vat was warm under its lid when they came down to it, and the shed was full of the same close green smell, and the light coming in round the door was the first light of a place that did not have a sun to bring it.

Ailsa knelt on the planks and took the lid off. She looked at the surface. There was a skin of copper on it, and the blue bubbles at the edges had drawn together into a ring, and she put her nose almost to it and breathed in, and nodded.

“It’s ready. Take the other pole.”

Riarhin took it. She knelt on the far side of the vat, opposite, and they put the poles down into the dark together, slowly, feeling for the cloth that Ned had lowered in the evening before. It was heavy. It was heavier than it looked, the way wet cloth always is, and the liquid was warm against her wrists and smelt like the inside of a stable.

“Under,” said Ailsa. “Find the fold. There. Now up. Slowly.”

They brought it up. It came out of the dark streaming, yellow-green, and they held it over the vat between them and let it drain, and watched.

It went faster than yesterday. The green came at once, and then the blue behind it, deeper than the first dip had gone, coming up through the cloth from the edges inwards in long uneven drifts like weather moving over a hill. Riarhin held her pole steady and did not move. Across the vat Ailsa was watching the cloth with her lips a little apart, the way she had watched it the day before, and the day before that, Riarhin supposed, and on all the days in this shed before Riarhin had come.

“Second dip’s always better,” Ailsa said. “The first is only telling it what it’s going to be.”

They hung it over the rail beside the first length. Ailsa stood back and wiped her hands and looked at the two of them side by side, the pale and the deeper, and was pleased, and did not say so.

“Ned’s going to be furious,” she said.

“Why?”

“He wanted to see the second one come up. He asked me to wake him.” She put the lid back on the vat, gently. “I didn’t.”

Riarhin packed in the long room over the barn.

There was not much. She laid it all out on the bed first, because she always did: the spare linen, Asta’s, folded small; the whetstone in its cloth; the oilcloth; the tinderbox; the rope, which Cadan had coiled for her at some point without being asked and which was dry now; the water bottle; the last of the dried meat in its paper; the comb. She put the linen in, and the oilcloth round it. She put the whetstone in. She picked up the comb and looked at it, the gap where the three teeth had gone, and there was a long fine hair caught in the gap, brown, with grey along its length.

She did not take it out.

She put the comb in the pack, and then she took it out again and held it, and sat down on the bed with it in her hands.

The window over the bed was open its hand’s breadth. Down through the orchard she could see the shed and the steam coming up from its eaves. She could see the lines between the trees with the sheets on them, and the blue lengths, and a small figure moving along them that would be Ned, up now, going from length to length with his hands behind his back as if he were inspecting troops. Beyond the shed, the path through the long grass. Beyond that, the turf roof.

She could put the pack under the bed. She could go down the stair and tell Cadan to go on with Dusana as far as the far marker and wait there, and then tell him not to wait. He would do it. He would do exactly that. She could go down to the shed and take the other pole for the third dip tomorrow, and walk to Cold Ford the day after behind Wenna’s sister, who drove people as if they were horses. She sat on the bed with the comb in her hands and wanted it, all of it, the vat and the fair and the cold foot, and the wanting did not argue with her. It sat beside her on the bed.

Somebody came up the outside stair. It was not Cadan. Cadan made no noise on stairs.

Dusana came in, and looked at her, and sat down on the next bed with her hands on her knees.

“You’ve got blue on your face,” she said.

“It doesn’t come off.”

“So I heard. Wenna’s sister told Jelka, and Jelka told me before I’d got my boots on.” Dusana looked at the pack, and at the things on the blanket, and at the comb. “Going, then.”

“After breakfast.”

Dusana nodded. The raven was not with her. Riarhin realised she had not seen it since the evening before, when it had been asleep on the fender, steaming.

“Where’s the bird?”

“In Wenna’s kitchen. Waiting for cream.” Dusana rubbed her thumb along the side of her second finger, slowly. “I’ll say one thing and then I’ll not say it again.”

Riarhin waited.

“I was glad for you,” said Dusana. “When Jelka told me. I sat in her doorway and I was glad, and then I was other things as well.” She looked at the floor between the beds. “They’re mine. I’ll see to them. I only didn’t want you to think I hadn’t got them.”

Riarhin looked at her. She could think of nothing that would not be a lie or a kindness, and Dusana would know either.

“All right,” she said.

“Don’t you all-right me.” Dusana looked up. Her silver tooth showed. “That’s mine. I had it first.” She stood up and brushed her skirt down. “Breakfast. Wenna’s making a thing with oats.”

“With cream.”

“If there are people she likes.” Dusana stopped at the door. “There are.”

Riarhin put the comb in the pack and tied it shut.

There were more people at the long table than there had been beds in the long room. Riarhin did not know where they had all slept.
