The girls from the mangle were there, and the woman who shelled beans, and Hob at the end with his elbows out, and Wenna’s sister, who was square and red-faced and sat with her feet planted wide and told everyone where to pass the jug. Cadan sat halfway down with a bowl in front of him. He looked up when Riarhin came in and inclined his head, and when she sat down beside him he said, at once and at the proper time, “Good morning,” and she knew he could hear again.

The oats had been soaked overnight and cooked slowly and there was cream on them, thick, yellow, poured from a jug that went up and down the table and came back empty. There was honey in a pot with a wooden spoon standing in it. There was bread. The raven had got onto the end of the bench by Wenna’s place and was looking at the cream jug with one eye and then the other, and Wenna was ignoring it with an expression of great firmness, and when the jug came back to her she poured a little cream into a saucer and put the saucer on the floor without looking down.

Ailsa came in late, with blue hands and Ned behind her. Ned was sulking.

“She didn’t wake me,” he said to the table at large.

“He was asleep,” said Ailsa.

“That’s why you were meant to wake me.”

“It came up beautifully,” said Ailsa, sitting down opposite Riarhin. “Ask her.”

Ned looked at Riarhin with deep reproach.

“It did,” said Riarhin.

He sat down and ate his oats with his shoulders up, and Wenna’s sister told him to sit straight or he’d grow like that, and he said he was dead, he wasn’t going to grow any way at all, and she said that was what he thought.

Riarhin ate. The oats were soft and sweet and the cream ran into the hollow where the spoon had been. She ate slowly. She looked across the table at Ailsa, who was eating too, and talking to the woman who shelled beans about a skirt, and who did not look back at her very often, and when she did, did not look away.

When the bowls had been pushed back, Riarhin asked Wenna about the road.

“From the arch it goes along the ridge,” Wenna said. She was gathering spoons. “You can see it from the threshold if you stand right. It goes along the top till it comes to the far marker. That’s Sefe’s. There’s a stop about halfway, where people rest, if they’ve a mind to. I don’t.”

“Why?”

“I don’t like the light there.” Wenna put the spoons in a bowl. “Past Sefe’s it isn’t made. Not since the summer. Sefe makes it when somebody needs it. She’ll give you its word, if you ask her and she likes the look of you.” She looked at Riarhin, then at Cadan, then at Dusana. “She’ll like him. She likes people who don’t talk.”

“What word?”

“The road’s. She’ll tell you. I’ll not.” Wenna sat down on the bench, which she had not done the whole of breakfast. “But I’ll tell you how to stand when you say it, so you’ll know she’s given you the right one and not some other. You say it going out, not coming in. On the last step before the stone. With your hand flat on the top of it, so.” She laid her floury palm flat on the table. “And you say it on the breath going out of you. Not the breath coming in. Sefe’s fussy about that.”

“Going out,” said Riarhin. “The last step. Hand flat. On the out-breath.”

Wenna looked at her for a moment.

“You’ve a good memory.”

“Yes.”

“Then you’ll not need telling twice.” She got up. “What else?”

Riarhin hesitated.

“Have you a snakeskin?”

Wenna did not seem surprised. She went to a dresser at the end of the kitchen and opened the bottom cupboard and took out a wooden box, and set it on the table in front of Riarhin, and opened it.

It was full of things. Buttons. A child’s shoe, red, the strap mended with string. A clay pipe with the stem snapped. A key too big for any door in the house. A thimble. A folded paper with nothing written on it. And at the bottom, coiled in a twist of brown paper, the colour of a fingernail, dry and light and almost transparent, a cast skin.

“People leave things,” said Wenna. “Somebody left that a while back. Said someone would come and ask for it, with a sword on her back, and when they did I was to hang it out on the arch.”

“Who left it?”

“A small brown woman with a scar on her arm. Talked like a northerner.” Wenna looked at the skin. “I said what for. She said she’d see it.”

“Will you hang it today?”

“On the arch. Today.” Wenna put the box back in the cupboard, all but the skin, which she laid on the dresser shelf beside the salt. “Is that all?”

“If she comes,” said Riarhin, “tell her we’ve gone to the far marker and we’ll come back to the arch.”

“I’ll tell her.”

Ailsa walked down with them to the arch.

She had brought bread. Wenna had given it to her in the kitchen, two loaves wrapped in a cloth, and cheese in a paper, and Ailsa had added apples from the basket by the door, and Hob had watched the apples go into the cloth from the end of the bench and said, “Not off my tree.” Ailsa had said they were off the tree by the gate and he could count them if he liked. He had looked as if he might.

Now she walked beside Riarhin down the slope with the bundle in her arms, and gave it to her at the threshold.

“Which way do you go?” she said.

“Along the ridge. To the far marker.”

“Is there water?”

“I don’t know.”

“Where will you sleep tonight?”

“There’s a stop, Wenna says. Halfway.”

“And after the marker?”

Riarhin looked out through the arch. The ridge was there, going away along the top of the hill in the grey light, a pale line of road on the green. Beyond it the mist lay in the valleys. She could not see the end of it.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Ailsa looked at her.

“That’s not much of an answer.”

“No.”

“Hm,” said Ailsa. She tucked the end of the cloth in round the bread so it would not come undone. “There’s two loaves. Make the monk eat one. He eats like a sparrow pretending to be polite.”

Dusana, waiting on the grass a little way off with the raven on her shoulder, laughed out loud. Cadan, beside her, looked at Ailsa with interest.

Riarhin put the bundle under her arm. Then she put her other hand to the lapel of her coat, where she had pushed Ailsa’s needle through the cloth the night before after the sleeves were done, and drew it out, with its tail of thread. She took Ailsa’s wrist, and turned it, and pushed the needle carefully into the front of Ailsa’s sleeve, where she always kept it.

Ailsa looked down at it.

“There,” said Riarhin.

Ailsa put her hand flat on the sleeve over the needle.

Riarhin bent and kissed her. Ailsa’s mouth was warm and tasted of honey from the breakfast and she put her blue hand up to Riarhin’s face and held it there, and Riarhin did not want to straighten up, and did, after a while.

“Go on, then,” said Ailsa.

She did not say anything else. She stood a moment on the dry stone of the threshold. Then she turned round and went back up the slope towards the house, not quickly, with her hand still on her sleeve. Halfway up she stopped and called something down into the orchard, where Ned was, about the line, about not touching the deep one, and Ned called back that he wasn’t touching it, and she went on up past the house and along the path to the shed, and the shed door opened and closed behind her.

The steam went on going up from under its eaves.

Riarhin stood in the arch.

For the length of a breath there was someone on the ridge road, at the place where the grass gave way to the pale line of the road. A figure in a travelling coat, standing with its back to her, the way a guide stands who has gone a little ahead and is waiting for you to see which way they mean. She could not have said who. It had the shape of a great many people. Then she blinked, and it was a flat grey stone set into the turf where the road began, and nobody.

“Riarhin?” said Dusana.

She stepped through.

The wind met her on the other side. It came along the ridge out of the grey, cold, smelling of wet stone and distance, and it went through her coat at the mended elbows and lifted the hair off her neck. Behind her the threshold stone was dry. In front of her the turf was wet. Her boot left a dark print on the pale stone as she stepped off it.

When she looked back through the frame, the house was there, and the yard, and the smoke from the chimney going up straight, and the cat on the mounting block, and Wenna in the doorway with something in her hand, coming out into the yard towards the arch, slow, unrolling it as she came.

Cadan shifted the ropes on his shoulder.

“Which way?” he said.

Dusana pointed along the ridge. The raven went off her shoulder and flew a little way ahead and landed on the flat stone at the road’s beginning, and looked back at them, and waited.

They went.

Chapter 29 — Two perfect fathers

The road along the ridge was old and well made, two cart-widths of packed grey gravel with a stone kerb on the uphill side, and the wind came along it the whole way. It did not gust. It blew steadily from in front, cold, the same strength at every step, as though somewhere ahead a door had been left open and nobody had gone back to shut it.

They walked into it with their heads down. The raven went ahead in short flights and landed on the kerb and waited and went on, its feathers blown up the wrong way. After a while it gave up and rode on Dusana’s pack.

At the first place where the kerb was broken down and there were stones to sit on, Riarhin unwrapped Ailsa’s bundle and broke one of the loaves in three and gave Cadan the largest piece.

He looked at it.

“She said you were to eat one,” Riarhin said.

“A whole loaf?”

“She said you eat like a sparrow pretending to be polite.”

Cadan considered this. Then he ate the bread, all of it, slowly, looking out over the valley, and when he had finished he brushed the crumbs from his habit into his palm and gave them to the raven.

“I liked her,” he said.

Dusana, chewing, nodded.

The stop was a low roof over the road.

Riarhin saw it from a long way off, in the grey: a long dark shape across the ridge, and under it, small and yellow and steady, light. As they came closer the shape became a roof of old timbers and slates on stone pillars, built straight across the road from kerb to kerb, so that the road ran under it like a lane under a barn. There were benches along both sides under the roof, and a stone hut at the uphill end with its door open and smoke coming from its chimney, and everywhere, on the benches and on ledges along the pillars and in niches in the hut wall, candles.

They were tallow. She could smell them before she could see them properly, the warm greasy animal smell of cheap candles, and under it something sweeter, honey or fat. The light they gave was yellow and close. It did not flicker in the wind. The wind stopped at the edge of the roof as if it had come to a wall, and inside, the air was still and warm and yellow and smelt of tallow, and there were people sitting on the benches.

Riarhin stopped outside the edge of the roof.

There were perhaps a dozen of them. They sat with their backs against the pillars or the hut wall, travellers, dead, by their clothes from a dozen different places: a carter in a smock, a woman with a basket, an old man with a stick across his knees, two young men side by side. Each had a candle burning on the bench beside them or on the ground at their feet. They were looking at the candles. Their faces were warm in the light and very peaceful, and some of their lips were moving, and the old man was smiling.

“Wenna doesn’t like the light,” said Dusana quietly.

“No.”

“Neither do I.” Dusana did not go any closer. The raven on her pack had pushed its head down into its shoulders. “Don’t take anything from his hand.”

“Whose hand?”

Dusana did not answer. A man had come out of the hut.

He was plump and soft-moving and pale, very pale, with a round smooth face that the candlelight made pleasant. He wore a leather apron over a brown coat, and the apron was spotted all down its front with old white drips of tallow, and his hands were very smooth, the fingers shiny, as if they had been dipped in wax so many times the wax had become skin. He had a bundle of new candles hanging from one hand by their joined wicks, like a bunch of white carrots. He smiled at them.

“Come in out of the wind,” he said. “Sit. Rest your feet. It’s a long ridge.”

“We’re going on,” said Riarhin.

“Of course you are. Everybody does.” He gestured with the candles at the road under the roof, which ran straight through between the benches and out the other side into the grey. “You’ll go through. It’s the road. Sit first, though. I don’t charge for the benches. I pay for your rest.”

“Pay how?”

He smiled more widely. He had small even teeth.

“In what I have.”

Riarhin looked at the road under the roof. There was no way round. The kerb on the uphill side ran into the hut wall; on the downhill side the ground fell away steep and loose to the mist. The road went through the stop or it did not go.

“We’ll walk through,” she said. “We don’t need to sit.”

“As you like.” He stepped aside to let them pass, courteously, and held the candles out of their way. “That’s a Lethai sword.”

She had taken a step under the roof. She stopped.

“Old work,” he said. He was looking at the grip over her shoulder with a mild, interested face. “Al work, by the guard. Plain. Somebody’s father carried that, I should think.” He looked at her face, and his own face became sympathetic. “Oh. Yours. Did you know him?”

“I met him.”

“After.” He nodded slowly. “That’s the hard way round to meet a father. You never had the before.” He turned one of the candles in his fingers. “I can give you some before. If you’d like it. Only for the rest of your feet. It won’t cost you a thing.”

“No,” said Dusana, behind her.

Riarhin did not turn round.

The Tallow Man was holding out a candle. It was white, clean, new. He had not lit it. He was only holding it, not quite towards her, at the level of her hand, the way you hold out a cup to someone who has not yet said whether they are thirsty.

“You were seven,” he said gently. “Weren’t you. When he came to the room.”

She did not know how he knew. She thought afterwards that he did not know; that he had said seven because people who have lost a father before they knew him were often about seven, and her face had told him the rest. At the time she only heard the number go into her like a hook into a fish.

He lit the candle from one on the pillar and set it on the bench beside her.

She looked at it.

The shutter was the one with the good side and the bad side. The bad side let the street lamp in at the corner in a thin bar across the floor. She knew the bar. It lay across the boards to the foot of the bed and up over the blanket, and she was under the blanket with her knees drawn up, and she was not asleep.

The bolt on the door was polished on the side where a hand pushed it. Bea’s shoe was under the bed, one of them, the other somewhere else. The room smelt of Bea’s scent and of the oil in the lamp on the table, and of the street.

The door opened. Bea’s voice, low, saying she’s asleep, and a man’s voice answering, lower, and the sound of a cup set down on the table, and Bea’s small complaint about the mark it would leave on the wood.

She did not pretend to sleep.

She turned over under the blanket and looked.

He was standing by the table in the lamplight with his back half to her, and then he turned, because he had heard her move, and she saw his face. The long Lethai face, not old. The dark quick eyes that went everywhere and then came to her and stopped. She knew it. She had seen it in a workshop under an overhang, looking at her across a forge, and here it was in the lamplight in Silkstrand, younger, the same, bending down towards the bed. He crouched by the bed. He smiled at her.

“Hello,” he said.

He held out his hand, closed, and opened it. In his palm lay two little wooden fish. Pear wood. The scales cut as small crescents, the eyes drilled. She could smell them, faintly sweet.

He took her hand from under the blanket and laid the fish in it, and closed her fingers over them with his own fingers, and kept his hand round hers.

“These are for you,” he said. “I made them.” He said her name. He said it the way nobody had ever said it, carefully, as if it were the name of something he had carried a long way. “I’ll come back. I’ll always come back. You’ll grow up, and one day my sword will come to you, and it will know you. And your brothers and your sisters will find you, every one of them, and you’ll all sit down together at one table. You’ll see.” He bent and kissed her forehead. “You’ll see. It will be good.”

And she saw.

She saw it as you see the end of a road from the top of a hill. A long table under green leaves, and at it, turning to her as she came in, glad, all of them: Zemia with her lists, Linias ducking under the beam, Coryd at the end with her back to the wall, Harissa with her bad leg tucked under her chair, laughing, lifting her cup. All of them glad to see her. All of them come because he had said they would.

The fish were warm in her hand. His hand was warm round hers.

She had wanted this. She had not known how much. It went into the place where the not-looking had always been, the place under the blanket where a child had been too frightened to turn over, and it fitted. It fitted exactly. It was the right shape. The bar of lamplight, the shoe, the cup set down, the complaint. All of it hers, all of it right, and his face in the middle of it at last.

She looked down at his hand round hers.

The fingers were long and brown. He was holding her small hand closed round the fish with his right hand, and the fingers lay along the back of hers, straight. All of them. The first, the second, the third. Straight and even, like the fingers of a man who has never broken one.

Saelith had told her which finger, at the bench, with the stone still warm on its shelf: the second of the right hand, broken once and badly set. And in the workshop he had picked up his cup with that hand and turned it, looking for a chip, and the second finger had stood out from the others at its wrong angle, crooked, like a twig that has healed after a fall. She had watched it. She had attended to nothing else for the length of that turn.

This hand was straight.

“That one’s too sweet for you,” said the Tallow Man. “I can see. Here.”

She was sitting on the bench. She did not remember sitting. The white candle was burning beside her with a little well of clear grease round its wick. The Tallow Man was lighting another from it, a yellow one, stubby, that smoked.

“This is the other way it went,” he said.

She smelt the smoke and the room came back.

The same room. The bar of lamplight across the floor. But she was pretending, this time; she lay under the blanket with her eyes shut and her breath slow, and she listened. She could hear it all. The cup set down. Bea not complaining.

“She’s getting tall,” his voice said. It was cool. “Keep her hungry. Don’t teach her the court. Teach her to want out of it.”

“And then?” Bea, tired.

“Then she’ll go to the forest of her own accord. They always do, the ones with the gift. She’ll go in angry and she’ll go straight, and I’ll have left her something there to find.” The chink of coins on the table, counted out. “For the winter. You’ll want to sell the fish. Sell them. She won’t miss them.”

“And the others?”

“The others are seen to. Every one. When the time comes they’ll go to her whether they like it or not. They won’t be able to help it. I’ve made sure of that.” The scrape of the chair. “She’ll do exactly what she was made to do. They all will.”

And she saw the table again, the same long table under the green leaves, the same faces turned towards her. Not glad, this time. Waiting. Zemia, Linias, Coryd, Harissa in her place with her leg folded under her, still, attentive, every one of them sitting where they had been put.

That one was easier to bear, in its way. If it were true, then nothing she had done was hers. Not the forest, not the sword, not the anger. She could put all of it down at his door and walk away from it. She could feel how light that would be.

She looked at the table.

Harissa was sitting in her place.

Harissa was sitting at a table she had been put at, with her leg folded under the chair where there was no room for it, and she was not saying anything. She was not refusing. She was not sending back her key to the sabre cupboard because it made her listen to the door. She was not saying It’s the turn before you stab somebody across a courtyard with her arms folded, while the children laughed. She was not refusing to travel with Zemia. She was sitting there because she had been told to, in the bright one and in the cold one alike, and smiling in the one and waiting in the other, and in neither of them was there anywhere for her to say no.

Riarhin had stood in that courtyard. She had heard the no. She had been angry about it for a week.

She opened her eyes.

The two candles were burning on the bench beside her, the white and the yellow. The Tallow Man was sitting on the bench opposite with his smooth hands folded on his apron, watching her with his pleasant face.

“Keep whichever you like,” he said. “Keep both. People do. They don’t fight, you’ll find. They lie down together quite comfortably.” He leaned forward. “You’ll want one at Sefe’s marker. She’ll ask you who sends you down her road. They all have to say. And you’ll know, then. You’ll remember him sending you. You’ll be able to tell her so, and she’ll hear that it’s true, because it’ll be true for you.” He smiled. “I’ll walk with you as far as that, if you like. I’ve not been along to the marker in a long time. She doesn’t let me by on my own.”

Riarhin looked at him.

Then she reached out and pinched the white candle out between her finger and thumb. It hissed. The grease was hot and stuck to her skin. She pinched the yellow one out after it.

“That isn’t mine,” she said.

“It’s paid,” said the Tallow Man, mildly. “You can’t hand back what’s paid. You had it.”

“I know I had it.”

She picked up the two stubs. They were warm and soft. She stood up, and her thigh had set on the bench, and she put her weight on it anyway, and walked across the road under the roof to the hut wall where his candles stood in their niches, and put the two stubs down in an empty niche, side by side.

“Keep them for the next one,” she said.

The Tallow Man looked at the stubs. He did not seem angry. He seemed, if anything, a little sorry for her, the way a man is sorry for someone who has refused a coat in the rain.

“As you like,” he said. “You’ll still have them, you know. You’re that sort. I can see it. You’ll have them as long as you have anything.”

“Yes,” said Riarhin.

She went out from under the roof the far side, into the wind.

Dusana and Cadan were there. They had not come through under the roof. They had gone round, Riarhin saw, down the steep loose slope below the kerb and along it and up again, and Cadan’s habit was grey with dust to the knees, and Dusana had a scrape on the back of one hand and her skirt knotted up. The raven was on the kerb, facing into the wind with its eyes shut.

“There was a way round,” Riarhin said.

“There was a goat-track,” said Dusana. “I wasn’t going to tell you about it in front of him.” She looked at Riarhin’s face. “Well?”

“He gave me two.”

“Two what?”

“My father.”

Dusana was quiet. Cadan, beside her, put down the ropes.

“One of them had his hand wrong.” She looked at her fingers. There was tallow on the tip of the finger and the thumb, gone white and hard. She scraped it off with her nail.

“And the other?”

“My sister said no to me once. About a dance.” Riarhin wiped her hand on her coat. “He didn’t have that.”

Dusana looked at her a long moment. Then she untied her skirt and let it fall and shook the dust out of it.

“Do you still have them?”

“Yes.”

She did. She would have them as long as she had anything. She could see his face now, bending over the bed in the lamplight in Silkstrand, younger, smiling, and the bar of light across the blanket, and his hand round hers with all its fingers straight. She would be able to see it whenever she liked. She would be able to see it when she did not like. She knew which it was. She would have to know which it was every time, the way you have to step over the same loose board on a stair every time you go down it in the dark, and you do not stop knowing it is there.

“All right,” said Dusana.

Cadan picked up the ropes. He looked back once at the long low roof with its yellow light, and the shapes of the travellers sitting under it with their candles, peaceful, with their lips moving.

“Do you wish me to do anything about him?” he said.

“No.”

“Very well.”

They walked on along the ridge into the wind. Behind them the light under the roof went on, steady and yellow and warm, and did not flicker. After a while the road turned along the shoulder of the hill and it was gone.

When it was too dark to see the kerb, they stopped in the lee of a fallen wall and did not light a fire. Dusana broke the second loaf and gave out the cheese. The raven took its piece of crust down behind a stone, out of the wind, and they heard it there for a long time, pulling the bread apart.

Chapter 30 — A name with a reach

The ridge ended in the air.

It narrowed through the morning, the hill falling away on both sides, until the road ran along a spine of grass no wider than a wagon with the mist below it on the left and the right, and then it came to a stone, and stopped. The stone stood at the very end of the ridge with its back to the drop, a squat grey block as high as Riarhin’s hip, its top worn smooth and a little hollow, like a step that many feet had used. Beyond it there was nothing. The mist began at its far face and went on.

Somebody was sweeping the road.

She was lean and dark-skinned and bow-legged, a woman of middle years with a red scarf wound round her head and neck and up over her chin against the wind, and she had a broom of birch twigs and was sweeping the gravel towards the kerb in short hard strokes, as if it had done something to her. Behind her, on the uphill side, there was a hut of turf and stone, and an awning of sailcloth stretched out from its wall on poles, and under the awning a few people sitting on bundles. A cooking fire. Children.

The woman stopped sweeping and looked at them over the scarf.

“You’re from the Arch,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Wenna’s bread.” She sniffed the air. Riarhin had the last of the loaf under her arm. “She puts the salt in late. Nobody else does.” She looked at Cadan, up and down, and seemed satisfied, and then at Dusana, and the raven on Dusana’s shoulder, and then at Riarhin, and the sword. “Who sends you down my road?”

Riarhin had known the question was coming. She had known it since the candles. She had thought, in the dark behind the fallen wall, what she would say, and had said several things to herself, and had not liked any of them.

“Nobody,” she said. “I’m going.”

The woman looked at her a while longer.

“That’ll do,” she said, and went back to her sweeping. “Most of them say a name. I’m Sefe. Sit down if you’re stopping. Don’t sit on the children.”

The people under the awning, children and all, were from the Weir.

Sefe told it while she swept, without looking up, in short pieces between strokes. The Weir was below, past the third stone, a mill village in a valley with a river and a weir across it and willows; it was where they lived, these people, where they had been settled a long while. They had come up to the fair at Cold Ford in the summer, a few of them, the way they did every year, with cheese to sell. When they came back, the road down to the Weir had gone wrong.

“Gone wrong how?” said Riarhin.

“The air.” Sefe swept. “It goes thin. A few steps past the third stone you can’t get your breath. Further on you can’t get it at all. And the steps aren’t where they were. It goes down different every time.” She stopped and leaned on the broom and looked at the people under the awning. “I can make the road as far as the third stone. I can’t make it past. That bit isn’t mine any more.”

A woman under the awning was watching them. She was young, broad-faced, with her hair in a plait over one shoulder and her sleeves rolled up, and she had a child of perhaps four asleep across her knees. Beside her an old man sat with his hands on his stick and his chin on his hands, looking at the mist.

“Merel,” said Sefe, nodding at the young woman. “Her man’s down there, and the baby. She came up with her father-in-law to sell the cheese and left them at home. That’s the father-in-law. Gerrit.” She went back to sweeping. “He says he’ll go down when the road’s mended and not before.”

“And her?”

“She says she’ll go down if she has to crawl with her face in the dirt. She’s tried. Twice. Her lips went blue.” Sefe swept the last of the gravel to the kerb and stood the broom against the stone. “So. You want the road’s word.”

“Yes.”

“Wenna send you?”

“Wenna told me how to stand.”

“Tell me what she said.”

Riarhin told her. Going out, not coming in. The last step before the stone. The hand flat on the top of it. On the breath going out, not the breath coming in.

Sefe listened with her head on one side. When Riarhin stopped she nodded once.

“That’s right,” she said. “That’s how.” She pulled the scarf down off her chin. Her face was lined and her mouth was wide and she had a gap between her front teeth. “I’ll show you, and then you’ll do it, and then we’ll see. There’s Tobin wants to go to the second stone. I’ll make it for him and you watch.”

Tobin was a carter, dead, with a handcart full of sacks. He had been waiting under the awning since the night before for Sefe to have time for him, and he did not seem to mind. He brought the handcart up to the marker and stood holding its shafts.

Sefe stood back from the stone four paces, on the road, with her back to the hut.

She walked to it. She walked as if she were going somewhere else entirely, briskly, her bow-legs taking the gravel in short strides, and on the fourth step she was at the stone and her hand came down flat on its hollow top, and as it came down she breathed out and said the word.

It was one syllable. It was short, and it had the whole of her breath behind it, and it was not in any tongue Riarhin had. It was not Tulkani, or Ykrul, or Lethai, or anything she had heard on any road. Riarhin heard it and kept it; she kept everything she attended to, and she had attended to that with all of her. She could have said it back at once. She did not.

Dusana, beside her, had turned her face away towards the hut.

The road came up out of the mist.

It came the way a beach comes up when the tide goes out, all at once and slowly. The grey beyond the stone thinned and darkened and was gravel, packed and pale, wet, with water running off it into the mist on either side, and the kerbstones came up along its uphill edge one by one like the backs of sheep rising from long grass. It ran out from the foot of the marker into the grey, straight, a hundred paces or so, and at the end of it, dim, there was another stone, squat, the same.

“Go on,” said Sefe.

Tobin lifted the shafts and went on. The handcart’s wheels crunched on the new gravel. He walked to the second stone without hurrying and without looking down, and stood by it, and turned round and raised a hand.

Behind him, the road went back into the mist. It went from the near end first, the end by Riarhin’s feet, quietly; the gravel greyed and thinned and was mist again, and the kerbstones went down under it, and the grey closed up the hundred paces until there was only the second stone, dim, and the carter beside it, and his handcart, standing on what there was over there.

“It doesn’t stay,” said Riarhin.

“It stays while it’s wanted.” Sefe pulled her scarf back up over her chin. “It’s not a name, you know. Wenna calls it one. It’s not. A name is what a thing is. This is only where it is.” She jerked her head at the marker. “Your turn.”

“Before you do,” said Dusana, “I’ll go over.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“To the second stone. Somebody should watch the far end. When it takes, the far stone goes warm. You feel it if you’ve your hand on it.” Dusana shrugged. “I know that much. I’ve been along roads like this.” She looked at Sefe. “Will you make it for me?”

Sefe made it for her. Dusana went across the hundred paces of new gravel with her skirt held up out of the wet and the raven on her shoulder, and at the second stone she stopped beside Tobin and put her right hand flat on the stone’s top, the scarred palm down, and lifted her other hand to show she was there. Then the road went back into the mist behind her.

The raven had come off her shoulder somewhere in the middle and flown back. It landed on the near marker beside Riarhin’s hand, and shook itself, and looked at her.

“Bird,” said Riarhin.

It looked at the mist.

She did it wrong four times.

The first time her thigh was the trouble. She walked to the stone as Sefe had walked, briskly, four steps, and the right leg, stiff from the night against the wall, shortened the last stride without asking her, and her hand came down on the stone a breath before her foot was where it should be. She said the word. Nothing. The mist stayed mist. Across it, faint, Dusana called, “Nothing.”

“Your foot,” said Sefe.

“I know.”

“Then put it where it goes.”

The second time she put her foot where it went and her hand came down late, after the breath had begun, so that the word came out on the end of the breath, thin. Nothing. “Nothing,” called Dusana.

“You’re thinking about your hand,” said Sefe. “Don’t. Your hand knows where the stone is.”

The third time she said it too loud. She heard it go out over the mist like a shout over water, and Sefe winced.

“It’s not for the far end,” she said. “It’s for here. You’re telling the stone. Not calling the other one.”

“Nothing,” called Dusana.

Riarhin stood back at the four paces. She was hot under her coat. She had been made to do things over and over, all her life, in the Valewood, by someone who stood by with his hand turning palm down when she got them wrong and said nothing at all, and she had hated it then, the stillness of him, the not-saying, and she hated it now, in Sefe, who was not him and who was saying a great deal. She breathed. She looked at the stone.

The raven was not on it.

She had not seen it go. It was on the road, or what would be the road, a pace beyond the marker, standing on the mist.

It was standing on the mist. It was not flying. Its feet were on the grey, both of them, and the grey was under its feet the way water is under a duck’s, only it was not floating, it was standing, and as she watched it began to walk. It walked out into the mist away from the stone with its rolling stiff-legged walk, the walk it used on Wenna’s table and the kitchen flags, putting one foot in front of the other on nothing, unhurried, its head going forward and back. It did not open its wings. It walked straight. She watched it go farther and farther out over the grey, a small black shape on the white, walking.

Sefe had stopped with her mouth open.

Across the mist, faint, Dusana said, “Bird?”

The raven walked the whole hundred paces. Riarhin watched every step. When it reached the far end it hopped up onto the second stone beside Dusana’s hand and shook itself and looked back the way it had come.

“Has it done that before?” said Riarhin.

Sefe closed her mouth.

“Not here,” she said.

The fourth time was wrong too, because she was thinking about the bird.

The fifth time she was not thinking about anything. She stood at the four paces and looked at the stone and let her body go, the way it went down a stair in the dark, the way it went along a roof. Four steps. The right foot where it went, the thigh taking it. The hand flat, landing as the foot landed, the cold hollow top of the stone under her palm. The breath going out. The word riding the breath, low, for the stone.

The road came up.

It came up out of the mist in front of her, gravel and kerb, wet and pale, straight out to the second stone, and across the hundred paces she heard Dusana say, “There,” not loud, and then louder, “It’s warm. It’s gone warm.”

Riarhin looked down at the new road.

The gravel was fresh and smooth, unmarked, the way a beach is when the tide has just gone off it. No wheel had been on it. No foot. Except that down its middle, starting a pace from the marker, there was a line of prints. Small, three-toed, a hand’s breadth apart, pressed into the wet gravel. They went straight out along the road away from her, evenly, not wavering, all the way to the second stone, where the road ended under Dusana’s hand.

Sefe came and stood beside her and looked at them.

She did not say anything for a while. Then she bent and put her finger in the nearest print, the way you would test whether a loaf was done.

“It’s where it walked,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That’s where the road comes. Every time. That line.” Sefe straightened up. “I’ve made this road a thousand times. It comes up there, down the middle, just there, where those are.” She looked across at the second stone, where the raven was sitting beside Dusana’s hand, preening the bent feather in its tail. “And it walked it first.”

Riarhin did not answer. She was looking at the prints.

She had seen a district, days ago, where the air went thin and you could see the sleepers’ dreams going on above the roofs like weather, shifting, folding, a corner of a kitchen, a field, the side of a boat. She had watched one of them change. A wall in it had moved before the dreamer’s hand reached it, as though the dream knew where the hand was going. She had seen an arch on a hill that the raven would not go through, and when they tested it the arch had sent their pebble back from the wrong side. She had seen the raven go into the trough at Wenna’s as if it owned the water.

She thought about the prints. She thought about the bird walking out over the mist where there was nothing, unhurried, because there was going to be something, and it had got there first.

She did not say any of it.

Dusana came back across the road, with the raven on her shoulder, and stood at the marker and looked down at the prints in the gravel.

“Huh,” she said.

“Has it done that before?”

Dusana thought about it. She rubbed her thumb along the side of her second finger.

“Once,” she said. “On a hill road, a long way back. Before I had you to quarrel with. It went out over a slip where the road had gone and I thought it was showing off, and then the road came back.” She looked at the bird on her shoulder. It looked at her with one eye. “I thought it was showing off,” she said again.

“Where did you get it?”

“It got me.” Dusana did not look up. “I was sitting on a step. I don’t know whose. I’d been walking a long while and I sat down. It came and stood on my foot. I said get off. It didn’t.” She put up a finger and the raven nibbled at it, gently, along the side. “That was all.”

Beyond the second stone, the mist went on. There was a third stone somewhere out in it, Sefe said, and past the third stone the road went down to the Weir, the road that had gone wrong.

“Try it on the next bit,” said Sefe.

Dusana looked at her. Then she walked out along the new gravel to the second stone, and put the raven down on the top of it, and pointed into the grey beyond.

“Go on,” she said. “Like you did.”

The raven looked at the grey. It looked at her. It walked to the edge of the stone and put one foot out over the mist and held it there, and drew it back, and looked at her again.

“Go on.”

It did not go. It sat down on the stone with its feathers out, the way it sat on the fender at Wenna’s, and shut its eyes.

Dusana stood a moment longer. Then she laughed, short.

“Well,” she said. “That’s ravens.”

Riarhin made the road out to the third stone herself, from the second, with Sefe standing beside her saying nothing at all, which was worse than the corrections. It came up first time. The third stone stood at the end of it, smaller than the others and tilted, and beyond it the ground went down steeply in the mist, and there was a smell on the air from below, cold and flat, like the inside of a stone jar.

The raven went with them as far as the third stone, riding on Dusana’s shoulder, and when they got there it got down and stood on the stone and faced the slope.

It did not walk out. It did not sit down either. It stood with its head stretched forward and its feathers lying flat and close, and it called, once, down into the grey, a harsh loud single note that went away down the slope and did not come back.

Then it got back onto Dusana’s shoulder and pushed its head in under her ear.

“That’s the Weir road,” said Sefe. “That’s where it goes wrong. Your word won’t make that. It makes as far as here.” She looked at Riarhin. “It’s yours now. It’s this road’s, and you’ve got it, and it’ll make this road for you and no other. Don’t go saying it at other stones. They’ll not like it.”

“I won’t.”

“You came past the candles,” Sefe said.

“Yes.”

“And you didn’t bring him.” Sefe looked at her a moment longer. Then she turned and started back along the gravel towards her marker and her hut and her broom. “There’s soup. It’s not Wenna’s.”

From the far marker, if you stood on the uphill side by Sefe’s hut and looked back along the ridge, you could see a long way. The ridge went back in a long curve over the grey, and at the far end of it, small, a green slope with a speck of grey on it that would be the arch.

There was something hanging on the arch. It was very small at that distance and very pale, a thread, a scrap, lifting a little and falling.

Riarhin stood with Sefe’s soup in a bowl in her hands and looked at it.

“That your skin?” said Sefe, beside her.

“Yes.”

“Wenna’s hung it, then.” Sefe drank her own soup. “She does what she says. You’ll want to be getting back.”

Chapter 31 — Aurine at the appointed place

They went back along the ridge in the afternoon, with the wind behind them for once, pushing.

It was easier walking that way and worse thinking. Going out, the wind had been something to lean on. Coming back it shoved at her pack and lifted her coat and made her hurry, and she did not want to hurry towards the arch. She had left it once that morning. She had not meant to come back to it the same day.

The long roof came up out of the grey on its pillars, yellow under its slates. The travellers sat on their benches with their candles. Riarhin did not look for the Tallow Man and he did not come out. Cadan walked on the far side of her from the hut wall without being asked. When they were out from under the roof the wind came back and the smell of tallow went off it, and Dusana, who had said nothing for an hour, said, “Well, that’s done,” as if they had been to the dentist.

The raven rode on Dusana’s pack facing backwards, which it seemed to prefer when the wind was behind.

The arch stood on its green slope with the skin hanging from the keystone on a thread.

It lifted and fell. Close to, it was smaller than it had seemed from Sefe’s marker, a twist of grey translucence the length of Riarhin’s forearm, and the thread that held it was red wool. Wenna had tied it in a bow.

Through the frame Riarhin could see the yard, the mounting block with nobody on it, the house with its chimney going. Below the house, in the orchard, the dyers’ line, and on the line the lengths of blue hanging still, because there was no wind on that side. She looked at them once. She did not look again.

There was nobody at the arch.

“We’re early,” said Dusana.

“Or she’s late.”

“That’s the same thing from where we’re standing.” Dusana put her pack down on the turf, out of the wind behind the arch’s left post, and sat on it. “I’ve waited for people in worse places. Sit down. You make the bird nervous, standing.”

Riarhin sat. Cadan stood a little apart with his ropes, and then, after a while, he too sat, on the turf with his legs folded under him, and took the brass lantern off his belt and set it beside him with the white candle unlit, as he had in Theona at the end of a long table, waiting for an instruction.

It came on to rain a little. The rain did not cross the arch. It fell on their side and stopped at the frame as though at a window.

Aurine came along the ridge from the other direction, the way none of them had come, out of the grey where the road was not made and Riarhin had been told it went nowhere. She came on foot with her satchel across her body and a stick in her hand, and beside her, half a step behind and leaning on her arm, came somebody wrapped from head to foot in a grey blanket with a hood pulled forward.

Riarhin knew Aurine at three hundred paces by her walk. She was small and quick and walked as though the ground had been told about her. She had always walked like that, across the yards in Theona, across the roof in Valora with a pot of something that should not have been carried across roofs, up the mountain with her packets of herbs that had been dried too hot.

She stopped twenty paces off. She looked at Riarhin. She looked at Cadan, and something went across her face and was gone. She looked at Dusana and the raven, and at the skin on its red thread, and then she said, in the northern way she had, that made everything sound like a remark about the weather:

“I’ll not apologise. We were held up.”

“I can see,” said Riarhin.

“You can’t. You can see some of it.” Aurine turned to the figure in the blanket. “Sit there. Under the post. Out of the light.”

There was no light worth speaking of. The day was grey and the rain was fine and the arch made a little shadow on the turf on the side away from the house. The figure in the blanket went into it and sat down, carefully, with her back against the stone, and pushed the hood back an inch.

She was a woman of thirty or so with a long jaw and a cropped head. Her skin, from the jaw down into the collar of the blanket, was a grey-green, finely patterned, the way the skin on the back of a lizard is patterned, with a sheen on it that the grey day caught. Her eyes were brown and very large in the pupil, and when she turned her head towards Riarhin they took what light there was and gave it back, flat and gold, the way a cat’s do at a lamp.

“Tamsin,” said Aurine. “This is Riarhin. That’s Brother Cadan. I don’t know the woman with the bird.”

“Dusana,” said Dusana.

“Don’t show me,” said Tamsin. Her voice was hoarse, with a Mistlands burr. “That’s all I ask. She shows me. Everywhere we go. I’m not a fair-booth.”

“I didn’t say anything,” said Aurine.

“You said this is Tamsin. With your hand out. Like a woman with a calf.”

Aurine looked at her own hand, which was indeed out, and put it in her pocket.

The thing that had held them up was the light.

Aurine told it while she made a fire in the lee of the arch, which she did as she did everything, quickly and without waste, with a handful of dry stuff out of her satchel and a flint that she struck once. They had come in by a way Aurine had been told about in a letter from a woman in Isoka’s service who lived on the Mistlands edge, a way that went in under a hill near the Weeping Gate and came out in a place of white stones in a bright sky. In the bright place Tamsin had gone blind.

“Not for good,” said Tamsin, from under the hood.

“Not for good. For three days. We sat in a hole under a stone with a blanket over the top until it got dark enough. It doesn’t get properly dark there. It gets less bright.” Aurine fed a stick into the fire. “I had a note from you saying come by the turn of the season. I’d no way to send to say we were sitting in a hole.”

“It doesn’t matter.”

“It matters to me. I said I’d be somewhere. I wasn’t.” She looked at the fire. “You’d have gone on without me.”

“We were going to.”

“Good.” Aurine sat back on her heels. “That’s right. That’s what I’d have wanted.”

She had grown older. Her cropped hair had gone grey at the temples and over the ears, and the lines by her eyes had deepened and spread so that she looked, when she was not speaking, as if she were narrowing them against a sun. The long pale scar on her left forearm, neat as a hem, showed where her sleeve was pushed up. Her hands were the same. Small and brown and dry, with the nails cut very short, and stained at the fingertips a yellowish green that would not come off.

Riarhin had last seen her in a hut on the mountain with the cradle closed behind them, sorting herb packets on a bench and telling Riarhin which one was the good one. They had made their peace there, as far as either of them had wanted it made. It had been a small peace. It had held.

“Your letter,” Riarhin said.

“Which?”

“The one with the questions in it.”

“You sent me eleven questions,” said Aurine. “I answered four. The others weren’t questions. They were you telling me what you thought I’d say.” She took a pot out of the satchel and stood it in the edge of the fire. “I said I’d bring you somebody it had helped. That’s her. She’ll tell you herself or she won’t.”

“I won’t,” said Tamsin. “Not yet.”

There was a thing about Aurine’s cooking that Riarhin had forgotten and remembered all at once, when the pot was warm and the lid came off, which was that Aurine put too much of everything in. Too much barley, too much salt, too many dried things out of too many packets, so that what came out was thick enough to stand a spoon in and tasted of six different afternoons.

“It’s too thick,” said Tamsin, before anybody had eaten.

“It’s exactly thick enough.”

“It’s porridge with opinions.” Tamsin held out her bowl anyway, from under the hood, and Aurine filled it. “There’s no bread. There’s never any bread. There’s been no bread with a crust on it since the Weeping Gate.”

Dusana reached into her pack without a word and took out the last of Wenna’s second loaf, the heel, and broke it in two, and passed half across the fire.

Tamsin looked at it. Then she took it, and turned it over in her grey-green fingers, and put it to her face and smelt it, for a long moment, with her eyes shut.

“Salt in late,” she said.

“That’s what Sefe said.”

“Who’s Sefe?”

“A woman with a broom,” said Dusana.

Tamsin ate the bread. She ate it slowly, crust first, and when it was gone she sat with her bowl in her lap and did not touch the porridge until Aurine said, “Eat that or I’ll give it to the bird,” and then she ate that too.

Cadan had his bowl in both hands. He was eating with his small neat movements and saying nothing. Aurine had not spoken to him. She had looked at him once at twenty paces, and once again when she said his name to Tamsin, and since then she had not looked at him at all.

Watching the steam rise from Cadan’s bowl into the rain, Riarhin remembered a farmhouse in Theona. She and Cadan and Aurine had sat with the other five at a table made for shearing crews. It had been raining then as well. Somebody had been telling a story about a ship’s mate, and Amari had been doing the voice, and Aurine had said, “It’s the way he says ballast,” and had put her forehead down on her folded arms on the table and laughed until her shoulders shook, and Cadan had laughed too, a sound none of them had heard him make, high and surprised, and it had gone round the table and come back. Nessa had been there. Nessa had laughed into her cup.

Nessa was dead on a stair in Valora. Amari was somewhere. Berent was somewhere, and nobody would know him if he walked into the room.

“Ballast,” Riarhin said.

Aurine’s spoon stopped.

She looked across the fire. For a moment her face did not do anything. Then the lines by her eyes moved, and she put her spoon down in her bowl and put her hand over her mouth, and her shoulders went.

“Don’t,” she said through her fingers. “Don’t you dare.”

Cadan had put down his bowl. He was looking at the fire with his head on one side. Riarhin heard him make a small sound, a breath with an edge on it, and then another, and then he lowered his head so that she could see only the shaven top of it, pale in the grey, and it was shaking.

“What?” said Tamsin. “What’s funny?”

“Nothing,” said Aurine, wiping her eyes with the heel of her hand. “A man we knew. You’d have had to be there.” She picked up her spoon again. “It wasn’t even funny then.”

She did not look at Cadan. But her gaze rested on his side of the fire, where it had not rested before.

After they had eaten Aurine laid it out.

She did it the way she had once laid out the contents of a poisoner’s shelf for the company in Theona, item by item, each with its use, nothing dressed up.

“I came to find where she can be reached,” she said. “Isoka. Not to pray to. To find where her attention is, in here, if it’s anywhere. I’ve carried her skin eleven years and more and asked once a season and most seasons it lies there. In the Mistlands this summer it moved four times. That’s more than it’s moved in all the years before put together.” She looked at the arch, where the cast skin hung from its red bow. “That one’s Wenna’s. Mine’s in the box. I asked for Wenna to hang one so I’d not be parted from mine.”

“And Tamsin?”

“Tamsin was a ferrywoman at the Weeping Gate,” said Aurine. “Her crossing went bad in the summer. The air on it went thin, like the air past your third stone. People who went over came back blue or didn’t come back. She kept crossing because there were people on the far side.” She stirred the fire with a stick. “On her last crossing she didn’t come back blue. She came back not breathing. They brought her to the woman in Isoka’s service, who sent for me because I was the nearest thing to a doctor for twenty miles who’d not run from her. She’d have died in a day. I asked what she wanted. She said to cross. I said I could give her something that would let her cross, and she wouldn’t be what she was after.”

“She said,” said Tamsin from under the hood, “‘Will I still be me?’ And Aurine said, ‘I don’t know. You’ll still be you to me.’ Which isn’t the same.” She pushed the hood back another inch. “I said do it anyway.”

“That’s what I came to show you,” Aurine said. “Not a cure. A way across for people who want to go across badly enough to stop being what they were. Some of them want it. Some don’t. Some can’t be asked properly because they’re too far gone or too young. I’ve not worked out what I do about them.” She looked at Riarhin. “You’ve a broken road past a third stone and people on the far side of it. Sefe told me in the letter. She writes a fair hand for a woman who sweeps all day.”

Riarhin had not known that Sefe wrote letters. She thought about Merel on her bundle under the awning, with her child across her knees and her husband and her baby down the road that had gone wrong, and her lips going blue the two times she had tried it.

“You want to try it on the Weir road,” she said.

“I want to offer it there. To anybody who wants it. Not to everybody.” Aurine put the stick in the fire. “And I want somebody there who isn’t me, when I offer. Somebody who can say no for them if they can’t say it for themselves.” She looked at Riarhin across the fire, steadily, for the first time since she had arrived. “I don’t trust myself with that. I’m proud of what I know. I can feel myself being proud of it. Isoka’s people get like that. Somebody refuses and you think, well, they’ve not understood. That’s how it starts.”

Riarhin did not answer at once.

Through the arch, on the other side, the light was going yellow in the house windows. Somebody had come out into the orchard with a lantern and was taking the blue down off the line, length by length, folding it over one arm. It was too far to see who. The lantern swung. The blue went into the dark.

“Yes,” Riarhin said.

Aurine nodded once, the way she had nodded in the hut on the mountain over the herb packet. Then she turned to Cadan, at last, directly.

“And you,” she said. “Brother. You’ll be on the road too, I suppose.”

“I will go where I am asked to go,” said Cadan.

“I know you will.” Aurine looked at him a moment longer. “That was always what I minded.”

She got up and went to the arch and stood on her toes and untied Wenna’s skin from the keystone, carefully, by the end of the red wool, and wrapped it in a twist of paper from her satchel and handed it in through the frame to nobody. Riarhin saw a hand come out of the yard side and take it. A broad hand, floury. Then it went away.

The rain stopped at the arch’s frame and went on falling on their side, fine and grey and soft.

Chapter 32 — Those who can cross

Sefe had swept the road again by the time they came back to her marker, and was sitting on an upturned bucket beside it with the broom across her knees, as though she had been expecting them since the morning and had found the wait reasonable.

“You’ve got her, then,” she said, looking at Aurine.

“She’s got me,” said Aurine.

“That’s what they all say.” Sefe got up off the bucket. “Merel’s been at me since you went. Twice she’s been to the third stone on her own and twice she’s come back. Second time she sat down past it and I had to go and get her.” She nodded at the awning. “She’s there. Gerrit’s there. The little one’s asleep. Don’t wake the little one, she bites.”

Tamsin had stopped beside the marker with her hood pulled down over her eyes. Under the awning the light was the grey of the ridge and no worse; she went in under it and sat on a bundle at the back, as far from the open side as she could, and Gerrit looked at her, and then at her hands, and then away.

Merel was standing.

She had seen Aurine’s satchel and Tamsin’s hands and had put the two together before anybody spoke, and she stood in the mouth of the awning with her plait over her shoulder and her sleeves rolled to the elbow and said, “Is it true?”

“Is what true?” said Aurine.

“Sefe said there was a woman coming who could make it so you could breathe down there.”

“Sefe said that.”

“Can you?”

Aurine looked at her for a long moment. Then she took off her satchel and put it down on the gravel, and sat down beside it, cross-legged, so that she was lower than Merel, and said, “Sit down. I’m not going to tell you standing up.”

She told it plainly. Riarhin, sitting a little apart with her back to the marker, listened to her tell it and could not have told it better and could not, listening, have said what in it was being sold.

There was a cut. Here, along the arm, wrist to elbow. Aurine pushed her own sleeve up and laid her finger along the old scar there, the neat pale line, and did not say whether that was where hers had come from, and Merel did not ask. The skin went over it, bound on with linen. You slept. It hurt in the night, not badly, like a fever. In the morning you were changed and you did not change back.

“What changes?”

“I don’t know for you. I know for her.” Aurine nodded at Tamsin, at the back of the awning. “The skin goes. Here, and here.” Neck. Forearm. “The eyes. The breath gets slow, and then it gets so it doesn’t need much. The sun hurts. There’s no sun in here, so that’s no great loss in here.” She paused. “If you go back out, it’s a loss.”

“I’m not going back out.”

“You don’t know that.”

“I know where my husband is,” said Merel. “And the baby. I know where they are. They’re three miles down that road and I can’t get to them.” She did not raise her voice. “What else changes?”

“Some people find they like things they didn’t. Some find they don’t like things they did.” Aurine looked at her own stained fingers. “One man I did, his wife said he never laughed at the same things again. He laughed. Only at other things. She minded that more than the skin.”

Merel was quiet.

“Ask her,” said Tamsin, from the back of the awning. “Go on. She wants to.”

Merel turned round. Tamsin had pushed her hood back. In the grey light under the canvas her eyes were gold and flat and her neck, above the blanket, had the sheen of a wet stone.

“Do you mind it?” Merel said.

Tamsin thought.

“I mind bread,” she said. “I can’t taste it the same. It’s like eating it through a cloth. I mind my sister looking at me like that.” She nodded at Gerrit, who was looking at the ground. “I don’t mind being alive. I crossed eleven times in the summer after. Eleven boatloads. Nobody else could.” She pulled the hood back up. “Don’t do it for the boatloads. Do it for whatever it is you’re doing it for.”

Merel’s child woke at dusk and did bite, a little, on Sefe’s thumb, and was given a crust to bite instead.

She was four, square and fair and sullen with sleep, and she sat on her mother’s knee while her mother asked the question Riarhin had been waiting for since the afternoon.

“Her too,” Merel said. “Can you do her too? Then we can all go down. Then we’re all there.”

Aurine did not answer.

She looked across the awning at Riarhin. It was a short look and there was nothing in it that asked, and Riarhin knew it for what it was: the reason Aurine had wanted somebody there who was not her.

“No,” said Riarhin.

Merel turned on her. “Who asked you?”

“She did.” Riarhin did not move from the marker. “I’m saying it because she can’t ask. She doesn’t know what it is. You don’t either, not really, but you can find out and still say yes. She can’t.” She looked at the child, who was looking at her with the crust in her fist. “When she’s old enough to ask, she can ask.”

“And till then she stays up here with no mother.”

“Till then she stays up here with her grandfather, and Sefe, and a road that’s made as far as the third stone.” Riarhin kept her voice level. “And with you, if you want. Nobody’s said you have to go.”

Merel stared at her. Then she put her face down against the top of the child’s head, the way Riarhin had once seen a queen do in a nursery, enduring an objection, and stayed like that.

Gerrit spoke for the first time since they had come.

“I’ll have her,” he said. He did not lift his chin off his stick. “I’ll have her. I’m not going down that road till it’s mended. Not like that.” He looked at Tamsin’s hands, and away. “No offence to you.”

“None taken,” said Tamsin. “I’d not have done it either, at your age.”

“I’m dead,” said Gerrit. “What’s age?”

“Something you’ve still got plenty of, by the look of it,” said Tamsin, and the old man made a sound that might have been the beginning of a laugh and might not, and put his chin back on his hands.

Aurine did it by lamplight at the back of the awning, with Riarhin holding the lamp.

She had expected more to see. Merel looked like a woman having a cut dressed. Aurine washed Merel’s arm with something out of a bottle that smelt of vinegar and pine. She took a small knife out of a roll of soft leather and drew it down the inside of the forearm from wrist to elbow, once, quickly and shallow, the way you would score bread, and Merel hissed through her teeth and did not pull away. The blood came up in a line of beads. Aurine opened the oiled box from her satchel and took out the skin that was in it, her own, the one she had carried for eleven years and more and asked once a season, and laid it along the cut.

It moved.

Riarhin saw it move. It lay along the cut and the end of it, the narrow end, lifted very slightly off the skin, the way a leaf lifts in a draught, and lay down again. There was no draught under the awning. Aurine did not look up. She bound the skin on with clean linen, round and round, and tied it off, and put Merel’s arm down on her lap, and said, “Now sleep. If you can’t sleep, lie still. It’ll hurt about midnight. Don’t scratch.”

Merel lay down beside her child on the bundles with her bound arm across her body. Gerrit put his coat over the both of them.

Aurine wiped the knife and rolled it up and put it away. Her hands were quite steady. When she stood up she put one of them on the awning pole for a moment.

“It moved,” Riarhin said, low.

“I saw.”

“Does it do that?”

“It did that in the Mistlands. Four times.” Aurine looked at the lamp. “Five, now.”

In the night Merel cried out once, about midnight, as she had been told she would, and then lay still.

Riarhin did not sleep much. She sat at the mouth of the awning with her back to the pole and listened to the people under it breathe. Gerrit snored. The child talked in her sleep, two words over and over that might have been a name. Tamsin did not seem to sleep at all; whenever Riarhin looked round at her, her eyes were open in the dark, two flat gold coins, looking at nothing. Dusana slept sitting up against her pack with the raven inside her coat. Cadan had gone out to the end of the road at the third stone after dark with a lantern, saying he wished to see what it did at night, and had not come back.

Merel’s breathing changed towards morning. Riarhin heard it change. It had been a sleeper’s breathing, slow and catching, and somewhere in the dark before the grey came it slowed further, and then further, until there was more time between the breaths than Riarhin would have liked, and she got up and went and crouched by her. Aurine was already there.

“That’s right,” Aurine said, very softly. “That’s it doing it.”

In the morning Merel sat up and looked at her own hands.

The change on her was less than on Tamsin. Her neck had gone a soft grey-green under the jaw, and the backs of her hands, and when she pushed up the sleeve over the linen there was a pattern coming on the inside of the forearm, fine, like the grain in a split board. Her eyes were the same brown. In the grey light they did nothing. When Sefe brought the lamp near her face to look, they gave the light back gold, and Merel flinched and put her hand up.

“Leave the lamp,” said Tamsin.

The child woke and looked at her mother. She looked for a long time. Then she put out a fist and touched the grey place under her mother’s jaw, and rubbed it, as you would rub at a smudge, and when it did not come off she lost interest and asked for bread.

Merel went down the Weir road at midmorning with Sefe’s road made for her as far as the third stone, and from the third stone on, on her own.

Riarhin made the road. She stood at the first marker and walked her four steps and put her hand on the stone on the breath going out, and the gravel came up out of the mist to the second stone, and from the second to the third. Merel walked it with her bound arm held against her and a bundle on her back, and did not look back at the awning, where Gerrit sat with the child on his knee and his chin on the child’s head.

At the third stone Merel stopped. Below it the ground fell away into the grey, and the smell came up from it, cold and flat, the inside of a stone jar.

She stepped off the stone onto the slope.

She went down five paces, and ten, and stopped, and Riarhin saw her shoulders go up as she took a breath, and come down. And go up. Slowly. Then she went on. She went on down into the grey until she was a shape and then until she was not.

“She’s breathing,” said Tamsin, who had come as far as the second stone in her hood with Aurine’s hand under her elbow. “Listen. You can hear her.”

Riarhin could. A long way down, slow, steady, a long way between each breath. Then the grey took that too.

Merel came back up at the end of the day, alone.

She came up out of the mist below the third stone with mud to the knee and her bound arm held against her and her face wet, and she sat down on the third stone and put her face in her hands, and when Aurine reached her she said, without lifting it, “They’re there.”

“Both?”

“Both. He’s at the mill. He’s been keeping it going.” She lifted her face. It was wet and grey-green under the jaw and her eyes gave back the last of the light. “He looked at me. He knew me. It took him a moment.” She wiped her face with the back of her hand. “The baby didn’t know me at all. She cried.”

Nobody said anything.

“I can’t bring them up,” Merel said. “I asked. He tried. He came as far as the willows with her and his lips went blue at the willows and she started to choke and I took her back.” She looked at Aurine. “I can go down. I can come up. I can carry things. That’s all. They’re there and I’m the only one who can go in between.” She looked up the gravel to the awning, where the child was sitting in the mouth of it on Gerrit’s knee, watching the road. “She’s there. They’re there. I’m the road.”

“Yes,” said Aurine.

“You said you didn’t know what it would be.”

“I didn’t.”

Merel nodded. She got up off the stone. She walked back up the gravel towards the awning, and halfway along it the child saw her and slid off Gerrit’s knee and came running, and stopped a pace short of her mother and looked at her, and then put up her arms to be lifted, and was.

The others came after that. Not many.

A man from the far end of the awning who had a brother at the Weir. A girl of fifteen, dead young, who had been at the Cold Ford fair with her aunt and wanted to go home and could not say what home was without her voice cracking. Aurine sat with each of them as she had sat with Merel, cross-legged on the gravel, lower than them, and told it plainly, and Riarhin sat with her back to the marker and listened.

The man said yes. The girl said no, and then yes, and then, the next morning, with the knife already out of its roll, no, and burst into tears, and Aurine put the knife away and gave her a cup of something hot and said, “That’s right. That’s an answer. Drink that.”

An old woman who had sat under the awning without speaking for three days, since before Riarhin had first come, did not answer at all when she was asked. She looked at Aurine with a face that had nothing in it, and then looked at the mist, and her lips moved.

“She can’t,” said Sefe. “She’s been like that since the road went. She doesn’t know where she is.”

Aurine looked at the old woman a long while. Then she got up and went back to her satchel.

“Then she stays,” she said. “Somebody bring her soup.”

Somebody did. It was Cadan, who had come back up from the third stone in the night with his lantern guttering and his face grey and had said nothing to anybody about where he had been. He carried the bowl across the awning to the old woman and knelt and held the spoon for her, patiently, until she took it.

“It’s worse,” Merel said, the third time she came back up.

She sat on the bucket by Sefe’s marker with a sack of the mill’s flour at her feet, which she had carried up on her back for the people under the awning, and drank Sefe’s soup.

“Worse how?” said Riarhin.

“The willows. Below the village there’s a row of willows along the water. There were eleven. I counted them going down the first time because I was frightened and it gave me something to do.” She drank. “Today there were nine. The two at the end, the mist’s got. They’re not there. It’s not that they’re in mist. They’re not.” She put the bowl down. “He says the mill-race is running slower. He says the far bank is coming closer every day and there’s no water between, only the grey.”

Riarhin looked down the slope beyond the third stone.

The first repair, at the stair in the hill district, had held where they tested it. The ford at the Painted Stone had been empty of its stair on the morning Gulshat’s voice came down the tunnel. That was true and it was still true. And the deep thing under it had shifted, and gone on shifting, here and at the Weir and wherever else it had gone, and the people who could cross it were the ones who had stopped being what they were.

“It’s not stopping,” said Aurine, beside her.

“No.”

“The crossing’s good. It works. It’s working.” Aurine said it flatly, as a fact she was not going to argue about. “It doesn’t stop the thing that made the crossing necessary.”

Cadan had been sitting by the third stone with a slate on his knee. He had borrowed it from Sefe, who kept it in the hut to write down what people left with her. Now he got up and brought it to Riarhin, and held it out.

“I went down past the village,” he said. “In the night. Twice.”

“How far?”

“I do not know how far. There is a turn, below the mill, where the path goes down between two banks. Whichever way I take it, I come out at the third stone. I took it four ways. I came out four times at the third stone.” He turned the slate so she could see it. “Once, the last time, before I came out, I saw this. It was there for a moment. Below the turn. Then it was the stone again.”

He had drawn it with the slate pencil, carefully, as he did everything. A door. Not much more than a rectangle with a curved top, in a wall that was not drawn, low, as though set into the face of a hill. A line across it where it closed. Nothing else.

“It was shut,” he said.

The raven had been on the marker beside Riarhin’s shoulder. When Cadan held out the slate it put its head on one side and looked at it, and then it got up off the marker and flew, not far, a few lengths along the kerb, and landed there, and faced away. Dusana called it. It did not come. When Cadan put the slate face down on the bucket it came back, and settled, and began to preen the bent feather in its tail.

Dusana watched it. She did not say anything. She rubbed her thumb along the side of her second finger, slowly, from the knuckle to the tip and back, the way she had at Sefe’s marker when the raven walked out over the mist.

“Sefe,” said Riarhin. “Who lives below the turn? Below the Weir?”

Sefe was sweeping. She stopped.

“Nobody you’d want to go and see on purpose,” she said. “There’s a hollow where three of the low roads cross. There’s a household lives in it. A woman called Halla, and her man, and a girl they took in. The girl was born down there. She knows the low roads like you know your hand.” She swept. “They don’t come up. I sent down to them once, in the summer, when the road went. The girl sent back that she’d come up when there was a reason.”

“Is there a reason?”

Sefe looked at the slate, face down on the bucket.

“You’d better take her one,” she said.

Part V — What the sleeper holds

Chapter 33 — The household on the road

Wren came up to the Weir on the fourth day, in the afternoon, when Merel had stopped expecting her.

Riarhin was at the mill. She had come down the Weir road behind Merel that morning, as far as she could, with Aurine and Tamsin and a bottle of something of Aurine’s that was meant to help, and had found that the bottle helped as far as the willows and then stopped helping all at once, like a lamp running out. She had sat down on the bank under the last willow with her lips going numb and her ears singing, and Tamsin had sat down beside her in the grey and waited, without remark, until she could stand. Then Tamsin had walked her back up the slope to the place where the air thickened, holding her elbow as Aurine held Tamsin’s.

“Now you know,” Tamsin had said.

So the others had gone on to the mill without her, and Riarhin had sat on the slope above the willows where she could breathe, and watched the village through the grey. It was a small place. A dozen houses along one street, a chapel with a squat tower, the mill at the end with its wheel turning slowly in a race that ran slower than water should. Smoke went up from four chimneys. A woman crossed the street with a basket. The willows stood along the water, nine of them, and beyond the last of them the bank simply stopped, as though somebody had drawn a line and rubbed out what was past it.

She was watching the two places where the willows had been when somebody sat down on the slope beside her.

She had not heard her come. That was the first thing. Riarhin heard most things. She heard Cadan, generally, when he was trying not to be heard, if she was attending; she heard Dusana’s raven shift its feet on a pack at forty paces. She had not heard this one come down the slope, or along it, or up it, or from wherever she had come.

“You’re the one with the sword,” the girl said.

She was about twenty, thin and brown and quick, with hair cut short and badly, as if she had done it herself without a glass, and a jacket that had been a man’s and was too broad in the shoulder. She sat with her knees drawn up and her arms round them. She was humming something under her breath, three notes over and over, and she did not stop humming when she spoke, so that the words came out on top of the tune.

“Yes.”

“Merel sent. She said there was a woman with a sword wanted to know about the low roads, and a monk, and a witch, and a woman with a bird.” The girl looked at the village. “I said that’s a lot of people to want to know about one thing.”

“It’s more than one thing.”

“It always is.” She stopped humming. “I’m Wren. I don’t come up. I came up.”

The hollow was an hour below the mill if you knew the way and nowhere at all if you didn’t.

Wren knew it. She took them down in the evening, all of them: Riarhin and Cadan and Dusana with the raven, Aurine with her satchel, Tamsin in her hood. She went ahead without looking back and without a light, and she went down the turn between the two banks that had sent Cadan back to the third stone four times in the night, and at the place where the path seemed to go on straight she went left, through what looked like a bank of earth and was not, and they followed her, and came out on a lane.

Cadan stopped on the lane and looked back at the bank.

“I walked into that,” he said. “Twice. It was earth.”

“It is earth,” said Wren. “It’s earth if you’re going down. It’s a gap if you’re going home.” She hummed her three notes. “You weren’t going home.”

“No.”

“That’s why.”

The lane went down between hedges into a hollow, a round green dip in the land like the inside of a bowl, with three roads coming into it from three directions and meeting in the bottom by a pond. There was a house there, long and low with a turf roof gone shaggy, and a byre built on to the end of it, and a kitchen garden in rows behind a wattle fence, and goats. A great many goats. They lifted their heads from the grass all together as the company came down the lane, and stared, and one of them, a brown nanny with a broken horn, came trotting up to Wren and butted her in the thigh.

“That’s Mother,” said Wren. “Not my mother. The goat’s called Mother. Halla named her. You’ll see why.”

Halla was in the yard. She was broad and grey and slow-moving, a woman of sixty with forearms like a smith’s and a face that did not hurry to make up its mind. She was standing at the gate with a pail, and she looked at the six of them coming down her lane and at Wren in front of them, and she did not say anything at all until they had stopped.

“You said you’d come up when there was a reason,” she said to Wren.

“I did.”

“That’s six reasons.” Halla set down the pail. “Seven, with the bird. Well. I’ve made enough for four. Brann!”

A man came round the end of the byre. He was big and fair and bald with a sunburnt scalp and he was carrying a hurdle on his back, and behind him, holding on to the tail of his shirt, came two small children, a boy and a girl, both fair, both barefoot, both staring.

“We’ve guests,” said Halla.

Brann looked at them. He looked longest at Riarhin’s sword.

“Have we,” he said.

They ate in the long room with the door open, because there was not room otherwise, at a table that had been made longer at some point by putting a door across two trestles at the end. Halla had made enough for four and it fed eight and the children, because she went to the crock and the shelf and the hook in the chimney three times while they ate and came back each time with something else. Bread. Cheese, goat, white and crumbling. Onions roasted in their skins. A pot of something brown with beans in it.
