The children were Bel and Nonny. Bel was six and would not speak. Nonny was four and would not stop. She sat on the bench next to Tamsin, who had put her hood back in the dim of the long room, and looked at Tamsin’s neck for a long time, and then said, “Are you a lizard?”

“Nonny,” said Brann.

“No,” said Tamsin. “I was a ferrywoman. I’m a ferrywoman that changed.”

“Into a lizard?”

“Into a ferrywoman that can breathe where you can’t.”

Nonny considered this. “Can you breathe in the pond?”

“I don’t know. I’ve not tried.”

“Try tomorrow,” said Nonny, and went back to her beans, and Tamsin looked across the table at Aurine with an expression that Riarhin had not seen on her face before, and that made her look ten years younger.

Wren did not sit at the table. She sat on the step of the open door with her bowl on her knees and her back to the yard, half in and half out, humming between mouthfuls.

“She won’t,” said Halla, catching Riarhin’s look. “Never has. Not under a roof, not all the way. She was born on the road outside. Right at the crossing.” She pointed with her spoon at the dark beyond the door, where the three roads met by the pond. “I found her there in a ditch, with no mother, nobody. Wasn’t even a cord to cut.” She put the spoon back in her bowl. “Never had a navel. Never had a fever either, or a day lost. You put her on a road she’s not walked and she walks it like she built it.”

“I’m right here,” said Wren, from the step.

“I know you are. You’re always right there. Half in.”

The trouble was the lane.

Brann told it after supper, out in the yard by the byre wall, with the children put to bed and the goats shut up and a lantern hung on a nail. He told it the way a man tells about a neighbour who keeps moving a boundary stone, with his arms folded and his jaw set.

There was a walker on the lane. He came along it every day, the lane from the pond up to the gap in the bank that Wren had brought them through, and back. He was thin and grey and you could not see his face because he walked with his back to where he was going. He walked backwards. Slowly. Every step he took, something behind him went back to how it had been.

“Before,” said Brann. “How it had been before. You mend a fence on the lane in the morning. In the afternoon he comes along and steps past it and it’s broken again. You lay a path of flags to the pond so the children don’t go in the mud. Next day he walks it and the flags are in a stack by the wall, where they were before you laid them.” He spat. “I had a wall up along the ditch, waist high. Four days’ work. He walked past it once.”

“Unstep,” said Halla. She was sitting on the mounting block with her hands on her knees. “That’s what Wren calls him. Unstep.”

“That’s what he does,” said Wren.

“And you want him off the lane,” said Riarhin.

“I want a gate,” said Brann. “On my lane. At the top, at the gap. He can walk wherever he likes. Not through my yard every day undoing my wall.”

“It’s not your lane,” said Wren.

She had come out of the house behind them and was standing at the corner of the byre. She was not humming.

“It’s the low road,” she said. “It’s one of the three. It was a road before your wall and before your yard and before Halla found me in the ditch by it. He was walking it when I was small. He walked it the night she found me. I remember him.” She looked at Brann. “You’d put a gate across a road.”

“I’d put a gate across my yard.”

“Your yard’s on a road.”

Halla said nothing. She sat on the block and looked from one to the other and her face did not hurry.

“He comes faster than he did,” she said at last, to nobody in particular. “Since the summer. Used to be once a week he came by. Now it’s every day. And what he undoes goes back further.” She looked at Riarhin. “Brann laid the flags in spring. He walked them in spring and they went back a day. Now he walks them and they go back to before Brann had the stone.”

He came in the morning.

Riarhin saw him from the yard, where she had been helping Brann carry hurdles to mend the fence along the lane, because if she was going to stand in the man’s yard and have opinions she would at least carry his hurdles. The light was grey and even, the light of a place without a sun. The goats were out. Bel was sitting on the wall with his heels against the stones, watching the lane, and Riarhin followed his look.

The walker came down from the gap.

He was as Brann had said, thin and grey, in a long coat the colour of old ash, and he came backwards, with his face towards the gap he had come through and his back to the pond. His feet felt behind him for each step. He did not look round. He did not hurry. He put one foot back, and set it, and brought the other back past it, and set that, the way a man walks in the dark in a room he knows.

Behind him the lane changed.

Riarhin watched it change. The hurdle she and Brann had set at the top of the lane an hour before, lashed to its stakes with new withy, was standing. Unstep stepped back past it. The withy came undone. It did not snap, it unwound, loop by loop, the way it had been wound on, backwards, and the hurdle leaned, and lay down on the grass, and the stakes drew themselves up out of the earth a finger’s breadth, and stood loose.

She did not stop to think about it. She went up the lane past Bel on the wall and took up a flat stone from Brann’s heap by the gate, a big one, and carried it up the lane ahead of the walker, and set it down on the lane behind him. Between him and the next hurdle. Where his heel would come down.

“No,” said Wren, behind her.

His heel came down on the stone.

The stone went. It did not move; it went back to where it had been, which was Brann’s heap, and Riarhin heard it land there with a clack. And the place on the lane where she had set it went back further. The grass there drew together and greyed and was not grass but bare earth, and then not earth but the grey that the willows had gone into below the Weir, a patch of it the size of a table, flat and blank.

Unstep stepped back past it. He did not seem to notice.

The patch stayed.

Riarhin stood with her hands still open where the stone had been. Her heart was going hard. Behind her Brann swore, low and long.

Wren came past her up the lane at a walk.

She did not go in front of the walker. She fell in beside him, a little behind, on the side towards the hedge, and she walked as he walked, backwards, slowly, step for step. She did not touch him. She did not look at him. She hummed her three notes. When he passed the next hurdle she was beside it, and as the withy began to unwind she put her hand on it, flat, and held it, the way you hold a page in a wind, and walked on with him and let it go once he was past. The withy stopped. The loops that had come undone stayed undone. The rest held.

She walked him down the whole lane like that. At the flags by the pond she walked on the flags with him and they stayed down. At the place where the mud began she stopped, and he went on into it without her, backwards, and through it, and out the far side of the hollow along the second road, and was gone.

Wren came back up the lane. She stopped at the grey patch and looked at it.

“You don’t stop him,” she said. “He’s going where he’s going. You go with him. You put your hand on what you want to keep and you walk.” She looked at Riarhin. “You put a thing in his way, he takes it back and he takes the place it was.”

“I know that now.”

“You knew it before.” Wren crouched by the patch. She did not touch it. “You just didn’t believe it.”

Brann had come up the lane behind them with his face red. He stood at the edge of the grey and looked at it, at the table-sized blank in his lane where there had been grass that morning, and Riarhin waited for him to say what he was going to say to her.

He did not say it to her.

“There,” he said to Wren. “There. Now tell me about gates.”

Aurine had made a camp in the byre loft by midday, with the hay forked into heaps for beds and a lantern on a beam and her satchel open on a crate, its packets laid out in rows. She did not ask Halla’s leave. She asked Halla where the loft ladder was, and Halla told her, and that seemed to do for both of them.

Cadan had gone.

He had gone in the morning, while Riarhin was at the hurdles, down the second road the way Unstep had gone, with his lantern unlit on his belt and his ropes on his shoulder. He had told Dusana, not Riarhin, that he wished to follow the walker a little way, to see where the disturbance went when it left the hollow, and that he would be back before dark.

“He said a little way,” said Dusana. She was sitting on the edge of the loft with her legs over, and the raven was walking up and down the beam above her head, tick-tick, tick-tick, its claws on the wood. “I said how little. He said as little as was needed.”

Riarhin looked at the second road from the loft door, going out of the hollow between its hedges into the grey.

“Did he say which way he thought it went?”

“Down,” said Dusana. “He said it went down.” She looked up at the raven on the beam. It had stopped walking. It was standing at the end of the beam nearest the second road with its head stretched out and its feathers flat. “It’s been doing that since he left.”

Below them in the yard, Nonny was sitting by the pond with her feet in the water, telling Tamsin that if she was a ferrywoman she ought to be able to get the ball back that had gone in at the deep end. Tamsin was sitting on the bank with her hood back and her grey-green hands in her lap, looking at the pond.

After a while she took her boots off.

Riarhin watched her go in. She went in to the knee, and the waist, and stood in the brown water with Nonny shrieking on the bank, and then she took a breath, a slow one, and put her face under, and stayed.

She stayed a long time. Nonny stopped shrieking. Halla came to the door of the house with her hands floury and stood there. Wren, on the wall, stopped humming.

Tamsin came up with the ball.

She stood in the pond with the water running off her grey-green neck and her cropped head, and held the ball up, and her eyes in the grey light were gold, and she was laughing.

Chapter 34 — One mouth closed

Cadan came back after dark with his coat torn at the shoulder and nothing to say about the tear.

He sat on a crate in the byre loft with a cup of Halla’s goat milk going cold in his hands and told what he had seen, in order, as he had been taught to tell things in his house: what came first, what came next, nothing that he had not seen himself. Riarhin listened and kept it.

The walker went out of the hollow by the second road and down. The second road went down a long way, between hedges and then between banks and then between walls of rock, and it came at the bottom to the same turn between two banks that sent a man back to the third stone. The walker went through the turn backwards and did not come out at the third stone. He went on. Cadan had followed him to the turn and through it, and had come out at the third stone, and had gone back down by the low road Wren had shown them and followed again. Three times. The third time, at the turn, before the stone, he had seen the door again, low in the face of the hill, shut. The walker had been standing with his back to it.

“Standing?”

“Standing. Then he stepped back. Towards it. And I was at the stone.” Cadan turned the cup in his hands. “The disturbance goes down that road. Everything I could see of it goes that way, the way water goes to a drain. It goes to the door. It comes up again from the door. The hollow is on its way.”

“Since the summer,” said Halla, from the foot of the loft ladder, where she had been standing with a lamp.

“Since the summer,” said Cadan.

“And the Weir?” said Aurine.

“The Weir hangs on it.” He put the cup down. “There is a place on the turn, between the banks, where the ground has a seam in it. I put my hand on it. The Weir is on the other side of the seam. I could feel the mill-race running through it, the way you feel a pipe in a wall. It is being drawn down. Slowly.”

“Like the willows,” said Riarhin.

“Like the willows.”

There was a silence in the loft. The raven, on its beam, had put its head under its wing.

“The snake cut the second,” said Aurine.

They looked at her. She was sitting cross-legged among her packets with her knees up and her stained hands loose between them, and she did not look up.

“It’s a thing they say,” she said. “Isoka’s people. An old thing. The spider wove the first, the snake cut the second, the raven forgot the third. About cords. Nobody knows what cords. It’s what you say when you cut something away that’s killing the rest.” She looked up. “If the Weir is hung on that thing by a seam, the seam can be cut.”

“And then?” said Riarhin.

“And then the Weir’s not hung on it.”

“And the weight that was on the Weir?”

Aurine looked at her steadily.

“Goes somewhere else,” she said. “I don’t know where. I’ll not pretend I do.”

They spent a day on the warning before they spent an hour on the cut, and Brann thought the day was wasted, and said so, and helped anyway.

Merel went up to the Weir with word. She went up the Weir road with a sack on her back and came down again by evening with her husband’s answer, which was that the mill would be stopped and the wheel chained, and everyone in the street told, and they would be in the chapel, which had stone walls, and if the grey came up the street they would go out of the back of it towards the hill. He had sent a twist of flour with her for Halla, which Halla took without a word and put on the shelf beside the salt.

The hollow had its own warning, because Wren said so.

“It goes somewhere else,” she said at supper, from the step. “That’s what she said. Somewhere else is here. We’re on the road it goes down.” She hummed. “It might be here.”

“Might be,” said Brann.

“Might be anywhere. Might be here.” Wren looked at Halla. “The goats should be out of the byre.”

So the goats were turned out of the byre into the top field, the far side of the house from the lane, and the children were told they would sleep that night in the top field too, under a hurdle-roof Brann made against the field wall, with Halla. Riarhin helped carry the bedding. Nonny thought it was a great adventure. Bel said nothing and carried his own blanket and would not let anyone else carry it, and at the top field he spread it on the ground in a careful square under the hurdle-roof and lay down on it at once, at midday, with his eyes open.

Halla would not stay in the field. She had the bread in the oven. She would come up when it was out.

The cut was at the turn.

There was not much to see there either. Riarhin had stopped expecting there to be. Two banks of earth with grass on their tops and roots hanging out of their faces, and between them a path of beaten earth going down and turning left, and on the path, if you knew where to look, a line. It ran across the path from bank to bank at a slant. It was finer than a hair. When she crouched and put her face close to it she could see that the earth on the far side of it was a shade paler than the earth on the near side, as if two pieces of cloth had been sewn together that had been dyed in different vats.

Cadan put his hand on it and held it there with his eyes shut.

“Yes,” he said. “There. The race.”

Riarhin put her own hand down a little way along from his. She felt nothing. Earth, cold, a little damp. Then, because she was attending to it, something else: a faint steady pull in the palm of her hand, as when you hold your hand over the mouth of a jar that someone is drawing the air out of.

She took her hand away.

They placed themselves. Wren at the head of the turn, where she could see up the second road towards the hollow. Riarhin halfway between the turn and the hollow, where she could run either way. Dusana at the top of the second road at the hollow’s edge, with the raven, where she could see the house and the lane and the top field and call. Tamsin had gone up to the Weir with Merel, to be there if the street had to be cleared, because she could breathe in it and nobody else could.

Aurine knelt at the seam with her knife out of its roll.

“You hold it,” she said to Cadan.

“I will hold it.”

“Thin?”

“Thin.” He looked at Riarhin, up the path. She nodded. “Tell me when to let it go.”

“When she says.” Riarhin pointed at Aurine.

“When I say,” said Aurine. “Not before. Not if it looks bad. When I say.”

“When you say,” said Cadan.

He knelt on the far side of the seam from Aurine and put both his hands down on it, flat, side by side, and bowed his head. And went thin.

Riarhin had seen it twice now, in the Rain Doors and at the drain under Farieth’s grey rooms, and she did not like it any better for having seen it. There was no change in him that the eye could fix on. He was there, kneeling, grey habit and shaven head and long hands on the earth. And then the light went through him a little. The roots hanging out of the far bank showed through his shoulder, faintly, as you see the shape of a hand through a blind. His lantern, on his belt, slid off the hook, because the hook was not holding it, and dropped onto the path with a small sound, and rolled, and lay there.

Under his hands the seam opened.

Not much. A finger’s width. A dark line across the path, slanting from bank to bank, and out of it, up, cold, the smell of the inside of a stone jar.

Aurine drew the knife along it.

She drew it the way she had drawn it down Merel’s arm, once, quick and shallow, from one bank to the other, kneeling and shuffling along on her knees to keep the cut straight. The knife did not seem to meet anything. It went along the dark line through the air above the earth, and where it had gone the line was not dark any more.

“Now,” she said.

Cadan let go.

For a breath nothing happened.

Then the pull in the earth was gone. Riarhin felt it go from where she stood, twenty paces up the path, through the soles of her boots: a slackening, as when a rope you have been leaning back on is let go at the other end and you have to take a step to keep your feet. The air in the turn changed. The cold stone-jar smell went out of it.

From the Weir, far off and up, faint, a bell rang. Once. The chapel bell. That was the agreed thing. Once for still here.

Aurine sat back on her heels with the knife in her hand and let her breath go.

“There,” she said.

Riarhin heard Dusana call.

It came down the second road from the hollow’s edge, high and sharp, one word, her name, the first time Dusana had used it to shout. Then the raven. It went up off Dusana’s shoulder at the top of the road with a harsh, tearing cry, and turned in the air over the hollow, and turned again, and did not come down.

Riarhin ran.

The lane was going.

She saw it from the top of the second road, where Dusana stood with both hands at her mouth. The grey patch she had made the day before with Brann’s stone, the table-sized blank, was not table-sized. It was spreading. It went out from its edges across the lane the way water spreads across a floor, in a slow flat tongue, and where it went the grass and the earth went grey and flat and blank, and the hurdles along the lane leaned into it and lay down and were not there. It had reached the hedge on the near side. The hedge was going into it. It was moving down the lane towards the yard.

Towards the byre.

The byre was empty. The goats were in the top field. The children were in the top field.

Halla was in the house with the bread.

Riarhin went down the lane on the hedge side, where the grey was not yet, and the earth under her feet felt wrong, too soft, as if it were deciding. She went into the yard. The grey was at the byre’s end wall. The end wall of the byre, the one against the lane, was leaning, and as she came round the corner its stones began to go grey from the bottom, course by course, the mortar first, and the wall came away from the roof-beam with a long groan of timber.

The roof-beam ran from the byre into the house. One beam, the great one, a whole tree trunk barked and squared, that carried the byre roof and went on through the gable into the long room and carried that too. The byre end of it was dropping.

Halla was in the long room. Riarhin could see her through the open door, at the oven with the long peel in her hands, turning round slowly, the way she did everything, to see what the noise was.

Riarhin got under the beam.

She did it without deciding to. She was under it in the byre doorway with her back against the doorpost and her hands up, and the beam came down onto her hands and her shoulders like a cart onto a jack, and her knees went, and she stopped them going. The weight was enormous. It came down through her arms into her shoulders and her back and her hips, and her thigh, the right one, the one that had been cut in the north and stiffened on the ridge, took it and began to shake.

She set her feet.

She did it carefully. She did it as if each foot were something she had been handed to hold. Square. Both of them square under her, hip-wide, the weight down the middle and not to the side. She thought of nothing. She thought, very clearly, of a man walking backwards after a cart in the dark with a pole in his hands, and a bowman on a roof, and the man moving sideways towards a hedge to spoil the bowman’s line, and the scarred leg taking the sideways weight and folding.

She did not move sideways. She kept it straight down.

“Halla,” she said. It came out flat and quiet. “Come out. Now.”

Halla came out.

She came out slowly. She could not come out any other way; her knees were bad and the floor of the long room was uneven. She came across it with the peel still in her hand and the loaf still on the peel, and through the door, and past Riarhin under the beam, and Riarhin heard her breathing and heard the timber groan over both their heads, and the beam came down another finger’s width onto her hands, and her thigh shook.

Halla stopped in the yard. She turned round with the loaf.

“Go on,” said Riarhin.

“You,” said Halla.

“Go on.”

Halla went on. Up the yard towards the top field, slowly, the peel held out in front of her like a lantern.

Riarhin stood under the beam. She could not let it go without it coming down; she could not hold it for long. She looked along it into the long room, the table with the door across two trestles, the crock, the shelf with the salt and the twist of flour from the Weir, and down the yard at the lane, where the grey had reached the byre door and was coming in across the floor of the byre, flat and slow, towards her feet.

Then Wren was there.

She came down the lane at a walk, on the grey. Riarhin saw her come. She walked on it the way the raven had walked on the mist at Sefe’s marker, without looking down, humming her three notes, and she walked straight to the byre’s end wall where the grey had taken the stones and put her hand flat on what was left of it, and leaned.

The grey stopped.

It did not go back. It stopped where it was, at the byre door, a hand’s breadth from Riarhin’s boot, and lay there, flat and blank, the way water lies when the floor levels out.

“Now come out from under,” said Wren. She did not stop humming. “Not fast. Step back into the house. It’ll come down. Let it.”

Riarhin stepped back into the long room.

The beam came down. The byre end of it hit the floor of the byre with a crash that shook dust out of the thatch over her head, and the byre roof came down after it, and the gable groaned and held, and the house end of the beam stayed up, cocked at an angle over the long room like a man leaning on a stick.

Wren took her hand off the wall. The grey stayed where it was.

Aurine came down the second road at a run, with Cadan behind her walking, not running, his lantern back on his belt and his face grey, and Dusana met them at the top and told them in four words, and Aurine stopped running.

She came into the yard and stood and looked at the byre.

It was half there. The house end of it stood, and the hay in the loft over it, and Aurine’s packets laid out in rows on the crate. The lane end was a heap of fallen roof-timber and thatch half sunk in a level grey that ran from the byre door up the lane to the gap in the bank and lay there, flat, where the lane had been. The hedge on the near side was gone. The hurdles were gone.

From the Weir, far off, the bell rang again. Once.

Merel came down the low road in the evening with Tamsin and stood at the edge of the yard with her grey-green hands clasped in front of her and told them that the willows had stopped. Nine. The two that had gone were still gone. The other nine stood. The race had come up to a proper running by afternoon and her husband had unchained the wheel, and the far bank was where it had been at noon and had not moved since. The chapel bell had rung twice because that was what they had agreed. Her husband sent his thanks.

She said it to Aurine. Then she looked at the byre, and at the grey where the lane had been, and at Brann, who was standing on the edge of it with his hands hanging, and she stopped saying it.

Brann did not look at her.

“You saved yours,” he said, to the grey. “With ours.”

“Brann,” said Halla. She was sitting on the mounting block with the loaf on her knees. It was a good loaf. She had not let go of it all afternoon.

“With ours,” said Brann. “That’s what she did.” He turned round at last and looked at Aurine. “You said it goes somewhere else. You knew it’d come here.”

“I said I didn’t know where,” said Aurine.

“You knew it might.”

“Yes.”

“And you did it.”

“Yes.”

Brann looked at her a long time. Then he looked at Riarhin, standing in the door of the long room with her arms hanging and her shoulders on fire and her right thigh shaking, still, in little runs, like a horse after a hard gallop.

“And you held the beam up,” he said. “For Halla.”

“Yes.”

He opened his mouth. He shut it. He walked away up the yard towards the top field, where Bel was sitting on the field wall with his blanket round his shoulders, watching, and lifted the boy off the wall and carried him.

Halla broke the loaf on her knee and held out half.

“They’ve a baby down there,” she said to nobody. “At the Weir. Merel’s baby.” She held the bread out further. “Take it. It’s still warm.”

Riarhin took it. Her hands were shaking. She held it in both of them and did not eat it for a while.

Wren was sitting on the step of the long room, half in and half out, with her back against the doorpost where Riarhin had stood under the beam. She was looking at the grey where the lane had been. She was not humming.

“He’ll not come by now,” she said.

“Who?”

“Unstep. That was his road.” Wren looked at the grey. “There’s no road there now for him to walk.”

Chapter 35 — The closed door again

The keeper was at the turn when they came down to it in the morning, as though she had been there all night, which Wren said she probably had.

She was a stooped woman in a long coat of oiled black stuff that hung to her boots, with a hood pushed back off grey hair cropped as short as Aurine’s. She was sitting on the bank above the turn with her knees drawn up and a lantern on the grass beside her. The lantern was lit. Its flame was blue, a cold pale blue like the inside of ice, and it stood perfectly upright in its glass and did not move, though there was a little wind along the banks that morning, enough to stir the roots hanging from their faces.

“Osk,” said Wren, and stopped humming. “She keeps the door.”

“I keep the road to it,” said the woman. Her voice was low and dry and she did not get up. “Nobody keeps the door.” She looked at Cadan. “You’ve been down four times. Five.”

“Six,” said Cadan.

“Six. And come out at the stone every time.” She looked at Riarhin. “And you cut the Weir off it. Yesterday.”

“Aurine cut it.”

“You stood there.” Osk looked down at the turn, at the place where the seam had been and was now only a faint paleness across the earth, like a healed scar. “It was a good cut. Clean.” She looked at the hollow, up the second road. “It went to Halla’s.”

“Yes.”

Osk nodded. She did not say anything else about it. She reached into her coat and took out a small knife and a piece of wood and began to whittle, slowly, shaving off curls that dropped onto the grass by the blue lantern.

Riarhin went down with Cadan the seventh time.

She had asked Osk, first, whether it was safe, and Osk had said that nothing in the place was safe and that the turn was no less safe than the rest of it, and that the worst that had happened to anybody yet was being put back at the third stone with wet feet. Riarhin had asked her whether anybody had ever got past the turn, and Osk had stopped whittling and looked at her for a long moment and said, “Some,” and gone back to it.

They went roped. Cadan’s tarred rope, from his waist to hers, a dozen paces of it, and from Riarhin’s waist back up the path to Dusana, who sat on the bank beside Osk with the raven inside her coat and the end of the line round her wrist. Two pulls, Riarhin said, and Cadan comes back. Whatever he can see. Two pulls.

“Two pulls,” said Cadan. “And if you are not there to pull?”

“Dusana pulls.”

“And if I am not there to be pulled?”

“Then I come and get you.”

He considered that. Then he nodded, as though she had supplied a figure he had been missing.

The turn went down between its banks and left. Riarhin attended to it. She had walked it the day before on the way to the cut and it had been the same then: forty paces of beaten earth sloping down; a root as thick as her wrist hanging out of the left-hand bank at the height of her shoulder, forked at the end like a hand with two fingers; a white stone half buried in the path where the turn began to bend; the bend itself, sharp, the banks closing in so that she could have touched both at once; and then the path straightening and going down into grey.

She kept it all. She would have it as long as she had anything.

Cadan went ahead. He went down the path with the rope paying out behind him from his waist, and Riarhin went after with the slack in her hand, and at the white stone he stopped.

“Here,” he said. “From here.”

He did not go thin. He walked on, round the bend, in the ordinary way, and she followed, and the banks closed in, and she put her hand out to the left one and felt the earth cold and gritty under her fingers, and round the bend the path straightened and went down, and there was the grey.

And there was the third stone.

It was at her feet. Small and tilted. Behind her, the gravel of Sefe’s road ran away to the second stone along the ridge. In front of her the ground fell away into the grey, and the cold flat stone-jar smell came up out of it, as it had come up the first day.

Cadan was standing beside her with the rope round his waist. The rope went from him to her and from her back, down, into the grey below the third stone, taut, and somewhere down there at the end of it was Dusana on a bank above a turn an hour’s walk away.

“Yes,” said Cadan. “Like that.”

They went back down by Wren’s low road and the gap in the bank that was a gap only if you were going home, which took the rest of the morning, and the rope came with them, which Riarhin had not been sure it would. Osk was still whittling. The pile of curls by the lantern had grown.

“Same?” she said.

“Same.”

“Tell me.”

Riarhin told her. She told it as she had kept it, every step: the forty paces, the forked root at the height of her shoulder, the white stone where the bend began, the bank cold under her fingers, the straight, the grey.

Osk stopped whittling.

“Again,” she said. “The bend.”

Riarhin told it again. The banks closing in. Her hand out to the left one. The earth cold and gritty under her fingers.

“The left one,” said Osk. “Only the left.”

“Yes.”

“The right bank was there?”

Riarhin went back to it. She found it where she had put it. The bend. Her left hand on the bank. Her right hand had held the slack of the rope. She had not put it out. She had looked ahead at Cadan’s back and the straight going down, and to her right—

To her right there had been nothing she had attended to. A shape. Earth, she had thought. A bank.

She had not looked.

“I don’t know,” she said.

Osk nodded slowly, as though that were an answer, and a good one.

“Every one of you,” she said, “that’s come down here and gone round and come out at the stone, you’ve all said the bend, and you’ve all said the left bank. Not one of you’s said the right.” She put the knife away in her coat. “It’s not there. Not the whole way. There’s a bit of the bend where there’s no right bank and there never was. That’s the door. You walk past it with your face on the other side and the road comes back round on itself, because that’s what a shut door is, to a road. A place it can’t go. So it goes back to where it started.”

“And if you look?” said Riarhin.

“Then you see it’s shut.”

Cadan went down the eighth time with his face to the right.

He did not tell anyone he was going to. He stood at the white stone with the rope at his waist and Riarhin behind him with the slack in her hand, and he turned his head, and walked on into the bend with his face turned to the right-hand side, the way a man walks past a shop window he is interested in, and Riarhin, behind him, turned her head too.

The left bank was where it had been. Cold earth, roots.

The right bank was not there.

For three paces, perhaps four, there was no bank on the right. There was a wall. Rough grey stone, old, set without mortar, going up into the dark under an overhang of hill. And in the wall, low, so that a tall man would have had to stoop, a door.

It was as Cadan had drawn it on Sefe’s slate. A rectangle with a rounded top. Old wood, black, with iron bands. No handle that she could see, no latch. Shut.

It was not quite shut.

There was a line of something along its edge, on the hinge side, a crack no wider than a knife-blade, and out of the crack came warm air. She felt it on her face as she passed, like the breath from an oven door. It smelt of honey. It smelt, under the honey, of iron gone to rust, the way a gate smells after rain.

And through the crack, for as long as it took her to take one step, she saw—

A hand.

It lay on something pale, a heap of something that was not straw and not cloth, a heap of cords, a great many cords knotted together, knot on knot, the way a fishing net is knotted, and lying on them. The hand was a woman’s. Long. The fingers a little curled, as fingers curl in sleep. The skin of it was neither dark nor pale; it was the colour of the light that came through the crack, which was a dim gold. And she heard, very faint and very slow, so slow she could have fitted three of her own breaths into it, breathing.

Then the third stone was at her feet.

Cadan was already turning round.

He had the rope in both hands and he was turning back towards the grey below the third stone, towards the long way down by the low road, and his face—

She had seen his face do a great many things. She had seen it do pleasure, at the waystation the day he came through the shut path with Vessa on his arm, and it had embarrassed her how glad she had been. She had seen it do nothing at all, which it did most often, while he held a knife to a man’s throat and waited to be told. She had never seen it do this. It was alight. There was colour in it. His eyes, which were of no particular colour, were wide and wet, and he was breathing quickly through his mouth, like a man who has run.

“Again,” he said. “Now. It is opening. It was not open before. We go again now and I will hold the right side and you will go through—”

“No.”

“It is opening, Riarhin.”

“It was open the width of a knife.” She had the rope. She had not let go of it. She took a turn of it round her own hand. “You don’t know what it was doing. Nor do I. We go back to Osk and we tell her and we sleep.”

“She is lying there.” His voice had gone high, and soft, and quick, nothing like the level voice of the house. “She is lying there and she is asleep and the door is opening. I have been in the house twenty-six years. I have kept the Bell twenty-six years. I have never—” He stopped. “You saw her hand.”

“I saw it.”

“Then you know.”

“I know I saw a hand.” She kept her voice level for both of them. “Cadan. What did I tell you, at the white stone?”

He looked at her.

“Two pulls,” he said, after a moment.

“And what happens on two pulls?”

“I come back.”

“Then come back.”

She pulled. Twice. Short and hard, the rope going taut between them and slack and taut again. He felt it at his waist. She watched him feel it. She watched the light go down in his face, slowly, the way a lamp goes down when someone turns the wick, until his face was the face she knew, long and smooth and of no particular expression, and his hands on the rope loosened.

“Yes,” he said. “I come back.”

He coiled the rope. He did it carefully, the length of it, from his waist to hers, and when he reached her he stopped, and stood with the coil in his hands a pace from her.

“If you had not pulled,” he said, “I would have gone.”

“I know.”

“You must always pull.” He said it quite simply. “When it is like that. You must pull.”

They came back down to the turn by the low road in the late afternoon with their legs aching. Dusana had pulled the line in long since; it had come back up the path to her slack, she said, and dry, as though nobody had been on the end of it. Osk had built a small fire on the bank beside her blue lantern. The two lights did not mix. The fire’s light was yellow and moved; the lantern’s was blue and stood still, and where they overlapped on the grass the grass was a colour Riarhin had no name for.

Riarhin sat down by the fire and told Osk what they had seen, all of it, in order. The wall. The door. The crack on the hinge side. The warm air. The honey and the rust. The hand on the cords.

Osk listened with her face in the firelight. At the hand she shut her eyes. At the cords she opened them.

“Knotted,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Like a net.”

“Yes.”

Osk looked at Dusana, who was sitting with the raven on her knee. The raven had come out of her coat when Riarhin began. It sat on her knee with its feathers flat and its head down and did not move while Riarhin spoke, and at the word hand it turned its head away from the fire, towards the dark, and kept it there.

“Your people tie cords,” Osk said to Dusana.

“Some of my people. Not my family.” Dusana stroked the raven’s back with one finger. “My grandmother tied them. There was a woman in her circle who slept in a wagon on the ninth night with a cord round her wrist. Nobody was to wake her. I used to go and look at her through the curtain.” She looked at the fire. “I was about Bel’s age. She looked like anybody asleep.”

“The door opens because she’s dreaming it,” Osk said. “She dreams it shut, it’s shut. That’s what it’s been, as long as I’ve kept the road. Shut. A road that goes round. Since the summer it goes round faster. Since the summer there’s something hung on it, pulling, and it goes round faster and harder and everything near it goes round with it.” She looked at Cadan. “And today it opened the width of a knife.”

“Is that the pull?” said Riarhin. “The thing hung on it. Is that what’s opening it?”

“I don’t know.” Osk turned the stick she had been whittling in her fingers. It was a peg, Riarhin saw, a tent peg, smooth and pointed. “I know she’s been asleep since before there was a road. I know the road goes round because she’s asleep. I know the thing hung on her since the summer is pulling everything else round with it and down. Your Weir. Halla’s lane. Wherever it goes next.” She put the peg on the pile beside her. There were a great many pegs on the pile. “I know if it goes on, it doesn’t stop at Halla’s.”

Nobody said anything.

Cadan was sitting a little apart, on the bank, with his coiled rope on his knees and his lantern on the grass beside him with the white candle in it unlit. He was looking down the turn, towards the place where the right-hand bank was not.

Riarhin watched him look.

After a while he felt her watching, and turned his head, and met her eyes, and nodded once, as he had nodded at the white stone. Then he looked away again down the turn, and his hands on the rope did not move, and his face in the firelight was quite still.

Chapter 36 — What the sisters feared

Osk’s page was kept in a tin.

It was a flat tin, the kind tobacco comes in, with the paint worn off the lid down to the metal except for a red rim, and she took it out of the inside of her coat the next morning at Halla’s table, where they had all come up to eat, and set it down among the bowls.

“There’s a house of us,” she said. “Keepers. Not many. There’s been one on this road since before Halla, and one before that. This came down with the lantern.” She put her finger on the tin. “The one before me said it was copied from a book. The one before her said it was copied from a copy. Nobody’s seen the book. Read it if you like. It won’t help.”

Riarhin opened the tin.

The page inside was folded in four, soft as cloth with handling, and brown at the folds. She unfolded it on the table between the bread and the cheese. It was covered on one side with writing in a close small hand, faded to the colour of weak tea, and the writing went in lines, and the lines were broken.

She could read it.

That was the first wrong thing. She did not know the letters. They were not Lethai or Tulkani or any hand of the Nine Kingdoms or the north, and she had seen a great many hands. She did not know the letters, and her eyes went along the first line, and she knew what it said.

… nine hundred wells, the Mother of Wells, drowned by hooves and flame, by the three who came in the shape of spider, snake and raven.

She looked up. Aurine, across the table, had leaned over to look, and her face had gone still.

“You can read that,” said Aurine.

“So can you.”

“I can’t read. Not that. I can’t read that.” Aurine looked at the page. “I can read that.”

“Everybody can,” said Osk. “That’s what the one before me said. Nobody knows the letters and everybody can read it.” She broke a piece off the loaf. “I told you it wouldn’t help.”

It was broken all the way down. The lines stopped in the middle and began again somewhere else. Words were gone out of them, so that what was left stood about on the page like posts where a fence had been. Riarhin read it through once and kept it, the way she kept everything she attended to, and found that what she had kept was not much more use than the page.

The spider wove the first cord. The snake cut the second. The raven forgot the third.

“That’s ours,” said Aurine quietly. “That one. That’s what Isoka’s people say.”

They rang it eight times. The ninth toll not toll. Was answer. The raven whispered the answer to the dreamer, but the dreamer slept.

Bury it. Seal it. If wakes, world burns.

… could be killed. could only contained. containment required sacrifice …

The raven wept when the waters rose.

And near the bottom, standing on its own on a line of its own with a space above it and below, a word. Riarhin read it, and knew it for a place, and knew, without anything on the page to tell her, that it was the name of the place the wells had been.

Khasurah.

Dusana had not leaned over to look. She was sitting at the end of the bench with her bowl in her lap and the raven on the table by her elbow, picking at a crust. When Riarhin said the word aloud, not meaning to, Dusana’s spoon stopped.

“Say it again,” she said.

Riarhin said it again.

Dusana put her spoon down in her bowl.

“That’s not how she said it,” she said. “My grandmother. She said it softer. She said it like it had a sh in it.” She looked at the page from where she sat, without reaching for it. “She said kethra was a word from there. From a place her grandmother’s grandmother’s people came from, a long way back, before the roads. She said they talked differently there and it all got lost but a few words. That was one.” She picked up the spoon again. She did not eat. “Other. That’s what it meant there. Somebody who came from somewhere else.”

“Did she say what happened to it?”

“She said it wasn’t there any more.” Dusana looked at the raven, which had stopped picking at its crust and was standing very still on the table with its head on one side, looking at the page. “She said it was under water. I thought she meant a lake.”

The raven put its beak down on the page.

It did not peck. It laid the tip of its beak on the paper, lightly, at the line about the waters rising, and held it there. Its eye was half shut. Then it took its beak away and hopped off the table onto the bench beside Dusana and pushed its head in under her arm, and stayed there.

Nobody said anything for a while. Halla, at the oven, had stopped with the peel in her hands.

“Well,” said Dusana at last, to the top of the raven’s head. “That’s ravens.”

But she did not say it the way she had said it on the ridge.

Brann came in from the yard in the middle of it with straw in his hair and said that the goats had broken out of the top field in the night because the wall there had a gap at the corner and Mother had found it, and that four of them were in the kitchen garden eating the kale, and could somebody who was not reading come and help.

Everybody who was not reading went. Riarhin folded the page into its tin and gave the tin to Osk and went too.

It took the rest of the morning. The goats resisted being driven out of the kale, and the gap at the corner of the top field had to be stopped, which meant hurdles, which meant going up the lane for the hurdles Brann had stacked by the gap in the bank, which meant going round the grey where the lane had been, the long way, by the pond and the second road. The grey lay where it had stopped, flat and blank, the edge of it a hand’s breadth from the byre door. Nonny wanted to throw a stone into it. Halla said if she did she would have no supper for a week, and Nonny weighed the stone in her hand for a while, and then put it in her pocket.

They needed a new pen, too, with the byre half gone. Riarhin and Brann and Wren built it against the house wall under the cocked end of the great beam, out of hurdles lashed to stakes, and while they built it Brann did not look at her and did not speak to her except to say hold that and there and no, the other way, and she held it and put it there and turned it the other way.

When it was up he stood back and looked at it. Then he looked at the beam over their heads, cocked at its angle, the byre end of it buried in the fallen thatch.

“That’ll want a prop,” he said.

“Yes.”

“A good one. Oak.” He looked at her for the first time since the day before. “There’s oak in the top wood. I’ll want somebody on the other end of the saw.”

“All right.”

He nodded and went off to find the saw. Wren, sitting on the new pen’s top rail with her heels against the hurdle, began to hum again.

They talked about it in the afternoon in Aurine’s half of the loft, the half that still stood, with the hay smelling of summer and the lantern on its beam and Aurine’s packets on the crate.

Aurine had the page. She had asked Osk for it and Osk had given her the tin, and she sat with it open on her knee, not reading it, her stained finger resting on the line about the cords.

“Isoka’s people don’t tell it like that,” she said. “Not all of it. We say the three. We say there was a sleep, and that she’s in it. We say the sleep was for a reason.” She looked up. “I was taught the reason. I was taught it by a woman in Ubral who had it from a woman in Ecktoria, and she said the sleep was put on her because she would have gone to meet it. The thing in the page. The thing that has to be kept. She dreamed of going to meet it. Face to face. And the other two thought if she met it, it would wake.”

“What is it?” said Riarhin.

“I don’t know. I don’t know what it is.” Aurine closed the tin. “The woman in Ubral said it was the thing that makes a good tool better. That it’s in every workshop. That it’s in the wheel and the axle and the mill, and it’s good, it feeds people, until it gets to where it won’t stop. Where it wants the whole world to be one good tool with nothing in it that isn’t the tool.” She turned the tin over in her hands. “I thought she was mad. She was a bit mad. I thought she was mad and right.”

“And they put her to sleep for it.”

“They put her to sleep so she wouldn’t go and meet it.” Aurine’s voice was even. “And she’s been asleep since. And whatever it is has been asleep, or kept, or not woken, since. And people have had mills.” She looked at Riarhin. “I’m not saying they were right. I’m saying they were afraid of something. And they were the ones who could see it. I can’t. You can’t.”

“I can see the Weir’s willows,” said Riarhin.

“Yes.”

“I can see Halla’s lane.”

“Yes. I cut that. I know what I cut.”

“I can see Merel going up and down that road for the rest of her life because she’s the only one who can,” Riarhin said. “And the old woman at Sefe’s who can’t say what she wants. And the girl from the fair. And whatever’s next, past Halla’s. Osk said it doesn’t stop at Halla’s.” She kept her voice level. “It’s not asleep, Aurine. Whatever Nereth hung on it. That isn’t asleep. It’s pulling.”

“I know it is.”

“Then what do we do? Cut every place off it, one at a time, and let the weight go to the next?”

Aurine did not answer.

Cadan was sitting against the far wall of the loft with his legs crossed and his hands in his sleeves. He had not spoken since they came up. Dusana was by the loft door with the raven, looking out at the hollow.

“There’s a thing I don’t know,” said Riarhin. “I don’t know if waking her stops it. I don’t know if anything does. Osk doesn’t know. The page doesn’t say. You don’t.” She looked at Cadan. “He doesn’t. He thinks he does. He doesn’t.”

Cadan did not move.

“I would like,” he said, after a moment, in his level voice, “to say that I do. I find I cannot.”

“Then before anybody wakes anything,” Riarhin said, “I want to know what happens if she’s only let a little way. For a little while. If the door goes round slower. If the thing that’s hung on it slips. If anything does anything.” She looked at Aurine. “Osk’s people can let the road go. One part of it. She said so. I want to see what happens when they do.”

Aurine looked at the tin in her hands.

“And if the world burns,” she said.

“Then it’ll burn a little,” said Dusana from the door, without turning round. “For a little while.”

Aurine looked at her back. Then, unexpectedly, she laughed, short and hard, the way she had laughed at the farmhouse table in Theona with her forehead on her arms.

“That’s ravens,” she said.

Osk said yes.

She said it at the turn that evening, by her blue lantern, with her pegs in a pile beside her. She said there were three of them on the road now, keepers, her and two others she had sent for from up the low roads, and that between the three of them they could let one of the outer returns go. Not the door. The door was not theirs. The road that came back round to the third stone when it met the door. They could hold that open. For a short while. As long as a lamp-wick took to burn down a thumb’s width, she said; she had never held it longer and she did not know what happened if you did.

“And when it closes?”

“It closes. The road comes back round. Like before.” She picked up a peg and turned it. “If anything’s different after, you’ll see it.”

“Is it dangerous?”

Osk looked at her.

“I told you,” she said. “Nothing here is safe.” She put the peg down. “Tomorrow. Bring everybody who’s going to stand anywhere. I want to know where they all are. I want to count them before and I want to count them after.”

Walking back up the second road to the hollow in the dark, Riarhin found Dusana beside her. The raven was not on her shoulder. It was inside her coat, a lump at her breast, and every so often the lump moved.

“It doesn’t like it,” Dusana said.

“The test?”

“Any of it.” She walked a while. “It walked out over the mist on Sefe’s road like it owned the road. It put its beak on that page like it knew the page. It won’t go near that door.” She stopped on the road. The hollow was below them, the long house with one yellow window, the new pen against its wall, the grey where the lane had been lying flat and pale in the dark like a pond with no reflection in it. “What’s it going to do, if she wakes?”

“I don’t know.”

“No.” Dusana put her hand flat on the lump at her breast. “I didn’t think you would.”

She stood there a while longer. Then she walked on down into the hollow, and Riarhin walked beside her, and neither of them said anything else.

Chapter 37 — A moment without return

Osk’s two keepers came down the low roads in the night and were sitting by her lantern in the morning when the company came down to the turn, each with a lantern of their own.

One was a young man with a broken nose and enormous red hands, who said his name was Haske and then said nothing else all day. The other was a woman of Halla’s age, thin as a rake, with a crooked shoulder and a voice like a gate that wanted oil. She was called Toller. She looked at the company coming down the second road and said, “That’s a lot of them,” in the tone of a woman counting plates, and Osk said, “I asked them all,” and Toller said, “I can see you did.”

The three lanterns stood on the bank side by side. Three blue flames, upright, unmoving, the same height to a hair. The fire Osk had made the evening before had gone to ash. Nobody relit it.

“Where are you all going to be?” said Osk.

Riarhin told her. She had worked it out in the loft in the night with Aurine and Wren, and she said it now in order, and Osk listened, and nodded at each, and at the end of it she got up off the bank with the help of a peg stuck in the earth and walked down the turn and back, slowly, and put each of them where they were going to be with her own hand, the way a woman sets out cups.

Cadan at the white stone, where the bend began. He would go round the bend when it was let go, with his face to the right, and see what there was to see. Riarhin a pace behind him on the rope. Wren at the top of the turn, where the second road came down, because Wren could feel a road change under her feet the way other people feel weather in a knee. Aurine beside her with the satchel. Tamsin at the far end of the low road, at the gap in the bank, with a horn of Brann’s to blow if anything came out of the grey at Halla’s. Dusana on the bank by the lanterns, with the raven, and the end of Riarhin’s line round her wrist.

Osk counted them. She did it aloud, pointing with the peg.

“Six,” she said. “And us three. And the bird.” She looked at the raven on Dusana’s shoulder. “I’ll count the bird.”

“Count it,” said Dusana.

The keepers did not do anything Riarhin would have known for a working if she had not been told.

They sat down on the bank in a row behind their lanterns, Osk in the middle, and each of them put a hand flat on the grass in front of their lantern, and Haske took a stub of candle out of his coat and lit it from his blue flame, where it burned yellow, and stood it on a flat stone in front of Osk, and Osk put her thumb against the side of it, below the flame, to mark where it was.

“When it’s burned to my thumb,” she said, “we let go.”

Then the three of them bent their heads.

Riarhin, at the white stone with the rope at her waist, felt it go.

It was not like the cut. The cut had been a rope let go at the far end. This was a sound she could not hear. It was like the moment, in a house, when a clock that has been ticking in another room stops, and you did not know you had been hearing it until you were not. The air at the turn was the same air. The banks were the same banks. Something that had been going round, and round, under everything, at a pitch she had not known she was attending to, had stopped going round.

Cadan, at the white stone, had lifted his head like a dog.

“Now,” said Osk from the bank, not loud.

Cadan went round the bend with his face to the right.

Riarhin went after him, on the rope, with her face to the right too, and the banks closed in, and the left bank was cold earth under her fingers, and on the right—

The wall. The door, low in it, black wood, iron bands.

And they went on past it.

They went on. The path straightened and went down, and there was no third stone. There was the path, going down, beaten earth between banks of earth, and below them, ten paces down, where the grey had always been, there was more path. A turn to the left. A root hanging out of a bank. The path went on down past the door and out of the bend and into the hill like any path, and nothing sent them back.

Cadan stopped on it. He stood with his hands open at his sides and looked down the path that went on, and Riarhin heard him make a sound that was not a word.

“Look up,” said Riarhin.

He looked.

It was hung on the door.

She had not seen it the day before, in the one step she had been given past the crack. She saw it now because the door was not going round, and everything near it was standing still for once, the way a wheel’s spokes show when the wheel stops.

It was a stair.

It came down out of the overhang of the hill above the door at an angle that was wrong for any stair she had ever climbed, steep and turning, too steep for comfort, with a hollow worn in its stone at the height of a man’s shoulder where hands had gone, and a chip out of the edge of its third step. She knew it. She had stood at the foot of it in the Mistlands with Nereth beside her saying there shouldn’t be a division there. She had stood at the foot of a copy of it in the hill district on the far side of the first waystation, and gone up it, and come down at a grey stone with a hole worn through. It was Nereth’s stair. It was the thing the girl’s wards had answered from, and fastened, and could not unfasten.

It was not standing on anything. It hung. Its foot did not reach the ground. Its foot reached the door, at the hinge side, at the crack, and was jammed into the crack the way you jam the toe of a boot into a door to keep it from shutting. Where it went in, the black wood of the door was split. Around the split, the iron band had buckled outwards.

And from the stair, out and away in every direction, so fine she could only see them because everything had stopped, there went lines. Threads. Taut. Like the guys of a tent. One of them went up the turn, past Riarhin’s shoulder, towards the hollow. She could have touched it. She did not. It ran up the second road to Halla’s, and she knew without following it where it ended. Another had gone to the Weir and was not there now; there was a frayed end of something, short, hanging slack from the stair’s side where Aurine had cut. Others went off into the hill in directions she could not follow, a great many of them, and all of them were pulling, and all of them were pulling the stair, and the stair was pulling on the door.

“Cadan,” she said.

He was already reaching up.

He did not go thin. He went up on his toes on the path below the stair, a tall man reaching for a shelf, and put both hands on the stair’s lowest step where it went into the crack. And lifted.

It moved. A finger’s width. The foot of the stair came up out of the split in the door with a sound like a cork, and the door, released, shifted on its hinges by the same finger’s width, the split closing up, the iron band easing, and the threads that ran from the stair went slack, all of them, all at once, so that Riarhin saw them sag in the air like washing lines when someone lets the prop go.

Cadan set the stair down. Not in the crack. Beside it. On the stone of the wall beside the door, where its foot rested on a ledge, and hung there, still steep, still wrong, but not driven in.

“There,” he said. His voice shook. “There. It is not in the door. It is only near the door.”

From the top of the turn Riarhin heard Wren call out. Not a word. A long note, the first of her three, held, rising.

And then, from the far side of the hollow, faint, Brann’s horn. Once. Short.

Still here.

“Thumb,” said Osk, from the bank.

They waited about as long as it takes to boil an egg. The stub of candle on its flat stone had burned down to the ball of Osk’s thumb, and the yellow flame touched it, and Osk did not move her thumb, and said “Thumb,” and the three keepers lifted their hands off the grass.

The clock in the other room began to tick.

Riarhin felt it start. The going-round, under everything, at the pitch she had not known she heard. It started slow and came up to speed in three heartbeats, like a wheel that has been held and let go, and the path below her that went on down into the hill was not there. It was grey. The third stone was there, tilted, small, and behind her Sefe’s gravel road ran back along the ridge to the second stone, and Cadan was beside her, on the gravel, with his hands still up as if he were holding something.

She was already turning back.

Not for him. For the threads. She had kept them, the threads she had seen going out from the stair, slack, sagging like washing lines. She had kept the one that went past her shoulder to the hollow. And in the instant before the third stone, in the instant when the road came round and took them, she had seen that thread go taut again.

They came down to the turn by the low road at a run, or as near a run as the low road allowed, which was a fast walk with Wren in front.

Halla’s lane had not moved. Tamsin met them at the gap in the bank with Brann’s horn in her hand and said so. The grey lay where it had lain, a hand’s breadth from the new pen. It had not come on. It had not gone back. When the keepers let go, Tamsin said, it had been lying very still, stiller than it had lain since the beam came down, and the edge of it at the byre door had looked to her, with her eyes, which saw in the grey better than anyone’s, as though it had drawn back a little. A finger. Two. Then the horn-time came, and after the horn-time it had stopped drawing back, and lain still. And then—

“Then it was the same,” said Tamsin. “Like it was holding its breath and breathed.”

At the turn the stair was not in the door. Cadan had set it beside the door on a ledge of the wall, and it had stayed there when the road came round. Riarhin could not see it now. The road went round and the right bank was a bank and she could not see past it. But Osk, when she had heard it all, sat a long time with her hands on her knees, and then said that the road was going round slower. A little. She could tell by her pegs. She had put one in the bank at the white stone every morning for forty years, she said, and she knew how the bank felt to a peg, and today the earth took it easier.

“Slower,” said Riarhin.

“A little.”

“For how long?”

“For as long as the stair stays out of the door.” Osk looked at the turn. “It won’t stay out. You saw the lines. Everything it’s hung on is pulling it back. It’s working back now, a hair at a time. I can feel it in the bank.” She put her hand flat on the grass. “Couple of days, it’ll be back in the crack where it was. Pulling.”

“And if we let the road go again? And put it out again?”

“Then it’ll work back again.” Osk did not take her hand off the grass. “That’s a thing you could do every few days for the rest of your life. I’ve done worse for longer.” She looked up at Riarhin. “It won’t mend. Not while she’s dreaming the door shut. The door’s shut, the road goes round, the stair’s hung on the road. It’ll always work back. You saw it. While it was let go, it was only near the door. When she shuts it, it’s in.”

Riarhin had seen it. She had kept it. The stair hanging beside the door on its ledge, not driven in, and the door eased on its hinges, and the threads gone slack, and then the clock in the other room starting up, and the thread past her shoulder going taut.

Osk counted them.

She did it at the turn with the peg, as she had in the morning, pointing. Cadan. Riarhin. Wren. Aurine. Tamsin. Dusana.

She stopped.

Dusana was standing by the lanterns with both hands cupped against her breast and her face white.

The raven was in her hands.

It had been on her shoulder when the keepers let go. Dusana told it afterwards, sitting on the bank with the bird in her lap and Aurine kneeling beside her, in a flat quick voice, as if she were telling somebody else’s trouble. It had been on her shoulder, the way it always was, its claws in the cloth of her coat, and when the going-round stopped it had come off. Not flown. Fallen. It had fallen straight down off her shoulder the way a thing falls off a shelf when the house shakes, without opening its wings, and hit the grass and lain there on its side with its feet going.

“Like the ground had gone out from under it,” Dusana said. “Out of the air. Like it had been standing on something all this time and the something stopped.”

It had lain there with its feet going and its beak open, and she had picked it up, and it had not known her. That was what she said, flatly. It had not known her. It had looked at her with its eye and there was nobody in it.

And then the candle burned to Osk’s thumb, and the going-round started again, and the raven in her hands had shut its beak and stopped its feet and turned its head, and looked at her, and been the bird.

It was sitting in her lap now with its feathers fluffed up as if it were cold. It had its head pushed in against her stomach. Every so often it shivered.

Aurine had a hand on its back.

“It’s all right,” she said. “Its heart’s all right. It’s going fast but it’s all right.”

Dusana did not look at her. She looked at Riarhin.

“That was a thumb of candle,” she said.

“Yes.”

“That was the road let go a thumb.” Her voice was quite steady. “You want to let it go all the way.”

Riarhin did not answer.

There was nothing to answer with. She had seen the bird walk out over the mist on Sefe’s road with nothing under it, because something was going to be under it, and get there first. She had seen it put its beak on the page at the line about the waters. She had seen it turn its head away from the fire at the word hand. She did not know what it was. She knew where it walked.

“I don’t know what it’ll do,” she said.

“No.” Dusana put her hand over the bird. “You said that.”

Osk had come down the bank with her peg. She stood over them, and looked at the bird, and then she pointed at it with the peg, the way she had in the morning.

“Six,” she said. “And us three.” The peg moved. “And the bird.”

She went back up the bank to her lantern.

Haske had put the stub of candle out between his finger and thumb. Toller was already packing her lantern into a bag. The three blue flames had not moved all day and did not move now. When Riarhin looked back from the top of the turn, with the second road going up into the dark towards Halla’s, they were standing on the bank in a row, the same height to a hair, and Dusana was still sitting below them with her hands over the raven, not getting up.

Chapter 38 — The person who says no

She asked them one at a time, because that was the only way she knew how to ask anything.

She did not want to. She would have liked very much to stand on Halla’s mounting block in the yard with everybody in front of her and say it once, all of it, and have it done. She had seen it done. She had stood at the back of a hall in Valora and watched a queen do it, and the queen had done it well. She had stood at the edge of a meeting at Kelva’s mill in the Mistlands and watched a dozen people try to do it at once over the noise of the stones, and fail, and go back to work beside each other anyway.

She went to Halla first, because it was Halla’s house.

Halla was in the kitchen garden on her knees with a trowel, putting kale back in where the goats had pulled it up. She did not get up. Riarhin knelt on the path beside her, which was the only way to talk to Halla at the same height, and told her what Osk had said at the turn. The stair. The door. The threads. That it would work back, a hair at a time, until it was in the crack again, pulling. That letting the road go a thumb had taken the stair out for a couple of days and no more. That the only thing anybody could think of that would keep it out was for the door to open. That the door was shut because somebody behind it was asleep.

“And you’re going to wake her,” said Halla, firming the earth round a kale stalk with her thumbs.

“I don’t know yet. I want to know what it costs first.”

“It’ll cost.” Halla took up another plant. “Everything does. What does it cost me?”

“If we go, we’ll need the keepers to let the road go all the way. Not a thumb. For as long as it takes.” Riarhin looked at the hollow, at the three roads coming down into it and meeting by the pond. “Osk says it might go through here. All three roads. She says she doesn’t know what they’d do. She says if it were her house, she wouldn’t be in it.”

Halla put the plant in. She firmed it. Then she sat back on her heels with her earthy hands on her knees and looked at her house, the long low roof gone shaggy, the byre end fallen in, the great beam cocked over the long room with Brann’s new oak prop under it.

“Forty years,” she said.

Riarhin waited.

“I’ll go,” said Halla. “If it comes to it. I’ll not have the children in it.” She looked down at the kale. “I’ll want time. I’ll not leave it like this.”

Brann said no.

He said it in the top wood, with the saw between them and a fresh-cut oak lying on the trestles, and a crosscut halfway through it, and the sawdust on both their boots.

“You held the beam,” he said, “and I’m grateful for it. I’d not have Halla under that and that’s the truth. And I’ll say no.” He took his end of the saw again. “Pull.”

She pulled. He pushed. The saw went through the oak with its long breathing sound.

“I’ve been here eleven years,” he said, between strokes. “Halla took me in when I came down the low road with nothing. Wren was a girl. The children were born in that house. I built the wall Unstep took. I built it twice.” Pull. Push. “And now you want me to walk out of it with the goats so a monk can go and knock on a door.”

“I don’t want you to walk out of it. I want you not to be in it when—”

“When it goes, if it goes. Yes. I heard.” He stopped the saw and straightened his back with his hand on it. “I said no. I didn’t say I’d stop Halla. I didn’t say I’d stop the children.” He took the saw again. “I’ll carry what they want carrying. I’ll take them to wherever’s safe and I’ll come back. I’ll stay with the house.”

“Brann.”

“My house,” said Brann. “Pull.”

She pulled.

At the Weir it was Merel’s husband.

Riarhin did not go down; she could not. She sat on the slope above the willows where the air still held, with Tamsin beside her in her hood, and Merel went down with the question and came back up with the answer, and that was how it was done, with Merel going between.

His name was Joost. He was the miller. Merel had never said his name before, and when she said it now she said it carefully, as though it were something she had been carrying in her apron and did not want to drop.

“He says he’ll not leave the mill.”

“All right.”

“He says the wheel’s running and the race is up and the willows have stopped going and he’s not going to walk off and leave all that because something might happen to it.” Merel sat down on the slope. Her grey-green hands were in her lap. “He says if the grey comes up the street they’ll go to the chapel like before. He says that worked.”

“It did work.”

“He says send down flour sacks. Empty ones. And salt. And lamp-oil. He says if there’s a thing coming he wants the chapel stocked.” She looked at Riarhin. “That’s what he says yes to. Sacks and salt. He says no to the rest.”

“Then we send sacks and salt.”

Merel looked at her a moment longer. Then she nodded, and got up, and went off along the slope towards Sefe’s road to start fetching them.

Wren said yes and no in the same breath, which Riarhin had half expected.

She was sitting on the step of the long room, half in and half out, with her bowl, and she listened with her head on one side and her three notes going under her breath, and when Riarhin finished she said, “I’ll show you where.”

“Where?”

“The roads. When they let it go. All three of them’ll go somewhere. I don’t know where. I’ll feel where.” She hummed. “I’ll mark them for you. With chalk. So you know which is going where.”

“And the door?”

“No.” Wren did not stop humming. “I’ll not go near the door. Not past the white stone.”

“Why not?”

Wren looked at her as though it were a foolish question, which perhaps it was.

“Because I was born on that road,” she said. “Out there. In the ditch by the crossing. Halla said there wasn’t even a cord to cut.” She looked at the three roads meeting by the pond. “I don’t know whose I am. I don’t know what I’d do, if she woke up and I was there. I don’t know what she’d do.” She went back to her bowl. “I’ll mark your roads. You’ll not need me past the stone. You’ll have the monk.”

The keepers were the last, and the hardest, because Toller said no to the question and not to the thing.

“Who asked you to ask us?” she said.

They were at the turn, by the three blue lanterns. Osk was whittling. Haske was asleep on the bank with his red hands folded on his chest and his broken nose pointing at the overhang of the hill.

“Nobody,” said Riarhin.

“Then why are you asking?”

“Because it’s your road. I can’t let it go. You can.”

“It’s Osk’s road. I’m from up the low road. I keep the bridge at Neddick.” Toller crossed her thin arms. “I came down because Osk sent. Osk sent because you came. You came because—” She looked at Riarhin with her head on one side, the crooked shoulder up. “Why did you come? What are you? You’re not a keeper. You’re not dead. You’ve a sword on your back that somebody made for a lord, and a monk who does what you tell him, and a witch, and a woman with a bird. Who gave you the asking?”

Riarhin opened her mouth.

She knew what she could say. It was there, all of it, ready, like a coat on a peg by the door that somebody had hung there for her. My father carried this sword. It was made for a ruler. He hoped I would be one. He hoped I would bring the divided peoples back to one table. I have both of the gifts that were divided. I have killed a queen and walked out of a regency’s court under safe-conduct and carried a queen’s amulet up a mountain and left it there. I have done a great many things that people do not do. She could say it, and it would be true, and Toller would hear that it was true, the way Sefe had wanted to hear somebody say who sent them down her road. And it would be an answer. It would be the kind of answer people stood up for.

She had stood in a green-lit hall in a dream and seen the places set out for her brothers and sisters with nobody in them. She had seen the larger chair at the head, bare.

“Nobody,” she said. “Nobody gave it me. I’m asking because I want it done and I can’t do it.”

Toller looked at her a long time.

“That’s not much of a reason.”

“No.”

“It’s the only one I’ve heard yet that wasn’t a lie.” Toller uncrossed her arms. “I’ll hold. As long as Osk holds. Not longer. If Osk lets go, I let go. I’ll not be the last one holding a road on my own. I did that once at Neddick and I’ll not do it again.”

“All right.”

“Don’t all right me. I’ve not said I like it.” Toller looked at Osk. “Has she asked you yet?”

“She asked me yesterday,” said Osk, not looking up from her peg.

“And?”

“I said I’d hold as long as there’s anybody on the other side of it.” Osk shaved off a curl. “That’s what the road’s for.”

On the bank, Haske opened his eyes, looked at the overhang of the hill for a moment as if it had said something, and shut them again.

The word for Dusana came that evening, by the oddest messenger Riarhin had yet seen in that place, which was a goat.

It was not one of Halla’s. It came down the first road into the hollow at dusk, a small black nanny with a bell round its neck and a twist of red cord tied to the bell, and it walked into the yard as if it had been there before, and went straight to Dusana where she sat on the mounting block, and butted her in the knee.

Dusana looked at the cord on the bell. She untied it. It had three knots in it, close together, and a fourth at the end, larger.

“Altan,” she said. “He’s calling the gathering.”

“Now?”

“Three days. It’s a long way up.” She turned the cord in her fingers, reading the knots the way Altan had read his, with her thumb. “Odval’s people are coming. The Red Ford circle. All that are here.” She looked up. “I said I’d stand with him.”

“I know.”

“You’re going to the door in three days.”

“Four,” said Riarhin. “Osk wants four. To get the evacuation done.”

Dusana tied the cord round her own wrist. She did it slowly, three turns, and knotted it.

“Four,” she said. “I can be back in four. If I go tonight.”

Cadan had been sitting by the pond with his ropes, going over them the way he went over them every evening, foot by foot, feeling for wear. He had heard. He did not look up from the rope. But his hands stopped on it, and Riarhin saw them stop, and saw him make them go on.

“You wish to go,” he said, when Dusana had gone in to pack, without looking up. “To this gathering. She wishes to go.”

“She said she’d stand with him.”

“We need her at the door. She marks the approach. Nobody else can mark it as she can.” He felt along the rope. “Every day we wait, the stair works back into the crack. Osk says so. Every day it pulls.”

“I know.”

“Then—”

“Cadan,” Riarhin said. “She said she’d stand with him. Before she knew we were coming. Before she knew there was a door.” She sat down on the pond’s edge beside him. “She’s been with us since the Rain Doors because she wanted to be. Not because we needed her. She goes. She comes back if she can. We get ready.”

He was quiet a while.

“Yes,” he said. “I dislike it.”

“So do I.”

“I will have the ropes ready. And the marks. And the cords to tie at the stone.” He coiled the rope and set it down. “If she is not back in four days?”

“Then we wait a fifth.”

He looked at her. Then he nodded, once, and picked up the next rope.

Dusana left in the dark, on foot, with the black goat going ahead of her up the first road and its bell knocking softly. She had her pack and her stick. She did not have the raven.

She had meant to take it. She had stood in the yard with her pack on and held out her arm, and the raven had come off the long room’s roof-ridge and landed on her wrist, and she had lifted it towards her shoulder, and it had got down. It had walked off her arm onto the mounting block and from the mounting block onto Riarhin’s knee, where Riarhin was sitting, and from her knee up onto her shoulder, where it had never been, and settled there with its claws through her coat, and put its head down.

Dusana looked at it for a long moment.

“Well,” she said. “There’s gratitude.”

“I didn’t ask it.”

“I know you didn’t. Nobody asks it anything.” Dusana looked at the bird on Riarhin’s shoulder, and at Riarhin, and her face did a thing Riarhin could not read in the dark. “Feed it the crust. It likes the crust. Don’t let it in the butter.”

“All right.”

“And don’t go knocking on any doors till I’m back.”

“Four days.”

“Four.” Dusana hitched her pack. She looked down the hollow, at the three roads, at the pond, at the grey where the lane had been lying pale in the dark.
