She looked at the second road going down towards the turn. Then she put out her hand and touched the raven on the back of its head, once, with one finger, the scarred palm turned up.

“Bird,” she said.

She went up the first road after the goat. Riarhin heard the bell for a long while. Then she did not.

The raven did not move on her shoulder. It was heavier than she had expected. It smelt of feathers and of something under the feathers, warm, sweet and a little sharp, that she had smelt once before, coming through a crack in a door.

Chapter 39 — Three approaches

The pilgrim came down the third road on the second morning with his pack full of stones.

Riarhin heard him before she saw him. Something heavy coming down the road between the hedges, slowly, with a grinding, clacking sound at every step, like a cart of rubble on a bad axle. The raven on her shoulder lifted its head. Bel, on the pond wall, stood up.

He was an old man, very bent, in a coat that had been good once, and on his back he had a pack of grey canvas so large and so full that it stood up above his head, and every time he took a step the things inside it shifted and knocked together. He came down into the hollow and stopped at the pond, where the three roads met, and set his stick in the mud and leaned on it, and looked about him with a face of mild, polite bewilderment, as if he had walked into the wrong house at a wedding.

“Is this the way home?” he said.

“Whose home?” said Halla, from the gate.

The old man thought about it. He thought for a long time.

“Mine,” he said at last. “I was going home. I’ve been going a while.” He looked at the three roads. “It was one of these. I’m sure it was one of these.”

He took the pack off. It came off his back with a groan of canvas and hit the ground with a sound like a dropped hod, and he sat down beside it on the pond wall next to Bel, who moved up, and undid the strap at the top, and took out a stone.

It was a grey stone the size of a hen’s egg, smooth, river-worn. He turned it over in his fingers and looked at it.

“I promised something,” he said. “With this one. I don’t remember what.” He put it down on the wall. He took out another. “Or this one.”

Bel picked up the first stone and held it.

“You can keep that,” said the old man kindly. “I’ve plenty.”

Halla gave him the bed in the long room that had been Wren’s when Wren was small and would sleep under a roof, which was not long. He said he would stay a night. He did not say where he was going after. He did not know.

“He comes through,” said Wren, on the step. “Every few years. He did when I was little. Always asks the way home. Always sits there and takes his stones out.” She hummed. “Halla always gives him a bed.”

“Does he ever get home?”

“Never seen him go anywhere but out by another road.” Wren looked at the old man through the door, sitting on the edge of the bed with a stone in each hand, weighing them. “He says a thing, at the crossing, when he goes. Every time. He says, one of them’s the right one, they’re all where you need to be. Then he picks one and goes.” She went back to humming. “I used to think it was nonsense. It might be nonsense.”

On the third morning Osk let the road go again, a thumb, so they could see.

That was Riarhin’s asking, and she had asked it of all three keepers, and Toller had said a thumb, and no more, and not for sightseeing, and Riarhin had said it was not for sightseeing, it was so that she did not walk six people down a road she had not looked at.

It was the old man who gave her the idea of it, though he did not know he had. She had sat with him in the evening by the pond while he took his stones out one by one and could not remember any of them, and she had looked at the three roads coming in and thought: when the road is let go, the turn does not send you back. It goes on. Down. Past the door. She had seen it go on. And if the turn goes on past the door, then the door has a way to it from below, and from below is where the three roads go.

“All three,” said Wren, when Riarhin told her. “When they let it go. All three of mine go to the door. I felt it last time. Under my feet. They all went that way.” She was quiet a moment. “Like water to a drain.”

So on the third morning the keepers sat behind their lanterns at the turn and lit their stub of candle, and Osk put her thumb to it, and the going-round stopped. And Wren walked the three roads out of the hollow one after the other with a lump of chalk, and Riarhin walked them with her on a rope, and Cadan walked behind with his lantern and the slack. And at each road Riarhin went as far as she dared in a thumb of candle, and looked, and came back, and kept what she saw.

The first road was the lane.

Not the lane that was gone, the grey; the first road came into the hollow from the north, from the gap in the bank, past the grey on its other side, and ran down to the pond. When the road was let go it changed under their feet. Wren felt it first. She stopped, and crouched, and put her hand flat on the earth, and hummed her first note, held, and said, “That way,” and pointed.

Not up the lane. Into the house.

Riarhin went where she pointed. Into the yard, across it, to the door of the long room, which stood open. Through the door. Across the floor, past the table with the door laid across two trestles, past Brann’s oak prop under the cocked beam, to the back wall.

There was a door in the back wall.

There had been no door in the back wall. There had been the hearth and the oven and the shelf with the salt and the twist of flour from the Weir, and the crock, and the hook in the chimney. They were there still. And between the oven and the shelf, where there had been whitewashed stone, there was a low black door with iron bands, and a crack on its hinge side, and out of the crack came warm air that smelt of honey and rust.

Riarhin stood and looked at it. Behind her in the yard she heard Nonny ask Halla what that was, and Halla say hush.

It was close. It was a dozen paces from the table where they ate. If the keepers let the road go and held it, the door to the sleeper would be at the back of Halla’s kitchen, and anyone who went through it could come back the same way, a dozen paces, into the long room, out into the yard. It was the quickest way she had seen to anything in this place.

And the floor between the long room’s door and the black door had gone grey.

Not all of it. A path. A strip the width of a man, running from the threshold straight across the floor past the table to the black door, and the flags along it were going pale, and flat, and blank, the way the lane had gone, and the leg of the trestle at the end of the table that stood in it had begun to go too, from the floor up, grey to the ankle.

“Thumb,” said Osk, far off, at the turn, and Riarhin heard it as though Osk were in the next room.

The door in the back wall was whitewashed stone. The strip across the floor was flags. The trestle leg was a trestle leg with a grey stain round its foot to the height of an ankle, that did not wash off, afterwards, when Halla scrubbed at it.

The second road went down past the Weir.

It went the way Riarhin had come, up and round by the mill and the willows, and when the road was let go it went straight. No turn between banks. It ran from the Weir down through the hill to the door the way a gutter runs to a drain, and it ran fast, and the air on it was the air below the willows, thin, so thin that Riarhin, ten paces down it on the rope, felt her lips go numb at once and her ears begin to sing.

Tamsin, behind her, breathing slow and easy in her hood, took her by the elbow and turned her round.

“Not you,” Tamsin said. “Not this way.”

They came back up out of the thin air with Riarhin’s head ringing, and stood at the top of it, and looked down. The road went down straight and grey. Halfway along it, where it passed below the village, the thin air was spreading outwards from it, sideways, into the street, the way water spreads from a broken pipe. Riarhin saw a woman come out of a house there with a basket and stop in her doorway and put her hand to her throat and go back in.

“If they let it go and hold it,” said Aurine, who had come with them, “that road takes you straight down. It’s the fastest of the three. And it takes the air off the Weir while it’s open.” She looked down at the street. “Everyone in that village would have to be changed to live through it. Joost. The baby. The old woman at Sefe’s, if she was down here. Everyone.” She looked at Riarhin. “Whether they want it or not.”

“Could you?”

“I could. I could do the lot of them in a night, with enough skin and enough linen.” Aurine’s voice did not change. “And they’d breathe. And some of them would never laugh at the same things again. And the baby would never be asked.”

“No,” said Riarhin.

“No,” Aurine agreed. “I wanted you to see it.”

The third road was the turn.

Not the second road down to the turn; the turn itself, from below. Wren found it. She went down the second road past the white stone with her chalk in her hand and her head on one side, humming, and at the bend, where the right-hand bank was not, she did not look right at the door. She went on past it, down the path that went on when the road was let go, and down, and round a corner of the hill, and stopped, and crouched, and chalked a circle on a stone.

“Here,” she said. “It comes back up here. From under. The door’s got a way to it from under. All three of my roads come to it. This one comes in by the door’s own step.” She looked up the path at the black door in its wall, twenty paces up and round the corner. “It’s the slowest. It goes round. You’d walk a long way under the hill before you came up here. But it doesn’t go through anybody’s house.”

“What does it go through?”

Wren stood up. She looked back up the path, the way they had come, the turn and the white stone and the second road going up to the hollow.

“It goes through the way back,” she said.

Riarhin did not understand her at first. Then she did, and stood very still on the path below the door.

The road went round. When the keepers held it, it did not go round. When it went round, it brought anyone who walked the turn back out at the third stone, on Sefe’s road, every time. Cadan, six times. Herself. Every time. That was what the turn was, and she had hated it for days.

It was also how they got out.

“If they let it go and hold it for as long as it takes,” Wren said, “there’s no going round. There’s no third stone. You walk down the turn and you’re not put back. You’re on the road under the hill. You go on. And if anything goes wrong, nobody’s going to come out at the third stone with wet feet.” She looked at Riarhin. “That’s the price of mine. You go in by the door’s step. You come back however you can.”

“Thumb,” said Osk, far up the turn.

The path under the hill was grey. The third stone was at Riarhin’s feet, small and tilted, and Sefe’s gravel road ran back along the ridge, and Wren was beside her with the chalk in her hand, and Cadan behind with the slack, and the raven on Riarhin’s shoulder had its claws locked through her coat, and was shivering.

They talked it over that night at Halla’s table, all of them, with the old pilgrim asleep in Wren’s old bed at the end of the long room and his pack of stones on the floor beside him. Halla had scrubbed the trestle leg four times. The grey stain round its foot was still there.

Riarhin laid it out the way Aurine had laid out her proposition at the arch, plainly, item by item. Three ways in. The first, through the long room. Quickest, and the way back a dozen paces, and it drew its opening across the floor of a house where children slept. The second, past the Weir. Fastest of all, and it took the air off a village, and to live through it everyone there would have to be made into something else whether they asked or not. The third, round under the hill to the door’s own step. Slowest, through nobody’s house, past nobody’s mill. And to keep it open the keepers would have to let go the thing that had sent them all back to the third stone for a week, and hold it let go for as long as it took, and for as long as it took there would be no way out except forward and whatever they could find.

“And everybody who isn’t going in,” said Riarhin, “has to be out first. Here. The Weir. Wherever the roads go when they’re let go. Wren says all three go to the door when the road’s let go, and nobody knows what they do on the way. So everybody out first. Then we go in.”

Nobody said anything for a while.

“The third,” said Aurine at last.

“The third,” said Osk, from the end of the table, where she had come up for once to eat.

Cadan did not say anything. He sat with his hands round his cup.

“Cadan,” said Riarhin.

“The third,” he said. He looked at her. “It is the one where nobody pays but us.”

“That’s why.”

“I know that is why.” He turned the cup. “I wished only to say it, so that it was said.”

Toller, at Osk’s elbow, grunted.

“There’s people won’t come out,” she said. “Your miller. Your man here.” She jerked her chin at the door, where Brann was standing in the yard in the dark with his back to the house, looking at the grey. “What about them?”

“Joost has the chapel,” said Riarhin. “He’s stocking it. It held before.” She looked at the door. “Brann—”

“Brann’ll come,” said Halla.

Everybody looked at her. She was sitting at the head of the table with her hands round her own cup and her face doing nothing in a hurry.

“He’ll come,” she said again. “He doesn’t know it yet. He will.”

Aurine came to Riarhin afterwards, by the pond, in the dark, with her satchel over her shoulder.

“There’s people at Sefe’s,” she said. “Under the awning. And at the Weir, some, and at Halla’s neighbours up the low roads, the ones Wren’s been sending to. Some of them want to come out and can’t get out by the long way because they’re too old or too sick or too far. Some of them asked me. About the skin.” She stood looking at the pond. “I’ll not do anybody who hasn’t asked. And I’ll not do anybody who can’t ask. I’ll do the ones who ask and want it. And then I’ll take them out by the Weir road and up past the third stone, the way Merel goes. Changed. That’s my road. It’s mine. It’s not yours.” She looked at Riarhin. “I want it said that it’s mine.”

“It’s yours.”

“And if the world burns when you open your door, it’ll burn on my road too.” She shifted the satchel. “I’m not saying that to stop you. I’m saying it so it’s said. The brother’s right. Things should be said.”

She went off up the yard towards the loft ladder. Halfway there she stopped, and turned round, and said, “The bird’s on your shoulder,” as if Riarhin might not have noticed, and went on.

Dusana came back on the fourth evening, down the first road, alone, without the goat.

Riarhin was at the pond. She heard her before she saw her, the stick on the road, and then her shape coming down out of the dusk between the hedges, short and broad, with her pack on her back and her head down. She walked as though her feet hurt. She came down to the pond and stopped, and looked at Riarhin, and at the raven on Riarhin’s shoulder.

The raven did not move. Then it did. It got up, and stood, and opened its wings and flew the three lengths between them and landed on Dusana’s shoulder so hard that she staggered, and pushed its head in under her ear, and made a sound Riarhin had not heard it make, low and continuous, like a kettle a long way off.

“Well,” said Dusana. Her voice was not quite steady. “There you are. Was she good to you?”

“I fed it the crust,” said Riarhin.

“And the butter?”

“It got at the butter once.”

Dusana laughed, short, and put her hand up and held the bird’s back.

“How was the gathering?” Riarhin said.

Dusana looked at her for a moment over the raven’s head.

“Long,” she said. “It was long. I’ll tell you after.” She shifted her pack. “What’s happening here? Where are we going in?”

“We were going to wait for you.”

“I know you were. That’s why I walked all last night.” Dusana looked down the hollow at the second road, going down into the dark towards the turn. “Osk let the road go again. I felt it on the way down. Like a clock stopping.” She looked at Riarhin. “The third road, is it. Under the hill. The one where there’s no coming back out at the stone.”

“How do you know?”

“Because I know you a bit.” Dusana took her pack off and put it down on the pond wall. “I’ll mark it. That’s what I’m for. I’ll go in and I’ll mark it and I’ll come out if I can, same as you.”

“You don’t have to.”

“No,” said Dusana. “I don’t. I’ve not had to do anything for a long time, that I can tell.” She sat down on the wall beside her pack, heavily, with the bird on her shoulder. “I’m not going in because I don’t matter. I want that understood. I matter to me. I matter to Altan’s lot and to my grandmother and to that fool Toghrul.” She looked at Riarhin. “I’m going in because I said I’d guide you, back at the Rain Doors, and I said that far, and that far’s got further. That’s all.”

Riarhin sat down beside her on the pond wall. After a while she put her hand down on the stones between them, open, palm up, and left it there.

Dusana looked at it. Then she put hers in it, the scarred one, and the raven on her shoulder shifted its feet and settled, and none of them said anything for a long time.

Chapter 40 — The things carried out

Halla wanted the loom.

She said so at first light, standing in the long room in her boots with her shawl crossed over and tied behind, while the children ate their porridge at the table with the door across the trestles and Brann stood in the yard loading the handcart. The loom stood in the corner by the hearth where it had always stood, a big upright frame of dark wood with a half-finished web on it, brown and cream in a pattern like a field seen from a hill. Riarhin had seen it every day for a week and had not attended to it, the way you do not attend to a wall.

“It was my mother’s,” said Halla. “She brought it down the low road on her back when she came. I’ll not leave it.”

Brann came in from the yard. He looked at the loom and at Halla and at the size of the long room’s door.

“It won’t go on the cart,” he said.

“Then it’ll go on you.”

“It won’t go through the door.”

“It came in through the door.” Halla did not move. “Take it down. The cross-beams come out. My mother took it down every winter to dress the warp. You’ve seen me do it.” She looked at the web. “Leave the cloth on the beam. Roll it.”

Brann looked at her a moment longer. Then he went and fetched a mallet.

It took the whole of the morning and they did not have the whole of the morning.

Osk had said noon. She had said it the night before at the turn, with her pegs. By noon everybody who was going out had to be out of the hollow and up the low road past the gap, and the Weir had to be in its chapel, and the people Aurine had changed had to be up the Weir road past the third stone, and everybody who was going in had to be at the turn. At noon she would send Haske up the low roads to see that they were. In the morning the keepers would let the road go, and hold it. She had said noon, and she had looked at Riarhin, and Riarhin had said noon.

The loom came apart in Brann’s hands, peg by peg, the cross-beams knocked out with the mallet, the uprights lifted out of their feet. Halla stood over him and told him which peg went to which hole and he said I know, I know and did it her way. The cloth-beam with the web rolled on it was as long as a man and as heavy as one, and Riarhin took one end of it and Brann the other, and they carried it out through the long room door and across the yard to the cart.

She carried it badly. She had it at the wrong height, too low, with her arms straight and the weight hanging from her shoulders, and halfway across the yard her shoulders began to burn where the beam had come down on them a week before, and her grip went. She felt it go. The beam slid a finger’s width in her hands.

“Down,” she said.

They put it down on the yard. Brann looked at her across it.

“Again,” she said. “Higher. On the shoulder. Will you take the front? You’re taller.”

“I’m taller,” Brann agreed. He came round and took the front, and she took the back, and they lifted it onto their shoulders, and it sat there, and they carried it to the cart without a word, and laid it on the sacks, and Brann lashed it down.

“You asked,” he said, not looking at her, pulling the lashing tight.

“Yes.”

“First thing you’ve asked me that wasn’t whether I’d leave my house.” He tied off. “Get the uprights.”

The children went first, because Halla said so.

Bel would not let anybody carry his blanket. He carried it himself, folded in a square, and he walked at the front of the little procession up the first road from the hollow behind Wren, who went ahead with her chalk and her three notes, marking the turnings on the low road with a circle and a tick for the people who would come after. Nonny rode on Brann’s shoulders. Halla walked behind the cart with one hand on its tail. Mother the goat walked beside her with the other goats behind, in a long uneven line, because Halla had said the goats were coming and that was the end of it.

At the gap in the bank, Wren stopped.

She looked back down the hollow. Riarhin, who had walked them up that far with the cart, stopped beside her and looked too.

It was a small place from up there. A round green dip with three roads coming in and a pond at the bottom. A long low house with a shaggy turf roof and the end fallen in, and a great beam cocked across its long room on an oak prop, and a new pen of hurdles against its wall with nothing in it. A kitchen garden with the kale put back. And the grey, lying where the lane had been, flat and pale, a hand’s breadth from the empty pen.

“It’ll be there,” said Wren. “When they come back. It’ll be there or it won’t.”

She did not hum.

Brann came back down at midmorning, alone, with the empty cart.

Riarhin was in the long room. She had come back to look, because she had not looked properly, and because there was no time to look and she had come anyway. The room was empty. Halla had taken the crock and the twist of flour from the Weir and the salt and left the shelf bare. The table with the door across its trestles was still there; it was too big for the cart, and Brann had said it would keep. The trestle leg at the end of it had its grey stain round its foot, to the ankle.

Brann stood in the doorway with his hands hanging.

“They’re up past the gap,” he said. “With Wren. On the low road. She’s taking them round to Sefe’s by the long way.” He looked at the room. “I said I’d come back for the rest.”

“There isn’t any rest.”

“No.” He came in. He walked to the hearth, where the loom had stood, and looked at the four square marks its feet had left in the floor, pale, where the floor had been under them for forty years. He looked at the oven. He looked at the shelf.

“I said I’d stay with the house,” he said.

“You did.”

“I meant it.”

“I know.”

He stood there. Riarhin did not say anything. She had nothing to say that he did not know, and the light through the door was getting nearer to the light it would be at noon, and she stood with her back to the wall and let him stand.

He put his hand on the oak prop under the beam. He had cut the oak himself, in the top wood, with her on the other end of the saw. He had set it under the beam with a wedge and driven the wedge home with the mallet, and it had held.

“I’ll go up as far as the gap,” he said. “Just to see them on the low road. Then I’ll come back.”

“All right.”

He did not move.

“I don’t know,” he said. His voice cracked on it. He was a big man and fair and bald and sunburnt, and his voice cracked like a boy’s. “I don’t know. I said I’d stay. I said it to Halla and to you and I said it to myself in the top wood every day this week. And Halla went up the road with the goats and didn’t look back once, not once, and I don’t know.”

Riarhin came away from the wall.

“Haske comes up the low road at noon,” she said. “That’s not long. If you go up to the gap now you’ll be there when he comes, and in the morning, when they let it go, you can look down from there. If the house is here after, you come back down. If it isn’t, you’ll have seen it.” She looked at him. “You don’t have to know now. You only have to be at the gap.”

He looked at her for a long moment.

“At the gap,” he said.

“At the gap.”

He took his hand off the prop. He went to the door, and stopped in it, and looked back once at the room, the bare shelf and the table and the four pale marks on the floor. Then he went out and took up the shafts of the empty cart and went up the yard, and up the lane past the grey, the long way round it, towards the gap, and did not look back again.

At the Weir, Joost rang the chapel bell at midmorning, once. That was for in.

Merel brought the word down to the willows where Riarhin could hear it from the slope. She had been up and down the Weir road all morning with sacks: empty flour sacks for the chapel and salt and lamp-oil and a cask of water and two loaves of Halla’s. She was grey-green to the elbow now. The pattern on her forearm had spread to her wrist. She said the chapel was full. Thirty people in it, and the children in the vestry, and Joost had barred the door from inside and sat down against it with the baby on his knee.

“He says thank you for the salt,” she said. “He says if it comes up the street they’ll be out the back to the hill like before.”

“And you?”

Merel looked down the slope at the village. Smoke from four chimneys, then three, then two, as people put out their fires and went to the chapel.

“I’m going with Aurine,” she said. “Up the Weir road. With the others she did. I can breathe it and they can’t all walk it on their own.” She looked at her hands. “Then I’m coming back down. When it’s over. I’m the road.”

Aurine’s people went up past the third stone at the end of the morning.

Ten travellers went with Aurine, which was more than Riarhin had thought and fewer than Aurine had feared. The man from Sefe’s awning with a brother at the Weir. Four from the Weir itself, who had asked, and been told, and asked again the next day, and been done in a night, two by two, at the back of the chapel by lamplight. A family from up the low roads that Wren had sent to: a woman and her grown son and her old father, who had walked down to the hollow on the second day and sat at Halla’s table and asked Aurine in front of everybody, the old man first. Merel. Tamsin, in her hood, at the back, with her hand under the old man’s elbow.

The girl from the fair was not among them. She had said no, at the last, and gone round to Sefe’s by Wren’s long way with Halla’s goats.

The old woman from the awning was not among them either. She was at Sefe’s still, Sefe had sent down to say, sitting under the awning with Gerrit and Merel’s child, eating soup when it was given her. She had not been asked again. Aurine had said she would not be.

Riarhin watched them go up from the third stone. They went up the slope out of the grey in a line, slowly, grey-green at the neck and the wrist, breathing long slow breaths, and at the stone they stepped up onto Sefe’s gravel road and stood there and turned round and looked back down at the grey they had come up out of. Some of them were crying. The old man was not. He stood on the gravel and looked at his own hands, and turned them over, and looked at the backs of them, and then looked at Aurine and said something Riarhin could not hear.

Aurine said something back. The old man nodded. Then he went on along the gravel with his grandson towards the second stone and Sefe’s marker and the awning, and Tamsin with him.

Aurine stood on the third stone with her satchel. She looked down the slope into the grey.

“I said I’d stay down,” she said, when Riarhin came up to her. “At the turn. With the keepers. To help them hold.” She looked at her stained hands. “I can’t hold a road. I know that. I can stand there and if somebody comes out changed and frightened I can be there.”

“Yes.”

“That’s all I can do.” She looked at Riarhin. “It’s mine. It’s what I said.”

The last thing was the marker.

Cadan had tied it the first day they came down to the turn, before Osk, before the door, before any of it: a length of red cord from the post of the third stone down the slope into the grey, pegged at every ten paces, so that whoever came out at the third stone with wet feet would have a line to follow down again, and whoever was below and lost could find the stone. He had renewed it every morning. He had whipped its cut ends with thread from his sleeve. It had been the one thing in the whole of the low country that had stayed where it was put.

He untied it now.

He did it from the bottom, from the last peg at the edge of the grey, working up, the way he did everything, slowly and in order. He pulled each peg and wiped the earth off it on his habit and put it in his pocket. He coiled the cord as he went. He did not look up. Riarhin stood on the third stone and watched him come up the slope towards her out of the grey, coiling, and the red line went up out of the grey behind him and was not there any more, peg by peg, until there was nothing on the slope at all but the grey and the cold flat smell coming up out of it and Cadan at the top with the coil in his hands.

He untied the last of it from the stone’s post and stood holding the coil.

“If the road is held,” he said, “the stone will not take anybody back. A line to it would only bring them here. To a stone that is not a door.” He looked at the coil. “I did not want anybody following it.”

“I know.”

“So.” He held it out.

She did not know what he meant her to do with it. Then she did. She took it from him and turned and walked back along Sefe’s gravel road to the second stone, where Tamsin had stopped with the old man and the grandson to let them rest, and held it out to Tamsin.

“Give this to Sefe,” she said. “Tell her it was on her third stone. Tell her it’s hers now.”

Tamsin took it. She looked at it in her grey-green hands. Then she looked past Riarhin down the gravel to the third stone, where Cadan stood alone at the top of the slope with his empty hands at his sides, looking down into the grey.

“He’s not got one,” she said.

“No.”

Tamsin put the coil inside her blanket. She did not say anything else. She took the old man’s elbow again and went on along the road towards Sefe’s marker, and the grandson after her, and Riarhin went back the other way, to the third stone, and stood beside Cadan, and the two of them looked down into the grey together.

Aurine had already gone down. Riarhin could see the top of her cropped grey head going away below them along Wren’s low road, small and quick, with the satchel bumping at her hip.

“Noon,” said Cadan.

“Not yet.”

“Not yet,” he agreed, and they went down after her, the long way, at a fast walk, with the light on the ridge behind them as near to noon as that place ever came.

Part VI — The waking road

Chapter 41 — Where each will stand

Osk laid it out on the bank with pegs.

She had a great many pegs, a whole pile of them, smooth and pointed, whittled over forty years of sitting by a blue lantern at a turn in a road, and she took them one by one off the pile and pushed them into the turf of the bank in a pattern, while the others sat round and watched, and the raven on Dusana’s shoulder watched hardest of all, with its head on one side.

“That’s the turn,” she said. A line of pegs. “That’s the white stone.” A peg with a pebble on it. “That’s the bend. That’s the door.” A peg pushed in sideways, lying flat. “That’s the road under the hill, when it’s let go.” A long curving line of pegs going away from the flat one, round, and coming back up to it from the other side. “And that’s the step. Where the girl’s chalk is. Where the road from under comes up to the door.”

She sat back.

“Now put yourselves on it,” she said.

They did it with objects first and then with their bodies, because Osk said that was how her house had always done it, and because Riarhin said it was how she had been taught too, once, in a wood, by somebody who stood with his hand turning palm down when she got it wrong.

Each chose a thing to stand for them. Osk had a peg. Toller had a thimble she took out of her coat and said had been her mother’s and was not to be lost. Haske, after a long time, took a button off his own cuff. Aurine had a twist of paper out of her satchel with something in it that rattled. Cadan had the white candle out of his brass lantern, unlit. Dusana had a crust, which the raven tried to take off the bank twice and was prevented. Wren had her lump of chalk. She had come back down the low road in the dark after seeing Halla, the children and the goats to Sefe’s, and sat at the very end of the bank with her knees up.

Riarhin had nothing. She looked at what she had. Remorse on her back, and the knives at her belt. She did not want to put any of them down on the bank as a token for herself.

Dusana held out the comb.

It was Riarhin’s comb, the one with three teeth missing, which had been in her pack since the first harbour and had come out at Ailsa’s to do Ailsa’s hair, and had then, somehow, gone into Dusana’s pack. Riarhin had not noticed it go. She looked at it in Dusana’s hand.

“You left it on the step at Halla’s,” said Dusana. “I picked it up. Here.”

So the comb was Riarhin, and they set the things out on the pegs.

The three keepers’ things went at the turn, on the bank, where the lanterns stood. That was where they would be. Sitting, with their hands on the grass. Holding the road let go.

Aurine’s twist of paper went beside them. Then Osk moved it, without being asked, a little way down the turn to the white stone, and Aurine looked at it and said, “Yes,” and left it there.

Wren’s chalk went at the top of the turn, at the second road. Then, after a moment, Wren reached out and moved it herself, back up the bank, past the keepers, to the very top, where a peg stood on its own at the edge of the turf.

“That’s the shelter,” said Osk. “That’s where she goes.”

“That’s where I go,” said Wren. “I’ll mark the roads first. All three. Chalk at every turning, the way I did for Halla. Then I go to the shelter and I keep it.” She did not hum. “I’ll not go down the turn. I said.”

“You said,” said Riarhin.

Cadan’s candle went on the step. On the peg where the long curving road came up under the hill to the flat peg that was the door. He set it upright in the turf with great care, so that it stood straight.

“There,” he said. “I hold that. The step. The door’s own step, where the road comes up to it. If the step holds, the road under the hill stays a road to the door. If the step lets go, the road goes round again, under the hill, and anybody on it is on it until it stops.”

“How long can you hold it?”

He looked at the candle.

“I do not know,” he said. “Longer than I have held anything.” He looked at her. “You will tell me when to stop.”

“I’ll tell you.”

“If you are not there to tell me?”

Riarhin looked at the candle on the step, and at the flat peg lying on its side beside it, which was the door.

“Then you hold it till I come out.”

“And if you do not come out?”

She knew what he was asking. She had heard him ask it before, in Valora, with a kill-order between them, word for word. She did not want to give him the same answer. She had to give him an answer.

“Then you hold it,” she said, “until Dusana tells you to stop. Not me. Dusana.”

He was quiet a long moment.

“Dusana,” he said.

Dusana’s crust went in the turn, on the long curving road of pegs, halfway between the step and the door. Then she took it up again and put it down a little farther along. Then she took it up again.

“I don’t know where I go,” she said. “That’s the trouble. I go where it needs marking. I don’t know where that is till I’m there.”

“Put it on the road,” said Osk. “Anywhere on the road. Move it when you’re there.”

Dusana put the crust down on the road under the hill. The raven looked at it and did not try to take it.

Riarhin put the comb on the flat peg.

Then they walked it.

The keepers would not let the road go for a rehearsal. Toller had said so the night before and said so again, flatly, and Osk did not argue. So they walked it as it was, going round, and each of them went to where they would be and stood there, and said what they would be doing, out loud, and the others listened.

“I sit,” said Osk, sitting. “I put my hand here. When the others are ready I let go.”

“I sit,” said Toller. “I put my hand here. When Osk lets go I let go. When Osk takes her hand up, I take mine up. Not before. Not after.”

Haske sat, and put his hand on the grass, and said nothing.

Aurine stood at the white stone with her satchel.

“I stand here,” she said. “If anybody comes back up the turn changed, or frightened, or not breathing, I’m here.” She looked down the turn. “If anything comes up that isn’t anybody, I’m here too. I don’t know what I do then. I stand here.”

Wren walked her three roads with her chalk and marked them, every turning, a circle and a tick, as she had said she would, and came back, and climbed the bank past the keepers to the shelter.

The shelter was a hut. Riarhin had seen it every day she had been at the turn and had taken it for a heap of stones. It was built into the top of the bank under an overhang of root and turf, a low dry-stone hut with a turf roof and a doorway you had to stoop for, and inside it there was a floor of beaten earth and a bench and a store of pegs and oil and a blanket, and a hook in the roof for a lantern. Osk said it had been there before her and before the keeper before her. When the road went round faster or harder, she said, sometimes it went round under the turn and took the turn with it; but it had never taken the hut.

“Never yet,” said Toller.

“Never yet,” Osk agreed.

She went up the bank to it then with a peg in her hand, slowly, leaning on Haske’s shoulder, and stooped in at the low door, and Riarhin heard her drive the peg into the floor with the heel of her hand, and wait, and pull it out again. She came out with the peg and looked at its point. There was earth on it, dry and brown and ordinary, to the depth of a thumb.

“Holds,” she said.

“How do you know?”

“It’s dry.” Osk held the peg out. “If the hut were going round with the road the earth’d come up wet, or grey, or not at all. It’s dry.” She gave the peg to Wren. “You keep that. When we’ve let go, you put it in the floor every so often and pull it out and look. If it comes up wet, you get out. You don’t wait for us.”

Wren took the peg. She looked at it in her hand.

“And if you come back up and I’ve gone?”

“Then we’ll know it came up wet,” said Osk.

Riarhin went down the turn last, alone, to the white stone and the bend and past the place where the right-hand bank was not, and came out, as she had known she would, at the third stone, with Sefe’s road behind her and the grey below. She stood there a while. Then she went back down by the low road the long way, to the turn, and took her pack off on the bank by the keepers, and emptied it on the grass.

She had decided what to carry in the night, lying awake in the shelter under the turf with the others breathing round her, and she had decided it wrong twice, and she did it now again from the beginning, slowly, the way Cadan went over his ropes.

Water. One bottle, full, on its strap.

Bread. A heel of Halla’s last loaf, wrapped in a cloth. Dusana’s crust she gave back to Dusana.

Rope. Cadan’s tarred rope, a dozen paces of it, coiled and tied off and slung across her body, so that it could not slip down and catch on anything. Its end would go round her waist when they went in, and the other end would go round Cadan’s waist on the step, and nobody else would touch it.

Remorse she kept on her back. She took it off, and drew it a hand’s breadth to see that the block was seated and did not tick, and slid it home, and put it back on her back, and pulled the strap through the buckle at her shoulder a hole tighter, so that the scabbard lay flat against her spine and would not swing. She had thought in the night of leaving it on the bank. She did not know what she would need it for, in a room where somebody was asleep. She had decided she would rather carry it than leave it to be wondered about.

The knives she took off her belt.

She sat on the bank with Toothpick in one hand and the Last Blade in the other and looked at them. Toothpick, the old steppe knife she had killed Tulgoth with, so long ago that she had stopped counting years on the day she used it. The Last Blade, Kargath’s knife, with its point shortened and dressed where the cradle in the mountain had chipped it. She had carried them both into a great many places. She had never thought about how she carried them.

She put them back on her belt, in their sheaths, side by side, and then she took a thong out of her pack and tied each of them into its sheath through the guard, a turn and a knot, so that they could not fall out if she was upside down, and could be got out in a moment if she pulled the knot. She did it twice to be sure she could undo it one-handed. She could.

“You’re tying your knives in,” said Dusana, who had been watching from further down the bank.

“Yes.”

“In case you fall over.”

“In case I go upside down.”

“Do people go upside down, in there?”

“I don’t know.”

Dusana considered this.

“I’ll tie my teeth in, then,” she said.

Nobody laughed. Haske looked at her. Toller looked at her. Aurine, at the white stone, did not hear. Cadan, on the bank with his ropes, went on feeling along the rope for wear. Dusana sat with her face set and the raven on her shoulder and looked down the turn, as if she had said nothing at all.

And then, a long breath later, from the top of the bank by the shelter, Wren laughed.

It came out of her all at once, a high startled honk, the way a goose goes off when you come on it in a ditch, and she clapped her hand over her mouth, and it came out round her hand, and she bent over with her forehead on her knees and her shoulders going.

“Teeth,” she said into her knees. “Her teeth.”

Toller made a noise like a gate. Haske’s broken nose went red. Osk shut her eyes. And Dusana, still looking down the turn, still with her face set, began to shake, silently, all over, and put her hand up to her mouth over the silver tooth, and Riarhin sat on the bank with her knives tied into their sheaths and laughed until her ribs ached where the beam had come down, and could not stop, and did not try very hard.

It got dark, as much as it got dark there.

The keepers lit their lanterns. Three blue flames, side by side, upright. Wren lit the lamp in the shelter and hung it on the hook, and its yellow showed through the low door. Aurine sat at the white stone with her satchel in her lap and her back to the bank. Nobody built a fire.

Cadan came and sat by Riarhin on the bank. He did not say anything for a long time.

“I would like to say,” he said at last, “that I am not afraid.”

“Are you?”

“I find I am.” He looked down the turn, at the place where the right bank was not. “I have kept the Bell twenty-six years. It is very quiet in the house. Nobody makes a sound until it is wanted.” He folded his hands. “I have been waiting a long time for the sound that is wanted. I do not know if this is it.”

Riarhin did not answer.

After a while, Dusana came and sat on her other side, with the raven, and the three of them sat on the bank in the blue light and the yellow, and nobody said anything else.

Chapter 42 — The unmade step

They let it go at first light, or what passed for it.

Riarhin was at the white stone with the rope round her waist and Cadan’s end of it coiled on his shoulder, and Dusana beside her with the raven and a bag of pegs and a skein of red wool and Wren’s lump of chalk, which Wren had given her at the shelter door without a word. Aurine was sitting against the bank by the white stone with her satchel. Up the turn, on the bank, the keepers sat in a row behind their three blue lanterns with their hands flat on the grass.

Osk did not light a candle. There was no thumb to burn down to.

She looked down the turn at Riarhin. Riarhin nodded.

The clock in the other room stopped.

The third road went under the hill.

It began where Wren’s chalk circle was, round the corner past the door, and it did not look like a road. It looked like a fold in the hillside, a dark seam in the turf at the foot of a bank, and when Riarhin stooped into it the turf closed over her head and she was in a passage of earth and root, low, so that she went bent with one hand on the wall, and it went down.

Cadan went first. That was the plan, and he kept to it. He went ahead with his lantern lit for once, the white candle burning in the brass with a yellow flame that moved, and the rope paying out from his shoulder back to her waist. Behind her came Dusana, and the raven, on Dusana’s shoulder, with its head pulled down into its feathers.

The passage went down and turned and went down. The air in it was warm. It was the first warm air Riarhin had felt in that country that had not come out of a crack in a door, and it smelt of earth and roots and, very faint, under them, honey.

Dusana stopped every few paces to mark.

She did it the way she did everything, without fuss. A peg pushed into the wall at the height of her shoulder, and a twist of red wool tied round the peg, and a circle of Wren’s chalk on the root beside it. Then on. Then another. Riarhin had watched her practise it on the bank, and had asked her why three things where one would do, and Dusana had said that the peg was for knowing where you were, and the wool was for knowing which way you’d come, and the chalk was so that Wren would know her own mark if she ever came looking, and that she had not been asked to explain her methods since she was nine and did not mean to start now.

The first time the road tried to go back, Riarhin did not know it for what it was.

They had come down a long slope and round a turn to the left, and the passage widened and the roof lifted, and there was a place where roots hung down from it like the hair of a drowned woman, and they went through the roots, parting them with their hands. And on the far side of the roots there was the long slope again, going down, and the turn to the left at the bottom of it.

Riarhin stopped.

She knew the slope. She had just come down it. She had kept it: the run of the roof, a stone in the floor halfway shaped like a loaf, the way the wall on the right bellied out above the stone.

“We’re back,” she said.

Cadan had stopped ahead of her with the lantern. He did not turn round.

“No,” said Dusana.

She had gone past Riarhin and was standing at the head of the slope with her hand on the wall. On a peg. There was a peg in the wall at the height of her shoulder, with a twist of red wool round it and a circle of chalk on the root beside.

The wool was tied the other way.

“I tie it with the end pointing back,” said Dusana. “Back the way I came. Always. That’s what the wool is for.” She touched it. The tied end pointed down the slope. “That’s pointing down. That’s pointing to where I came from. That’s not the peg I put up there.” She looked down the slope. “That’s the peg I’m going to put down there.”

Riarhin looked down the slope. At the bottom of it, at the turn to the left, there was no peg in the wall. Not yet.

“It’s showing us the old way,” said Dusana. “It wants us to think we’re back. We’re not back. We’re further on.” She took her hand off the peg. “Come on.”

They went down the slope. At the bottom, at the turn, Dusana stopped and put a peg in the wall at the height of her shoulder, and tied a twist of red wool round it with the end pointing back up the slope, and made a circle of chalk on the root.

When Riarhin looked back up the slope from the turn, there was no peg at the top of it.

It did that more, the further they went.

It was not like the turn, which had sent them back to the third stone whole. This was smaller and meaner. A stretch of passage would be the stretch before. A step that had been there would not be. The roof would come down where it had been high, and go up again. The place was trying, all the time, the way a sleeper tries to turn over, to be what it had been a moment ago, and every time it tried, Dusana’s pegs told them whether it had managed or not.

When it had not managed, they went on. When it had, they went on anyway, and the next peg came up the right way round.

Riarhin kept all of it. She did not know what else to do with it. Every stretch of passage and every peg and every stretch it had pretended to be. She had it in order, the way she had the road from the Painted Stone and the stair in the hill district and the Rain Doors and Pell’s ravine and the ferry and the grey rooms and Wenna’s yard. It went into her and stayed. She thought, going down the third road under the hill on her bent legs with one hand on the wall, that she would have it when she was the age the old pilgrim was, and older, and that there would be nobody alive to tell it to who had been there, and then she stopped thinking it because the floor went.

It went under her right foot.

She had put the foot down where the floor was. She had seen the floor there, packed earth, and her foot had gone down onto it, and the earth had not been there. There had been nothing under her boot, the way there is nothing under a boot at the top of a stair you thought had one more step. Her weight went forward and down.

Her body caught her.

It did it without asking her, the way it had always done. Her left foot was planted and her left leg took all of her, and her hips turned, and her right leg came up and back, and her hand on the wall closed on a root and held, and she hung there for a breath with her right foot over nothing and her weight on her left heel and the rope at her waist gone taut back towards Dusana and forward towards Cadan.

“Don’t,” said Dusana, behind her, very quietly. “Don’t put it down. Wait.”

Riarhin waited. Her left thigh, the good one, held. Her right, out over nothing, began to shake.

The floor came back.

She saw it come. It came up under her raised foot out of the dark like a stair coming up out of the mist at Sefe’s, packed earth, ordinary, as if it had never not been there, and she put her foot down onto it, slowly, and it held.

Ahead of her, Cadan let his breath go.

“The step,” said Dusana. “That one. It tried to be a step that wasn’t made yet.” She came up beside Riarhin and pushed a peg into the wall above the place, and tied the wool. “Tell me if it does it again.”

“How will I know?”

“You’ll fall,” said Dusana, and patted her arm, and went on.

The raven left Dusana’s shoulder at a fork.

The passage split. To the left it went down, steeply, into warm dark. To the right it went level, and a little up, and there was a draught along it that smelt of grass. Cadan had stopped at the split with the lantern held up, looking from one to the other.

The raven went to the right.

It went off Dusana’s shoulder without a sound and flew low along the right-hand passage, a dozen lengths, and landed on the floor and turned round and looked back at them, and walked towards them a little way, its stiff rolling walk, and stopped, and looked back at the passage behind it.

“That’s a way it knows,” said Dusana. “Look at it. It knows that one.”

Cadan was looking at the left-hand passage, down into the warm dark.

“The door is down,” he said. “The step is down. I can feel the step from here.” He did not move. “That way is not to the door.”

“No.” Dusana was watching the raven. “That’s a way it knows. It knows it the way it knew Sefe’s road. It went out over the mist there because the road was going to be there.” She stood still. “I don’t know where that one goes.”

The raven walked back along the right-hand passage to the fork, and stood there at the place where the two passages split, and looked down the left one. Into the warm dark.

It put one foot out over the edge where the floor began to go down.

And drew it back.

It did it again. One foot out. The claws spread, as if feeling for something. Drawn back. It stood at the top of the left passage with its feathers lying close and its head stretched forward, and it opened its beak, and no sound came out.

“Bird,” said Dusana.

It looked at her.

She bent down and picked it up. She did it the way she picked up anything, without ceremony, both hands round its body, and it let her, and she put it inside her coat, at the breast, and buttoned the coat over it, all but the top button, so that its head showed.

“That’s far enough for you,” she said to it. “You can come and look. You’re not going first.” She looked at Riarhin. “It doesn’t know this one. It knows the one next to it. Not this one.”

“How do you know?”

“Because it’s frightened,” said Dusana. “It wasn’t frightened of the mist.”

They went down the left-hand passage.

It went down steeply, and turned, and went down. The warmth came up stronger. The honey came up stronger, and under it now, plainly, the other smell, iron gone to rust, the smell of a gate after rain. The roots on the walls were thicker and paler, and some of them were not roots. Some of them, Riarhin saw, when Cadan’s lantern went past them, were cords. Old, grey, frayed, knotted, hanging down out of the earth of the roof among the roots, and the knots on them were the same knots she had seen through the crack in the door, the net-knots, knot on knot.

The passage did not try to go back so often now. When it tried, it tried hard. Twice it was the passage above them, the fork and the raven’s passage, whole, round them, for a breath, and Dusana’s pegs told them it was not, and they went on. Once Riarhin put her hand on the wall and the wall was not there and her hand went into warm dark to the elbow and came out again with nothing on it.

She was afraid. She had been afraid for some time and had not attended to it, and now she did. Her breath was coming short and high in her chest, too fast for her legs, the way it came when she had been running and had stopped, and her hands were cold on the rope and the wall though the air was warm.

She did not know how to slow it. Ghere had taught her a great many things and none of them had been that. Ghere had taught her to be somewhere else before the blow landed, and there was nowhere else here to be.

Somebody was singing, a long way off.

A woman’s voice came to her, a big voice, untidy, too loud for the room it was in, singing badly the way people sing badly when they are happy and know they are singing badly and do not care, and singing round something held in her teeth, a ribbon, so that the words came out mashed. Riarhin could not hear the words. She could hear the tune. She had heard it all her life without hearing it, the way you do not hear the wall of the room you were born in. It went up and along and up, and then at the end it came down. It fell. It did not rise, the way Linias played it, the way a man with a crooked finger had preferred it. It came down at the end and stopped, like somebody sitting down on a bed.

It was not in the passage. It was in her. She had kept it.

She breathed in on the going-up. She held it on the along. She let it go on the falling end, all the way down, and sat down on the bottom of it, and breathed in again.

Her breath came down out of her chest to where it belonged.

“Riarhin,” said Cadan, ahead, very low.

She looked up.

The passage had ended. It came out into a space under the hill, round, with a floor of beaten earth and a roof she could not see, and in the far side of the space, in a wall of old grey stone set without mortar, there was a door.

It was the same door. Low, black, iron bands. Seen from below, from inside the hill, the side she had never seen. On this side there was a step in front of it, a single broad stone worn hollow in the middle by feet that had come up to it and stood there, and on the step, in the middle of the hollow, there was a circle in chalk, and a tick.

Wren had been here. Or had felt here. Or had known.

On the hinge side of the door, the crack was not the width of a knife. It was the width of a hand. Warm air came out of it in a slow breath, and went in again, and came out, and the cords that hung from the roof of the space all moved with it, very slightly, all together, in and out.

Beside the door, on a ledge of the wall, hung the stair. Nereth’s stair, steep and wrong, with the hollow worn at its shoulder-height and the chip out of its third step. It was not in the crack. It hung beside the crack where Cadan had set it, a hair’s breadth from the edge, and from it went the threads, slack, sagging, in every direction.

As she watched, one of them drew a little tighter.

“It is working back,” said Cadan. “The keepers have let the road go and it is still working back.” He put the lantern down on the step. “If the door is shut, it will be in again.”

He took the coil off his shoulder, and stood on the hollow of the step with his feet apart, and looked at Riarhin.

“Tell me,” he said.

Chapter 43 — The door held open

“Hold the step,” Riarhin said. “Hold it till I come back through the door. Don’t follow me through. Whatever you hear. Whatever you see through the crack. You stay on the step and you hold it.”

“Hold the step,” said Cadan. “Till you come back through the door. Do not follow. Whatever I hear. Whatever I see.” He looked at the crack. “And if you do not come back through the door.”

“Then you hold it till Dusana says stop.”

“Till Dusana says stop.”

“And when she says stop, you stop. You don’t hold it one breath longer because you think I might be just inside.”

He was silent.

“Cadan.”

“When she says stop, I stop,” he said. His voice was quite level. “Not one breath longer.”

He tied his end of the rope round his waist. He did it with the knot the house used, the one he had shown her in the Rain Doors, that would not slip and would come undone with one pull of the tail. He put the tail in her hand.

“If you need to be free of me,” he said, “pull that.”

She looked at the tail of rope in her hand. Then she gave it back to him.

“You keep it,” she said. “If you need to be free of me, you pull it.”

He looked at it in his palm for a long moment. Then he tucked it into his cord belt, beside the hook where the lantern hung, and stood on the hollow of the step with his feet apart, and bowed his head.

He had Brann’s bar under his arm.

It was an iron bar as long as a forearm, flattened at one end into a wedge, that Brann used for lifting flags and that Riarhin had asked him for the night before at the gap, because she had looked at the door through the crack from the turn and thought about what doors do. Doors shut. If this one had been dreamed shut for longer than there had been roads, it would try to shut again, and she would rather have iron in it than her hand.

Cadan had carried it down the third road under his arm without a word, and when he went thin, it fell.

She saw him go. She had seen it three times and it was worse each time, and this time was the worst, because this time he did not go a little. He went a long way. The lantern on its hook at his belt was suddenly not on its hook; it was on the step at his feet, rolling, its white candle still burning, and the light from it went up through him as though he were made of smoke. She could see the wall behind him. She could see the iron bands on the door behind him, through his chest. His face was there, bowed, his shaven head, his long hands open at his sides, and he was as thin as the breath on a cold window, and under his feet the step was not trying to go back. It was holding.

The bar went through his arm. It dropped from where his arm had been and hit the edge of the step with a clang that went round the space under the hill and came back, and bounced, and went over the edge of the step, down, and rolled away across the beaten earth floor towards the passage they had come out of.

Towards where the floor was not quite floor. Towards the place where the passage met the space, which was trying, still, every few breaths, to be the passage above.

Riarhin went for it.

“No,” said Dusana. “Stay on the step.”

She was already going. She went past Riarhin down off the step, short and broad and quick, with the raven’s head poking out of the top of her coat, and across the floor after the bar. It had stopped rolling a pace from the passage mouth. Dusana bent for it.

The passage mouth was not there. The space under the hill was the passage above, for a breath, round her, the roots and the cords hanging, the fork, and the raven’s passage going away level to the right with its draught of grass. Dusana straightened up with the bar in her hand in the middle of the fork.

Then the space was the space, and she was standing at the passage mouth with the bar.

She did not move for a moment. Riarhin saw her face, by Cadan’s fallen lantern. It had gone very still. Then she came back across the floor, not fast, carefully, putting her feet down where she had put them going, and up onto the step beside Cadan’s smoke-thin shape, and held out the bar.

“There,” she said. “It went where it was going to go. I went where I was going to go. We both came back.” Her voice was steady. Her hand on the bar was not. “Don’t drop it again.”

The door was trying to shut.

Riarhin could see it now that she was close. She stood on the step beside Cadan with the bar in her hands and looked at the crack on the hinge side, and it was not the width of a hand. It was a little less. And a little less. The black wood was moving, slowly, the way the hand of a clock moves when you are not watching it and then you are.

She put the bar in.

She set the flat wedge of it into the crack at the height of her waist and leaned on it, and felt the door push back. It was not like pushing on a door. It was like pushing on somebody’s shoulder when they are asleep and do not want to be pushed. A slow heavy settling, not angry, not anything, only very much heavier than her.

She set her feet. Square. Both of them, on the hollow of the step, hip-wide.

The crack held at the width of a hand.

Warm air came out of it against her face, honey and rust, and went in, and came out.

“It is pushing,” said Cadan. His voice came out of the smoke beside her as though from the far end of a long room. “I can feel it pushing on the step. The step is holding.”

“Is it holding long?”

“I do not know what long is.” A pause. “Go.”

She left the bar in the crack. She took her hands off it. It held: iron in the black wood, the wedge driven in to the width of a hand, and the door leaning on it, slowly, heavily, and not getting past it. Not yet.

She went sideways through the crack.

It was a room under a hill.

It was the room she had seen through the crack from the turn, from the outside, in one step. She knew that the way she knew where her own hand was. And it was also the room she had seen through the crack from the turn, from the outside, in one step: the same, again. The floor was the same floor. She took a step into it and it was the step she had just taken. She took another and it was that step again.

It was trying to put her back.

Not to the third stone. Not outside. To the moment of coming in. Every step she took into the room, the room took her back to the door, the way a stream takes a leaf back to the bank, and she was standing just inside the crack again with the bar in the black wood beside her shoulder and the warm air on her face and the room in front of her, dim, gold, the same.

She did not know how to stop it. Nobody had told her. Osk did not know and the page did not say. She stood just inside the door and her breath began to go high again and she put it down on the falling end of Bea’s tune, and kept it there.

She did not know how to stop it. She knew how to keep a thing.

She took a step. She kept it. The floor under her boot, beaten earth, a little soft, a little warm. The light, dim gold, coming from nowhere. The cords. There were cords hanging from the roof here too, a great many, grey and knotted, hanging down like a curtain between her and the far side of the room, moving in and out with the warm breath. She kept them. The room put her back at the door. She did not let it put back what she had kept. She took the step again, and it was the same step, and she kept that too, the second time, alongside the first, the way she had kept the stretch of passage and the stretch it had pretended to be.

She took the step a third time.

The room did not put her back.

She was one pace in. She was standing one pace inside the door with the cords swaying in front of her, and when she looked back the bar was a pace behind her in the crack, and Dusana’s face was in the crack beside it, white, and Cadan was a shape of smoke on the step beyond.

She took the next step. The room put her back. She kept it. She took it again. And again. And she was two paces in.

She walked and kept each step, three times, and the room gave way as a stiff lock gives on the third turn of the key. Her thigh hurt. Her ribs hurt where the beam had come down on her shoulders and sent the weight into them. She did not attend to either. She attended to the floor and the cords and the light and the warm breath going in and out, and to each step, all of it, three times, and kept it.

At the curtain of cords the floor went up.

It went up the way the floor of a barn goes up where hay has been heaped against a wall, a slope, soft, rising, and it was not floor. It was cords. Knotted, grey, heaped, a great slope of net going up into the gold dimness, giving under her boots. She climbed. She went up it on her hands and her knees with her fingers through the knots, and it gave and swayed and she kept every handhold, and the room tried to put her back at the bottom of the slope and she would not go.

Her right foot went through.

It went through the net between two knots, to the knee, and the cords closed round her shin, and held. She pulled. They held. They were not tight. They were simply there, round her leg, many of them, knotted, the way a fishing net is round a fish that has gone into it head first, and every pull drew more of them in.

The room was trying to put her back. She felt it. It was trying to put her back at the bottom of the slope with her foot caught, and she would not go, and she could not go up.

She reached back over her shoulder and drew Remorse.

She drew it left-handed, as she always had, half out of the scabbard on her back in one movement and then the rest, and the block above her fingers was seated and did not tick, and Ghere’s binding was under her hand, cut on the slant at the end where she had cut it. She did not swing. There was no room to swing. She laid the edge against the cords round her shin, below the knee, and drew it towards her, the way you draw a knife through bread, and the cords parted.

They parted as cords part. Grey strands, cut, frayed ends. Nothing else. There was no light, no sound. The ends hung down. Her leg came free.

She sheathed it. She climbed.

At the top of the slope of cords the gold was brighter, and the warm breath came stronger, in and out, slow, so slow she could fit three of her own breaths into one of it, and she came up over the crest of the heap on her hands and knees with her hair in her face and her thigh shaking, and stopped.

She was on the edge of a bed.

Chapter 44 — The sleeper

It was a bed the way the heap of cords had been a floor. It went on further than her eyes would go. It was made of cords, the same grey knotted cords, finer here and closer, knot on knot on knot, so many that they were not a net any more but a cloth, a mattress, a pale shelving stretch like a beach seen from a cliff, and it was warm under her hands. It was warm the way a bed is warm that somebody has been lying in for a long time.

Somebody was lying in it.

She could not see all of her. She could not see most of her. The gold dimness lay over the bed like water over a sand-bar, and what lay under it was a shape, long, and too large, and too near, so that Riarhin, crouched at the edge of the cords on her hands and knees with her hair in her face, could not tell where it began or how far it went. She could not have said it was a woman. She would not have known how to say what else it was. There was a slope that might have been a shoulder. There was a long fall of something dark across the cords that might have been hair, or shadow, or the edge of a cloak. There was the breath.

It was close. She heard it as you hear the breath of somebody lying next to you in the dark, in your own ear, in through the nose, slow, so slow, and held, and out. The warm air of it moved the hair off her face. It smelt of honey. Under the honey, of rust.

And there was the hand.

It lay on the cords an arm’s length in front of her, palm down, the fingers a little curled, as fingers curl in sleep. It was the hand she had seen through the crack from the turn, in one step. It was long. Its skin was the colour of the light, which was a dim gold, and there were lines on the backs of the knuckles where the skin creased when the fingers bent, and the nails were short and plain, and one of them had a chip at its edge. It was a hand. It was a hand you could have seen on a woman across a table at a fair.

It was very large. She had not understood that through the crack. Laid on the cords in front of her, it was as long as her forearm from the wrist to the tip of the second finger.

Riarhin knelt up on the edge of the bed.

The room tried to put her back.

She felt it go. She felt the bed tilt away from her and the slope of cords drop behind her and the door come up at her back, the bar in the crack, the warm air on her face from the outside, and she would not go. She kept the bed. She kept the warmth of it under her knees and the breath in her ear and the hand an arm’s length in front of her, and the room gave, and she was kneeling on the bed.

She put out her hand.

She took the sleeper’s hand in hers.

It was warm. It was warmer than a hand should be, a little, like a hand that has been held round a cup. The skin was dry. She had to put both her own hands round it to hold it at all, round the palm, below the fingers, and she did, and held it, the way you hold the hand of somebody who is ill.

Nothing happened.

The breath went in, slow, and was held, and went out. The fingers did not move in hers. The gold lay over everything. The room was still.

She did not know what she had thought would happen. She had not let herself think it. She knelt on the bed with the hand in both of hers and waited and nothing happened at all, and somewhere behind her, a long way off and down, through the crack and the bar and the slope of cords, she heard Dusana’s voice, very small, say something she could not make out.

She had to say something. She knew that the way she knew where her feet were. Nobody had told her. The page had not said. She knew it the way you know, in a wagon in the morning, that the person beside you will not wake unless you speak.

She did not know what to say.

She did not have a name to say. Nobody had a name. She had a word from Sefe’s road that would make Sefe’s road and nothing else. She had her father’s sword on her back and her grandmother’s blood in her and both the gifts that had been divided, and none of them was a word, and she did not want any of them to be.

“Wake up,” she said.

It came out very quiet, and cracked, like Brann’s voice in the long room. She said it again, louder.

“Wake up.”

The breath went in. Was held. Went out.

The room tried to put her back. It was harder this time. It was harder because she was holding the hand; she could feel the hand going away from her, back across the bed, the way the floor had gone away under her foot in the passage, and her own hands closing on nothing. She held on. She kept the hand. Its warmth, its dryness, the chip on the nail, the creases at the knuckles. She would not let it be put back. The room gave.

“You have to wake up,” she said. “I know you don’t want to. I know it’s warm. It’s morning.” She did not know why she said that. There was no morning in that place. She said it anyway. “It’s morning. There’s people waiting for you. There’s a door with a stair jammed in it and it’s pulling the whole country down a drain. There’s a woman at a mill who has to go up and down a road all her life because she can’t breathe. There’s a man in a yard who doesn’t know if his house is still there. There’s a girl born in a ditch who won’t come past a white stone because she doesn’t know whose she is.” Her voice was going. She let it go. “There’s a bird. A raven. It walks on the mist where the road’s going to be. It knows you. I think it knows you. It’s frightened. It wasn’t frightened of the mist.”

The breath went in.

It was not held.

It went out again, at once, short, a little catch at the end of it, the sound a sleeper makes when something has got into the dream.

Riarhin’s hands were shaking round the hand. She held on harder.

“Please,” she said.

The cords shifted under her knees. For a moment her weight went backwards, though she had not moved, and the hand began to slide out of hers.

She caught it at the fingers. Her knees were against the edge of the bed again. She dragged one onto the weave and leaned forward, holding on.

Cadan had said he would hold the step. The keepers were to hold the road. She could hear none of them now. She waited for the room to take her back to the door, to leave her standing outside with her hands empty.

The skin beneath her palms stayed warm.

She brought her other knee up.

She bent her head over the hand. Her hair fell across it. She put her forehead down on the back of it, on the warm dry skin between the knuckles, the way you put your forehead down on the hand of somebody in a sickbed when you have run out of things to say.

“It’s morning,” she said into it. “Come on. It’s morning.”

The fingers moved.

They closed. Slowly. They closed round her two hands the way the hand of a sleeper closes round whatever is put in it, a cup, a finger, a child’s fist, without knowing, and held.

The grip was warm. Then it was hot. It was very strong.

The breath went in. It went in fast. It went in the way a breath goes in when somebody wakes all at once from a dream of falling, a great sharp gasp, and the whole of the gold dimness went in with it, sucked in, the light pulling towards the shape on the bed like water to a drain, and the smell of honey and rust went past Riarhin’s face so thick she choked on it.

The cords moved.

All of them. All at once. Under her knees and round her and above her, hanging from the roof, heaped in the slope behind her, the whole bed and the whole room. The knots were coming undone. She heard them. A sound like rain beginning on a roof, a pattering, a soft multitudinous slither, everywhere, knot after knot after knot letting go, and the cords under her knees went slack, and she sank into them as you sink into a feather bed, and the hand held her, hot, so hard that the bones of her fingers ground together.

There were birds.

She could not see them. She heard them. Somewhere above, in the dark that had been gold, wings. A great many wings, and then voices, harsh, calling, raven-calls, not one bird but a whole rookery of them going up at once off a wood when a stone is thrown into it, calling and calling, and going away.

The breath went out.

It went out long and shuddering and loud, and it was not the slow breath of the sleeper any more. It was a breath. An ordinary breath, too large, in a body too large, that had been held in for a very long time. And then another, quick. And another.

The shape on the bed was moving. She felt it move through the slack cords under her and through the hand in hers. It was turning over. It was coming up. The slope that might have been a shoulder was rising, and the long fall of dark that might have been hair or shadow was sliding, and something vast was gathering itself above her in the dark the way somebody in a narrow bed gathers themselves to sit up, and the hand round hers was pulling her with it, up.

Riarhin was more afraid than she had ever been.

She had been afraid in a great many places. On a slab in the Red Wastes with Na’ar hanging over the dark. On a stair in Valora with Tylan’s sword coming. In the binding chamber with her grandmother’s spear in her ribs. She had been afraid and done the thing anyway, every time, because there was a thing to do, and her body knew how. Her body did not know how to do this. There was nothing to do. There was a hand round both of hers, hot, enormous, and something sitting up in the dark above her that was not looking at her, because it had no reason to look at her, because she was something in its hand that it had closed its fingers on in its sleep, and it was waking.

She did not let go.

She did not know why. She would not have been able to say. She knelt in the slack cords with her two hands in the sleeper’s hand and her face turned up into the dark where the birds had gone and the breath was coming quick and loud, and she did not let go, because she had said she would not go anywhere, and she had meant it, and she was not going to be put back now.

The hand let go of her.

It opened. All at once. The fingers spread and her two hands fell out of them and she fell forward onto the cords, and the cords were not a bed. They were cords, loose, a heap of loose grey cords, undone, smelling of old dust and honey, and she was lying face down in them in the dark.

The gold was gone.

The breath was gone.

Above her, in the dark, something very large was sitting up. She could not see it. She felt it there the way you feel a cliff in the dark when you have walked to the edge of it, the air different, the space. It did not move. It did not speak. It was there, and it was awake, and the room around it was not a room any more. She felt the walls go. She felt the door go, and the bar in it, and the slope of cords, and the floor under them, the way a dream goes when you open your eyes, not all at once but the edges first, folding away.

Far off, down, and getting further, Dusana was calling her name.

Riarhin lay in the loose cords and could not move.

The thing in the dark above her drew one more breath. A long one. Riarhin heard it go in, and hold, and she waited with her face in the dusty cords for it to go out.

It went out as a sound. It was not a word. It was the sound somebody makes on waking who has been dreaming for a long time of something terrible, and wakes, and is not sure yet that it was a dream.

Then the floor was gone and she was falling.

Chapter 45 — The work after

She came down on stone.

She fell through dark that smelt of dust and honey and came down on her side on something hard and flat and cold, and the breath went out of her, and she lay there with her cheek on the stone and could not get it back.

Somebody had her by the coat.

“Breathe,” said Dusana. “Go on. Breathe. You’ve not forgotten how.”

She breathed. It came in ragged. She was lying on the step. She knew it by its hollow under her hip, worn by feet.
