The space under the hill was round her, and it was dark, and Cadan’s fallen lantern was still burning on the edge of the step with its white candle, the yellow flame moving, and in its light she could see the wall of old grey stone set without mortar.

There was no door in it.

There was a hole. Where the door had been, low, rounded at the top, there was an opening into dark, and the dark beyond it had no gold in it and no breath. Cold air came out of it, ordinary cold, the cold of a cellar. The iron bands lay on the step at her feet, twisted, and among them, black and splintered, pieces of wood. Brann’s bar lay across the hollow of the step with its wedge end bent.

Beside the opening, on the floor of the space, lay the stair.

It had come off its ledge. It lay on its side across the beaten earth, steep and wrong, with its hollow worn at the height of a shoulder and the chip out of its third step. It was stone. It was a short flight of old stone steps lying on a floor, and it was broken across the middle where it had fallen, and the threads that had gone out from it in every direction were not there. Riarhin looked for them. She looked hard. She could not see a single one.

“Cadan,” she said.

He was on the step above her. He was smoke. She could see the wall through him, and the opening through him, and the lantern-light went up through his chest and his bowed head as though he were not there at all, and he was standing with his feet apart on the hollow of the step and his hands open at his sides, holding it.

“She’s out,” Dusana said to him. “She’s on the step. You can stop.”

He did not stop.

“Cadan,” said Dusana. “Stop.”

He stopped.

He came back slowly, from the feet up, the way water comes back into a pond, and when he was solid to the waist his knees went and he sat down on the step, hard, and when he was solid to the shoulders he was shaking so that his teeth knocked. Dusana got her coat off and round him. The raven came out of the front of her shirt and stood on the step and looked at him with one eye.

Riarhin got up onto her hands and knees. Then onto her knees. Her right thigh would not take her the rest of the way. She sat on the step against the wall with her legs out in front of her and looked at the opening where the door had been.

“What happened?” said Dusana.

“She woke up.”

Dusana looked at her for a long moment.

“And?”

“And let go of me.”

Dusana looked at the opening. She looked at the stair lying broken on the floor, and the iron bands on the step, and Brann’s bent bar. Then she looked back at Riarhin, and her face did a thing Riarhin had seen it do once before, on the bank by the lanterns when the raven came back into itself in her hands.

“Well,” she said. “That’s that, then.”

The way back was not the way they had come.

They knew it at once. Dusana went to the mouth of the passage with the lantern and stood there and looked up it, and her pegs were not in the wall. The passage went up from the space under the hill as it had come down, low, root and earth, and there was not a peg in it, nor a twist of wool, nor a circle of chalk. It was a passage nobody had walked.

“It’s moved,” said Dusana.

“Moved where?”

“How should I know? It’s moved. They’ve all moved.” She held the lantern up. “Feel that.”

Riarhin felt it. There was a draught coming down the passage. Not the warm breath. Cool air, fresh, with a smell on it of wet grass.

“It goes somewhere,” said Dusana. “Things that go somewhere have a draught.” She looked back at Cadan, sitting on the step in her coat with his teeth going. “Can you walk?”

“I can walk,” said Cadan.

He could not, very well. Riarhin could not, very well. They went up the passage with Dusana in front holding the lantern and Riarhin behind her with one hand on the wall and the other on the rope, which still ran from her waist to Cadan’s, and Cadan last, holding the rope with both hands like a man on a ship in a storm. The raven walked. It would not be carried. It walked up the passage in front of Dusana’s feet with its stiff rolling gait, and stopped at every turning, and looked up it, and went on.

The passage did not try to go back. Riarhin waited for it to. She kept every stretch of it, out of habit, ready for the moment it would be the stretch before, and the moment did not come. It was a passage. It went up and turned and went up. When a step was there, it stayed there. When she put her hand on the wall, the wall was where she had put it.

At a turning the raven stopped and did not go on.

The passage forked there, the way the other had forked, coming down: one way going on up towards the draught, the other going off level to the right. The raven stood at the fork and looked up the right-hand way, the level way, for a long time. Then it walked a few steps along it, and stopped, and stood with its head stretched forward and its feathers flat.

It turned round. It looked back at Dusana.

It came back to the fork, and looked up the right-hand way again, and made a small sound in its throat, and did not go.

“What is it?” said Riarhin.

Dusana held the lantern up and looked at the bird.

“It doesn’t know that one,” she said slowly. “It went that way coming down like it owned it. It doesn’t know it now.” She crouched and held out her arm, and the raven walked up it onto her shoulder, and pushed its head in under her ear. Its feathers were all on end. “It’s all right,” she said to it. “It’s all right. Neither do I.”

They went on up towards the draught.

They came out under a sky.

It was not a sky Riarhin had seen in that country. It was grey, as everything had been grey, but there was a lightness in it towards one side, low down, that was very like the place in a mortal sky where the sun is about to come up. They came out of the passage into it through a fold in the turf at the top of a bank, and stood up straight for the first time since the space under the hill, and there below them was the turn.

The white stone. The bend. The banks with their hanging roots.

The right-hand bank was not there.

Where the bend had been, where the right bank had not been and the wall with the door had been, there was a gap. Wide, rough-edged, as though the hill had been split, and through it the turn went on. It went on down past the place where the door had been and round the corner of the hill and away, a path of beaten earth going down between banks, the way a path goes. There was no third stone at the bottom of it. There was more path.

And on the bank above the turn, three blue lanterns stood in a row.

Two of them were burning. Osk sat behind the middle one with her hands in her lap. Toller lay on the grass behind hers on her back with her crooked shoulder up and her eyes shut and her chest going. Haske’s lantern was dark, and Haske was not there.

The hut was behind them, at the top of the bank. Its low door was open and its yellow lamp hung on its hook inside, and Wren was sitting in the doorway with her knees up and Osk’s peg in her hand.

“She said the hut might hold,” said Dusana. “Osk. She said it never had taken the hut.”

“It hasn’t,” said Riarhin.

Osk did not get up when they came down the bank. She looked at them, the three of them and the bird, one after the other, and her lips moved, and Riarhin knew she was counting.

“Three,” said Osk. “And the bird.”

“Where’s Haske?”

“Down the second road with the lantern. Pegging the new turns, as they come.” Osk looked at her hands. They were shaking, a little, in her lap. “The roads went everywhere when she let go. Like a sack of grain split. The girl’s marks were the only reason we knew which was which.” She nodded up at Wren in the doorway. “She walked them all again after. While you were down. With her chalk. And Haske’s been going after her with pegs. Every turning that’s stayed put, a peg. Every one that hasn’t, a peg the other way.”

“And Aurine?”

“At the Weir road.” Osk looked down the turn. “The bit below the willows went. The bit she took her lot up. It went under while they were on the far side of it, at Sefe’s. Nobody on it.” She was quiet a moment. “It’ll not come back. She came by an hour since and said so. She said tell you it was hers and it went and she’s not sorry she had it.”

“The Weir?”

“The bell’s rung three times since noon.” Osk looked at her. “Three’s out. They’ve come out of the chapel.”

Riarhin sat down on the bank beside her. Her thigh would not hold her any longer and she did not ask it to.

“The turn,” she said. “It’s not going round.”

Osk did not answer at once. She took a peg off her pile, one of the old ones, smooth, and leaned over and pushed it into the bank at the white stone, where she had pushed one every morning for forty years. It went in. She left it there a moment. Then she drew it out and looked at its point.

The earth on it was dry and brown and ordinary, to the depth of a thumb.

“No,” she said. “It’s not.”

She put the peg back on the pile. Then she put her face in her hands, and sat like that, and did not say anything else.

Haske came back up the second road at dusk with his dark lantern under his arm and his red hands full of chalky earth, and sat down on the bank beside Toller, who had woken and was sitting up, and said the first thing he had said since he came: that there was a stretch of the third road, below Halla’s, that would not hold a peg. He had tried four times. It took the peg and the peg came up grey.

“Leave it,” said Toller.

“I left it,” said Haske.

He did not say anything else that night.

Aurine came at full dark, alone, from the Weir road, with her satchel, and stood at the foot of the bank and looked up at them all, and at Riarhin, and her face in the blue lantern-light was grey and very tired.

“Well,” she said. “Did you?”

“Yes.”

“And the world?”

Riarhin looked down the turn, through the gap where the bank had been, at the path going on down past the place where the door had been, into the dark.

“It’s still there,” she said.

Aurine nodded slowly. She came up the bank and sat down on Riarhin’s other side and took a packet out of her satchel and opened it and held it out. It was dried apple, in rings. Riarhin took one. Aurine took one. Dusana, beyond her, held out a hand without looking and was given one, and broke a bit off it for the raven, and the raven took it and went off along the bank with it to eat it behind a stone.

Cadan was lying on the grass beside the hut door with Dusana’s coat over him and Wren’s blanket over that. He had stopped shaking. He had not spoken since the step. Riarhin had looked at him twice, coming down the bank, and he had been looking up at the sky, at the place towards one side where it was lighter, low down, the way a mortal sky is lighter where the sun is about to come up.

He was still looking at it.

Riarhin ate the apple ring. It was sweet and leathery and tasted faintly of the satchel. When it was gone she lay down on the bank where she was, on her back, on the dry ordinary grass, and shut her eyes, and the last thing she heard before she slept was Wren in the doorway of the hut behind her, very softly, beginning to hum her three notes, and then a fourth, which Riarhin had never heard her sing, falling.

Chapter 46 — The place she keeps

The house was there.

Riarhin saw it from the top of the second road on the third morning, when her thigh would carry her that far, and stopped. Brann had come down from the gap the evening before. She had heard that from Haske, who had heard it from Wren. She had not been down to see.

It stood at the bottom of the hollow by the pond where it had always stood, long and low with its shaggy turf roof and the byre end fallen in and the great beam cocked across the long room on Brann’s oak prop. Smoke was going up from the chimney. The kitchen garden had its kale. The new pen against the wall had goats in it, three of them, Mother with her broken horn among them, standing on their hind legs with their front hooves on the top rail, looking at the yard.

The grey was there too.

It lay where the lane had been, flat and pale, from the gap in the bank down to the byre door, the way it had lain since the beam came down. It had not gone. Riarhin had half thought it would. It lay there in the grey morning light like a pond with nothing reflected in it. But round its edge, on the side away from the house, somebody had cut a new way through the hedge. A path of trodden grass went round the grey from the gap to the yard gate the long way, by the top field, and there were hurdles along it, new ones, lashed to stakes with withy.

Brann was at the gate. He had a mallet in his hand and he was driving a stake.

He looked up and saw her at the top of the second road, and stopped with the mallet raised, and then put it down.

He did not wave. He did not call. He stood at the gate a moment longer, looking at her. Then he went back to the stake.

Halla came down that afternoon, with the children and the rest of the goats and the cart, by Wren’s long way from Sefe’s, which had taken them two days.

Riarhin was in the yard when they came. She had come down to help with the hurdles because she could not sit on the bank by the turn any longer and watch Osk push a peg into it every hour and draw it out dry. The cart came down the new path round the grey with Bel walking in front of it carrying his blanket, and Nonny riding on the cloth-beam of the loom, and Halla walking behind it with her hand on its tail as she had walked behind it going.

Halla stopped at the gate. She looked at the house.

She did not say anything for a long time. She looked at the roof, and the byre end, and the prop under the beam, and the goats in the pen. She looked at the grey where the lane had been. She looked at the new path round it, and the hurdles, and Brann at the gate with his mallet.

“You came back down,” she said to him.

“I came back down.”

“You said you’d stay with it.”

“I went to the gap,” said Brann. “Like she said. I stood there. In the morning, when they let it go.” He looked at the mallet in his hand. “The house went grey. All of it. From the gap I saw it go. The roof, the byre, the pond, the yard. I stood there and I watched it go and I thought, that’s it. And then it came back.” He put the mallet down on the gatepost. “It just came back. Like a man turning over in bed. And the lane didn’t.”

Halla looked at him for a while.

Then she went past him through the gate and across the yard to the long room door, and in, and Riarhin heard her moving about in there. The crock going down on the shelf. The twist of flour from the Weir going down beside it. The salt. A long pause. Then her voice, quite ordinary, asking Brann where he had put the bar, the iron one, for the flags, because the hearthstone had lifted.

“Riarhin has it,” said Brann. “It’s bent.”

“Then straighten it,” said Halla.

They put the loom up again that evening in the corner by the hearth, where its four feet had left their four pale marks on the floor, and the marks were still there, and the feet went back into them as though they had been waiting.

Riarhin held the uprights while Brann knocked the cross-beams in with the mallet and Halla told him which peg went to which hole. Bel sat on the table and watched. When the cloth-beam was back in its place with the web rolled on it, brown and cream, Halla went to it and unrolled a hand’s length and looked at the pattern, and ran her thumb along the last row, and said, “That’s where I was,” and rolled it back.

She did not weave. She went and put the porridge on.

The Weir’s news came with Merel, the next morning, down the Weir road.

The thin air was gone.

Merel said it standing in the yard with her grey-green hands clasped in front of her, and her face did a great many things while she said it. The bit below the willows had gone under, where Aurine had taken her people up; it was gone, and it was not coming back, and nobody would ever walk it again. But the rest of the Weir road was a road. You could breathe on it. Joost had walked up it, the morning after, as far as the third stone, with the baby on his back, and stood at the stone and breathed, and his lips had not gone blue, and the baby had not choked, and he had stood there a long time.

“Did he come up?” said Riarhin.

“As far as the stone. He stood there.” Merel looked at her hands. “Then he went back down. He said the wheel wanted watching.” She looked up. “He says he’ll come up to Sefe’s at the next fair. With the baby. To see her sister.”

“And you?”

Merel did not answer at once. She looked at her hands, at the grey-green going up past her wrist now, the fine pattern of it like the grain in a split board.

“It’s not going back,” she said. “Aurine said. I’m what I am now. I can’t bear the light much. I can breathe where they can’t.” She closed her hands. “There’s nowhere they can’t now. Not on the Weir road.” She looked at the hollow, the house, the goats, the grey where the lane had been. “So I don’t know what I’m for.”

“You’re for whatever you’re for.”

“That’s no answer.”

“No,” Riarhin agreed. “You’ll find one.”

Merel looked at her a moment longer. Then she laughed, short, the first time Riarhin had heard her laugh, and it was a good laugh, rough, with a Weir burr in it.

“You’re no use at all,” she said, and went in to see Halla.

Wren was staying.

She said so on the step of the long room that evening, half in and half out, with her bowl on her knees, when Halla asked her. Halla had asked her whether she would go up to Sefe’s for the fair with Merel and Joost, and see the world above the third stone, now there was a road.

“No,” said Wren.

“Why not?”

“Because I was born here.” Wren hummed. Three notes, and a fourth, falling, the one she had found on the bank by the hut the night Riarhin came up out of the hill. “I know whose I am now. I didn’t before.” She looked out at the three roads meeting by the pond in the dark. “I’m the crossing’s. I’ll stay at the crossing.”

Halla looked at her a long time.

“You could still sit at the table,” she said.

Wren thought about it.

“I could,” she said. “Not tonight.”

Dusana told her about the gathering on the last morning, walking up the first road out of the hollow with her pack on her back and the raven on her pack.

Riarhin walked with her as far as the gap in the bank. That was what they had agreed. Dusana was going up. Three days, by the low roads, to Altan’s plain by the dry river, and Riarhin was not going with her. Riarhin was going back to the turn, to Osk, to see whether the road that went on past where the door had been went where Osk thought it went.

“It was long,” Dusana said. “I said that. It was very long. Altan read his cord. All of it. From the beginning.” She walked a while. “Then Odval stood up, and her people. Red Ford. All that are here. There were more than I’d thought. There was a man who’d been a boy when the barrels didn’t come. He stood up and said his piece. Then Toghrul stood up.”

“Did he talk?”

“He talked.” Dusana’s mouth went tight at the corner. “I’ve never heard him talk. Not in all the time I’ve known him. He stood in front of the carpet and he talked for longer than Altan read. He told it all. The cart. The market at Kesh. How much they paid him. What he bought with it.” She was quiet. “He bought his daughter a red coat. That’s what he bought. With the water. A red coat, for the winter fair. She wore it once. Then she died in the spring of the same fever as half Red Ford, because there was no water there, and some of it went through our circle too.” She walked on. “He’d never told anybody that. Not the coat.”

Riarhin did not say anything.

“Altan didn’t take him back,” Dusana said. “I didn’t think he would. He gave him a place. At the edge of the ring. Where the grass starts. He can have a wagon there. His own. Where he can see the fires and they can see him. And at the next gathering he can stand up and tell it again, if he wants, and they’ll listen.” She hitched her pack. “That’s not a yes. Altan said so. Odval said so. That’s what Toghrul’s got. A wagon at the edge where they can see him.”

“Is it enough?”

“It’s what he asked for.” Dusana stopped walking. They had come to the top of the first road, where the gap in the bank was. “To be let near. To be where he can be seen being sorry. That’s what I said for him, the first day. I said it and lost my temper.” She looked back down into the hollow. “He’s got it. I don’t know if it’s enough. It’s what there was.”

The gap in the bank was earth if you were going down and a gap if you were going home.

Dusana stopped in it. Riarhin stopped beside her.

“You’re staying,” Riarhin said.

“At Altan’s.” Dusana did not look at her. “My grandmother’s there. She’s got opinions about Toghrul and I want to hear them. I’ve a wagon promised me at the far side of the ring from his, which Altan thinks is very funny.” She put her hand up and touched the raven on her pack. “I’ve somewhere to be. I didn’t have, for a long while.”

Riarhin had known. She had known since the pond wall, the evening Dusana came back down without the goat and said that far’s got further, and she had kept it, and she had not looked at it. She looked at it now.

She did not like it.

“I don’t want you to,” she said.

“I know you don’t.” Dusana looked at her at last. Her face was older than it had been at the fire, a great many years older, grey in the hair, a broken vein under the left eye, the silver tooth. It was the same face. “I didn’t want you to go off at the bridge. That morning. With that boy’s face cut and the oxen not yoked. I shut the door on you.” She held Riarhin’s eye. “I didn’t want you to, and you went, and I shut the door. I’m not shutting it now. I’m going up the road. That’s different.”

“Is it?”

“It is to me.”

Riarhin did not answer.

Dusana took her pack off. She put it down in the gap in the bank, on the earth, and sat down on it, and moved over, to one end, the way a person moves over on a bench by a fire to make a space beside her. She left a hand’s breadth. She put her hand down on the pack in the space, open, palm up, the scar across it white.

Riarhin sat down.

The pack was not big enough for two. They sat on it anyway, hip against hip, in the gap in the bank, and the raven walked off the pack onto Dusana’s knee, and from Dusana’s knee onto Riarhin’s, and stood there and looked at her, and walked back. Dusana’s fingers found Riarhin’s hand on the pack between them. They sat like that.

Below them in the hollow, Halla came out of the long room with a pail and went to the pen, and the goats stood up on the rail and shouted at her. Bel was on the pond wall with his heels against the stones. Wren was on the step, half in, half out. Smoke went up from the chimney straight, because there was no wind.

“What do ravens know?” said Riarhin.

Dusana laughed. Not much. A breath, and the silver tooth.

“Ask it,” she said.

Riarhin looked at the raven on Dusana’s knee. It looked at her with one eye. It had a crumb of Halla’s porridge on its beak. It did not know one turning it had known; Dusana had said so, at the fork. It did not know the level way to the right, the way it had walked like it owned it going down. It knew Dusana’s knee. It knew the crust.

“No,” said Riarhin.

“No,” Dusana agreed. “Nor do I.”

After a while she got up, and put her pack on, and the raven walked up her arm to the top of it and settled, and she stood in the gap in the bank and looked at Riarhin.

“All right,” she said.

“All right.”

She went up the road. Riarhin sat on the earth in the gap where the pack had been and watched her go, short and broad, with her stick, and the raven on top of her pack facing backwards, which it preferred, looking back down the road at Riarhin until the road turned.

Chapter 47 — No throne waiting

The turn went down to the river.

Osk had said it would, sitting on the bank with her pegs. She had walked it the second day, slowly, on Haske’s arm, past the white stone and through the gap where the right bank had been, past the place where the door had been, which was a hole in a grey wall now with cold air coming out of it, and on, round the corner of the hill and down. She had pegged it as she went. She had come back in the evening and said that it went down a long way between banks and came out on shingle, and there was black water there, and that she had never in forty years seen where the turn went because the turn had never gone anywhere.

“The river only goes down,” said Riarhin.

“Then it goes down past there.” Osk had pushed a peg into the bank. “It’s the long reach. I know it by the smell.”

So on the fifth morning Riarhin went down the turn alone, past the white stone and the hole in the wall and round the corner of the hill, following Osk’s pegs, and came out on shingle in the grey, and there was the river.

It was the river she knew. Black and smooth and going one way, with mist lying on it so you could not see the far side. The shingle was the shingle she had walked with Dusana and Cadan, wet and hurt, after Farieth’s grey rooms; she recognised a stone on it, a long flat one with a white band through it, that she had stepped over going the other way. The long reach. Upstream, round the bend, the grey rooms had been. She did not go and look whether they still were.

She sat down on the flat stone with the white band and waited.

The ferryman came down out of the mist at midday, or what passed for midday, sculling stern-first so that he faced where he was going, as he always did.

His boat was full of luggage. Bundles, a chair legs-up, a sack that rustled, a box of something that clinked. He brought it in to the shingle a few lengths from her stone and held it there against the current with his oar, a big quiet man in an oilskin, and looked at her.

“You said after,” he said.

“It’s after.”

“I thought it might be.” He looked up the shingle, past her, at the opening in the bank where Osk’s pegs came down. “Something happened up there. The river went still. For about as long as it takes to say a prayer, the whole river stopped going down. I’ve never known it stop.” He shifted the oar. “Then it went on. A bit quicker, I thought. I’ve a word for you.”

“Give it.”

The ferryman drew a breath, the way he did when he was carrying a word in his head and not on paper, and said it in a voice that was not his own. It was a voice she had heard at a well under an overhang, with a cup turning in long hands.

“I am at the stream most days. If you come, I will be there. If you do not come, I will understand it.”

The ferryman let the breath go.

“That was the word,” he said. “He gave it me the day you asked me to keep it. Then he walked down to the Stream Landing again the next day, and the next, and asked if I’d given it. I said no, she said after.” He looked at the bundles in his boat. “Then the river stopped. And when it started again he was on the landing with his pack, and he said he’d come down, if I’d carry him. He said he’d not be cargo, he’d sit.” He jerked his head at the chair, legs-up, among the bundles. “He’s sat.”

Riarhin looked at the chair.

There was a man behind it. Sitting on a bundle in the stern of the boat behind the ferryman’s legs, with his knees drawn up, as he would have had to sit in so full a boat. She had not seen him. She had been looking at the ferryman.

He stood up.

He got out of the boat the way a man gets out of a boat who has done it a great many times, a hand on the gunwale and one long step over it onto the shingle, and the boat did not rock. He stood on the shingle. He was tall, taller than her, and lean, and pale-haired, and his face was the face she had seen at the well, under the overhang, in the lamp-light, the long face that she had looked at and looked away from and looked at again.

He did not come nearer. He stood a few lengths off on the shingle with the black water behind him and his hands at his sides. His right hand went up to his left shoulder, to the strap that was not there, and came down again.

“You said after,” he said.

“It’s after.”

“The river stopped,” he said. “I felt it from the stream. Everyone did. Saelith dropped a hammer.” He looked at her. “Was that you?”

“Some of it.”

He waited. When she did not say anything else he nodded, as though she had given him an answer he had expected, and looked about him at the shingle, and saw the flat stone with the white band through it beside her, and came and sat down on the far end of it.

“Tell me,” he said. “If you want to. You don’t have to.”

She told him.

She did not tell it well. She told it in the order it had happened, because that was the only order she had it in, the way Cadan told things, what came first and what came next. The stair in the hill district. The Rain Doors. Pell’s map and the wet climb. The ferry, and Na’ar, and the court, and the grey rooms. She did not tell him about Farieth. She had not told him before and she did not tell him now. She told him about Wenna’s, and the arch, and that Ailsa had been there, and that she had left after breakfast, and he did not ask who Ailsa was and she did not tell him. Sefe. The Weir. Halla’s hollow and the lane and the beam. The turn. The door. The cords.

“I took her hand,” she said. “I told her it was morning. She woke up.”

He was quiet a long time.

“That’s all?” he said.

“That’s all I did. Other people did the rest.” She looked at the river. “The keepers held the road. Cadan held the step. Dusana marked the way. Aurine carried people up a road that’s gone now. A girl born in a ditch chalked the turnings. A man I’d never met held the far end of a saw.” She looked at her hands. “I walked into a room and held on to somebody’s hand and wouldn’t let go.”

He turned his head and looked at her.

“That is not nothing,” he said.

“No.”

“I hoped—” He stopped. He looked at his own hands, long, the second finger of the right one crooked where it had been broken and badly set. “I hoped you would be able to do a thing like that. I hoped somebody would. One of you. I thought—” He stopped again. “You know what I thought. I told you at Saelith’s.”

“You thought I’d be a queen.”

“I thought you might lead people,” he said. “Our people. All three of them. Back to one place. I thought somebody with both the gifts that were divided might be able to stand between them and hold them, the way you held that door.” He turned the crooked finger with the thumb of his other hand. “And you did hold it. You held it for a lot of people. For a woman at a mill and a man in a yard and a girl in a ditch.” He looked at the river. “None of them Lethai.”

“No.”

“No.” He was quiet. “I minded that, a little, listening. I’m telling you so you know I minded it. I’m not telling you it was wrong.”

“Will you go back?” he said, after a while. “To them. The Lethai. Above. You could. You’ve the gifts. You’ve the sword. You’ve the—” He stopped. “You’ve what you did here. People would hear of it. The al would hear. They’d have to.”

Riarhin thought about it. She thought about it properly, because he had asked properly, and because he had sat on a bundle in a ferry full of luggage for a day and a half to ask it.

“I want to see Coryd,” she said. “My sister. Your daughter. She’s got a house in Valora with a woman called Sivelle, a stair that doesn’t creak, a newel post with acorns on it. She writes short letters and talks too long about stairs. She’s been expecting me on the first of the month for about a year.” She looked at the water. “I want to see Harissa. She dances. She has a bad leg and she does it anyway. She told me once my turn in her dance was the turn before you stab somebody. She was right. I want her to show me another one.”

He was very still.

“I want to hear Linias play the end of a song,” she said. “Bea’s song. He plays it going up. You liked it going up. Bea sang it going down.” She did not look at him. “I want to tell him which one I breathed to, in there. I want to see Zemia. I’m not sure I want to. I want to.” She stopped. “There’s a girl in Valora who’s queen. I killed her mother and her father. She writes to me. She asked if I’d answer and I said yes. I owe her a letter. I want to write it.” She stopped again. “And there’s a woman in a workshop under an overhang who wants a drawing of a bow. I said I’d send it.”

“Saelith,” he said.

“Saelith.”

He did not say anything for a long time.

“Those are people,” he said at last.

“Yes.”

“Not peoples.”

“No.”

He looked at her. Then he gave a short, surprised laugh, and he put his hand over his eyes, and his shoulders shook once.

“My mother would say you’d made a very bad bargain,” he said through his fingers.

“Your mother said a great many things.”

He took his hand down and looked at her, sharp, and she held his eye, and he did not ask. He looked at her a moment longer, and then away, at the river.

“Yes,” he said. “She did.”

He asked about the sword.

He asked it the way he had asked about the clasp at Saelith’s, as though it were the smallest thing in the world, a fitting, a matter of whether the block was seated.

“You still have it.”

“Yes.”

“I thought, when I heard you’d gone into the hill with it.” He looked at it on her back, the grip over her shoulder in Ghere’s binding. “I thought you might leave it. In there. In the cords. People do that, with things they’ve been given and didn’t ask for. Leave them somewhere that means something.”

“I cut myself free with it,” said Riarhin. “My foot went through the net. I drew it and cut the cords round my leg.” She looked at him. “That’s all it did. What a good knife would do. Only longer.”

He was quiet.

“It was made for a ruler,” he said. “That’s all I know of it. I’ve never known more. I don’t know whose hand it was in before mine. It made me think about things more slowly than I would have. I hoped it would do that for you.” He looked at the grip. “Did it?”

“I don’t know. I don’t know what I’d have been without it.” She put her hand up and touched the binding over her shoulder, Ghere’s pattern, cut on the slant at the end. “I’m keeping it. I’m not giving it a new name. I’m not taking it anywhere it belongs. I’m keeping it because it’s been on my back since the Valewood and my hand knows where it is.”

“And the bow?”

“On two pegs above a cutting table in a house on the Bronze Coast. With the mail, in the chest under the window. With the clasp that still opens badly.” She looked at him. “Ailsa’s daughter keeps them. I’ll go and get them. Some time. I’ll take Saelith her drawing first.”

He nodded slowly.

“The carving on the bow,” he said. “The back. I did that. The winter before. I told you at Saelith’s.”

“You did.”

“I never showed anybody. I meant to give it to—” He stopped. He did not say who. “Saelith will want to know it’s not her work. She’ll be cross about it.”

“She already is.”

He laughed again, the short surprised laugh, and then did not laugh, and they sat on the flat stone with the white band through it and looked at the river.

The ferryman shifted his oar.

He had held the boat against the shingle the whole time, in the current, without a word, a big quiet man in an oilskin with the luggage of the living piled round his knees. Now he moved the oar, and the boat moved a little, and he looked at the man on the stone.

“River’s going quicker,” he said. “Since it stopped. I’ve to be at the Reed Landing by dark.” He did not say it unkindly. “It only goes down.”

Her father stood up.

He stood on the shingle with his hands at his sides and looked down at her. His right hand went up to his left shoulder, to the strap that was not there.

“I walk back up from the Reed Landing,” he said. “It’s a long way. I don’t mind it.” He looked at the river, and back at her. “I’ll be at the stream. Most days. If you come by. If you don’t come by, I’ll understand it.”

“That was the word.”

“That was the word.” He hesitated. “It’s still the word.”

He went down the shingle to the boat. He put his hand on the gunwale and stepped in over it in one long step, and the boat did not rock, and he went past the ferryman’s legs to the stern and sat down on his bundle behind the chair with his knees drawn up.

The ferryman pushed off.

The boat went out from the shingle into the current, stern-first, the ferryman facing the way he was going, and the current took it, and it began to go, slowly and then less slowly, down the black water into the mist.

Riarhin stood up.

She had not asked him. She had sat beside him on a stone for the better part of a day and she had not asked him, and at the well under the overhang she had not asked him, and at the cord on the asking hook she had not asked him. She had not asked anybody. Not Saelith. Not Farieth, in the grey rooms, who would have told her, to wound her. She had told herself it did not matter. She had told herself that she had gone her whole life without numbering things and it was the same.

It was not the same.

She went down the shingle to the edge of the water. The boat was ten lengths out. Fifteen. The mist was coming down over it.

“What’s your name?” she called.

He heard her. She saw him hear her. He turned on his bundle in the stern, and half rose, and his face came round towards her over the luggage, the long pale face, and his mouth opened.

The mist came down.

It came down over the boat the way it had come down at the Reed Landing and on the long reach and over everything in that country, soft and grey and without hurry, and the boat was a shape in it, and then a darker place in the grey, and then the sound of the oar, going down, regular, and then the sound of the water on the shingle at her feet.

She stood there a long time.

Then she sat down on the flat stone with the white band through it, on the end where he had sat, and it was still a little warm, and she put her hand on the place.

Chapter 48 — The road with rain on it

Osk found them the way out, in the end, with her pegs.

For six days after the waking, she went out every morning from the turn with Haske on one side of her and Toller on the other and a bag of pegs on Haske’s back, and walked the low roads where they had moved, and pegged them, and came back in the evening and sat on the bank by her lantern and told Riarhin where they went. The roads had gone everywhere when the door opened, she said, like grain out of a split sack. Most of them had settled. Some had settled somewhere new. A few had not settled at all, and those she pegged the other way, and told Wren, and Wren went and chalked them with a cross instead of a circle, so that nobody would walk them.

On the sixth evening she came back and sat down by her lantern and did not say anything for a while.

“There’s a way up,” she said at last. “To your first station. The one with the well and the faceless one and the long tunnel. It goes up from the far side of Sefe’s ridge now, past the Weir, round the back of the arch. Long. Three days. It never went there before.” She turned a peg in her hands. “Wenna’s sent word along it already. She sent bread.”

“How do you know it goes there?”

Osk looked at her.

“Because Toller walked it,” she said. “All of it. Up and back. Her shoulder’s bad and she walked it, because I asked her.” She put the peg on the pile. “She said to tell you she still doesn’t like you.”

They went up past the Weir on the seventh morning, Riarhin and Cadan, with Wenna’s bread.

Merel walked them as far as the willows. Nine of them, still. The race running proper and the wheel turning and Joost in the door of the mill with flour to his elbows and the baby on his hip, lifting a hand. Merel did not go further. She stood at the last willow with her grey-green hands in her apron pockets and her eyes catching what light there was.

“If you come back,” she said, “come by the Weir. There’s bread here too.”

“I’ll come by.”

“You’ll not.” Merel smiled. It was a good smile, wry, with the Weir burr in it. “People say that. Come by anyway.”

Aurine had gone the day before.

She had gone the other way, down, with Tamsin, along a low road Osk had pegged that went towards a place Wren said smelt of shed skin and warm stones. Aurine had stood at the head of it in the morning with her satchel over her shoulder and the oiled box in her hand, and opened the box, and looked at the skin inside it.

It had moved. Riarhin had seen it. Lying in the box in the grey light, the narrow end of it had lifted, a little, towards the low road, and lain down.

“That’s six,” Aurine had said. She had shut the box. “That’s six times this year.” She had looked at Riarhin. “I’ll not say I’m going to find her. I’ll say I’m going where it moves.”

“And Tamsin?”

“Tamsin’s going where she likes,” said Tamsin, from under her hood. “Which is where the bread is better than this woman’s.”

Aurine had not looked at Cadan, going. Then, at the bend of the low road, she had stopped, and looked back, and lifted her hand to him. Just that. He had lifted his.

It rained on the long way up.

It rained the whole three days, a fine grey rain without wind that did not cross Wenna’s arch, when they passed it on the third morning, but fell on their side of it and stopped at the frame. Riarhin did not look through. She knew what was there. The yard, the mounting block, the chimney going. The orchard with its dyers’ line. Wenna in the yard with something in her hands. She walked past on the ridge road with the rain on her face and her coat wet through at the mended elbows, and did not look.

Cadan walked beside her and said nothing.

He had said very little since the step. He ate what was given him and slept when they stopped and walked when they walked, and in the evenings he went over his ropes, foot by foot, as he always had, feeling for wear. But he did not ask what she would like him to do. Riarhin had noticed it on the second day, and kept it, and not said anything. He had not asked once since Dusana said stop.

The first station was where it had been.

They came down to it by a way Riarhin did not know, round the back of a hill and through a stand of black thorn that had not been there, and out onto the slope above the court, and there it was below them: the rock-cut rooms round the court and the well in the middle with its tin cup, and the long tunnel’s mouth in the far wall, warm black stone. Radan was by the court wall with his wheel. Ambrus was going round the doors with his lamp-scissors, trimming. The keeper with no face of its own was in its doorway, wearing somebody’s face. Riarhin did not look to see whose.

Radan looked up from his wheel as they came down into the court. He looked at Riarhin, and at Cadan, and at the empty place on Riarhin’s shoulder and beside her where a short broad woman with a stick and a bird would have been.

“She stopped,” he said.

“At Altan’s. With her grandmother.”

Radan considered this, with the spoke he was trimming in one hand and the knife in the other. Then he nodded slowly, and went back to the spoke.

“She always did say she would,” he said. “Some day.”

The red cord was still at the iron ring by the tunnel’s mouth, where Cadan had tied it the first day. Wet, and darker than it had been, and the knot swollen tight. Cadan looked at it a long time. Then he put his hand on it, and followed it with his hand, into the tunnel.

They went up.

The tunnel was warm. The voices were in it. Riarhin heard them as she had heard them coming down, unfinished, a long way off and close, in tongues she almost had: the man asking if somebody had told Aud the gate was off its pin; a woman counting something; a child. She did not stop. She did not listen for any particular cadence. She walked up the warm black stone with her hand on the red cord and Cadan behind her, and somewhere about the middle of it she heard a voice say Sit down, courteously, in the tone of somebody who expects it to be done, and she did not stop for that either. She walked on. The voice did not follow.

The tunnel came up into grey light, and rain, and the smell of wet heather and woodsmoke and sheep.

The post was there. Brass-shod, with the red cord tied to it, and a lamp on a hook, unlit. A boy was sitting on a sack under it with a scab on his chin and his knees drawn up and a blanket round his shoulders against the rain. He looked up.

He pointed at her.

Then he got up off the sack and ran, through the rain, towards the wagons.

Gulshat came down from the circle on her stick, slowly, in the rain, with Batu running ahead of her and back and ahead of her again like a dog. She stopped a few paces off and looked at Riarhin. She looked at Cadan. She looked at the tunnel mouth behind them, dark under the heath.

“It’s not snowed,” she said.

“No.”

“You said the first snow.” Gulshat leaned on her stick. “It’s not snowed. So nobody’s had to ask for me. I’m glad of that. I’d have had to find something to tell them.”

She held out a hand. There was a letter in it, oilcloth wrapped, a little damp at the corners.

“Came by the carrier five days since,” she said. “Same man. Same mule.” She looked at Riarhin. “The ford’s had no stair in it since the morning I spoke down the tunnel. Not one morning. Hesk’s not been up asking for his pipe. Nobody’s come up the wrong way.” She paused. “Nine days ago, in the morning, the whole heath went still. Like the wind stopped. Only there wasn’t any wind. Sheep lay down. Then it was the same as before.”

Riarhin took the letter.

It was in Maren Thorne’s hand, square and careful, with a note at the bottom in Kelva’s, which sloped.

The line holds. Since the turn of the month we have had no displacement past the outer marks. The bell at the Weeping Gate answers the next post every night and the post answers back, which it has not done since Nereth. Pall Watch-Tower reports the stair has not shown in the crack for eleven nights. I do not know what you did. I am not writing to ask. We still owe the Protectorate the autumn grain and the escort has not left the export stores. Sivrik has a shipment agreed. Nereth is teaching Edda the second sequence. Esvin says to tell you he has been asked for the walking song four times since you left and has sung it wrong each time on purpose.

And under it, in Kelva’s sloping hand:

I am eating. Come back by the mill if you come back.

Riarhin checked the date. Maren had written before the waking. She read it twice, then folded it and put it inside her coat.

“Good news?” said Gulshat.

“News.” Riarhin looked at the rain. “The grain’s still owed.”

“It always is,” said Gulshat. “Come and eat.”

They ate at the cook fire under the awning, with the rain coming down beyond its edge in grey strings and the wagons standing round in their ring with their painted wheels.

It was barley again. Barley and mutton and onions and something sour she could not place, and she had eaten it on the night before they went down and had not placed it then, and did not place it now. Sovra was there. His daughter was on his knee, larger than she had been, with a spoon in her fist, and when she saw Riarhin she looked at her with great suspicion for a long time and then held out the spoon.

Riarhin took the spoon. The child took it back.

“That’s how she says hello,” said Sovra.

Cadan sat at the end of the bench by the fire with his bowl in his hands. He ate with his small neat movements. When he had finished he set the bowl down beside him, the spoon in it, the handle turned towards Gulshat, and folded his hands in his lap.

He did not ask what she would like him to do.

Riarhin waited for it. She waited through the bowl and the bread and Gulshat’s pot of something that was for wounds and was spread on the bread, and it did not come. He sat by the fire with his hands folded and looked at the rain.

“Cadan,” she said.

He turned his head.

“Where will you go?”

He was quiet a long time. The rain came down beyond the awning. Batu, on his sack by the post, was feeding the lamp.

“I have been thinking,” he said, “that I would like to go back to the house.” He said it slowly, as though trying the words for fit. “To tell them. What there is in the hill now, where she was. That the door is open. They will want to know. They have kept the Bell for her since before there was a house.” He looked at his hands. “They may not let me in. I was asked to leave. For what were called pedagogical severities.”

“Will you go anyway?”

“I will go anyway.” He looked at her. “Nobody has asked me to. I find I wish to.” He paused. “I do not think I have wished to do a thing that nobody asked me to do since I was a boy.”

She did not answer.

“You may ask me,” said Cadan, “to do something else. Instead. If you would like.”

“No.”

He looked at her a moment longer. Then he nodded, the small nod he gave when an omitted measurement had been supplied, and looked back at the rain.

In the morning she wrote three letters at Gulshat’s table, by the light of Batu’s lamp, with a pen that spluttered.

The first was to Coryd. It was short, because Coryd’s letters were short. I am coming. Not on the first. Soon. Tell Sivelle I’ll want to see the stair that doesn’t creak.

The second was to Matylde. It was longer, and she wrote it three times, and the third time she let it be. She did not tell Matylde what she had done. She told her that she had seen her father, at a distance, in a courtyard, practising alone. She told her that he had never gone more than a step from the wall. She did not know whether that was a thing a girl would want to know. She put it in.

The third was to Saelith, and it was not a letter. It was a drawing of a bow, the back of the limbs, the carving that followed the grain of the silvery wood, every line of it as she had kept it from the first day she had held it in the Valewood, with the rain on her face. She drew it slowly and exactly. When it was done she folded it and wrote on the outside For Saelith, at the overhang below the ravine. From the woman with the sword. After. and gave it to Gulshat, who said that the next person to go down with a knot would carry it, if she had to tie it round their neck.

Cadan had gone by the time she came out. He had left at first light, Batu said, pointing, east, along the heath, with his ropes and his lantern. He had left a coil of the tarred rope on the sack by the post, coiled the way he coiled things, and on top of it, the white candle out of his brass lantern, unlit, its wick untrimmed.

She stood at the Painted Stone in the rain.

It was warm under her hand. It was always warm. Its faded bands of paint went round it, red and ochre and blue, and the rain ran down them. Below her, the heath fell away to the river, and the ford, and the ford was a ford, brown water over stones, with mist lying on it, and no stair.

Her coat was wet through. Her boots were wet through. Her hair was stuck to her neck. She had a letter from the Mistlands inside her coat, and Gulshat’s pot of something for wounds in her pack, and Wenna’s last heel of bread, and Remorse on her back with Ghere’s binding gone dark in the rain, and the two knives at her belt, untied now from their sheaths, because she was not going to go upside down. She had the comb with three teeth missing, which Dusana had put back in her pack on the last morning without saying so.

The carrier’s track went north over the rise towards the coast road and the Mistlands and the mill behind the line. Another went west along the river towards country she had not seen. A third went east, the way Cadan had gone. Batu stood at the post with his lamp and watched her, waiting to see which she would take, so that he could point at it.

She stood a while longer with her hand on the warm stone and the rain on her face.

Then she picked up her pack, and settled it, and went.

Coda — The mist beyond the doors

The pump-house cellar had one door and Maudie had barred it with the pump handle.

It was the long iron handle off the town pump, the one that had stood in Tanner’s Square for as long as anyone could remember, and Old Siv had wrenched it off its pin in the dark with his bare hands when the impacts started, and carried it down the cellar steps on his shoulder, and put it across the door in the two brackets that had been made for a bar a hundred years ago and never used. It was the only thing in the town that fitted them.

There were eleven of them in the cellar. There had been fourteen when they came down.

The impacts had stopped an hour ago.

Nobody said so. Nobody wanted to be the one who said so. They had sat in the dark under the pump-house with the river going past the wall on the other side of the stones and listened to them come up the town from the bridge, one after another, at the same interval, the way a mill-wheel goes, the way a clock goes. Thud. And the count. Thud. And the count. Never faster. Never slower. Whatever it was had come up Bridge Street and across the square and down Tanner’s Row, and the sound of it had come through the earth into the cellar floor, into the soles of their feet and the seats of their trousers, at that interval, the same, until Tam had started counting it aloud under his breath and Maudie had put her hand over his mouth.

It had gone past the pump-house. It had not stopped.

It had gone on down Tanner’s Row towards the river stairs, and the sound had got fainter, still at the interval, and then it had stopped. Not faded. Stopped, between one and the next. As if somebody had put a hand on a turning wheel.

That was an hour ago.

Maudie had a lamp. It was a little brass thing with a glass chimney, from the shelf in the pump-house, and she had it on the floor by her knee turned down as low as it would go, and by its light she was binding Ennick’s arm.

It was a bad arm. He had been on the barricade at the bridge with the others when it came over, and he had put his arm up, and the thing had gone through the barricade and through the bridge-guard’s breastplates beside him, through all of them, at the interval, and something off the barricade had come down on his forearm and opened it. Maudie had cleaned it with water from the cistern and the last of the spirit from her bag and was binding it with a strip off her own petticoat, tight, and Ennick was sitting with his back against the wall and his face grey and his teeth set, and talking.

“It went through the plate,” he said. “Like it was cloth. Like it was cloth on a line. I saw it. Harl’s breastplate. Through the front and out the back and Harl with it, and it didn’t slow. It didn’t slow down.”

“Hold still,” said Maudie.

“It didn’t slow down. And the ones driving it.” His voice went up. “The ones driving it, the men, they’d run. They’d dropped their poles and run back over the bridge when we started shooting, and it didn’t stop. It came on. On its own. Nobody driving it. It came on up the street at the same—”

“Hold still.”

He held still. Maudie tied the strip off and sat back on her heels.

“There,” she said. “That’ll hold till we get you to somebody.”

Nobody asked who.

Old Siv was sitting on the cellar steps below the barred door with the pump handle across his knees.

He had taken it out of the brackets when the impacts stopped, to look at it, and nobody had made him put it back. He was turning it over in his big scarred hands in the lamp-light. He had been the town’s smith for forty years, before his eyes went, and the pump had been his. He had fitted the new barrel to it when Maudie was a girl. He had a thumb on a mark near the handle’s root, stamped into the iron, and he was rubbing at it.

“Look at that,” he said.

Nobody looked.

“Look at that,” he said again, louder. “Rosa. You’ve eyes. Look.”

Rosa came over from the corner where she had been sitting with her knees up. She was about forty, thin, with a bandage round her own head that Maudie had put there, and she crouched by the steps and looked where his thumb was.

It was a mark. A little device stamped into the iron. A wheel inside a ring, with a line through it.

“Pellam and Daughters,” said Old Siv. “Halvard’s Cross. I bought the barrel off them when this town had a pump that was a disgrace. Best barrel I ever fitted. That pump’s given clean water in a dry summer for twenty years and never seized.” He turned the handle in his hands. “You saw the side of that thing. On the bridge. When it came over.”

Rosa did not say anything.

“You saw the plate on the side,” said Siv. “By the joint. Where the arm went in.” He put his thumb back on the mark. “Wheel in a ring. Line through it.”

The cellar was very quiet. The river went past on the other side of the wall.

“That’s not right,” said Tam. He was ten and he was sitting against Maudie’s side with his knees up. “They make pumps.”

“They make pumps,” said Old Siv. “They made my pump.” He looked at the handle. “Somebody went and asked them for something else.”

“She’ll come,” said Ennick.

He said it into the dark at the end of the cellar, with his bound arm across his chest. Nobody had been talking. He said it the way you say a thing you have been saying in your head for an hour.

“She will. You’ve heard. At Oster’s Ford, the spring before last. They brought one of those things up to the ford and in the morning it was lying in the river on its side with the water going through it, and the men who’d brought it were—” He stopped. “She’ll come. They say she walks out of the mist. They say she always has. They say she was there before the mills.”

“Who says,” said Rosa.

“Everybody says.”

“Everybody said she’d come to Wendle.” Rosa had not moved from her crouch by the steps. Her voice was quite flat. “My husband was at Wendle. With the carters. When they came. He was in the grain store with the others and they barred the door like we have, and they waited, because everybody said she’d come. She walks out of the mist. Everybody said.” She looked at the lamp. “They waited three days.”

Nobody said anything.

“They found them in the grain store,” Rosa said. “All of them. Sitting with their backs to the wall. Waiting.” She looked at Ennick. “Did she come to Wendle?”

“I don’t know.”

“No.”

“Something did,” said Ennick. “After. To the men who did it. I heard. They were found on the Wendle road, on their own, a week after, with that thing of theirs stopped dead in the ditch beside them like a clock that’s been—” He stopped. “She came after.”

“After,” said Rosa.

“That’s something.”

Rosa looked at him for a long time across the lamp.

“It’s not my husband,” she said.

Maudie gave out the water.

There was a cistern in the corner of the cellar, a stone tank the pump drew from, and there was water in it, cold, a little brackish because the river was high and seeped in through the stones. She had found a dipper on a nail. She went round with it, one by one, all eleven, and gave each of them two mouthfuls, no more, and watched them drink, and took the dipper back. Tam she gave three. Nobody argued.

When she came to Rosa, Rosa drank her two mouthfuls and gave back the dipper and said, without looking up, “Thank you.”

“There’s more tomorrow,” said Maudie.

“Is there.”

“The river’s high. The cistern fills.” Maudie hung the dipper back on its nail. “There’s always more tomorrow, with water. That’s the one thing.”

The old woman in the corner, Ada, whom nobody had been able to get to speak since they came down the steps, had begun to cry, very quietly, in little catches, with her hands over her face. Maudie went and sat by her and put an arm round her and said nothing at all, and after a while the crying stopped.

It was Tam who heard it first.

He was the youngest and his ears were the best. He sat up against Maudie’s side and said “Listen,” and they listened, and at first there was nothing but the river on the other side of the stones.

Then it came. Faint, through the barred door and down the cellar steps, from outside, from the square. A voice. A man’s voice, calling, the way an officer calls across a yard. Two words, the same two, and then nothing, and then the same two again.

“That’s from the river stairs,” said Old Siv. He had his head up. “That’s down by the river stairs. Where it stopped.”

They listened.

The voice called again. The same two words. They could not make them out through the stones. It was the tone of somebody calling a name. Calling somebody who was expected. Here. Report. The tone a man uses at a doorway to a man who ought to be coming back.

Nobody answered him.

He called again. Louder. The same two words. And again. There was something in it now that had not been in it the first time, a lift at the end, a crack, the way a man’s voice goes up at the end when he calls into a house that ought to have somebody in it and does not.

Nothing answered.

“That’s them,” said Ennick, in a whisper. “That’s one of theirs. He’s calling for the one that was driving it. The one that went down to the stairs with it. He’s calling for him to come back.”

The voice called once more.

Nobody answered.
